The silence in the drawing-room was heavy, broken only by the rhythmic ticking of the clock and the distant murmur of the street below. It was a stillness that felt less like peace and more like a held breath, waiting for something to shatter.

Franklin Blake stood by the window, his back to the room, staring out at the darkened garden where the moon hung low and pale, casting long, distorted shadows across the lawn. He did not turn when the door opened, though he knew who it was before the figure stepped inside.

"You look troubled, Mr. Blake," said Rosanna Spearman. Her voice was soft, carrying the same gentle cadence she had used since her arrival, yet there was a new edge to it now—a sharpness born of the strange occurrences that had plagued the house for days.

Franklin turned slowly. His face was drawn, the lines of worry etched deeper than before. "Troubled? Perhaps. Or perhaps merely weary of the absurdity of it all. The diamond, Miss Spearman, has been stolen. Again."

Rosanna's eyes widened slightly, but she did not flinch. She moved closer, her gaze fixed on him with an intensity that seemed to pierce through his exhaustion. "Stolen again? But we thought... we thought the police had secured it."

"They have," Franklin replied, his tone flat. "But security is a fragile thing when the mind is the weapon. Someone here knows how to move without being seen, to vanish into the very walls of this house. And someone else knows how to watch."

He walked away from the window, his footsteps echoing softly on the parquet floor. "There are those who believe the theft is a game, a cruel jest played by a child or a prankster. But I see it differently. This is not a game. It is a warning. A message sent in diamonds and shadows."

Rosanna followed him, her expression grave. "And what is the message?"

"That we are not alone in this house," Franklin said, stopping to face her. "And that whatever force is at work here, it is patient. It waits for us to make a mistake, to lower our guard. Until then, we must remain vigilant. For the Moonstone does not simply disappear; it chooses its path, and if it has chosen to leave, it will return when it pleases."

The air between them seemed to thicken, charged with an unspoken tension. Outside, the wind picked up, rustling the leaves of the trees and sending a chill through the room. Somewhere in the distance, a dog barked, a lonely sound that faded quickly into the night.

"We must be ready," Rosanna said quietly, her voice steady despite the fear that must be gnawing at her. "Whatever comes next, we face it together."

Franklin nodded, though his eyes remained fixed on the darkness beyond the glass. "Together," he agreed. "For better or worse, we are bound to this mystery until its final resolution."

And so, under the pale light of the moon, they stood together in the shadow of the theft, two souls united by the weight of a stolen gem and the unknown dangers that lurked in the quiet corners of their home. The night stretched on, full of promises and threats, as the house waited for the next move in the unfolding drama.

I write these lines from India to my kin in England with a singular purpose: to lay bare the reason for my refusal to extend the right hand of friendship to my cousin, John Herncastle. The silence I have maintained regarding this rift has been misconstrued by family members whose regard I hold dear, leading them to believe my distance stems from mere pride or caprice. I implore you to withhold judgment until you have read the following narrative. I swear upon my honor that what follows is the absolute, unvarnished truth.

Our estrangement traces its roots to a momentous public event in which we were both bound: the storming of Seringapatam, under the command of General Baird, on the fourth of May, 1799. To understand the gravity of our divergence, I must briefly revisit the atmosphere preceding the assault, specifically the wild rumors circulating within our camp concerning the treasures hoarded within the Palace of Seringapatam.

Among these tales, none was more extravagant than the legend of the Yellow Diamond, a gem renowned in the ancient chronicles of India. Tradition holds that this stone was once set in the brow of the four-armed deity who embodies the Moon. Its unique hue, coupled with a superstitious belief that it resonated with the divine presence it adorned—growing and fading in brilliance with the lunar cycle—earned it the enduring name in India: **THE MOONSTONE**. A similar, though lesser, superstition once gripped ancient Greece and Rome, applying not to a diamond serving a god, but to a semi-transparent stone of lower rank, believed to be swayed by lunar tides; this too bestowed a "moon" name upon the gem, a designation collectors still recognize today.

The saga of the Yellow Diamond begins in the eleventh century of our era. Then, the conqueror Mahmoud of Ghizni swept across India, seizing the holy city of Somnauth and stripping the centuries-old temple of its riches. Yet, amidst the looting, the moon-god alone escaped the greed of the invading forces. Preserved by three Brahmins, the inviolate deity, bearing the Yellow Diamond upon its brow, was spirited away by night and transported to Benares, the second sacred city of India.

Here, in a new sanctuary—a hall inlaid with precious stones beneath a roof upheld by pillars of gold—the moon-god was reinstalled. On the very night the shrine was consecrated, Vishnu the Preserver appeared to the three Brahmins in a vision. The deity breathed his divine essence upon the Diamond resting on the god's forehead. The priests knelt, veiling their faces in their robes, as the deity commanded that the Moonstone be guarded perpetually by three priests in rotation, day and night, unto the end of human generations. They bowed to this decree. The god foretold doom for any presumptuous mortal who dared seize the sacred gem, and for every soul and lineage that would inherit it thereafter. This prophecy was etched in gold letters above the shrine's gates.

Centuries passed, generation following generation, as the descendants of the three guardians kept their vigil. By the early years of the eighteenth century, during the reign of Aurungzebe, Emperor of the Moguls, chaos returned to the temples of Brahmah. Under his command, the shrine of the four-handed god was desecrated; sacred beasts were slaughtered, idols shattered, and the Moonstone was snatched by a high-ranking officer in the imperial army.

Unable to reclaim their treasure by force, the guardian priests pursued it in disguise. Generations succeeded one another; the sacrilegious warrior perished in misery, and the Moonstone, carrying its curse, passed from one unlawful hand to another. Yet, through every shift of fortune, the heirs of the three priests maintained their watch, awaiting the day Vishnu would restore their sacred charge. Time marched from the first to the final years of the eighteenth century until the Diamond fell into the possession of Tippoo, Sultan of Seringapatam. He ordered it mounted as an ornament in the hilt of a dagger, commanding it be kept among the choicest treasures of his armory. Even then, within the Sultan's own palace, the three guardian priests, disguised as officers of Tippoo's household who had feigned conversion to the Muslim faith, kept their secret vigil.

Thus ran the fanciful tale of the Moonstone as recounted in our camp. It left no mark on any of us save my cousin, whose penchant for the marvelous led him to accept it as fact. On the eve of the assault, he was absurdly incensed that I and others dismissed the story as mere fiction. A foolish quarrel ensued, and Herncastle's volatile temper overwhelmed him. In his boastful manner, he declared that if the English army conquered Seringapatam, we would see the Diamond gleaming upon his finger. The remark drew a roar of laughter, and so, we all believed that night, the matter ended.

Let us now turn to the day of the assault itself.

My cousin and I were separated at the outset. I did not witness him as we forded the river, nor when we raised the English flag at the first breach, nor as we crossed the ditch and fought our way into the town. We met only at dusk, once the place was ours and General Baird had discovered the corpse of Tippoo beneath a heap of the slain. Herncastle and I reunited then.

We were each assigned to a party ordered by the General to curb the plunder and confusion that inevitably followed our victory. The camp-followers had already committed abominable excesses; worse, soldiers had found their way through an unguarded door into the palace treasury, loading themselves with gold and jewels. It was in the courtyard outside the treasury that my cousin and I met to enforce discipline upon our own troops. I observed that Herncastle's fiery temper had been driven to a frenzied state by the terrible slaughter we had endured. In my judgment, he was wholly unfit to perform the duty entrusted to him.

There was riot and confusion within the treasury, though no violence that I witnessed directly. The men disgraced themselves with a good-natured recklessness; rough jests and catchwords flew among them. Suddenly, the legend of the Diamond resurfaced as a mischievous jest. "Who's got the Moonstone?" became the rallying cry, causing the plundering to flare up anew wherever it was momentarily halted. While I vainly attempted to restore order, I heard a dreadful yell on the opposite side of the courtyard and ran toward the sound, fearing a fresh outbreak of pillage.

I reached an open door and found the bodies of two Indians—judging by their dress, officers of the palace—lying across the entrance, dead. A cry from within hurried me into a room that served as an armoury. There, a third Indian, mortally wounded, was sinking at the feet of a man whose back was turned to me. The man spun around the instant I entered. I saw John Herncastle, torch in one hand, a dagger dripping with blood in the other. As he turned upon me, a stone set like a pommel at the end of the dagger's handle flashed in the torchlight, gleaming like fire. The dying Indian sank to his knees, pointed to the weapon in Herncastle's grasp, and spoke in his native tongue: *"The Moonstone will have its vengeance yet on you and yours!"* With those words, he fell dead upon the floor.

Before I could intervene, the men who had followed me across the courtyard crowded in. My cousin rushed to meet them like a madman. "Clear the room!" he shouted at me, "and set a guard on the door!" The soldiers fell back as he threw himself upon them, brandishing his torch and dagger. I stationed two sentinels from my own company, men I could trust, to hold the door. For the remainder of that night, I saw no more of my cousin.

Early the next morning, while plundering continued, General Baird announced publicly by beat of drum that any thief caught in the act, whoever he might be, would be hanged. The provost-marshal was present to demonstrate the General's seriousness. In the throng that gathered, Herncastle and I met again.

He held out his hand, as was his custom, saying, "Good morning."

I paused before returning the gesture.

"Tell me first," I said, "how the Indian in the armoury met his death, and what those final words meant when he pointed to the dagger in your hand."

"The Indian met his death, as I suppose, by a mortal wound," Herncastle replied. "What his last words meant, I know no more than you do."

I studied him closely. The frenzy of the previous day had subsided. I resolved to grant him another chance.

"Is that all you have to tell me?" I asked.

"That is all," he answered.

I turned my back on him, and we have not spoken since.

It must be understood that what I write here concerning my cousin (unless some necessity arises to make it public) is intended solely for the information of the family. Herncastle has uttered nothing that would justify me in accusing him before our commanding officer. He has been taunted more than once regarding the Diamond by those who recall his angry outburst prior to the assault; yet, as one might imagine, his own memory of the circumstances under which I surprised him in the armoury has been sufficient to keep him silent. It is reported that he intends to transfer to another regiment, ostensibly to separate himself from *me*. Whether this is true or not, I cannot bring myself to become his accuser—and I believe with good reason. If I were to make this matter public, I possess only moral evidence. I have no proof that he killed the two men at the door; I cannot even assert with certainty that he killed the third man inside, for my own eyes did not witness the deed being committed. It is true that I heard the dying Indian's words; but if those words were merely the ravings of delirium, how could I contradict such a claim based on my own knowledge? Let our relatives, on either side, form their own opinions on what I have written and decide for themselves whether the aversion I now feel toward this man is well or ill-founded.

Though I attach no credence to the fantastic Indian legend of the gem, I must acknowledge, before concluding, that I am influenced by a personal superstition in this matter. It is my conviction—or perhaps my delusion—that crime carries its own fatality. I am not only persuaded of Herncastle's guilt; I am even fanciful enough to believe that he will live to regret it if he keeps the Diamond, and that others will live to regret taking it from him if he gives it away.

The opening pages of *Robinson Crusoe* offer a warning that has haunted me since I first stumbled upon it yesterday: *"Now I saw, though too late, the Folly of beginning a Work before we count the Cost, and before we judge rightly of our own Strength to go through with it."*

It was only this morning, May twenty-first, 1850, that Mr. Franklin Blake, my lady's nephew, visited me. He spoke briefly but with a gravity that settled heavily in my chest.

"Betteredge," he began, "I have consulted the lawyer regarding certain family affairs. Among them, the loss of the Indian Diamond from my aunt's home in Yorkshire two years past. Mr. Bruff and I are of one mind: the full truth of this affair must be committed to writing, and soon."

I nodded, agreeing readily. It is always prudent to align with the lawyer's counsel, and peace is a commodity I value highly. Yet, Mr. Blake pressed on, his eyes fixed on me.

"In this matter," he continued, "innocent lives have already been tarnished by suspicion. Their reputations may suffer further without a permanent record for future generations to consult. This strange saga of our family demands to be told. Mr. Bruff and I believe we have found the proper method."

He explained their plan with the confidence of men who have already solved the puzzle. They intended to reconstruct the history of the Moonstone chronologically. First, they would detail how the gem fell into the hands of my late master, Captain Herncastle, during his service in India half a century prior—a narrative they claimed to possess in an old family document. Next, they would recount its arrival at the manor in Yorkshire and its subsequent disappearance within twelve hours.

"And then?" I asked, feeling the weight of the question.

"Then," Mr. Blake smiled, "we turn to you, Betteredge. You know the household better than anyone. You witnessed the days leading up to the theft. You must take the pen and begin where we leave off."

At that moment, I understood my role. I was to be the chronicler of the final act. My heart, however, did not leap with pride; it sank with dread. I feigned humility, declaring myself unequal to such a monumental task. Privately, I knew I possessed the wit to undertake it, provided I were given a fair chance. But Mr. Blake saw through my modesty instantly. He refused to accept my refusal and insisted I proceed.

Two hours have now elapsed since Mr. Blake departed. As soon as his footsteps faded down the hall, I retreated to my writing desk. I sat there, paralyzed, staring at the blank page. Despite my capabilities, I found myself unable to move. The words of *Robinson Crusoe* echoed in my mind, a prophecy I had foolishly ignored until it was too late.

Do not mistake my hesitation for ignorance. I am a scholar, a man of seventy years with an active memory and steady legs. I have spent decades debating the very existence of *Robinson Crusoe*, arguing that no such book could ever be written. Yet, I have relied on it for comfort in every crisis: when my spirits were low, when I sought counsel, when my wife vexed me, and even when I had taken a drink too many. I have worn out six sturdy copies of the book in my service; my lady gifted me a seventh on her last birthday. That night, I drank too freely, and it was *Robinson Crusoe* that restored my balance. A fine volume, bound in blue for four shillings and sixpence, complete with an illustration.

But does this rambling reflection truly constitute the start of the Diamond's story? Am I merely wandering aimlessly? Let us try again, on a fresh sheet, with my deepest respect for your patience.

I touched upon my lady a moment ago. Now, logic dictates that the Diamond could never have vanished from our household if it had not first been gifted to my lady's daughter. And that daughter, in turn, would never have existed to receive such a gift without the labor and pain of my lady bringing her into the world. Therefore, if we trace our inquiry backward to my lady, we shall certainly begin far enough back to catch the thief. And let me assure you, when one undertakes a task as complex as this, finding a solid starting point is a genuine comfort.

Those familiar with the high society of the day will know the three renowned Miss Herncastles: Adelaide, Caroline, and Julia—the youngest and, in my humble opinion, the finest of the trio. I possessed ample opportunity to judge their character, as you shall soon discover. I entered the service of their father, the old Lord, at the tender age of fifteen, serving as a page-boy in attendance upon the three honorable young ladies. I remained in that capacity until Miss Julia wed the late Sir John Verinder. He was an excellent man, though one who required management; and, between ourselves, he found someone to manage him. He prospered under her care, grew stout and content, and lived happily until the day she gently relieved him of his final breath and closed his eyes forever.

I must omit to mention that I accompanied the bride to her husband's estate. "Sir John," she declared, "I cannot function without Gabriel Betteredge." To which Sir John replied, "My lady, neither can I." Thus was my fate sealed. It mattered little to me where I served, provided my mistress and I were united.

Noticing that my lady took a keen interest in agricultural affairs and the management of the farms, I naturally adopted the same passion, especially since I was the seventh son of a small farmer myself. She placed me under the supervision of the bailiff, I performed my duties to the best of my ability, satisfied everyone, and earned my promotion. Years later, on a Monday, my lady remarked, "Sir John, your current bailiff is a senile old fool. Pension him generously and appoint Gabriel Betteredge to his place." By Tuesday, Sir John had agreed: "My lady, the bailiff is pensioned; Gabriel Betteredge takes his post." You hear much of couples living in misery; here is a testament to the contrary. Let it serve as a warning to some and encouragement to others. Meanwhile, I shall proceed with my narrative.

Thus, I found myself in a position of trust and honor, residing in a modest cottage of my own. My mornings were filled with rounds across the estate, my afternoons with accounts, and my evenings with a pipe and a copy of *Robinson Crusoe*. What more could a man desire to ensure happiness? Recall what Adam sought when he was alone in the Garden of Eden; if you do not blame Adam, do not blame me.

The woman who caught my eye was the housekeeper at my cottage, Selina Goby. I align with the late William Cobbett regarding the selection of a wife: observe that she chews her food thoroughly and places her feet firmly upon the earth when walking, and you are safe. Selina Goby excelled in both regards, which was one reason for my proposal. I had another, purely practical motive. As a single woman, she charged me a weekly fee for her board and services. As my wife, she could not charge me, and would instead provide her labor gratis. This was my perspective: economy tempered with affection. I presented this argument to my mistress as a matter of duty.

"I have been contemplating Selina Goby," I stated, "and I believe, my lady, it would be more economical to marry her than to retain her services."

My lady erupted in laughter, declaring she could not decide whether to be more shocked by my language or my principles. Some jest tickled her fancy, a sort of wit reserved for those of quality. Understanding nothing of the joke, I simply proceeded to act on my conviction. And what did Selina say? Good heavens, how little you must know of women if you expect otherwise. Of course, she said yes.

As the wedding date approached, and discussions arose regarding a new coat for the ceremony, my confidence wavered. I have consulted with other men in similar predicaments; they all admitted that roughly a week prior to the event, they privately wished to escape their situation. I went even further; I literally rose up and attempted to withdraw. For nothing! A man of justice cannot expect to exit such a contract without compensation. Adhering to the law, and after careful deliberation, I offered Selina Goby a feather-bed and fifty shillings to dissolve the engagement. You will scarcely believe it, yet it is true—she was foolish enough to refuse.

After that, the matter was settled. I procured the cheapest coat available and endured the remainder of the process with equal frugality. We were neither a happy nor a miserable couple; we were six of one and half a dozen of the other. I cannot explain why, but we seemed constantly to obstruct one another's path. When I ascended the stairs, she descended; when she sought to go down, I came up. Such is married life, according to my experience.

Five years of such stairway misunderstandings passed before Providence, in its infinite wisdom, relieved us of each other by taking my wife. I was left with my daughter, Penelope, and no other child. Shortly thereafter, Sir John passed away, leaving my lady with her own daughter, Miss Rachel, and no other heir. I have written in vain if you need to be reminded that my little Penelope was cared for under my mistress's direct supervision, sent to school, educated, and eventually promoted to become Miss Rachel's personal maid.

As for myself, I continued my duties as bailiff year after year until Christmas 1847, when a change occurred in my life. On that day, my lady invited herself to tea in my cottage. She noted that, counting from the year I began as a page-boy under the old lord, I had served her for over fifty years. She then handed me a magnificent woolen waistcoat, woven by her own hands, to keep me warm against the biting winter chill.

I received this splendid gift at a loss for words to express my gratitude for the honor she bestowed. To my astonishment, however, the waistcoat was not merely an honor but a bribe. My lady had discerned that I was aging before I had realized it myself. She had come to my cottage to wheedle me—though perhaps "persuade" is a kinder term—into relinquishing my arduous outdoor work as bailiff and accepting a life of ease as her house steward. I fought this indignity as best I could. Yet, my mistress knew my weakness; she framed the offer as a favor to herself. The dispute concluded with me wiping my eyes, like a simpleton, with my new woolen garment, and promising to consider the matter.

The turmoil in my mind regarding this decision was truly dreadful once my lady departed. I applied the remedy I have never yet found to fail in times of doubt and emergency: I smoked a pipe and turned to *Robinson Crusoe*. Within five minutes of reading that extraordinary book, I encountered a comforting passage on page one hundred and fifty-eight: "Today we love, what tomorrow we hate." My path became clear instantly. Today, I favored remaining the farm-bailiff; tomorrow, citing *Robinson Crusoe*, I would be entirely opposed. By taking myself tomorrow while in tomorrow's mood, the matter was resolved. Having thus relieved my conscience, I slept that night as Lady Verinder's farm-bailiff and awoke the next morning as her house-steward. All quite comfortable, and all thanks to *Robinson Crusoe*!

My daughter Penelope has just glanced over my shoulder to see my progress. She remarks that my writing is beautiful and every word true. However, she points out a significant flaw. She states that what I have written so far bears no resemblance to what was requested. I am asked to recount the story of the Diamond, yet I have been narrating the chronicle of my own life. This is curious and beyond my comprehension to explain. I wonder if the gentlemen who make a living writing books ever find their own selves intruding upon their subjects as I have? If they do, I can empathize with them. For now, here is another false start and more wasted paper. What is to be done? Nothing, I suppose, but for you to maintain your temper and for me to begin this tale anew for the third time.

The dilemma of how to commence this narrative properly has plagued me, leading to two distinct failures. First, I scratched my head until it ached, yielding nothing but confusion. Second, I consulted my daughter, Penelope, which resulted in a proposal so novel it nearly derailed the story entirely.

Penelope's suggestion was that I chronicle the events day by day, commencing with the specific date we received word that Mr. Franklin Blake was arriving. She argued that anchoring memory to such a rigid schedule would force the past to reveal itself. The only hurdle was retrieving the dates themselves, a task Penelope offered to solve by consulting her diary—a habit instilled during her schooling and maintained ever since. However, when I countered with the idea that she should narrate the tale directly from her journal, she reacted with a fierce glare and a flushed countenance. "My journal is for my own private eye," she declared, "and no living soul shall ever know its contents but myself." When I pressed her on this secrecy, she simply dismissed me with a sharp "Fiddlesticks!" to which I replied, "Sweethearts."

Accepting her initial plan, I must record that on a Wednesday morning, the twenty-fourth of May, 1848, I was summoned to my lady's private sitting room.

"Gabriel," my lady said, "there is news that will astonish you. Franklin Blake has returned from abroad. He has been residing with his father in London and is arriving tomorrow to stay until next month, in time for Rachel's birthday."

Had I possessed a hat in my hand, nothing short of profound respect would have stopped me from hurling it against the ceiling. I had not seen Mr. Franklin since his boyhood, when he lived within these walls. As I recalled him, he was the finest boy ever known for spinning tops or breaking windows. Miss Rachel, who was present, offered a starkly different recollection. "She" remembered him as the most atrocious tyrant who ever tormented a doll and the harshest driver of an exhausted child in string harness that England could produce. "I burn with indignation, and I ache with fatigue," Miss Rachel summarized, "whenever I think of Franklin Blake."

You may wonder how Mr. Franklin managed to vanish from his homeland for so many years, growing from a boy into a man abroad. The answer lies in his father's unfortunate status as the next heir to a Dukedom, a claim he could not legally prove.

Here is the brief history: My lady's eldest sister wed the celebrated Mr. Blake, a man renowned equally for his immense wealth and his protracted legal battle. For years, he besieged the nation's tribunals, attempting to displace the current Duke and secure the title for himself. He drained the pockets of countless lawyers and tore apart the lives of innocent bystanders in his quest. Yet, his wife and two of his three children died before the courts finally rejected his claims. When the dust settled and the rightful Duke remained in possession, Mr. Blake concluded that the only way to balance the scales was to deny his country the honor of educating his son. "How can I trust my native institutions?" he asked, "after the manner in which they have treated *me*?" Compounded by a deep-seated dislike for all boys, including his own, the decision was inevitable. Master Franklin was removed from England and sent to institutions in Germany, a land his father trusted. Meanwhile, Mr. Blake remained in England, continuing his parliamentary career and publishing an unfinished treatise on the Duke's legitimacy that remains incomplete to this day.

Thank heavens for that explanation! We need trouble our heads no longer about Mr. Blake, the senior. Let the Dukedom handle him; let us focus on the Diamond.

The Diamond, however, brings us back to Mr. Franklin, the innocent instrument by which that cursed jewel entered our home.

Our dear boy did not forget us. He corresponded periodically, writing to my lady, Miss Rachel, and myself. Prior to his departure, he had borrowed from me a ball of string, a four-bladed knife, and seven shillings and sixpence—a coin of a color I have never seen again and never hope to witness. His letters mostly concerned further borrowings. Through my lady, I learned of his progress abroad. After mastering the German institutions, he turned to France, then Italy, becoming something of a universal genius among them. He wrote, painted, sang, played, and composed, though I suspect he borrowed the inspiration for all these arts just as he had borrowed from me. Upon reaching adulthood, his mother's fortune of seven hundred pounds a year fell to him, only to vanish through him as quickly as water through a sieve. The more money he possessed, the more he craved; there was a hole in Mr. Franklin's pocket that no needle could stitch. Yet, wherever he went, his lively, easy demeanor made him welcome. He lived everywhere, his address often listed as "Post Office, Europe—to be left till called for." He resolved twice to return to England, but twice some unmentionable woman stood in his path. His third attempt, as you know, succeeded. On Thursday, the twenty-fifth of May, we were to behold for the first time the man he had become. He was of good blood, possessed high courage, and was twenty-five years old by our reckoning. Thus, you now know as much of Mr. Franklin Blake as I did before his arrival.

The Thursday was a summer day of exquisite beauty. My lady and Miss Rachel, not expecting Mr. Franklin until dinner, drove out to lunch with friends in the neighborhood.

Once they departed, I inspected the bedroom prepared for our guest, ensuring everything was in order. As the butler of my lady's household and, at my own request, the steward of the late Sir John's cellar, I felt it my duty to fetch some of our famous Latour claret. I set the bottles in the warm summer air to remove the chill before dinner. Deciding that what is beneficial for old wine is equally good for old age, I took my beehive chair to sit in the back court. Just as I settled in, a sound like the soft beating of a drum reached my ears from the terrace in front of the house.

Rounding the terrace, I discovered three mahogany-skinned Indians dressed in white linen frocks and trousers, gazing upward at the house. Closer inspection revealed they carried small hand-drums slung across their chests. Behind them stood a delicate, light-haired English boy carrying a bag. I initially judged them to be strolling conjurers, with the boy serving as their apprentice carrying their tools. One of the three, who spoke English and displayed manners of remarkable elegance, confirmed my assessment. He politely requested permission to perform his tricks in the presence of the lady of the house.

Now, I am not a sour old man. I generally favor amusement and am the last to distrust someone merely because their skin tone differs slightly from my own. However, even the best of us have weaknesses. My particular flaw, when I know a valuable plate-basket is left unattended on a pantry table, is to be instantly reminded of its vulnerability by the sight of a stranger whose manners surpass my own. Consequently, I informed the Indian that the lady was away and warned him and his party to leave the premises. He returned a beautiful bow, and they departed. On my side, I returned to my beehive chair, settled on the sunny side of the court, and, if the truth must be admitted, drifted into a state bordering on sleep.

I was abruptly roused by my daughter Penelope running out at me as if the house were ablaze. What did she want? She demanded the immediate arrest of the three Indian jugglers. Her reasoning was that they knew Mr. Franklin was coming from London and intended to commit some mischief against him.

The mention of Mr. Franklin's name snapped me awake. I opened my eyes and urged Penelope to explain.

It transpired that Penelope had just visited the lodge, where she had chatted with the lodge-keeper's daughter. The two girls had witnessed the Indians leaving after I had warned them off, followed by the little boy. Suspecting the boy was being mistreated by the foreigners—though for reasons I could not discern, perhaps simply because he was pretty and delicate—the girls had slipped along the inner side of the hedge separating us from the road to watch the proceedings from the outside.

Their observations revealed extraordinary tricks. First, they scanned the road in both directions to ensure they were alone. Then, all three turned to stare hard at our house. They began to jabber and dispute in their own language, looking at one another with expressions of doubt. Finally, they all turned to the little English boy as if expecting him to assist them. The chief Indian, who spoke English, then addressed the boy: "Hold out your hand."

Upon hearing these dreadful words, Penelope confessed she did not know what prevented her heart from flying straight out of her body. I privately suspected it might have been her stays. "You make my flesh creep," I remarked. (*Nota bene:* Women appreciate such little compliments.)

When the Indian commanded, "Hold out your hand," the boy recoiled, shook his head, and refused. The Indian then asked kindly if he would prefer to be sent back to London, left where they found him sleeping in an empty basket in a market—a hungry, ragged, and forsaken child. This seemingly ended the difficulty. Reluctantly, the boy held out his hand. Immediately, the Indian produced a bottle from his bosom and poured a black substance, resembling ink, into the palm of the boy's hand. Touching the boy's head and making signs in the air, the Indian said, "Look." The boy became rigid, standing like a statue gazing into the ink pooled in his hand.

At this point, it appeared to me to be mere juggling accompanied by a foolish waste of ink. I was beginning to drift back to sleep when Penelope's next words stirred me again.

The Indians scanned the road once more. Then the chief Indian instructed the boy: "See the English gentleman from foreign parts."

"I see him," the boy replied.

"Is it on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today?" the Indian asked.

"It is on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today," the boy answered.

The Indian paused, then asked a second question: "Has the English gentleman got It about him?"

"Yes," the boy answered, also after a brief pause.

A third and final question followed: "Will the English gentleman come here, as he has promised to come, at the close of day?"

"I can't tell," the boy said.

"Why not?" the Indian asked.

"I am tired. The mist rises in my head, and puzzles me. I can see no more today."

With that, the interrogation ended. The chief Indian spoke to his companions in their own tongue, pointing to the boy and then toward the town where they were lodged. After making further signs on the boy's head and blowing on his forehead to wake him with a start, they all proceeded toward the town, disappearing from the girls' sight.

Most things possess a moral if one looks for it. What was the moral here?

As I saw it: First, the chief juggler had overheard discussions of Mr. Franklin's arrival among the servants and saw an opportunity for profit. Second, he and his men intended to linger until they saw my lady drive home, then return to foretell Mr. Franklin's arrival via magic. Third, Penelope had heard them rehearse their hocus-pocus like actors practicing a play. Fourth, I should keep a vigilant eye on the plate-basket that evening. Fifth, Penelope should calm down and allow me, her father, to doze in the sun.

This seemed the sensible course. Knowing the ways of young women, you will not be surprised to learn Penelope would not accept this view. To her, the moral was grave. She fixated on the Indian's third question: "Has the English gentleman got It about him?" "Oh, father!" she cried, clasping her hands, "do not joke about this. What does 'It' mean?"

"We shall ask Mr. Franklin, my dear," I said, "if you can wait until he arrives." I winked to indicate I was joking. Penelope took it entirely seriously. Her earnestness amused me. "What on earth should Mr. Franklin know about it?" I inquired. "Ask him," she insisted. "And see whether *he* thinks it a laughing matter, too." With that final remark, she left me.

Once she was gone, I decided I must indeed ask Mr. Franklin, primarily to ease Penelope's mind. What occurred between us when I did so later that day will be detailed in its proper place. However, I must warn you here, before we proceed further, that you will find not a ghost of a joke in our conversation regarding the jugglers. To my great surprise, Mr. Franklin, like Penelope, took the matter very seriously. How seriously, you will understand, when I tell you that, in his opinion, "It" referred to the Moonstone.

I apologize for holding you captive with my own affairs and this beehive of a chair. I am fully aware that a dozing elderly man in a sun-drenched garden is hardly captivating material. Yet, facts must be recorded exactly as they unfolded, and I implore your patience for a few more moments while I await the arrival of Mr. Franklin Blake later this afternoon.

Before I could succumb to sleep again following my daughter Penelope's departure, the rhythmic clatter of dishes in the servants' hall signaled that dinner was served. As one who prefers to dine in my own sitting room, I had no part in the household meal save for offering my best wishes for their digestion before returning to my chair. Just as I stretched my limbs, another woman collided with me. It was not Penelope, but Nancy, the kitchen maid. I had been blocking her path, and I noted immediately that her expression was sullen—a demeanor I, as head of the staff, never permit to pass unchallenged.

"What are you neglecting your dinner for?" I asked. "What troubles you, Nancy?"

Nancy attempted to push past me without reply. Seizing the moment, I rose and grasped her by the ear. She is a pleasant, plump young woman, and it is my custom to demonstrate my personal approval of a girl in this manner.

"What is the matter now?" I insisted.

"Rosanna is late again for dinner," Nancy replied. "I've been sent to fetch her. All the heavy lifting falls on my shoulders in this house. Leave me be, Mr. Betteredge!"

The Rosanna in question was our second housemaid. Possessing a certain pity for her, and observing that Nancy intended to summon her with unnecessary harshness, I decided I had no urgent business elsewhere and might as well retrieve Rosanna myself. I knew she would receive a gentle reminder regarding punctuality far better from me.

"Where is Rosanna?" I inquired.

"At the sands, of course!" Nancy retorted, tossing her head. "She suffered another fainting fit this morning and requested fresh air. I have no patience with her!"

"Return to your dinner, my girl," I said. "I have patience with her, and I shall fetch her."

Nancy, possessing a formidable appetite, looked gratified. When she is pleased, she appears lovely; when she appears lovely, I affectionately pat her under the chin. It is not immorality—it is merely habit.

Thus, I took my stick and prepared to depart for the sands.

Wait! It would be premature to leave just yet. I must detain you further to recount the tale of the sands and the story of Rosanna, for the matter of the Diamond is intimately connected to both. How desperately I strive to advance my narrative without digression, and how poorly I succeed! But alas, persons and circumstances arise so vexatiously in this life that they demand attention. Let us proceed calmly and briefly; we shall soon be immersed in the mystery, I promise you!

Rosanna, placing the person before the thing as a mark of basic politeness, was the sole new addition to our household. Approximately four months prior to the present time, my lady visited London and toured a Reformatory designed to rescue fallen women from returning to criminal lives after release from prison. The matron, noting my lady's interest, presented a girl named Rosanna Spearman and recounted a most tragic history, one I lack the heart to detail here, for neither do I wish to be made wretched without purpose, nor do I believe you would relish it. The conclusion was that Rosanna Spearman had been a thief; though not of the caliber to organize city-wide robberies, the law seized her, leading inevitably to prison and the reformatory. Despite her actions, the matron believed Rosanna was a rare soul, one in a thousand, who merely required an opportunity to prove her worthiness of Christian compassion. My lady, a woman of genuine faith, declared to the matron, "Rosanna Spearman shall have her chance, in my service."

One week later, Rosanna Spearman joined our establishment as the second housemaid.

No one was informed of her background except Miss Rachel and myself. My lady, consulting me on nearly all matters, sought my counsel regarding Rosanna. Having recently adopted the habit of aligning with my lady's views, I concurred heartily with her decision.

No girl could have received a fairer opportunity than this poor creature. None of the servants were privy to her past, and thus, no one could cast it in her face. She received her wages and privileges equal to the others, occasionally receiving a word of private encouragement from my lady. In return, she proved herself entirely worthy of such kindness. Though physically frail and occasionally plagued by the aforementioned fainting spells, she performed her duties modestly, uncomplainingly, and with care. Yet, strangely, she failed to make friends among the other female servants, save for my daughter Penelope, who treated her kindly, though never intimately.

I struggle to comprehend what offense she committed. She possessed no beauty to incite envy; indeed, she was the plainest woman in the house, compounded by the misfortune of having one shoulder larger than the other. What the servants chiefly resented, I believe, was her silence and solitude. While the others gossiped, she read or worked during her leisure hours. When it was her turn to step outside, nine times out of ten, she quietly donned her bonnet and walked alone. She never quarreled, never took offense; she maintained a stubborn yet civil distance between herself and the rest. Furthermore, despite her plainness, there was a distinct quality about her, something akin to a lady, evident in her voice or her face. Whatever it was, the other women seized upon it instantly, declaring—most unjustly—that Rosanna Spearman gave herself airs.

Having recounted Rosanna's story, I must now address one of the peculiar habits of this strange girl before proceeding to the tale of the sands.

Our residence stands high on the Yorkshire coast, adjacent to the sea. We possess magnificent walking paths in every direction except one. That single route is a dreadful walk. It extends for a quarter of a mile through a gloomy plantation of firs, emerging onto the loneliest and most unsightly little bay on our entire coastline.

Here, the sandhills descend to the sea, terminating in two rocky spits jutting opposite one another until they vanish into the water. One is known as the North Spit, the other the South. Between them lies the most treacherous quicksand on the Yorkshire shores, shifting backward and forward at certain seasons. At the turn of the tide, some unseen movement in the deep causes the entire surface of the quicksand to shiver and tremble in a manner remarkably visible, earning it the local name of the Shivering Sand. A massive bank, half a mile offshore near the bay's mouth, breaks the force of the incoming ocean. Whether winter or summer, when the tide flows over the quicksand, the sea appears to leave its waves behind on the bank, rolling its waters in smoothly with a heave, covering the sand in silence. It is a desolate and horrifying retreat. No boat dares venture into this bay. No children from our fishing village, Cobb's Hole, ever come here to play. Even the birds of the air seem to give the Shivering Sand a wide berth. That a young woman, with dozens of pleasant walks available and companionship at her command, should prefer this place, choosing to sit and read or work alone here during her break, passes belief. Nevertheless, it is true that this was Rosanna Spearman's favorite walk, aside from her occasional visits to Cobb's Hole to see the only friend she possessed in the neighborhood. It is also true that I was now setting out for this very location to retrieve the girl for dinner, bringing us full circle to our starting point and launching us properly toward the sands.

I saw no sign of the girl within the plantation. Upon emerging through the sandhills onto the beach, there she was, in her small straw bonnet and plain grey cloak, which she always wore to conceal her deformed shoulder as much as possible. She stood alone, gazing out at the quicksand and the sea.

She startled when I approached, turning her head away. Refusing to avoid eye contact being another transgression I never tolerate, I turned her toward me and saw she was weeping. My bandanna handkerchief—one of six exquisite pieces gifted by my lady—was conveniently in my pocket. I produced it and said to Rosanna, "Come and sit down, my dear, on the slope of the beach beside me. I will wipe your eyes first, and then I shall venture to ask what has brought you to tears."

At my age, you will find sitting on a beach slope a far more arduous task than it appears now. By the time I was settled, Rosanna had dried her own eyes with a far inferior handkerchief—cheap cambric. She appeared very quiet and deeply wretched, yet she sat beside me like a good girl when I instructed her. When one wishes to comfort a woman most effectively, one should take her upon one's knee. I considered this golden rule. But alas! Rosanna was not Nancy, and that is the truth of it!

"Now, tell me, my dear," I said, "what are you crying about?"

"About the years that are gone, Mr. Betteredge," Rosanna replied quietly. "My past life still returns to me sometimes."

"Come, come, my girl," I said. "Your past life is thoroughly washed away. Why cannot you forget it?"

She took hold of one of the lappets of my coat. I am a somewhat slovenly old man, and much of my food and drink often splashes onto my clothing. Sometimes one woman, sometimes another, cleans the grease from me. The day before, Rosanna had removed a spot from the lappet of my coat using a new composition guaranteed to remove anything. The grease was gone, but a dull patch remained on the fabric's nap where the grease had been. The girl pointed to that spot and shook her head.

"The stain is removed," she said. "But the place shows, Mr. Betteredge—the place shows!"

A remark that catches a man off guard via his own coat is difficult to answer. Moreover, something in the girl herself made me particularly sorrowful for her at that moment. She had lovely brown eyes, plain as she was otherwise, and she looked at me with a sort of reverence for my happy old age and good character, things forever beyond her own reach, which weighed heavily on my heart for our second housemaid. Unable to comfort her further, there was only one course of action left. That was to take her to dinner.

"Help me up," I said. "You are late for dinner, Rosanna—and I have come to fetch you in."

"You, Mr. Betteredge!" she exclaimed.

"They told Nancy to fetch you," I replied. "But I thought you might prefer your scolding if it came from me."

Instead of assisting me, the poor thing slipped her hand into mine and gave it a gentle squeeze. She struggled hard to prevent further tears and succeeded, which I respected. "You are very kind, Mr. Betteredge," she said. "I do not want any dinner today—let me remain here a little longer."

"What draws you to stay here?" I asked. "What brings you everlastingly to this miserable place?"

"Something draws me to it," the girl replied, tracing patterns in the sand with her finger. "I try to keep away from it, and I cannot. Sometimes," she whispered, as if frightened by her own thoughts, "sometimes, Mr. Betteredge, I think that my grave is waiting for me here."

"There is roast mutton and suet pudding waiting for you!" I declared. "Go to dinner immediately. This is what happens, Rosanna, when one thinks on an empty stomach!" I spoke severely, naturally indignant at my age to hear a twenty-five-year-old woman discussing her end!

She seemed not to hear me. She placed her hand on my shoulder and held me there, seated beside her.

"I think the place has laid a spell on me," she said. "I dream of it night after night; I think of it while stitching. You know I am grateful, Mr. Betteredge—you know I strive to deserve your kindness and my lady's confidence. But I wonder sometimes whether this life here is too quiet and too good for a woman like me, after all I have endured, Mr. Betteredge—after all I have endured. It is more lonely to be among the other servants, knowing I am not what they are, than it is to be here. My lady does not know, the matron at the reformatory does not know, what a dreadful reproach honest people feel within themselves toward a woman like me. Do not scold me, there's a dear good man. I do my work, don't I? Please do not tell my lady I am discontented—I am not. My mind is unquiet, sometimes, that is all." She snatched her hand from my shoulder and suddenly pointed downward to the quicksand. "Look!" she said. "Is it not wonderful? Is it not terrible? I have seen it dozens of times, and it is always as new to me as if I had never seen it before!"

I looked where she pointed. The tide was turning, and the dreadful sand began to shiver. The broad brown face of it heaved slowly, then dimpled and quivered all over. "Do you know what it looks like to *me*?" Rosanna asked, seizing my shoulder again. "It looks as if it had hundreds of suffocating people beneath it—all struggling to reach the surface, and all sinking lower and lower into the dreadful deeps! Throw a stone in, Mr. Betteredge! Throw a stone in, and let us see the sand suck it down!"

This was unhealthy talk! This was an empty stomach feeding an unquiet mind! My answer, a rather sharp one in the poor girl's own interest, was at the tip of my tongue when it was abruptly cut short by a voice among the sandhills shouting my name. "Betteredge!" cried the voice, "where are you?" "Here!" I shouted back, having no notion of who it was. Rosanna sprang to her feet and stood

The moment the carriage doors closed behind us, leaving us alone on the desolate stretch of sand, I rose to my feet, intending to stretch my stiffened limbs. But Mr. Franklin held up a hand to stop me.

"There is one comfort in this wretched spot," he observed, his voice cutting through the damp air. "We have the entire world to ourselves. Stay where you are, Betteredge. I have something important to discuss with you."

As he spoke, I studied him, searching for the ghost of the boy I remembered in the man standing before me. The search was futile. The rosy cheeks and the trim little jacket of my childhood memory were gone, buried under a pallor that suggested illness or worry. Most jarring of all, his face was now framed by a curly brown beard and moustache, a transformation that left me both surprised and disappointed. While he retained a certain lively, engaging manner, it lacked the effortless charm of his youth. Worse still, he had failed to keep his promise of growing taller; he remained neat and slim, but nowhere near the middle height he had boasted. The years had stripped him of almost everything, save for that bright, straightforward gaze in his eyes. There, finally, I found the essence of the old boy, and I decided to rest my investigation there.

"Welcome back to the old place, Mr. Franklin," I said, masking my unease. "It is doubly welcome that you arrived hours ahead of schedule."

"I have reasons for arriving early," Franklin replied, his tone shifting to something more serious. "I suspect I have been followed and watched in London for the past few days. That is why I took the morning train instead of the afternoon; I needed to lose sight of a certain dark stranger."

His words struck a chord deep within me, instantly recalling the three jugglers and Penelope's vague fears that they intended mischief against him.

"Who is watching you, sir? And why?" I pressed.

Instead of answering, Franklin pivoted. "Tell me about the three Indians who visited your house today. It is quite possible, Betteredge, that my pursuer and your jugglers are merely pieces of the same puzzle."

"How do you know about the jugglers, sir?" I asked, stacking questions upon questions—a breach of etiquette, I grant you, but human nature is rarely perfect.

"I saw Penelope," Franklin explained casually. "She told me. Your daughter promised to be a pretty girl, and she has fulfilled that vow. She has a small ear and a small foot. Did the late Mrs. Betteredge possess such inestimable charms?"

"The late Mrs. Betteredge had her share of defects, sir," I replied dryly. "One of them, if I may mention it, was an inability to stay on the subject at hand. She was more like a fly than a woman; she could not settle on anything."

"She would have suited me perfectly," Franklin admitted with a grin. "I never settle on anything either. Your daughter praised your edge to me, saying you express yourself beautifully. Penelope's own words, blushing divinely. My respect for you did not prevent me from noting that she knows you well. Let us be serious, however. What exactly did these jugglers do?"

A flicker of dissatisfaction crossed my mind—not that Franklin had kissed my daughter, for he was welcome to that—but that I had been forced to recount her foolish tale at second hand. Yet, there was no turning back. As I recounted the events, Franklin's merriment vanished. He knitted his brows and twisted his beard, his expression growing grave. When I finished, he repeated two of the questions posed by the chief juggler to the boy, seemingly committing them to memory.

"'Is it on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today?' 'Has the English gentleman got It about him?' I suspect," Franklin said, producing a small, sealed parcel from his pocket. "That 'It' refers to *this*. And 'this,' Betteredge, refers to my uncle Herncastle's famous Diamond."

"Good Lord, sir!" I exclaimed. "How do you find yourself in charge of the wicked Colonel's Diamond?"

"The Colonel's will bequeathed the Diamond as a birthday present to my cousin, Rachel," Franklin stated flatly. "And my father, acting as the Colonel's executor, has entrusted it to me to deliver here."

If the sea, slowly creeping over the Shivering Sand, had suddenly transformed into dry land, I doubt I could have been more stunned.

"The Colonel's Diamond left to Miss Rachel!" I stammered. "And your father, sir, the Colonel's executor! I would wager anything that your father would not have so much as touched the Colonel with a pair of tongs!"

"Strong language, Betteredge! What was there against the Colonel? He belonged to your era, not mine. Tell me what you know of him, and I will explain how my father became his executor, along with other matters. I have made disturbing discoveries in London regarding my uncle Herncastle and his Diamond, and I require your confirmation. You just called him the 'wicked Colonel.' Search your memory, my old friend, and tell me why."

Seeing his earnestness, I obliged.

Here is the substance of what I told him, written out for your benefit. Clear your minds of the children, the dinner, the new bonnets, or whatever trivialities occupy your thoughts. Forget politics, horses, city prices, and club grievances. I hope you do not take this direct address amiss; it is simply a method I use to engage the gentle reader. Have you not seen the finest authors in your hands, and do you not know how easily your attention wanders when a book demands it less than a person does?

I began with my lady's father, the old lord known for his short temper and long tongue. He had five children: two sons, followed eventually by three daughters, my mistress being the youngest and the finest of the lot. The eldest son, Arthur, inherited the title and estates. The second, the Honourable John, received a substantial fortune from a relative and entered the army.

They say an ill bird fouls its own nest. I consider the noble family of the Herncastles my own nest, and I shall be grateful if I am spared the details concerning the Honourable John. I honestly believe he was one of the greatest blackguards to ever live. He joined the Guards but was forced to leave before his twenty-second year—the army was too strict for him. He traveled to India to test if discipline was equally rigid there, seeking active service. In terms of bravery, he was a mixture of bulldog and game-cock, with a dash of savagery. He fought at the taking of Seringapatam. Soon after, he transferred regiments twice more, eventually reaching the rank of lieutenant-colonel. It was during this time he suffered a severe sunstroke and returned to England.

He came back with a reputation that barred him from every door in his family. My lady, then newly married, took the lead, declaring—with her brother's approval—that he should never enter her house again. There were numerous slurs attached to his name, but the stain of the Diamond is the only detail I need mention.

It was rumored he had acquired his Indian jewel through means too bold for him to acknowledge publicly. He never attempted to sell it, nor did he need money. He never gave it away, nor did he show it to a living soul. Some claimed he feared military authorities; others, less informed, suggested he feared losing his life. Perhaps there was a grain of truth in the latter. It was false to say he was afraid, yet it was true that his life had been twice threatened in India, and the Moonstone was widely believed to be the cause. Upon his return to England, finding himself shunned by everyone, the Diamond was once again blamed. The mystery of his life became his prison, outlawing him among his own people. Clubs refused him entry; women he sought to marry rejected him; friends and relations could barely bear to look at him in the street.

Most men in such a predicament would seek to clear their names. But to submit, even when wrong and facing universal opposition, was not the way of the Honourable John. He kept the Diamond in flat defiance of assassination in India, and in flat defiance of public opinion in England. This was the portrait of the man: a character who braved everything, with a handsome face that seemed possessed by the devil.

Rumours varied. Sometimes he was said to be addicted to opium and collecting old books; at other times, experimenting with strange chemicals; occasionally, carousing among the lowest slums of London. Regardless, he led a solitary, vicious, underground existence. Once, and only once, after his return to England, did I see him face to face.

About two years prior to our current timeline, and roughly eighteen months before his death, the Colonel appeared unexpectedly at my lady's house in London. It was the night of Miss Rachel's birthday, the twenty-first of June, and a party was in full swing. A footman summoned me to the hall, where I found the Colonel wasted, worn, old, shabby, and as wild and wicked as ever.

"Go up to my sister," he commanded. "Tell her I have called to wish my niece many happy returns of the day."

He had attempted reconciliation by letter on several occasions, presumably only to annoy her. But this was his first actual visit. I nearly mentioned the party, but his devilish look daunted me. I ascended with the message and, at his request, left him waiting in the hall. The servants stared at him from a distance, viewing him as a walking engine of destruction, loaded with powder and shot, ready to explode at any moment.

My lady possessed a dash of the family temper. "Tell Colonel Herncastle," she instructed me coldly, "that Miss Verinder is engaged, and that *I* decline to see him." I tried to plead for a more civil response, knowing the Colonel's constitutional superiority to normal gentlemanly restraints. It was useless. The family temper flared instantly. "When I want your advice," she snapped, "you know I ask for it. I do not ask for it now." I descended with the message, offering a slightly softened version of my own devising: "My lady and Miss Rachel regret that they are engaged, Colonel, and beg to be excused the honor of seeing you."

I expected him to erupt, even at such politeness. To my surprise, he reacted with unnatural quiet. His eyes, a glittering bright grey, settled on me for a moment, and he laughed—not out loud, but inwardly, in a soft, chuckling, horribly mischievous manner. "Thank you, Betteredge," he said. "I shall remember my niece's birthday." With that, he turned on his heel and walked out.

The following year, we heard he was ill in bed. Six months later—that is, six months before the time I am now writing—a letter arrived from a highly respectable clergyman to my lady. It announced two wonderful pieces of family news: first, that the Colonel had forgiven his sister on his deathbed; second, that he had forgiven everyone else and met an edifying end. I hold an unfeigned respect for the Church, despite the bishops and clergy, yet I am firmly persuaded that the devil remained in undisturbed possession of the Honourable John, and that his final abominable act was to take the clergyman in!

This was the sum total of my report to Mr. Franklin. I noted that he listened with increasing eagerness. The story of the Colonel being turned away from his sister's door on the occasion of his niece's birthday seemed to strike Franklin like a bullet hitting its mark. Though he did not acknowledge it, I saw plainly that I had made him uneasy.

"You have said your say, Betteredge," he remarked. "Now it is my turn. Before I reveal the discoveries I made in London and how I became involved with the Diamond, I must ask one thing. You look, my old friend, as if you do not quite grasp the purpose of this consultation. Do your looks belie you?"

"No, sir," I replied. "My looks, on this occasion at least, tell the truth."

"In that case," Franklin continued, "suppose I outline my perspective before we proceed. I see three very serious questions involved in the Colonel's birthday gift to my cousin Rachel. Follow me carefully, Betteredge; count me off on your fingers if it helps," he added with a certain pleasure in demonstrating his clear-headedness, a trait that reminded me wonderfully of his boyhood. "First question: Was the Colonel's Diamond the object of a conspiracy in India? Second question: Has that conspiracy followed the Diamond to England? Third question: Did the Colonel know the conspiracy followed the Diamond, and has he purposely left a legacy of trouble and danger to his sister, through the innocent medium of his sister's child? That is what I am driving at, Betteredge. Do not let me frighten you."

It was all very well for him to say that, but he *had* frightened me.

If he was right, our quiet English home was suddenly invaded by a devilish Indian Diamond, bringing with it a conspiracy of living rogues, set loose by the vengeance of a dead man. This was our situation as revealed by Franklin's final words! Who had ever heard the like in the nineteenth century? In an age of progress, in a country rejoicing in the blessings of the British constitution? Nobody had, and consequently, nobody could be expected to believe it. Yet, I would continue my story regardless.

When one receives a sudden alarm of this magnitude, nine times out of ten the sensation is felt in the stomach. When the stomach reacts, the mind wanders, and one begins to fidget. I fidgeted silently on the sand. Franklin noticed my agitation—whether of body or mind, they are often the same—and, checking himself just as he was about to begin his part of the story, asked sharply, "What do you want?"

What did I want? I did not tell him, but I will tell you in confidence. I wanted a whiff of my pipe and a turn at *Robinson Crusoe*.

I kept my private thoughts to myself and respectfully urged Mr. Franklin to proceed. "Don't fidget, Betteredge," he replied, and continued.

My young master's opening remarks revealed that his discoveries regarding the wicked Colonel and the Diamond had originated with a visit he paid, prior to arriving at our house, to the family lawyer in Hampstead. A casual remark dropped by Mr. Franklin one afternoon, after dinner, disclosed that his father had entrusted him with a birthday present for Miss Rachel. One thing led to another, culminating in the lawyer revealing the true nature of the gift and the origins of the friendly connection between the late Colonel and Mr. Blake, senior. The facts were so extraordinary that I doubted my own ability to render them justice; thus, I preferred to report Mr. Franklin's findings as nearly as possible in his own words.

"You remember the time, Betteredge," he said, "when my father was striving to prove his claim to that unfortunate Dukedom? Well, that coincided with the period when my uncle Herncastle returned from India. My father discovered that his brother-in-law held certain papers likely to aid his lawsuit. He called upon the Colonel under the pretext of welcoming him home. The Colonel was not easily deceived. 'You want something,' he said, 'or you would never compromise your reputation by calling on _me_.' My father realized his only chance was to show his hand; he immediately admitted he desired the papers. The Colonel asked for a day to consider, and his reply arrived in the form of a most extraordinary letter, which my friend the lawyer showed me. The Colonel began by stating he wanted something from my father and proposed an exchange of friendly services. The fortunes of war had placed him in possession of one of the largest diamonds in the world, and he believed neither he nor his precious jewel was safe in any house or quarter of the globe where they coexisted. Under these alarming circumstances, he resolved to entrust his Diamond to another. This person was not expected to run any risk; they might deposit the stone in any specially guarded location, like a banker's or jeweller's strongroom, for the safe custody of high-value items. His main responsibility was passive: he was to receive, either personally or through a trusted representative, at a prearranged address on specific days each year, a note from the Colonel certifying he was still alive. If the date passed without the note, the Colonel's silence would be deemed a sure token of murder. In that sole event, certain sealed instructions regarding the Diamond's disposal, deposited with it, were to be opened and followed implicitly. If my father accepted this strange charge, the Colonel's papers were at his disposal in return. That was the letter."

"What did your father do, sir?" I asked.

"Do?" Mr. Franklin said. "He applied the invaluable faculty of common sense to the Colonel's letter. He declared the whole affair simply absurd. Somewhere in his Indian wanderings, the Colonel had picked up some wretched crystal he mistook for a diamond. As for the danger of murder and the precautions devised to preserve his life and his piece of glass, this was the nineteenth century; any man in his senses would apply to the police. The Colonel had been a notorious opium-eater for years; if the only way to obtain the valuable papers was to accept opium as a matter of fact, my father was quite willing to take the ridiculous responsibility, especially since it involved no trouble to himself. The Diamond and the sealed instructions went into his banker's strongroom, and the Colonel's periodic letters reporting him alive were received and opened by our family lawyer, Mr. Bruff, as my father's representative. No sensible person in a similar position could have viewed the matter differently. Nothing in this world is probable unless it appeals to our own trumpery experience; we only believe in a romance when we see it in a newspaper."

It was plain that Mr. Franklin considered his father's notion of the Colonel hasty and incorrect.

"What is your own private opinion, sir?" I asked.

"Let's finish the story of the Colonel first," Mr. Franklin said. "There is a curious lack of system in the English mind; your question, my old friend, is an instance of it. When we are not occupied in making machinery, we are mentally the most slovenly people in the universe."

"So much," I thought to myself, "for a foreign education! He has learned that way of girding at us in France, I suppose."

Mr. Franklin resumed the thread. "My father obtained the papers he wanted and never saw his brother-in-law again. Year after year, on the prearranged days, the prearranged letter came from the Colonel and was opened by Mr. Bruff. I have seen the letters, piled together, all written in the same brief, business-like form: 'Sir,—This is to certify that I am still a living man. Let the Diamond be. John Herncastle.' That was all he ever wrote, regularly to the day, until some six or eight months ago, when the form varied for the first time. It now read: 'Sir,—They tell me I am dying. Come to me, and help me to make my will.' Mr. Bruff went and found him in the little suburban villa, surrounded by its own grounds, where he had lived alone since leaving India. He had dogs, cats, and birds for company, but no human being except the daily housekeeper and the doctor at his bedside. The will was simple. The Colonel had dissipated most of his fortune in chemical investigations. His will consisted of three clauses dictated from his bed in perfect possession of his faculties. The first provided for the care of his animals. The second founded a professorship of experimental chemistry at a northern university. The third bequeathed the Moonstone as a birthday present to his niece, on condition that my father act as executor. My father initially refused, but on second thoughts, he agreed, partly because he was assured the executorship involved no trouble, and partly because Mr. Bruff suggested, in Rachel's interest, that the Diamond might be worth something after all."

"Did the Colonel give any reason, sir," I inquired, "why he left the Diamond to Miss Rachel?"

"He not only gave the reason—he had it written in his will," Mr. Franklin said. "I have an extract you shall see presently. Don't be slovenly-minded, Betteredge! One thing at a time. You have heard of the Colonel's Will; now you must hear what happened after his death. It was formally necessary to value the Diamond before the Will could be proved. All consulted jewellers confirmed the Colonel's assertion that he possessed one of the largest diamonds in the world. Valuing it accurately presented serious difficulties. Its size made it a phenomenon in the market; its colour placed it in a category of its own; and, adding to these uncertainties, there was a defect, a flaw, in the very heart of the stone. Even with this serious drawback, the lowest estimate was twenty thousand pounds. Conceive my father's astonishment! He had been within a hair's-breadth of refusing to act as executor and allowing this magnificent jewel to be lost to the family. His renewed interest induced him to open the sealed instructions deposited with the Diamond. Mr. Bruff showed this document to me, and it suggests, to my mind, a clue to the nature of the conspiracy threatening the Colonel's life."

"Then you do believe, sir," I said, "that there was a conspiracy?"

"Not possessing my father's excellent common sense," answered Mr. Franklin, "I believe the Colonel's life was threatened exactly as he said. The sealed instructions explain how he died quietly in his bed. In the event of his death by violence—that is, the absence of the regular letter at the appointed date—my father was directed to send the Moonstone secretly to Amsterdam. It was to be deposited with a famous diamond-cutter and cut into four to six separate stones. These were then to be sold, and the proceeds applied to founding the professorship of experimental chemistry which the Colonel has since endowed by his Will. Now, Betteredge, exert those sharp wits of yours and observe the conclusion to which the Colonel's instructions point!"

I instantly exerted my wits. They were of the slovenly English sort and muddled the matter until Mr. Franklin took them in hand.

"Remark," says Mr. Franklin, "that the integrity of the Diamond as a whole stone is here artfully made dependent on the preservation of the Colonel's life from violence. He is not satisfied with saying to his enemies, 'Kill me—and you will be no nearer to the Diamond than you are now; it is where you can't get at it—in the guarded strongroom of a bank.' He says instead, 'Kill me—and the Diamond will be the Diamond no longer; its identity will be destroyed.' What does that mean?"

Here I thought I had a flash of wonderful foreign brightness.

"I know," I said. "It means lowering the value of the stone and cheating the rogues in that way!"

"Nothing of the sort," says Mr. Franklin. "I have inquired about that. The flawed Diamond, cut up, would actually fetch more than the Diamond as it now is; for this plain reason—that from four to six perfect brilliants might be cut from it, which would collectively be worth more money than the large but imperfect single stone. If robbery for gain were the motive, the Colonel's instructions absolutely made the Diamond better worth stealing. More money could have been got for it, and its disposal in the diamond market would have been infinitely easier if it had passed through the hands of the workmen of Amsterdam."

"Lord bless us, sir!" I burst out. "What was the plot, then?"

"A plot organized among the Indians who originally owned the jewel," says Mr. Franklin—"a plot with some old Hindoo superstition at the bottom of it. That is my opinion, confirmed by a family paper I have about me."

I saw now why the appearance of the three Indian jugglers at our house had struck Mr. Franklin as noteworthy.

"I don't want to force my opinion on you," Mr. Franklin went on. "The idea of certain chosen servants of an old Hindoo superstition devoting themselves, through all difficulties and dangers, to watching the opportunity of recovering their sacred gem, appears to _me_ perfectly consistent with everything we know of the patience of Oriental races and the influence of Oriental religions. But then I am an imaginative man; and the butcher, the baker, and the tax-gatherer are not the only credible realities in existence to _my_ mind. Let my guess go for what it is worth, and let us get on to the only practical question. Does the conspiracy against the Moonstone survive the Colonel's death? And did the Colonel know it when he left the birthday gift to his niece?"

I began to see my lady and Miss Rachel at the end of it all. Not a word he said escaped me.

"I was not very willing, when I discovered the story of the Moonstone," said Mr. Franklin, "to be the means of bringing it here. But Mr. Bruff reminded me that somebody must put my cousin's legacy into my cousin's hands—and that I might as well do it as anybody else. After taking the Diamond out of the bank, I fancied I was followed by a shabby, dark-complexioned man. I went to my father's house to pick up my luggage and found a letter there that detained me in London. I returned to the bank with the Diamond and thought I saw the shabby man again. Taking the Diamond out of the bank this morning, I saw the man for the third time, gave him the slip, and started by the morning train instead of the afternoon one. Here I am, with the Diamond safe and sound—and what is the first news that meets me? I find that three strolling Indians have been at the house, and that my arrival from London, and something I am expected to have about me, are special objects of their investigation when they believe themselves alone. I don't waste time on their pouring ink into the boy's hand and telling him to look for a man at a distance. The thing is 'hocus-pocus' in my opinion. The present question is: am I wrongly attaching meaning to a mere accident, or do we have evidence that the Indians are on the track of the Moonstone the moment it leaves the bank's safe keeping?"

Neither he nor I fancied dealing with this part of the inquiry. We looked at each other, then at the tide oozing smoothly higher over the Shivering Sand.

"What are you thinking of?" Mr. Franklin asked suddenly.

"I was thinking, sir," I answered, "that I should like to shy the Diamond into the quicksand and settle the question in _that_ way."

"If you have got the value of the stone in your pocket," answered Mr. Franklin, "say so, Betteredge, and in it goes!"

It is curious how a very small joke goes far toward relief when the mind is anxious. We found a fund of merriment in the notion of making away with Miss Rachel's lawful property and getting Mr. Blake, as executor, into dreadful trouble—though where the merriment lay, I am quite at a loss to discover now.

Mr. Franklin was the first to bring the talk back to its proper purpose. He took an envelope from his pocket, opened it, and handed me the paper inside.

"Betteredge," he said, "we must face the question of the Colonel's motive in leaving this legacy to his niece, for my aunt's sake. Bear in mind how Lady Verinder treated her brother from the time he returned to England until he told you he should remember his niece's birthday. And read that."

He gave me the extract from the Colonel's Will. I have it by me while writing these words and copy it for your benefit:
