"Thirdly, and lastly, I give and bequeath to my niece, Rachel Verinder, daughter and only child of my sister, Julia Verinder, widow—if her mother, the said Julia Verinder, shall be living on the said Rachel Verinder's next Birthday after my death—the yellow Diamond belonging to me, and known in the East by the name of The Moonstone: subject to this condition, that her mother, the said Julia Verinder, shall be living at the time. And I hereby desire my executor to give my Diamond, either by his own hands or by the hands of some trustworthy representative whom he shall appoint, into the personal possession of my said niece

Lost in a fog of confusion, I desperately needed a moment of solitude to regain my composure. My daughter, Penelope, however, intercepted me with the same relentless energy her late mother once displayed on the staircase. She demanded to know every detail of my recent conference with Mr. Franklin. Under the current circumstances, the only prudent course was to snuff out her curiosity immediately. I deflected her inquiry by claiming that Mr. Franklin and I had engaged in a lengthy debate regarding foreign politics until our tongues were tired, after which we succumbed to exhaustion and fell asleep in the scorching heat of the sun. This tactic, though effective against a persistent spouse or daughter, relies heavily on the natural inclination of women to forgive such evasions with a peck on the cheek at the next opportunity.

As the afternoon dragged on, my wife and Miss Rachel returned. Naturally, they were astounded to learn that Mr. Franklin Blake had arrived and departed on horseback within the span of a few hours. Equally predictable was their barrage of awkward questions; my fabricated tale of political discourse and solar slumber failed to satisfy them a second time. Deprived of further excuses, I attributed his arrival by the early train to one of Mr. Franklin's eccentric whims. When pressed whether his swift departure on horseback was another such whim, I admitted it was, slipping away from the interrogation with what I believed was considerable dexterity.

Once I had appeased the ladies, I returned to my own quarters, only to find Penelope waiting there. With her characteristic blend of affectionate reconciliation and insatiable curiosity, she pressed me for the truth regarding our second housemaid, Rosanna Spearman. Since our encounter at the Shivering Sand, Rosanna had returned to the house in a state of profound disarray. According to Penelope, the girl had shifted through every hue of the rainbow. She oscillated between unexplained mirth and inexplicable sorrow. In one breath, she interrogated me relentlessly about Mr. Franklin Blake; in the next, she flew into a rage at Penelope for suggesting that a stranger could hold any interest for her. She had been seen smiling broadly while scribbling Mr. Franklin's name inside her workbox, only to be overcome with tears while gazing at her deformed shoulder in the mirror. Could she and Mr. Franklin have known one another previously? I assured Penelope it was impossible, citing Mr. Franklin's genuine astonishment at her stare. Yet, Penelope's account of the girl's intense inquisitiveness seemed equally authentic. This circular dialogue grew tiresome until my daughter abruptly concluded it with a hypothesis so preposterous I deemed it the most monstrous notion I had ever encountered.

"Father!" Penelope declared with grave seriousness. "There is only one explanation. Rosanna has fallen in love with Mr. Franklin Blake at first sight!"

You may have heard of beautiful young ladies succumbing to instant passion and considered it natural. But imagine a housemaid emerging from a reformatory, possessing a plain countenance and a hunched shoulder, falling hopelessly in love at first sight with a gentleman visiting her mistress's home. Match that absurdity against any romance novel in Christendom if you dare. I laughed until tears streamed down my cheeks. Penelope, however, resented my merriment in a peculiar manner. "I never knew you cruel before, father," she said softly, before retreating to her room.

Her words struck me like a bucket of icy water. I felt a sudden, savage unease within myself the moment she spoke them, though I could not yet articulate why. Let us shift the subject, for I regret having dwelt on this matter prematurely, as its significance will become apparent shortly.

That evening, the dinner bell rang before Mr. Franklin returned from Frizinghall. I carried his hot water to his chamber, anticipating news of some calamity following his extraordinary delay. To my profound disappointment—and likely yours as well—nothing had occurred. He had not encountered the Indians either on his way to or from Frizinghall. He had safely deposited the Moonstone in the bank, describing it merely as a valuable of immense worth, and secured the receipt in his pocket. Descending the stairs, I felt a sense of anticlimax wash over us after the day's mounting excitement regarding the diamond.

The details of the meeting between Mr. Franklin, his aunt, and his cousin remain beyond my recollection. I would have preferred to wait at table that day, yet in my position within the household, doing so (save for major family festivals) would have compromised my dignity in the eyes of the servants—a lapse my lady already suspected I was prone to, without needing further provocation. The intelligence I gathered from the upper floors that evening came from Penelope and the footman. Penelope noted that she had never seen Miss Rachel so meticulous about her hair, nor so radiant and lovely as she appeared when descending to greet Mr. Franklin in the drawing-room. The footman reported that maintaining a respectful composure in the presence of his superiors while serving Mr. Franklin Blake at dinner represented the greatest challenge to his training in service. Later that night, we heard them singing and playing duets; Mr. Franklin played the pipe in high notes, Miss Rachel matched him with even higher tones, and my lady followed on the piano, weaving a melody over them that sounded wonderfully pleasant through the open terrace windows. Still later, I visited Mr. Franklin in the smoking-room with soda water and brandy, finding that Miss Rachel had completely displaced the diamond from his thoughts. "She is the most charming girl I have seen since returning to England!" was all I could draw from him when I attempted to steer the conversation toward more serious matters.

Approaching midnight, I began the nightly rounds to secure the house, accompanied as usual by Samuel, the footman. Once all doors were locked, save for the side entrance opening onto the terrace, I dismissed Samuel to bed and stepped outside for a breath of fresh air before retiring myself.

The night was still and stifling, with the moon hanging full and bright in the heavens. The silence was so profound that I could occasionally hear the faint, low crash of the sea as the ground-swell washed over the sandbank near the bay's mouth. The terrace side of the house lay in darkness, but the broad moonlight illuminated the gravel walk running along the adjacent side. Gazing upward at the sky, then turning my attention to the path, I perceived the shadow of a figure cast forward from behind the corner of the house.

Being old and shrewd, I refrained from calling out. Unfortunately, my age and weight betrayed me on the gravel. Before I could stealthily circle the corner as I had intended, I heard lighter footsteps—more than one pair, I thought—retreating in haste. By the time I reached the corner, the intruders, whoever they were, had fled into the shrubbery at the far end of the walk, disappearing among the dense trees and bushes. From that thicket, they could easily cross our fence into the road. Had I been forty years younger, I might have caught them before they escaped our grounds. As it was, I returned to summon a younger pair of legs. Samuel and I retrieved two guns and swept the perimeter of the house and the shrubbery. Satisfied that no one was lurking, we turned back. Passing over the walk where I had spotted the shadow, I noticed for the first time a small, bright object lying on the clean gravel beneath the moonlight. Picking it up, I discovered it to be a small bottle containing a thick, sweet-smelling liquid, black as ink.

I said nothing to Samuel. However, recalling Penelope's account of the jugglers and the pouring of the ink pool into the boy's palm, I instantly suspected that I had disturbed three Indians who were lurking about the house, bent in their heathen fashion on discovering the location of the Diamond that very night.

Here, for a brief respite, I must pause the narrative.

By summoning my own memory and consulting Penelope's journal, I find we can swiftly bridge the gap between Mr. Franklin Blake's arrival and Miss Rachel's birthday. For the greater part of that interval, the days drifted by, bringing nothing of consequence worth recording. With your permission, and aided by Penelope, I shall highlight only specific dates here, reserving the day-by-day recounting for the period when the Moonstone became the singular obsession of every soul in our household.

Thus, we resume our tale—beginning, naturally, with the bottle of sweet-smelling ink discovered on the gravel walk that night.

The following morning, the twenty-sixth, I presented this piece of trickery to Mr. Franklin and recounted what I had previously shared. His verdict was twofold: first, that the Indians had indeed been prowling for the Diamond; second, that they were foolish enough to believe in their own magic. By this, he meant the ritual of marking a boy's forehead and pouring ink into his palm, followed by the expectation that he would perceive entities beyond human vision. "In our country, as well as in the East," Mr. Franklin informed me, "there are individuals who practice this curious hocus-pocus—sans the ink, though—and who refer to it by a French term implying 'brightness of sight.' Depend upon it," he declared, "the Indians assumed we would keep the Diamond here. They brought their clairvoyant boy to guide them to it, should they succeed in breaching the house last night."

"Do you think they'll attempt again, sir?" I inquired.

"It depends," Mr. Franklin replied, "on the boy's actual capabilities. If he can locate the Diamond through the iron safe of the bank at Frizinghall, we shall be spared further visits from the Indians for the present. If he cannot, we shall have another opportunity to catch them in the shrubbery before many more nights pass overhead."

I waited with considerable confidence for that latter scenario; yet, strangely, it never materialized.

Whether the jugglers heard rumors of Mr. Franklin being spotted at the bank and drew conclusions accordingly; or whether the boy genuinely perceived the Diamond where it now rested—which I, for one, utterly disbelieve; or whether it was merely a matter of chance—the plain truth remains: not a single Indian approached the house again during the weeks preceding Miss Rachel's birthday. The jugglers remained in and around the town peddling their trade, while Mr. Franklin and I waited, resolved not to alert the rogues by revealing our suspicions prematurely. With this report of proceedings on both sides, my account of the Indians concludes for the present.

On the twenty-ninth, Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin devised a new method to occupy the time that might otherwise have felt heavy. There are reasons to pay particular attention to this occupation, for it bears upon events yet to unfold.

Gentlefolk generally face a formidable obstacle in life: the rock of their own idleness. Their days are largely spent searching for something to do, and it is curious to observe—especially when their tastes lean toward the intellectual—how often they drift blindly into distasteful pursuits. Nine times out of ten, they resort to torturing something or spoiling something, firmly believing they are cultivating their minds, when the reality is they are merely creating a mess within the house. I have witnessed ladies and gentlemen alike venture out daily with empty pill-boxes, capturing newts, beetles, spiders, and frogs, only to return home and pierce the miserable creatures with pins or dissect them without a pang of remorse into tiny fragments. One sees my young master or mistress poring over a spider's innards with a magnifying glass; or encounters a frog descending the stairs without its head. When one wonders what this cruelty signifies, the answer is invariably a supposed passion for natural history. Sometimes, they spend hours destroying a beautiful flower with pointed instruments, driven by a dull curiosity to understand its composition. Does its color become more vibrant, or its scent sweeter, once you know its parts? But there! The poor souls must pass the time; they must fill their empty heads and idle hands. Thus, they end up ruining canvases with paint, filling the house with the odor of tadpoles in dirty glass boxes, chipping stones and contaminating the food, or staining their fingers in photography while mercilessly altering everyone's likeness. It often weighs heavily on those compelled to labor for their livelihood, yet compare the hardest day's work you have ever endured with the idleness that splits flowers and probes spider stomachs, and thank your stars that your mind has something it *must* think of, and your hands something they *must* do.

As for Mr. Franklin and Miss Rachel, I am glad to report, they tortured nothing. They simply made a mess; and all they spoiled, to their credit, was the paneling of a door.

Mr. Franklin's universal genius, dabbling in everything, turned to what he termed "decorative painting." He claimed to have invented a new mixture to moisten paint, which he called a "vehicle." Its composition remains unknown to me, but its effect can be summarized in two words: it stank. Eager to try the new process, Miss Rachel requested materials from London. Mr. Franklin mixed them, accompanied by a scent so potent it made the dogs sneeze upon entering the room. Donning an apron and bib over her gown, he set her to decorating her small sitting-room—her "boudoir," as she lacked the English vocabulary for it. They commenced with the interior of the door. Mr. Franklin scraped away the fine varnish with pumice-stone to create a working surface. Miss Rachel then covered it, under his direction and with his assistance, with patterns and devices—griffins, birds, flowers, cupids—copied from designs by a famous Italian painter whose name escapes me: the one who stocked the world with Virgin Marys and had a sweetheart at the baker's. Viewed as work, this decoration was slow and filthy. Yet our young lady and gentleman never seemed to tire of it. When they were not riding, receiving guests, dining, or singing, they were there, heads together, as busy as bees, ruining the door. Who was the poet who wrote that Satan finds some mischief still for idle hands to do? If he had occupied my place in the family and witnessed Miss Rachel with her brush and Mr. Franklin with his vehicle, he could have penned nothing truer of either than that.

The next date worthy of note is Sunday, the fourth of June.

That evening, in the servants' hall, we debated a domestic question for the first time—one that, like the door decoration, foreshadows future events. Observing the pleasure Mr. Franklin and Miss Rachel derived from each other's company, and noting how well-matched they were in every personal respect, we naturally speculated on the possibility of their uniting with other objectives in mind. Some predicted a wedding before summer's end. Others, led by me, admitted Miss Rachel might marry, but doubted (for reasons that will soon emerge) whether her bridegroom would be Mr. Franklin Blake.

That Mr. Franklin was in love, on his part, was undeniable to anyone who saw and heard him. The difficulty lay in fathoming Miss Rachel. Permit me to introduce you to her; thereafter, I shall leave you to decipher her—if you are capable.

My young lady's eighteenth birthday was approaching on the twenty-first of June. If you appreciate dark-complexioned women (who, I am informed, have fallen out of fashion in the gay world lately) and harbor no prejudice regarding stature, I vouch for Miss Rachel as one of the prettiest girls your eyes have ever beheld. She was small and slim, yet perfectly proportioned from head to toe. To watch her sit, rise, and especially walk, was sufficient proof for any sensible man that the graces of her figure resided in her flesh, not merely in her clothing. Her hair was the blackest I have ever seen; her eyes matched it. Her nose was not quite large enough, I admit. Her mouth and chin were, to quote Mr. Franklin, morsels for the gods; and her complexion (according to the same undeniable authority) was as warm as the sun itself, with the distinct advantage that it remained perpetually presentable. Add to this that she carried her head as upright as a dart, with a dashing, spirited, thoroughbred manner; possessed a clear voice with a resonant quality; and wore a smile that began prettily in her eyes before reaching her lips—and there stands the portrait of her, to the best of my painting, life-sized!

And what of her disposition? Did this charming creature possess no faults? She had just as many as you have, ma'am—no more, no less.

To speak seriously, my dear pretty Miss Rachel, possessing a host of graces and attractions, had one defect that strict impartiality compels me to acknowledge. Unlike most girls of her age, she possessed her own ideas and was stiff-necked enough to defy the fashions themselves if they conflicted with her views. In trifles, this independence was commendable; but in matters of importance, it carried her (as my lady thought, and as I thought) too far. She judged for herself, as few women of twice her age do; she never sought your advice; she never announced her intentions beforehand; she never confided secrets to anyone, from her mother downwards. In both small and great matters, with those she loved and those she hated (and she treated both with equal fervor), Miss Rachel always followed her own path, sufficient for herself in the joys and sorrows of her life. Over and over, I have heard my lady remark, "Rachel's best friend and Rachel's worst enemy are, one and the other—Rachel herself."

Add one final element, and I am done.

Despite her secrecy and self-will, there was not even a shadow of falsehood in her. I never recall her breaking her word; I never remember her saying "No" while meaning "Yes." I can recall, from her childhood, more than one instance where the good little soul accepted the blame and punishment for a fault committed by a playfellow she loved. Nobody ever knew her to confess when the truth emerged and she was charged with it later. Yet, nobody ever knew her to lie about it either. She would look you straight in the face, shake her little saucy head, and plainly declare, "I won't tell you!" Punished again for this, she would admit regret for saying "won't," but, bread and water notwithstanding, she never revealed the truth. Self-willed—devilishly self-willed sometimes—I grant; but the finest creature, nevertheless, that ever walked the ways of this lower world. Perhaps you perceive a contradiction here? In that case, a word in your ear. Study your wife closely for the next twenty-four hours. If your good lady does not exhibit something in the shape of a contradiction within that time, Heaven help you!—you have married a monster.

I have now introduced you to Miss Rachel, which brings us face-to-face next with the question of her matrimonial views.

On June the twelfth, an invitation from my mistress was sent to a gentleman in London to assist in celebrating Miss Rachel's birthday. This was the fortunate individual on whom I believed her heart was privately set! Like Mr. Franklin, he was a cousin of hers. His name was Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

My lady's second sister (do not be alarmed; we are not delving deeply into family matters this time)—my lady's second sister, I say, experienced a romantic disappointment; and, adhering to the "neck or nothing" principle, she entered into what was termed a misalliance. There was terrible work in the family when the Honourable Caroline insisted on marrying plain Mr. Ablewhite, the banker at Frizinghall. He was very rich and very respectable, and he fathered a prodigiously large family—all to his advantage, so far. But he had presumed to elevate himself from a low station in the world—and that was against him. However, Time and the progress of modern enlightenment rectified matters; and the misalliance eventually passed muster very well. We are all becoming liberal now; and (provided you can scratch me if I scratch you) what do I care, in or out of Parliament, whether you are a Dustman or a Duke? That is the modern perspective—and I keep pace with it. The Ablewhites resided in a fine house and grounds, slightly outside Frizinghall. Very worthy people, greatly respected in the neighborhood. We shall not be much troubled by them in these pages—excepting Mr. Godfrey, who was Mr. Ablewhite's second son, and who must take his proper place here, if you please, for Miss Rachel's sake.

With all his brightness, cleverness, and general good qualities, Mr. Franklin's chance of surpassing Mr. Godfrey in our young lady's estimation was, in my opinion, a very poor one indeed.

First, Mr. Godfrey was, in terms of size, far superior to the other. He stood over six feet tall; he possessed a beautiful red-and-white complexion; a smooth, round face, shaved as bare as a hand; and a head of lovely long flaxen hair falling negligently over the nape of his neck. But why do I attempt to provide this personal description? If you have ever subscribed to a Ladies' Charity in London, you know Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite as well as I do. He was a barrister by profession; a ladies' man by temperament; and a good Samaritan by choice. Female benevolence and female destitution could accomplish nothing without him. Maternal societies for confining poor women; Magdalen societies for rescuing poor women; strong-minded societies for placing poor women in positions formerly held by men, leaving the men to fend for themselves—he was vice-president, manager, and referee to them all. Wherever there was a table with a committee of ladies seated in council, there was Mr. Godfrey at the bottom of the board, maintaining the committee's temper and guiding the dear creatures along the thorny paths of business, hat in hand. I suppose this was the most accomplished philanthropist (on a small independence) that England ever produced. As a speaker at charitable meetings, finding someone like him to draw your tears and your money was not easy. He was quite a public figure. The last time

June twenty-first dawned gray and uncertain, the sky heavy with the promise of unsettled weather, yet by midday the clouds had parted to reveal a brave, clear sun. In the servants' hall, we commenced the annual celebration of Miss Rachel's birthday with our customary offerings. As the senior steward, it fell to me to deliver the traditional speech, a ritual I followed with the solemnity of a monarch addressing Parliament. Like Her Majesty's annual address, my words were met with eager anticipation, though once delivered, they were received with the polite grumbling of an audience expecting novelty and finding only tradition. Yet, there is a comfort in predictability; an easy people to govern, whether in the statehouse or the kitchen.

Following breakfast, Mr. Franklin and I held a private consultation regarding the Moonstone. The time had arrived to transfer the gem from the bank at Frizinghall into Miss Rachel's direct custody. Whether Mr. Franklin was thwarted in renewed advances toward his cousin or simply plagued by the insomnia that fractured his resolve, he appeared ill at ease. His mind oscillated wildly regarding the diamond, shifting positions every few minutes. I, however, adhered strictly to the plain facts: no incident had occurred to warrant alarm, yet the legal imperative remained absolute for Mr. Franklin to deliver the jewel. Eventually, logic prevailed over his hesitation. We agreed that after lunch, he would ride to Frizinghall to retrieve the stone, returning with Mr. Godfrey and the two young ladies to accompany him home.

With this arrangement settled, Mr. Franklin returned to Miss Rachel. The morning and early afternoon were consumed by the arduous task of adorning the front door. Penelope mixed the paints under strict direction, while my lady paced the room, handkerchief pressed to her nose against the fumes of the linseed oil, vainly attempting to dismiss the artists. It was not until three o'clock that they finally removed their smocks, leaving Penelope—already compromised by the strong odors of the vehicle they had employed—and the floor covered in pigment. Yet, their labor was complete. The door was transformed, festooned with griffins, cupids, and floral entanglements so intricate and chaotic that they lingered in the mind long after the initial delight faded. I noted with a grim satisfaction that Penelope's contribution to the morning's festivities concluded with a bout of sickness in the back kitchen. If art demands such sacrifices, even from my own daughter, then let art have them.

Mr. Franklin seized a hurried bite from the luncheon table before departing for Frizinghall, ostensibly to escort his cousins, though privately he carried the Moonstone. While he was away, I assumed my station at the sideboard, overseeing the dining arrangements. After ensuring the wine was ready and reviewing the staff, I retired to compose myself. A momentary lapse into reverie, perhaps mistaken for sleep, was shattered by the clatter of hooves outside. I stepped into the hall to greet a cavalcade consisting of Mr. Franklin and his three cousins, accompanied by one of the late Mr. Ablewhite's grooms.

Mr. Godfrey struck me as unusually subdued, lacking his customary buoyancy. He offered a polite handshake and a perfunctory greeting, yet a shadow obscured his features. When I inquired about his father's health, he replied curtly, "Much as usual." In stark contrast, the two Miss Ablewhites were a whirlwind of vitality. Nearly as tall as their brother, these rosy, yellow-haired girls possessed an abundance of health and spirit that caused the horses beneath them to tremble. They dismounted without assistance, bouncing upon the ground with the resilience of india-rubber. Their speech began with a loud "O," their movements were punctuated by a "bang," and their laughter erupted at the slightest provocation. I dubbed them "Bouncers."

Taking advantage of the din, I approached Mr. Franklin in the hall. "Have you secured the Diamond, sir?" I asked. He nodded, tapping his breast pocket. "Have you seen any sign of the Indians?" he countered. "Not a glimpse," he replied, before requesting my lady. Upon learning she was in the small drawing-room, he proceeded immediately. Moments later, the bell rang, and Penelope was dispatched to summon Miss Rachel.

Half an hour later, crossing the hall, I was halted by a sudden eruption of screams from the drawing-room. Recognizing the familiar "O" of the Miss Ablewhites, I was not alarmed, yet curiosity drew me inside under the guise of seeking dinner instructions. The scene was one of mesmerized fascination. Miss Rachel stood at the table, holding the Colonel's diamond, transfixed. On either side of her, the two Bouncers knelt, devouring the gem with their eyes, shrieking with ecstasy as it caught the light. Opposite them stood Mr. Godfrey, clapping his hands like a delighted child and murmuring, "Exquisite! Exquisite!" Mr. Franklin sat by the bookcase, pulling at his beard and staring anxiously toward the window. There, framed by the glass, stood my lady, clutching the extract from the Colonel's Will, her back turned to the assembled company.

She turned to face me as I entered, her expression a mask of familial frowning and suppressed temper. "Come to my room in half an hour," she commanded. "I shall have something to say to you then." She departed, clearly wrestling with the same dilemma that had troubled Mr. Franklin and me at the Shivering Sand. Was the legacy of the Moonstone evidence of her brother's cruel injustice, or proof of his depravity beyond her wildest imaginings? These were weighty questions for my lady to resolve while her innocent daughter held the Colonel's birthday gift.

Before I could withdraw, Miss Rachel, ever considerate of the old servant who had served her since birth, called me back. "Look, Gabriel!" she commanded, flashing the jewel into a shaft of sunlight pouring through the window. Lord bless us, it was a diamond! As large as a plover's egg, it emitted a light akin to the harvest moon. Gazing into the stone was like peering into a yellow abyss that swallowed all else; it seemed unfathomable, a jewel held between finger and thumb that possessed the depth of the heavens. We set it in the sun, then extinguished the room's lights, and it glowed with a moony brilliance from within. No wonder Miss Rachel was captivated; no wonder her cousins screamed. The diamond's hold on me was so profound that I erupted in a roar of "O" rivaling the Bouncers. The only one maintaining his composure was Mr. Godfrey. He draped an arm around each sister's waist, casting a compassionate glance between the gem and me, declaring, "Carbon, Betteredge! Mere carbon, my good friend, after all!"

His intent was likely to instruct me, but he merely reminded me of the impending dinner. I shuffled downstairs to my army of waiters. As I exited, Mr. Godfrey embraced his sisters and ogled Miss Rachel, offering me a token of affection: "Dear old Betteredge, I have the truest regard for him!" It was a stockpile of love to draw upon. By comparison, Mr. Franklin seemed a savage.

At the appointed half-hour, I presented myself in my lady's room. Our conversation largely mirrored our previous discussion at the Shivering Sand, save that I carefully withheld any mention of the jugglers, as nothing had transpired to justify alarming her. Upon receiving my dismissal, I observed that she viewed the Colonel's motives in the darkest light possible and was determined to remove the Moonstone from her daughter's possession at the earliest opportunity.

Returning to my quarters, I encountered Mr. Franklin, who inquired if I had seen his cousin Rachel. I had seen none. He then asked for Mr. Godfrey. I claimed ignorance, though I suspected the cousins were not far apart. Mr. Franklin's suspicions aligned with mine; he tugged his beard and slammed the library door with a sound laden with meaning.

My preparations for the birthday dinner proceeded undisturbed until it was time to dress for the evening. Just as I donned my white waistcoat, Penelope appeared at my dressing table, ostensibly to brush my remaining hair and adjust my cravat. Her spirits were high, and she had news. She kissed the top of my bald head and whispered, "News for you, father! Miss Rachel has refused him."

"Who is 'him'?" I asked.

"The ladies' committee-man, father," she replied. "A nasty sly fellow! I hate him for trying to supplant Mr. Franklin!"

Had I possessed the breath, I would have protested this indecent characterization of an eminent philanthropist. However, Penelope was busy tightening my cravat, and the full force of her indignation found its way into her fingers. I was nearly strangled.

"I saw him take her away alone into the rose-garden," she continued. "I waited behind the holly to observe their return. They had gone out arm-in-arm, both laughing. They returned walking separately, grave as could be, avoiding eye contact in a manner impossible to miss. I have never been more delighted in my life! There is one woman in the world who can resist Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, at any rate; and if I were a lady, I should be another!"

I would have protested again, but Penelope had seized the hairbrush, transferring the entirety of her feelings into that implement. If you are bald, you understand the sacrifice; if not, skip this section and thank providence for the barrier between your brush and your head.

"Just on the other side of the holly," Penelope resumed, "Mr. Godfrey came to a halt. 'You prefer,' he said, 'that I should stop here as if nothing had happened?' Miss Rachel turned on him like lightning. 'You have accepted my mother's invitation,' she said; 'and you are here to meet her guests. Unless you wish to make a scandal in the house, you will remain, of course!' She advanced a few steps, then seemed to soften. 'Let us forget what has passed, Godfrey,' she said, 'and let us remain cousins still.' She offered her hand. He kissed it—an act I would have deemed a liberty—and she withdrew. He waited alone, head bowed, grinding a hole slowly into the gravel with his heel; you never saw a man look more discomfited. 'Awkward!' he muttered between his teeth when he looked up, then went on to the house—'very awkward!' If that was his self-assessment, he was correct. Awkward enough, I'm sure. And the conclusion, as I told you all along," she cried, finishing me off with a final, scorching stroke of the brush. "Mr. Franklin's the man!"

I reclaimed the hairbrush and prepared to deliver the reproof my daughter's language and conduct richly deserved. Before I could utter a word, the crash of carriage wheels outside silenced me. The first of the dinner guests had arrived. Penelope instantly fled. I donned my coat and checked my reflection in the glass. My head burned crimson like a lobster, yet in every other respect, I was impeccably dressed for the evening's ceremonies. I entered the hall just in time to announce the arrival of the first two guests: the philanthropist's parents, Mr. and Mrs. Ablewhite.

The rest of the company trailed behind the Ablewhites, forming a procession that eventually settled into a complete assembly of twenty-four souls. It was a dignified tableau, particularly when they were arranged around the dinner table and the Rector of Frizinghall rose to offer grace with a fluency that bordered on the theatrical.

There is no necessity to catalogue every guest; save for two specific individuals, none of them will appear again in my narrative. Those two, however, occupied the seats immediately flanking Miss Rachel, the undisputed queen of the evening. Her presence was the focal point of every gaze, a fact that caused a quiet irritation to my lady, for she wore the very jewel that had been her birthday gift—the Moonstone. Though unmounted, the gem had been ingeniously affixed by Mr. Franklin as a brooch against the bosom of her white gown. Its sheer size and brilliance commanded universal awe, yet it was only the two guests beside Miss Rachel who offered commentary of a peculiar nature.

To her left sat Mr. Candy, the local physician. He was a genial, diminutive man, though plagued by a penchant for ill-timed jests and a tendency to plunge into conversation with strangers without the requisite caution. In society, he frequently caused friction, but in his medical practice, he possessed an intuitive discretion that often saved him from error. His comment regarding the Diamond was delivered as a scientific prank. "We shall first heat it, Miss Rachel," he declared gravely, "to a precise degree, then expose it to an air current, and gradually—puff!—evaporate the stone, sparing you the burden of guarding such a precious treasure!" My lady watched with a furrowed brow, wishing the doctor were sincere enough to have convinced Miss Rachel to sacrifice her gift for the sake of science.

On Miss Rachel's right sat an eminent figure: the celebrated Indian traveler, Mr. Murthwaite. He was a tall, lean, wiry man of brown complexion, possessing a weary demeanor and an intensely steady gaze. Rumors suggested he was weary of domestic life and yearned to return to the wilds of the East. Throughout the entire dinner, he spoke scarcely six words and consumed not a drop of wine, his sole interest fixed entirely on the Moonstone. The legend of the gem had evidently reached him during his perilous wanderings. After gazing at it for so long that Miss Rachel grew confused, he spoke in his cool, immovable tone: "If you ever travel to India, Miss Verinder, do not take your uncle's gift. A Hindoo diamond is often consecrated to a religion. I know a city and a temple where, dressed as you are now, your life would not be worth five minutes' purchase." Miss Rachel, secure in England, found the warning delightful. The Bouncers, dropping their cutlery in excitement, cried out, "Oh, how interesting!" My lady fidgeted and hastily changed the subject.

As the meal progressed, I perceived that the festival was failing to prosper as previous celebrations had. Looking back with the benefit of hindsight, I am inclined to believe the cursed Diamond cast a blight upon the entire gathering. I circulated with wine, urging guests to try unpopular dishes, yet nine times out of ten they refused, citing my old loyalty to no avail. Silence stretched across the table, creating an atmosphere of personal discomfort. When speech resumed, it was innocently directed toward the worst possible ends. Mr. Candy, for instance, uttered unluckier phrases than I had ever heard him.

Consider the case of Mrs. Threadgall, widow of the late Professor Threadgall. This good lady habitually omitted the fact that her husband was deceased, assuming every adult in England should know this. When the topic of human anatomy arose, she seamlessly introduced her late husband as his favorite pastime. Unluckily, Mr. Candy, sitting opposite and unaware of the deceased, seized the opportunity to assist the Professor's anatomical recreations. "They have remarkably fine skeletons lately at the College of Surgeons," he announced loudly. "I recommend the Professor visit them." The company fell silent out of respect for the dead professor. Behind Mrs. Threadgall, I whispered encouragement, but she dropped her head and murmured, "My beloved husband is no more." Mr. Candy, oblivious, continued louder. "The Professor may not be aware that a College card admits him daily between ten and four." Mrs. Threadgall buried her face in her tucker and repeated, "My beloved husband is no more." I signaled frantically; Miss Rachel touched Mr. Candy's arm; my lady glared. To no avail. He pressed on. "I shall be delighted to send the Professor my card if you mention his present address." "His present address, sir, is the grave," Mrs. Threadgall snapped, her voice ringing with fury. "He has been dead ten years." "Oh, good Heavens!" cried Mr. Candy. Save for the Bouncers, who laughed, a profound blankness fell over the room, as if everyone were heading toward the grave alongside the professor.

The rest of the guests were equally provoking. Mr. Godfrey, usually eloquent, remained silent in private, perhaps sulking after his earlier discomfiture in the rose-garden. He confined his discourse to the lady seated beside him, a spiritually minded committee-woman with a fine collarbone and a taste for dry champagne. Overheard snippets revealed they were debating serious matters: religion equated to love, love to religion, and earth to a heaven slightly worn but repaired. Yet, why did Mr. Godfrey keep such profound insights to himself?

Mr. Franklin, too, failed to stir the company into merriment. Having recovered from his earlier setback, he was in high spirits, yet he invariably pitched conversations on the wrong subjects or addressed the wrong persons, offending some and puzzling all. His foreign training manifested in bewildering ways: he discussed a wife's admiration for another man with the vicar's maiden aunt in French; he lectured the lord of the manor on breeding bulls using German philosophy; and when the county member raged about democracy, Mr. Franklin replied in Italian that what remained were "Love, Music, and Salad." These outbursts terrified the company. Furthermore, when the English side of him emerged, he ridiculed the medical profession so sharply that he enraged good-natured Mr. Candy.

The dispute began when Mr. Franklin admitted to poor sleep. Mr. Candy diagnosed disordered nerves and prescribed medicine. Mr. Franklin retorted that medicine and groping in the dark were synonymous. Mr. Candy countered that Mr. Franklin was constitutionally groping in the dark and needed medicine to find his way. Mr. Franklin agreed, noting he had finally understood the phrase "the blind leading the blind." The exchange escalated until both were heated, Mr. Candy losing control in defense of his profession. My lady was forced to intervene, forbidding the dispute. This act of authority extinguished the remaining spirits. Talk resumed sporadically but lacked life. The Devil—or the Diamond—seemed to possess the dinner party, and it was a relief when my mistress rose to dismiss the ladies to the drawing-room.

While arranging decanters before old Mr. Ablewhite, a sound from the terrace startled me. It was the beat of an Indian drum. Here were the jugglers returning with the Moonstone! As they rounded the corner, the two Bouncers raced ahead like rockets to witness the tricks. The ladies and gentlemen followed. Before one could exclaim, "Lord bless us!" the rogues were performing salaams, and the Bouncers were kissing the little boy.

Mr. Franklin moved to Miss Rachel's side, and I positioned myself behind her. If our suspicions were correct, she stood there, innocent of the truth, displaying the Diamond to the Indians. I lost my composure amidst the vexation of the dinner and the timing of their return. The first distinct event was the sudden appearance of Mr. Murthwaite. Skirting the circle of guests, he approached the jugglers quietly and spoke in their native tongue. If he had stabbed them, they could not have reacted with greater tigerish speed. Instantly, they bowed and salaamed in their most polite, serpentine manner. After a brief exchange, Mr. Murthwaite withdrew as silently as he had arrived. The chief Indian, acting as interpreter, turned back to the guests. I noted his coffee-colored face had turned grey. He bowed to my lady and announced the exhibition was over. The Bouncers, despairingly disappointed, shouted at Mr. Murthwaite for stopping the show. The chief Indian reiterated that the juggling was finished. The little boy collected the hat, the ladies retired to the drawing-room, and the gentlemen (save Mr. Franklin and Mr. Murthwaite) returned to their wine. I and the footman escorted the Indians safely off the premises.

Returning via the shrubbery, I smelled tobacco and found Mr. Franklin and Mr. Murthwaite walking slowly among the trees, the latter smoking a cheroot. Mr. Franklin beckoned me to join them. "This," he said, introducing me to the traveler, "is Gabriel Betteredge, the old servant and friend of our family. Tell him what you have just told me."

Mr. Murthwaite removed his cheroot and leaned against a tree. "Mr. Betteredge," he began, "those three Indians are no more jugglers than you and I."

This was a new surprise. I asked if he had met them before. "Never," he replied. "But I know true Indian juggling. What you saw was a clumsy imitation. Unless I am mistaken, those men are high-caste Brahmins. I charged them with being disguised, and you saw how it affected them, despite the Hindoos' skill at concealment. There is a mystery here. They have doubly sacrificed their caste—first by crossing the sea, second by disguising themselves as jugglers. Such a sacrifice requires a tremendous motive and justification to recover their caste upon return."

I was struck dumb. Mr. Murthwaite continued smoking. Mr. Franklin, shifting between his various personas, broke the silence. He hesitated to intrude on family matters but felt bound, for the sake of Lady Verinder and her daughter, to share information that might provide a clue. Speaking in confidence, he recounted everything he had told me at the Shivering Sand. Even the stoic Mr. Murthwaite let his cheroot go out, captivated.

"Now," Mr. Franklin asked, "what does your experience say?"

"My experience," the traveler answered, "says you have had more narrow escapes of your life, Mr. Franklin Blake, than I have had of mine; and that is saying a great deal."

Mr. Franklin was astonished. "Is it really as serious as that?"

"In my opinion, yes," Mr. Murthwaite confirmed. "After what you told me, I cannot doubt that restoring the Moonstone to the Indian idol is the motive and justification for their sacrifice of caste. Those men will wait with the patience of cats and strike with the ferocity of tigers. How you have escaped them I cannot imagine. You carried the Diamond back and forth between London and here, and you are still alive! Let us account for it. Was it broad daylight when you retrieved the jewel from the bank in London?"

"Broad daylight," said Mr. Franklin.

"And many people in the streets?"

"Plenty."

"Did you settle to arrive at Lady Verinder's house at a specific time? It is a lonely stretch of country. Did you keep your appointment?"

"No. I arrived four hours early."

"I congratulate you! When did you take the Diamond to the bank here?"

"An hour after bringing it to this house—and three hours before anyone expected me."

"I congratulate you again! Did you return alone?"

"No. I rode back with my cousins and the groom."

"I congratulate you for the third time! If you ever wish to travel beyond civilized limits, Mr. Blake, let me know, and I will accompany you. You are a lucky man."

I interjected, finding this contrary to English sensibilities. "You do not truly mean to say, sir, that they would have taken Mr. Franklin's life to retrieve their Diamond if given the chance?"

"Do you smoke, Mr. Betteredge?" asked the traveler.

"Yes, sir."

"Do you care much for the ashes left in your pipe when you empty it?"

"No, sir."

"In the country those men came from, they care just as much about killing a man as you do about emptying pipe ashes. If a thousand lives stood between them and the Diamond, and they believed they could destroy those lives without discovery, they would take them all. The sacrifice of caste is serious in India; the sacrifice of life is nothing."

I declared them murdering thieves; Mr. Murthwaite called them wonderful people. Mr. Franklin, offering no opinion, steered us back to the immediate problem. "They have seen the Moonstone on Miss Verinder's dress. What is to be done?"

"What your uncle threatened," Mr. Murthwaite answered. "Colonel Herncastle understood these people. Send the Diamond tomorrow, under heavy guard, to be cut up in Amsterdam. Make half a dozen diamonds of it instead of one. There is an end to its sacred identity as The Moonstone—and an end to the conspiracy."

Mr. Franklin turned to me. "There is no help for it. We must speak to Lady Verinder tomorrow."

"What about tonight, sir?" I asked. "Suppose the Indians return?"

"The Indians will not risk coming back tonight," Mr. Murthwaite replied before Mr. Franklin could speak. "The direct path is rarely the one they choose, especially when a mistake could be fatal."

"But suppose they are bolder than you think?" I persisted.

"In that case," said Mr. Murthwaite, "let the dogs loose. Have you any large dogs in the yard?"

"Two, sir. A mastiff and a bloodhound."

"They will suffice. In this emergency, Mr. Betteredge, the mastiff and bloodhound have one great merit: they are unlikely to be troubled by your scruples regarding the sanctity of human life."

The piano's strumming drifted from the drawing-room as he fired this warning. He discarded his cheroot and took Mr. Franklin's arm to return to the ladies. I noticed the sky clouding over rapidly; Mr. Mur

The final carriage wheels had long since vanished into the twilight when I retreated to the inner hall, finding Samuel already stationed at the side-table, diligently dispensing brandy and soda. My Lady and Miss Rachel emerged from the drawing-room, trailed by the two gentlemen. Mr. Godfrey accepted a glass, but Mr. Franklin declined, sinking into a chair with the exhaustion of a man drained dry. The conversation of the evening, I presumed, had been too taxing for his constitution.

My Lady turned to bid them goodnight, her gaze lingering with a sharp, calculating intensity on the wicked Colonel's legacy, now glittering in her daughter's dress.

"Rachel," she asked, "where do you intend to deposit the Diamond tonight?"

Miss Rachel was in a buoyant, giddy mood, prone to the kind of nonsensical chatter that often passes for sense in young women when they are at the height of excitement. First, she claimed ignorance of where to place it. Then, she suggested her dressing-table, alongside her other trinkets. Suddenly, she recalled the Diamond's terrifying, moon-like luminescence in the dark, which might frighten her in the dead of night. Next, she conceived the brilliant idea of placing the Indian diamond in the Indian cabinet in her sitting-room, allowing two beautiful native treasures to admire one another.

"My dear!" My Lady interjected, cutting off her flow of nonsense. "Your Indian cabinet has no lock."

"Good heavens, Mamma!" cried Miss Rachel, feigning horror. "Is this an hotel? Are there thieves in the house?"

Ignoring this theatrical display, My Lady wished the gentlemen goodnight and then approached Miss Rachel, kissing her forehead. "Why not let *me* keep the Diamond for you tonight?" she proposed.

Miss Rachel received this offer with the same reluctance a child might show toward being parted from a beloved doll. My Lady saw there was no reasoning with her that night. "Come into my room, Rachel, the first thing tomorrow morning," she said softly. "I shall have something to say to you." With those words, she departed slowly, her countenance suggesting a deep, unspoken dissatisfaction with the path her thoughts were leading her.

Miss Rachel was the next to bid farewell. She shook hands first with Mr. Godfrey, who stood at the far end of the hall gazing at a picture, before turning to Mr. Franklin. He remained seated in a corner, weary and silent.

I cannot recount the words exchanged between them. But standing near the old oak frame holding our large mirror, I saw her reflection slip the locket Mr. Franklin had given her from the bosom of her dress. She held it up to him, a smile playing on her lips that held a meaning far deeper than usual, before she tripped off to bed. This incident shook my confidence in my own judgment. I began to wonder if Penelope might be right regarding the state of her young lady's affections after all.

As soon as Miss Rachel left, Mr. Franklin noticed me. His variable humor, which shifts constantly, had already shifted onto the topic of the Indians.

"Betteredge," he said, "I'm half inclined to think I took Mr. Murthwaite too seriously during our talk in the shrubbery. I wonder if he's been trying any of his traveler's tales on us? Do you really mean to let the dogs loose?"

"I'll relieve them of their collars, sir," I answered, "and leave them free to take a turn in the night if they smell a reason for it."

"All right," said Mr. Franklin. "We'll see what is to be done tomorrow. I am not at all disposed to alarm my aunt without a pressing reason. Good-night."

He looked so worn and pale as he nodded and took his candle that I ventured to suggest a drop of brandy-and-water as a nightcap. Mr. Godfrey, walking toward us from the other end of the hall, backed me up, urging Mr. Franklin to take something before retiring.

I noted these trifling details because, after all I had witnessed that day, it pleased me to observe that our two gentlemen were on as good terms as ever. Their verbal warfare, heard by Penelope in the drawing-room, and their rivalry for Miss Rachel's favor seemed to have bred no serious discord. They were both good-tempered and men of the world; there is certainly a merit in people of station that they are not nearly so quarrelsome as those of no station.

Mr. Franklin declined the drink initially but, on the landing, either persuaded by his cousin or simply changing his mind as he often did, he called down, "Perhaps I may want it in the night. Send up some brandy-and-water into my room."

I sent Samuel up with the drink and then went out to unbuckle the dogs' collars. They lost their heads with astonishment at being set loose so late, jumping upon me like puppies. However, the rain soon cooled them down; they lapped a drop of water each and crept back into their kennels. As I entered the house, I noticed signs in the sky promising a break in the weather. For the moment, it still poured heavily, and the ground was a perfect soup.

Samuel and I went through the house, securing everything as usual. I examined everything myself, trusting nothing to my deputy on this occasion. All was safe and fast when I rested my old bones in bed between midnight and one in the morning.

The worries of the day had been too much for me. I suffered a touch of Mr. Franklin's malady that night. It was sunrise before I finally fell asleep. Throughout the time I lay awake, the house was as quiet as the grave, disturbed only by the splash of rain and the sighing of the wind among the trees as a breeze sprang up with the morning.

About half-past seven I woke and opened my window to a fine, sunshiny day. The clock had struck eight, and I was just going out to chain the dogs again when I heard a sudden whisking of petticoats on the stairs behind me.

I turned, and there was Penelope flying down after me like mad. "Father!" she screamed. "Come upstairs, for God's sake! The Diamond is gone!"

"Are you out of your mind?" I asked.

"Gone!" said Penelope. "Gone, nobody knows how! Come up and see."

She dragged me into our young lady's sitting-room, which opened into her bedroom. There, on the threshold of her bedroom door, stood Miss Rachel, almost as white as the white dressing-gown she wore. Beside her stood the two doors of the Indian cabinet, wide open. One of the drawers inside was pulled out as far as it would go.

"Look!" said Penelope. "I myself saw Miss Rachel put the Diamond into that drawer last night." I went to the cabinet. The drawer was empty.

"Is this true, miss?" I asked.

With a look that was not like herself and a voice that was not her own, Miss Rachel answered as my daughter had: "The Diamond is gone!"

Having said this, she withdrew into her bedroom and shut and locked the door.

Before we knew which way to turn, My Lady came in, hearing my voice and wondering what had happened. The news of the loss seemed to petrify her. She went straight to Miss Rachel's bedroom and insisted on being admitted. Miss Rachel let her in.

The alarm ran through the house like fire, catching the two gentlemen next. Mr. Godfrey was the first to emerge from his room, holding up his hands in a state of bewilderment that did little credit to his natural strength of mind. Mr. Franklin, whose clear head I had confidently relied upon, seemed as helpless as his cousin. For a wonder, he had had a good night's rest, and the unaccustomed luxury of sleep had apparently stupefied him. However, after swallowing his cup of coffee, his brains brightened. The clear-headed side turned up, and he took the matter in hand, resolutely and cleverly.

He first sent for the servants, instructing them to leave all lower doors and windows exactly as they had been left when we locked up. He next proposed to his cousin and me to ensure the Diamond had not accidentally dropped somewhere out of sight—say, at the back of the cabinet or behind the table. Having searched both places and found nothing, and having questioned Penelope to no avail, Mr. Franklin suggested extending inquiries to Miss Rachel and sent Penelope to knock at her bedroom door.

My Lady answered the knock and closed the door behind her. The moment after, we heard it locked inside by Miss Rachel. My mistress came out among us, looking sorely puzzled and distressed. "The loss of the Diamond seems to have quite overwhelmed Rachel," she said. "She shrinks, in the strangest manner, from speaking of it, even to *me*. It is impossible you can see her for the present."

Adding to our perplexities, My Lady, after a little effort, recovered her usual composure and acted with her usual decision. "I suppose there is no help for it?" she said quietly. "I suppose I have no alternative but to send for the police?"

"And the first thing for the police to do," added Mr. Franklin, catching her up, "is to lay hands on the Indian jugglers who performed here last night."

My Lady and Mr. Godfrey both started, looking surprised.

"I can't stop to explain myself now," Mr. Franklin went on. "I can only tell you that the Indians have certainly stolen the Diamond. Give me a letter of introduction to one of the magistrates at Frizinghall—merely telling him that I represent your interests—and let me ride off with it instantly. Our chance of catching the thieves may depend on our not wasting one unnecessary minute."

Whether it was the French side or the English, the right side of Mr. Franklin seemed uppermost now. The only question was how long it would last.

He put pen, ink, and paper before his aunt, who wrote the letter he wanted a little unwillingly. If it had been possible to overlook such an event as the loss of a jewel worth twenty thousand pounds, I believe—with My Lady's opinion of her late brother and her distrust of his birthday gift—it would have been privately a relief to her to let the thieves get off with the Moonstone scot-free.

I went out with Mr. Franklin to the stables and asked him how the Indians, whom I suspected as shrewdly as he did, could possibly have entered the house.

"One of them might have slipped into the hall in the confusion when the dinner party was leaving," said Mr. Franklin. "The fellow may have been under the sofa while my aunt and Rachel were talking about where the Diamond was to be put. He would only have to wait till the house was quiet, and there it would be in the cabinet, to be had for the taking." With those words, he called to the groom to open the gate and galloped off.

This seemed certainly the only rational explanation. But how had the thief contrived to escape? I had found the front door locked and bolted as I had left it. The other doors and windows were still safe and fast. The dogs, too? Suppose the thief had escaped by dropping from an upper window; how had he evaded them? Had he been provided with drugged meat? As the doubt crossed my mind, the dogs themselves came galloping at me round a corner, rolling over on the wet grass in such lively health and spirits that it was with no small difficulty I brought them to reason and chained them up again. The more I turned it over in my mind, the less satisfactory Mr. Franklin's explanation appeared.

We had our breakfasts—whatever happens in a house, robbery or murder, you must have your breakfast. When we had done, My Lady sent for me, and I found myself compelled to tell her all that I had hitherto concealed regarding the Indians and their plot. Being a woman of high courage, she soon got over the first startling effect of my communication. Her mind seemed far more perturbed about her daughter than about the heathen rogues and their conspiracy. "You know how odd Rachel is," My Lady said. "But I have never, in all my experience, seen her so strange and reserved. The loss of her jewel seems almost to have turned her brain. Who would have thought that horrible Diamond could have laid such a hold on her in so short a time?"

It was certainly strange. Taking toys and trinkets in general, Miss Rachel was nothing like so mad after them as most young girls. Yet there she was, still locked up inconsolably in her bedroom. It is fair to add that she was not the only one thrown out of the regular groove. Mr. Godfrey, though professionally a consoler-general, seemed at a loss where to look for his own resources. Having no company to amuse him and no chance to comfort Miss Rachel, he wandered hither and thither about the house and gardens in an aimless, uneasy way. He was in two different minds about whether to relieve the family of his presence as a guest or stay on to offer humble services. He decided ultimately that staying was perhaps the most customary and considerate course. Circumstances try the metal a man is really made of. Mr. Godfrey, tried by circumstances, showed himself of weaker metal than I had thought him. As for the women-servants, excepting Rosanna Spearman who kept to herself, they took to whispering in corners and staring suspiciously, as is the manner of that weaker half of the human family when anything extraordinary happens. I myself acknowledged to have been fidgety and ill-tempered. The cursed Moonstone had turned us all upside down.

A little before eleven, Mr. Franklin came back. The resolute side of him had, to all appearance, given way under the stress. He had left us at a gallop; he came back at a walk. When he went away, he was made of iron. When he returned, he was stuffed with cotton, as limp as limp could be.

"Well," said My Lady, "are the police coming?"

"Yes," said Mr. Franklin. "They said they would follow me in a fly. Superintendent Seegrave of your local police force and two of his men. A mere form! The case is hopeless."

"What! Have the Indians escaped, sir?" I asked.

"The poor ill-used Indians have been

Thursday night slipped by in silence, unbroken by incident. But Friday dawned with two distinct shocks to the household. First, the baker's boy reported seeing Rosanna Spearman the previous afternoon, veiled heavily, walking toward Frizinghall across the moor. It was a baffling error; Rosanna's broad shoulders were unmistakable, and she had been confined to her sickbed all Thursday. The boy must have been mistaken. Second, the postman delivered news of Dr. Candy. Despite his waterproof coat, the rain had seeped through, bringing on a fever. He was now delirious, babbling nonsense in his high state. While we pitied the little doctor, Mr. Franklin seemed chiefly concerned for Miss Rachel's welfare, fearing that without the Moonstone's fate soon revealed, she desperately needed the finest medical attention available.

Before breakfast concluded, a telegram arrived from Mr. Blake, the elder. He had secured the aid of the Commissioner to find the right man for us: Sergeant Cuff, arriving by the morning train from London. The name caused Mr. Franklin to start; he recalled strange anecdotes about the detective from his father's lawyer. "I begin to hope we are seeing the end of our anxieties," he declared. "If half the stories are true, there is no equal in England to Sergeant Cuff!"

Excitement mounted as the hour approached. Superintendent Seegrave retreated to a room to draft his report, eager to hand it over. I wished to fetch the Sergeant myself, but Lady Verinder's carriage was unavailable, and the pony-chaise was reserved for Mr. Godfrey, who delayed his departure until the last train to hear the London officer's thoughts, though he must return to town by Friday night for a charity consultation.

When the time came, I stood at the gate. A fly pulled up, and out stepped a grizzled, elderly man so lean he seemed to have no flesh on his bones. Dressed in somber black with a white cravat, his face was sharp as a hatchet, his skin yellow and withered like an autumn leaf. His steely-grey eyes possessed a disconcerting habit of expecting more from you than you offered. His walk was soft, his voice melancholy, his long fingers hooked like claws. He looked less like a police officer and more like a parson or an undertaker—a stark, unsettling contrast to the bustling Superintendent Seegrave.

"Is this Lady Verinder's?" he asked.
"Yes, sir."
"I am Sergeant Cuff."

As we walked toward the house, I introduced myself to establish trust. Yet, Cuff said not a word of business. Instead, he admired the grounds, remarking on the brisk sea air. I privately wondered how such a man had built his reputation. Upon reaching the house, he asked for my lady, who was in a conservatory. While we waited, Cuff wandered into the rosery, displaying a surprising, almost childish fascination with the flowers.

"Ah, you've got the right exposure here," he said, stroking a white musk rose as if it were a child. "Gravel walks are too hard for them. Grass, Mr. Gardener—grass between your roses."

"You seem fond of roses, Sergeant," I ventured.
"I haven't much time for anything," he replied, his voice softening. "But when I do have a moment, the roses get it. I began my life among them, and I shall end it there, if I can. One day I shall retire from catching thieves and try my hand at growing roses. There will be grass walks between my beds."

"It seems an odd taste for a man in your line," I noted.
"If you look about you," Cuff countered, "you'll see a man's tastes are often the opposite of his business. Show me two things more opposite than a rose and a thief. I'll correct my tastes accordingly."

Just then, Lady Verinder appeared. Her presence seemed to unsettle her, causing a rare embarrassment. Cuff, however, immediately put her at ease. He asked if anyone else had been consulted regarding the robbery and, upon learning that Superintendent Seegrave was present, requested to speak with him first.

The two officers retired together. When they emerged, Seegrave was agitated, while Cuff was yawning.
"The Sergeant wishes to see Miss Verinder's sitting-room," Seegrave announced pompously. "Attend the Sergeant!"

I led the way upstairs. Cuff moved softly through the Indian cabinet and the boudoir, asking questions that drifted between the two of us, largely unintelligible. Finally, he stopped before the decorative painting on the door, laying a lean finger on the small smear beneath the lock that Seegrave had previously blamed on the servants' petticoats.

"That's a pity," Cuff murmured. "How did it happen?"
He asked me. I explained that the servants had crowded in and that Seegrave had ordered them out before further damage occurred.
"Right!" Seegrave interjected militantly. "The petticoats did it."
"Did you notice which petticoat?" Cuff asked, ignoring the superintendent and addressing me.
"No, sir."
"You noticed, I suppose?" Cuff turned to Seegrave.
"I can't charge my memory, Sergeant," Seegrave stammered, dismissing it as a mere trifle.

Cuff's expression darkened. "I made a private inquiry last week, Mr. Superintendent. At one end a murder, at the other a spot of ink on a tablecloth. In all my experience of this dirty world, I have never met a 'trifle.' Before we proceed, we must see the petticoat and know for certain when the paint was wet."

Seegrave bristled, offering to summon the women. Cuff sighed and shook his head. "No. Let us take the paint first. It is a question of Yes or No. The petticoats are a question of length. What o'clock was it when the servants were in here? Eleven. Is there anyone who knows if the paint was wet or dry then?"
"Her ladyship's nephew, Mr. Franklin Blake, knows," I said.

Franklin entered promptly. "That door was painted by Miss Verinder under my inspection. The vehicle I used dries whatever color is applied within twelve hours."
"Do you remember when the smeared bit was done?"
"Perfectly," Franklin answered. "It was the last piece finished on Wednesday afternoon, around three o'clock."
"Today is Friday," Cuff noted. "Three o'clock Wednesday plus twelve hours is three o'clock Thursday morning. At eleven Thursday morning, you held your inquiry. Take three from eleven... the paint had been dry for eight hours."

It was a devastating blow to Seegrave. From that moment, Cuff dismissed his colleague as incompetent and turned his attention to Franklin.
"It is quite on the cards, sir," Cuff said, "that you have put the clue into our hands."

Suddenly, the bedroom door opened and Miss Rachel emerged. She addressed Cuff directly, ignoring his strangeness.
"Did you say," she pointed at Franklin, "that *he* had put the clue into your hands?"
"This is Miss Verinder," I whispered to Cuff.
"That gentleman, miss," Cuff replied, his steely eyes studying her face intently, "has possibly put the clue into our hands."

She glanced at Franklin, then quickly looked away. A strange disturbance clouded her mind; she flushed, then paled, a new, startling look entering her eyes.
"Having answered your question, miss," Cuff continued, "I beg leave to inquire. There is a smear on your door. Do you know when it was done, or who did it?"
Instead of answering, she asked, "Are you another police-officer?"
"I am Sergeant Cuff, miss, of the Detective Police."
"Do you think a young lady's advice worth having?"
"I shall be glad to hear it, miss."
"Do your duty by yourself—and don't allow Mr. Franklin Blake to help you!"

She spoke with such savage ill-will toward Franklin that, despite knowing her since infancy and loving her next to my lady, I felt ashamed of her. Cuff's gaze remained immovable. "Thank you, miss. Do you happen to know anything about the smear? Might you have done it by accident?"
"I know nothing about the smear."
Turning away, she retreated to her room, where I heard her burst into tears.

I looked at Franklin, who appeared more distressed than I. "I told you I was uneasy about her," he said. "And now you see why."
Cuff remarked casually, "Miss Verinder appears to be a little out of temper about the loss of her Diamond. It's a valuable jewel. Natural enough!"
Here was the excuse I had made for her before Seegrave, now repeated by a stranger. A cold shudder ran through me. I realized then that Cuff had seen something in Miss Rachel that cast a new, horrid light on the case.

"A young lady's tongue is a privileged member, sir," Cuff told Franklin. "Let us forget what has passed. Thanks to you, we know when the paint was dry. The next thing is to discover when the paint was last seen without that smear. *You* have a head on your shoulders—you understand what I mean."
Franklin composed himself. "The more we narrow the question of time, the more we narrow the field of inquiry."
"Did you notice your work here after you finished it on Wednesday?"
"I can't say I did."
"Did *you*?" Cuff turned to me.
"I can't say I did either, sir."
"Who was the last person in the room the last thing on Wednesday night?"
"Miss Rachel, I suppose."
Franklin corrected him gently, "Or possibly your daughter, Betteredge."

Cuff took me aside to the window. "Your Superintendent has set the servants' backs up. It is vital to smooth them down. Tell your daughter and the others: First, I have no evidence the Diamond has been stolen, only that it is lost. Second, my business is simply to ask them to help me find it."
I relayed the message, adding a third point: that they were free to move about the house. This calmed the household instantly.

Cuff approved of Penelope. Her evidence was given prettily: she had helped mix the colors, noticed the unfinished spot under the lock, and confirmed she had left the room at midnight without a smear. She held up her new dress, a gift from Miss Rachel, and swore there was no stain. Her father verified the dress. No trace of paint was found.

Next, Cuff questioned about large dogs, ruling them out. He then produced a magnifying glass. The smear showed no skin marks, only signs of a loose article of clothing brushing against the wet paint. Combining Penelope's testimony with Franklin's timeline, the culprit must have been in the room between midnight and three o'clock on Thursday morning.

Summarizing for Seegrave, Cuff outlined three discoveries needed: identify the stained garment, find its owner, and determine how that person accounted for being in the room during those specific hours. "If the person can't satisfy you, you haven't far to look for the hand that has got the Diamond," Cuff declared, dismissing Seegrave to his regular duties.

Seegrave, humiliated, retorted that Cuff was making a mountain out of a molehill.
"There is also such a thing as making nothing out of a molehill, in consequence of your head being too high to see it," Cuff replied coolly.

Left alone, Cuff stood by the window, whistling softly "The Last Rose of Summer"—a melancholy tune that suited his character and his obsession with roses. Turning from the window, he stared at Miss Rachel's bedroom door, nodded to himself, and requested ten minutes with my mistress. As I left, Franklin asked, "Can you guess yet who has stolen the Diamond?"
Cuff answered simply, "Nobody has stolen the Diamond."
We both stared, begging for explanation.
"Wait a little," Cuff said. "The pieces of the puzzle are not all put together yet."

I found my lady in her private sitting room. Her composure fractured instantly upon hearing that Sergeant Cuff wished to speak with her, replaced by a visible irritation.

"Must I see him?" she demanded, her voice tight. "Can you not represent me, Gabriel?"

I was at a loss to interpret this sudden defensiveness, and my confusion was evident on my face. She quickly clarified her distress.

"My nerves are shaken," she admitted, her tone dropping to a whisper. "There is something in that police officer from London that makes me recoil—I cannot name the reason. I have a presentiment that he carries trouble and misery into this house. It is foolish, and entirely unlike me, yet it is true."

I struggled for words. In my observations, I found Sergeant Cuff to be a man of considerable integrity, far removed from the caricature of the constable. My lady rallied somewhat after confiding in me; she possessed a natural courage that often surfaced when she felt safe.

"If I must see him, I must," she declared, though her resolve wavered. "But I cannot bear to face him alone. Bring him in, Gabriel, and remain here until he leaves."

This was the first onset of the 'megrims' I had witnessed in my mistress since her youth. I returned to the boudoir where Mr. Franklin was strolling into the garden to join Mr. Godfrey, whose departure was imminent. Sergeant Cuff and I proceeded directly to my mistress's room.

Upon seeing the Sergeant, my lady paled perceptibly. She commanded herself, however, and immediately asked if he objected to my presence. She introduced me as her trusted adviser and old servant, suggesting that my counsel would be most profitable regarding household matters. The Sergeant graciously accepted, noting that he intended to discuss the servants generally and that my experience in that sphere had already proven useful. My lady indicated two chairs, and we seated ourselves for the conference.

"I have already formed an opinion on this case," Sergeant Cuff began, "which I beg your ladyship's permission to withhold for the present. My immediate purpose is to report what I discovered upstairs in Miss Verinder's sitting-room and to outline my proposed course of action."

He detailed the smear on the paint and the conclusions drawn from it, echoing his earlier report to Superintendent Seegrave but with greater decorum. "One fact is certain," he concluded. "The Diamond is missing from the drawer in the cabinet. Another is nearly so: the marks from the smear must belong to an article of dress worn by someone in this house. We must locate that garment before proceeding further."

"And that discovery," my mistress interjected, "presumably implies the identification of the thief?"

"I beg your ladyship's pardon," the Sergeant corrected gently. "I do not claim the Diamond is stolen, only that it is missing. Finding the stained dress may lead us to its recovery."

She turned to me. "Do you understand this?"

"Sergeant Cuff understands it, my lady," I replied.

"How do you propose to find the stained dress?" she asked, addressing the Sergeant again. "My faithful servants, who have served me for years, have already had their boxes and rooms searched by the other officer. I cannot and will not permit them to be insulted in such a manner a second time!"

(The irony of a mistress demanding such deference! There was a woman in ten thousand, if you will.)

"That is precisely the point I was about to raise," the Sergeant said. "The other officer has severely hampered this inquiry by allowing the servants to believe they were specifically suspected. If I cause them to feel accused a second time, the obstacles they might erect are unpredictable—particularly among the women. Yet, their boxes *must* be searched again. The first search sought the Diamond; the second must seek the stained dress. I fully agree with you, my lady, that the servants' feelings deserve consideration. However, I am equally convinced that their wardrobes require examination."

This appeared to be a deadlock. My lady expressed her dismay in refined language.

"I have a plan to resolve this difficulty," Sergeant Cuff offered. "I propose explaining the case to the servants."

"The women will immediately feel suspected," I interrupted.

"The women will not, Mr. Betteredge," the Sergeant countered, "if I inform them I intend to examine the wardrobes of *everyone* who slept in the house on Wednesday night—from your ladyship downwards. It is a mere formality," he added, glancing sideways at my mistress, "but the servants will accept it as equitable treatment between themselves and their superiors. Instead of hindering the investigation, they will likely make a point of honor of assisting it."

I recognized the logic. My lady, once her initial shock subsided, saw the validity of his approach.

"Are you certain the investigation is necessary?" she asked.

"It is the most direct path to our objective, my lady."

My mistress rose to summon her maid. "You shall speak to the servants," she said, "holding the keys to my wardrobe."

Sergeant Cuff halted her with an unexpected query. "Had we not better ensure first that the other ladies and gentlemen in the house consent as well?"

"The only other lady is Miss Verinder," my mistress replied, surprised. "The only gentlemen are my nephews, Mr. Blake and Mr. Ablewhite. There is no fear of refusal from any of them."

I reminded her that Mr. Godfrey was departing. As I spoke, Mr. Godfrey knocked at the door to bid farewell, followed by Mr. Franklin, who was accompanying him to the station. My lady explained the situation. Mr. Godfrey resolved it instantly. He signaled to Samuel through the window to retrieve his portmanteau and then placed the key directly into Sergeant Cuff's hand. "My luggage may follow me to London once the inquiry concludes," he stated. The Sergeant accepted the key with a polite apology, noting that the example of the masters would greatly ease the servants' acceptance of the search. After leaving a sympathetic farewell message for Miss Rachel—one that clearly indicated he had not taken her previous hesitation as a final rejection—he departed. Mr. Franklin, following his cousin, assured the Sergeant that all his belongings were open to inspection and held no locks. The Sergeant expressed his gratitude. His strategy had been met with remarkable readiness by my lady, Mr. Godfrey, and Mr. Franklin. Only Miss Rachel remained to align herself with their cooperation before we could convene the servants to begin the search for the stained dress.

As soon as we were left alone, my lady's aversion to the Sergeant seemed to intensify, making the conference increasingly unpleasant. "If I send you Miss Verinder's keys," she said, "do you presume I have fulfilled all your requirements for the present?"
