"I beg your ladyship's pardon," Sergeant Cuff replied. "Before we commence, I should like, if possible, to inspect the washing-book. The stained article may be linen. Should the search yield nothing, I wish to account for every piece of linen in the house and every item sent to the wash. If an article is missing, there will be at least a presumption that it bears the paint stain and was deliberately destroyed yesterday or today by its owner. Furthermore, Superintendent Seegrave drew the women-servants' attention to the smear when they crowded into the room on Thursday morning. That," he added, turning to me, "may prove to be yet another error of Superintendent Seegrave's."

My lady instructed me to ring the bell for the washing-book. She remained seated, ready to address any further requests the Sergeant might have.

Rosanna Spearman brought the book in. The girl appeared miserably pale and haggard, though sufficiently recovered from her recent illness to perform her duties. Sergeant Cuff observed her attentively—her face as she entered, her crooked shoulder as she exited.

"Do you have anything further to say to me?" my lady asked, eager to escape the Sergeant's company.

The great Cuff opened the washing-book, comprehended it within thirty seconds, and closed it. "I venture to trouble your ladyship with one final question," he said. "Has the young woman who brought us this book been in your employ as long as the other servants?"

"Why do you ask?" my lady queried.

"The last time I saw her," the Sergeant answered, "she was in prison for theft."

At that, there was no recourse but honesty. My mistress emphasized Rosanna's impeccable conduct and the high regard she held at the Reformatory. "You do not suspect her, I hope?" she added earnestly.

"I have already told your ladyship that I suspect no one in this house of theft—at present."

With that assurance, my lady rose to go upstairs and request Miss Rachel's keys. The Sergeant preceded me in opening the door for her, bowing low. My lady shuddered as she passed him.

We waited, and waited, but the keys never arrived. Sergeant Cuff made no comment. He turned his melancholy gaze to the window, shoved his lanky hands into his pockets, and softly whistled "The Last Rose of Summer."

Finally, Samuel entered, not with the keys, but with a scrap of paper for me. I fumbled for my spectacles while the Sergeant's somber eyes fixed upon me. Two or three lines, written in pencil by my lady, informed me that Miss Rachel had flatly refused to allow her wardrobe to be examined. When asked for reasons, she had broken into tears. Pressed again, she had declared: "I won't, because I won't. I must yield to force if you use it, but I will yield to nothing else." I understood my lady's reluctance to confront the Sergeant with such a defiant answer from her daughter. Had I not been past the age of youthful sentimentality, I might have blushed at the thought of facing him myself.

"Any news of Miss Verinder's keys?" the Sergeant asked.

"My young lady refuses to have her wardrobe examined."

"Ah!" the Sergeant said.

His voice lacked the usual discipline of his countenance. The "Ah!" was delivered with the tone of a man hearing an expected outcome. He unsettled me, half-angering and half-frightening me, though I could not discern why.

"Must the search be abandoned?" I asked.

"Yes," the Sergeant replied. "The search must cease because your young lady refuses to submit like the others. We must examine all wardrobes or none. Send Mr. Ablewhite's portmanteau to London by the next train, and return the washing-book, with my compliments and thanks, to the girl who brought it."

He placed the book on the table and, withdrawing his penknife, began to trim his nails.

"You do not appear much disappointed," I observed.

"No," the Sergeant said. "I am not much disappointed."

I attempted to elicit an explanation.

"Why does Miss Rachel obstruct your path?" I inquired. "Is it not in her interest to assist you?"

"Wait a little, Mr. Betteredge—wait a little."

Cleverer minds than mine might have perceived his drift. A person less enamored with Miss Rachel might have seen it too. My lady's horror of him might, as I have since surmised, indicate that *she* had perceived his intent, albeit "in a glass darkly." I did not see it then—that is all I know.

"What is to be done next?" I asked.

Sergeant Cuff finished trimming the nail, inspected it with a melancholy interest, and capped his penknife.

"Come out into the garden," he said. "Let us have a look at the roses."

The most direct route from my lady's sitting-room to the garden lay through the shrubbery path, a thoroughfare familiar to all who knew the estate. It was, however, the particular haunt of Mr. Franklin Blake; whenever he wandered the grounds and could not be located elsewhere, he invariably sought refuge there.

I confess, with some shame, that I am a stubborn old man. The more resolutely Sergeant Cuff attempted to shield his thoughts from my prying gaze, the more determinedly I endeavored to peer behind his back. As we turned onto the shaded path, I tried a different tack.

"As matters stand," I remarked, "if I were in your shoes, I should be utterly at a loss."

"If you were in my position," the Sergeant replied, "you would have already formed a conclusion. Any lingering doubt you might harbor regarding your own deductions would now be dispelled. For the present, Mr. Betteredge, let us not concern ourselves with the nature of those conclusions. I have not summoned you here to corner me like a badger; I have brought you out to solicit information. You could have offered it within the house, yet doors and eavesdroppers have a peculiar talent for converging. In my profession, we cultivate a distinct appreciation for the open air."

Who was capable of outwitting *this* man? I surrendered my resistance and waited with as much patience as I could muster for his next move.

"We shall not dwell on the motives of your young lady," the Sergeant continued. "It is merely a pity that she refuses to assist me, for her reluctance renders this inquiry more arduous than it need be. We must now attempt to unravel the mystery of the smear upon the door—which, I assure you, is synonymous with the mystery of the Diamond itself—through alternative means. I have resolved to interrogate the servants, to probe their minds and actions, Mr. Betteredge, rather than rummage through their wardrobes. Before I commence, however, I require your assistance. You are an observant man; did you detect anything unusual among the staff following the discovery of the Diamond's loss? Allow for the natural panic, of course. Was there any specific discord? Did anyone appear out of sorts, unexpectedly irritable, or strangely indisposed?"

I barely had time to recall Rosanna Spearman's sudden illness at yesterday's dinner before I noticed Sergeant Cuff's eyes dart sharply toward the shrubbery. A soft exclamation escaped him: "Hullo!"

"What is the matter?" I asked.

"A touch of the rheumatics in my back," the Sergeant announced in a volume loud enough to invite a third party to our conversation. "We shall likely see a change in the weather soon."

A few paces further brought us to the corner of the house. Turning sharply to the right, we ascended the terrace and descended the central steps into the garden below. The Sergeant halted in the open space, granting us a view of the surroundings on all sides.

"Regarding that young person, Rosanna Spearman?" he inquired. "Given her appearance, it is unlikely she possesses a lover. Yet, for her own sake, I must ask you immediately: has she, poor wretch, secured a sweetheart like the others?"

What on earth did he mean by such a question under the current circumstances? I stared at him, unable to formulate an answer.

"I observed Rosanna Spearman concealing herself in the shrubbery as we passed," the Sergeant stated.

"When you uttered 'Hullo'?"

"Yes—precisely when I said 'Hullo!' If a sweetheart is involved, her hiding is of little consequence. If there is none, as appears to be the case in this household, then her concealment is a highly suspicious circumstance, and it will be my painful duty to act upon it."

What, in God's name, was I to say? I knew the shrubbery was Mr. Franklin's favorite promenade; I knew he would likely return from the station via that route; and I knew that Penelope had repeatedly caught her fellow-servant lingering there, always declaring to me that Rosanna's intent was to attract Mr. Franklin's attention. If my daughter was correct, she might well have been lying in wait for Mr. Franklin's return when the Sergeant spotted her. I was trapped between the dilemma of presenting Penelope's fanciful notion as my own truth, or allowing an unfortunate creature to suffer the grave consequences of arousing Sergeant Cuff's suspicion. Driven by pure pity for the girl—on my soul and my reputation, driven by pure pity—I provided the necessary explanations, informing the Sergeant that Rosanna had been foolish enough to set her heart on Mr. Franklin Blake.

Sergeant Cuff never laughed. On the rare occasions when amusement struck him, he merely curled the corners of his lips upward, nothing more. He curled them now.

"Would it not be wiser to claim she is mad enough to be an ugly girl and merely a servant?" he asked. "Falling in love with a gentleman of Mr. Franklin Blake's manners and appearance does not seem to me to be the most insane aspect of her conduct by any means. Nevertheless, I am glad the matter is clarified; it relieves the mind to have things settled. Yes, I shall keep this secret, Mr. Betteredge. I like to be tender toward human infirmity—though I rarely get opportunities to exercise that virtue in my line of work. Do you suppose Mr. Franklin Blake harbors no suspicion of the girl's fancy for him? Ah! He would have discovered it swiftly enough had she been beautiful. Ugly women endure a harsh existence in this world; let us hope it is compensated for them in the next. You possess a lovely garden here, and a meticulously kept lawn. Observe for yourself how much better the flowers appear with grass surrounding them rather than gravel. No, thank you. I shall not take a rose. It breaks my heart to sever them from the stem. Just as it breaks yours, you know, when there is wrongdoing in the servants' hall. Did you notice anything you could not explain regarding the servants when the loss of the Diamond was first discovered?"

I had managed quite well with Sergeant Cuff thus far. However, the sly manner in which he slipped that final question into the conversation placed me on guard. In plain English, I did not relish the prospect of aiding his inquiries when they led him, like a snake in the grass, among my fellow-servants.

"I noticed nothing," I replied, "save that we all lost our heads simultaneously, myself included."

"Oh," the Sergeant said, "is that all you have to report?"

I answered, with (as I flattered myself) an impassive countenance, "That is all."

Sergeant Cuff's dismal eyes fixed themselves hard upon my face.

"Mr. Betteredge," he said, "do you have any objection to oblige me by shaking hands? I have developed an extraordinary liking for you."

(Why he should have chosen the precise moment when I was deceiving him to offer me this proof of his good opinion is beyond all comprehension! I felt a surge of pride—I truly did feel a little proud of having bested the celebrated Cuff at last!)

We returned to the house. The Sergeant requested a private room, instructing me to summon the indoor servants, one by one, in order of rank, from highest to lowest.

I escorted Sergeant Cuff into my own chamber and then gathered the servants in the hall. Rosanna Spearman appeared among them, much as usual. She moved with the same swiftness as the Sergeant, and I suspect she had overheard his remarks concerning the servants in general just prior to his discovery of her. There she stood, looking as though she had never heard of a place called the shrubbery in her entire life.

I sent them in individually as directed. The cook was the first to enter the Court of Justice, otherwise known as my room. She remained but a brief moment. Her report upon exiting: "Sergeant Cuff is depressed in his spirits; yet, Sergeant Cuff is a perfect gentleman." My lady's own maid followed. She remained considerably longer. Her report: "If Sergeant Cuff does not believe a respectable woman, he might keep his opinion to himself, at any rate!" Penelope went next. She stayed only a moment or two. Her report: "Sergeant Cuff is much to be pitied. He must have been crossed in love, father, when he was a young man." The first housemaid followed Penelope. Like my lady's maid, she lingered for a long time. Her report: "I did not enter her ladyship's service, Mr. Betteredge, to be doubted to my face by a low police-officer!" Rosanna Spearman went next. She remained longer than any of the others. There was no report upon her exit—only dead silence and lips as pale as ashes. Samuel, the footman, followed Rosanna. He stayed a minute or two. His report: "Whoever blacks Sergeant Cuff's boots ought to be ashamed of himself." Nancy, the kitchen-maid, went last. She remained a minute or two. Her report: "Sergeant Cuff has a heart; _he_ doesn't cut jokes, Mr. Betteredge, with a poor hard-working girl."

Upon returning to the Court of Justice to inquire if there were further commands, I found the Sergeant at his old trick—gazing out the window and whistling "The Last Rose of Summer" to himself.

"Any discoveries, sir?" I inquired.

"If Rosanna Spearman requests leave to go out," said the Sergeant, "let the poor thing depart; but inform me beforehand."

I might as well have held my tongue regarding Rosanna and Mr. Franklin! It was plain enough; the unfortunate girl had fallen under Sergeant Cuff's suspicions, despite all my efforts to prevent it.

"I hope you do not think Rosanna is implicated in the loss of the Diamond?" I ventured to say.

The corners of the Sergeant's melancholy mouth curled upward, and he looked hard into my face, just as he had done in the garden.

"I think I had better not tell you, Mr. Betteredge," he said. "You might lose your head, you know, for the second time."

I began to doubt whether I had truly been one too many for the celebrated Cuff, after all! It was a relief that our conversation was interrupted by a knock at the door and a message from the cook. Rosanna Spearman _had_ requested to go out, for the usual reason: her head was bad, and she craved a breath of fresh air. At a signal from the Sergeant, I agreed. "Which is the servants' way out?" he asked when the messenger had departed. I showed him the path. "Lock the door of your room," the Sergeant commanded. "And if anyone asks for me, say I am in there, composing my mind." He curled the corners of his lips once more and vanished.

Left alone under these circumstances, a devouring curiosity compelled me to make some discoveries of my own.

It was evident that Sergeant Cuff's suspicions of Rosanna had been aroused by something he uncovered during his examination of the servants in my room. Now, the only two servants (excluding Rosanna herself) who had remained under scrutiny for any significant duration were my lady's own maid and the first housemaid; these were also the two women who had taken the lead in persecuting their unfortunate fellow-servant from the outset. Reaching this conclusion, I casually visited them in the servants' hall. Finding tea in progress, I instantly invited myself to join the meal. (For, _nota bene_, a drop of tea is to a woman's tongue what a drop of oil is to a wasting lamp.)

My reliance on the tea-pot as an ally was not unrewarded. In less than half an hour, I knew as much as the Sergeant himself.

It appeared that my lady's maid and the housemaid had neither believed in Rosanna's illness of the previous day. These two devils—I ask your pardon, but how else can one describe a pair of spiteful women?—had stolen upstairs at intervals during the Thursday afternoon. They had tried Rosanna's door and found it locked; they had knocked and received no answer; they had listened and heard no sound within. When the girl descended to tea and was subsequently sent back to bed, still out of sorts, the two aforementioned devils tried her door once more, found it locked, looked at the keyhole and discovered it stopped up, saw a light beneath the door at midnight, and heard the crackling of a fire (a fire in a servant's bedroom in the month of June!) at four in the morning. All this they had reported to Sergeant Cuff. In return for their eagerness to enlighten him, he had eyed them with sour and suspicious glances, making it plain that he did not believe either of them. Hence, the unfavorable reports they carried from the examination. Hence, also (without reckoning the influence of the tea-pot), their readiness to let their tongues run wild on the subject of the Sergeant's ungracious behavior.

Having gained some experience of the great Cuff's roundabout methods, and having last seen him evidently bent on following Rosanna privately when she departed for her walk, it seemed clear to me that he deemed it inadvisable to let the lady's maid and the housemaid know how materially they had assisted him. They were precisely the sort of women who, if he had treated their evidence as trustworthy, would have been puffed up by it and said or done something that would have put Rosanna Spearman on her guard.

I walked out into the fine summer afternoon, feeling profound sorrow for the poor girl and deep unease regarding the turn events had taken. Drifting toward the shrubbery some time later, I encountered Mr. Franklin. After returning from seeing his cousin off at the station, he had been with my lady, engaging in a long conversation with her. She had informed him of Miss Rachel's inexplicable refusal to allow her wardrobe to be examined, and had put him in such low spirits concerning my young lady that he seemed to shrink from speaking on the subject. The family temper appeared in his face that evening for the first time in my experience of him.

"Well, Betteredge," he said, "how does the atmosphere of mystery and suspicion in which we are all living now agree with you? Do you remember that morning when I

The Sergeant stood silent, his mind working in the shadows of the plantation of firs that bordered the treacherous quicksand. When we finally stepped onto the shifting sands, he rallied, his demeanor sharpening into the resolve of a man who had reached a conclusion.

"Mr. Betteredge," he addressed me, "since you have shared the burden of rowing my boat and proven yourself a capable assistant, there is no further need for our mutual games of pretense. I propose to speak plainly. You are withholding information that would implicate Rosanna Spearman, driven by the belief that she is a good girl to you and that you pity her plight. These are noble sentiments, Mr. Betteredge, but in this specific instance, they are a luxury we cannot afford. Rosanna Spearman faces no danger whatsoever—not if I pin her to the crime of the Diamond's disappearance with evidence as undeniable as the nose on your face!"

"Are you suggesting my lady will not prosecute?" I asked, my voice tight.

"I mean that your lady *cannot* prosecute," Cuff corrected sharply. "Rosanna is merely an instrument in the hands of another. She will be held harmless for the sake of that other person."

He spoke with a gravity that brooked no denial, yet a cold knot tightened in my stomach. "Can you name that other person?" I pressed.

"Can *you*, Mr. Betteredge?" he countered.

"No."

Cuff froze, surveying me with a look of melancholy curiosity. "It is my nature to be tender toward human frailty," he mused. "I feel particularly tender toward you, Mr. Betteredge, and I suspect you feel the same toward Rosanna. Do you happen to know if she has acquired a new outfit of linen recently?"

The question was abrupt, yet I saw no harm in answering truthfully. "She was formerly scantily provided," I admitted, emphasizing her past diligence. "However, my lady, in reward for her good conduct, gifted her a fresh outfit not two weeks ago."

"A miserable world," Cuff sighed, his tone shifting to something akin to grim amusement. "Life is a target, Mr. Betteredge, and misfortune is the archer. Had it not been for that new outfit, we would have found a nightgown or petticoat among her effects and pinned her down immediately. You have examined the servants yourself; you know what the others discovered outside her door. Do you recall what she was doing yesterday after falling ill? You cannot guess? It is as plain as the strip of light at the end of the trees. At eleven on Thursday, Superintendent Seegrave pointed out a smear on the door to the women. Rosanna, suspecting foul play regarding her own clothes, seized the first chance to retreat to her room. She found the paint stain on her nightgown or petticoat, feigned illness, and slipped away to town. There, alone in her room on Thursday night, she lit a fire—not to destroy the evidence, for she knew two servants were watching her door—but to dry and iron a substitute garment she had made. She kept the stained dress hidden, perhaps wearing it, and is currently engaged in disposing of it on that lonely stretch of beach ahead of us. I have traced her to your fishing village and a specific cottage. She lingered there, emerging with something concealed beneath her cloak. A cloak is an emblem of charity, covering a multitude of sins. I saw her depart northward along the coast. Is your shoreline here considered a fine specimen of marine scenery, Mr. Betteredge?"

"Yes," I replied briefly.

"Tastes differ," Cuff observed, his gaze fixed on the desolate water. "From my perspective, I have rarely admired a seascape less. If you are following someone along this coast, there is no cover to hide you. I chose between arresting Rosanna on suspicion or allowing her to finish her current gambit. For reasons I shall not trouble you with, I decided to delay alarming a certain individual until later tonight. I returned to the house to request that you guide me to the northern end of the beach via an alternate route. Sand is one of my finest detective tools; it prints footsteps with ruthless accuracy. If we do not intercept Rosanna, the sand may reveal her secrets if the light holds. Here is the sand. If you will excuse my suggestion, perhaps you should remain silent and let me lead?"

A peculiar fever, akin to a contagion, had seized me. Cuff moved between the sand dunes toward the beach, and I followed, my heart hammering against my ribs, waiting anxiously for the outcome.

As fate would have it, I found myself standing nearly where Rosanna and I had conversed previously when Mr. Franklin first arrived from London. While my eyes tracked the Sergeant, my mind drifted involuntarily to that past encounter. I felt a phantom sensation of Rosanna slipping her hand into mine, offering a grateful squeeze of thanks for my kindness. I almost heard her voice again, speaking of the Shivering Sand's magnetic pull, and saw her face brighten as she imagined Mr. Franklin approaching. My spirits sank with these memories, and the view of the lonely bay only deepened my unease.

The last vestiges of daylight were fading, casting a still, awful calm over the desolation. The ocean heaved silently against the great sandbank; the inner sea lay dim and motionless. Patches of yellow-white ooze floated on the dead surface, while scum and slime glimmered faintly near the rocky spits jutting north and south. It was the turn of the tide, and as I waited, the broad brown face of the quicksand began to dimple and quiver—the only movement in that horrific place.

The Sergeant started as the sand's shiver caught his eye. After a moment's observation, he turned back to me. "A treacherous location, Mr. Betteredge," he noted. "No sign of Rosanna Spearman anywhere on the beach."

He guided me lower to the shore, confirming that our own footprints were the only marks visible.

"How does the fishing village bear, standing where we are now?" Cuff asked.

"Cobb's Hole," I answered, "lies due south."

"I saw the girl this evening walking northward from Cobb's Hole," Cuff stated. "Therefore, she must have been heading toward this place. Is Cobb's Hole on the other side of that point of land? Can we reach it now, at low tide, via the beach?"

"Yes," I confirmed.

"We shall move briskly," Cuff urged. "I wish to locate where she left the shore before darkness falls."

We walked perhaps two hundred yards toward Cobb's Hole when Cuff suddenly dropped to his knees on the beach, appearing seized by a sudden fervor for prayer.

"There is merit in your marine landscape after all," Cuff remarked, rising. "Here are a woman's footsteps, Mr. Betteredge! Let us assume they belong to Rosanna until proven otherwise. They are confused—deliberately confused. Ah, poor soul, she understands the detective virtues of sand as well as I do! Yet, she seems to have been in too great a hurry to tread them thoroughly. Here is a footprint moving *from* Cobb's Hole, and another returning. Note the toe pointing straight to the water's edge, and two heel-marks further down, close to the water. I fear Rosanna is sly. It appears she intended to reach the place we just came from without leaving a trail. Did she walk through the water to that ledge of rocks behind us and return, leaving only those two heel marks? That fits my theory that she carried something under her cloak when she left the cottage. Not something to destroy, for then why the precautions? Something to hide, I believe. Perhaps visiting the cottage will reveal what that something is?"

At this proposal, my detective-fever cooled instantly. "You do not require me," I protested. "What good can I do?"

"The longer I know you, Mr. Betteredge, the more virtues I discover," Cuff replied. "Modesty is rare in this world, and you possess it in abundance. If I go alone, the villagers will be tongue-tied. If I go with you, I arrive as a neighbor's guest, and conversation flows naturally. How does that strike you?"

Lacking a witty retort, I asked which cottage he sought. Upon his description, I recognized it as the home of a fisherman named Yolland, his wife, and their two children, a son and a daughter. As I had noted before, Rosanna occasionally visited friends at Cobb's Hole; these were the Yollands, respectable and worthy. Her acquaintance began with the daughter, Limping Lucy, who suffered from a deformed foot. The two deformed girls shared a bond of sympathy. The fact that Cuff had traced Rosanna to *their* cottage changed the nature of my involvement. She was merely visiting familiar ground; proving her association with the Yollands would demonstrate her innocence. Convinced by Cuff's logic, I agreed to assist.

We proceeded to Cobb's Hole, following the tracks on the sand as long as the light permitted.

Upon reaching the cottage, the fisherman and his son were out at sea. Limping Lucy, weak and weary, rested upstairs. Good Mrs. Yolland received us alone in her kitchen. Hearing that Cuff was a celebrated figure in London, she produced a bottle of Dutch gin and two pipes, staring at him with wide-eyed admiration.

I sat quietly in the corner, waiting for Cuff to steer the conversation toward Rosanna. His usual roundabout method proved even more circuitous this time. He began with the Royal Family, the Primitive Methodists, and the price of fish, eventually winding his way, in his dismal, subterranean style, to the loss of the Moonstone, the malice of the first housemaid, and the general harsh treatment of Rosanna by the other servants. Having reached his topic, he claimed his inquiries aimed both to recover the Diamond and clear Rosanna from unjust suspicion. Within twenty minutes, Mrs. Yolland was convinced she was speaking to Rosanna's best friend, pressing Cuff to drink from the Dutch bottle to revive his spirits.

Persuaded that Cuff was wasting his efforts on Mrs. Yolland, I sat observing the interaction like an audience member at a play. The great Cuff displayed wonderful patience, firing shot after shot at random, aiming only at Rosanna's credit and avoiding any hint of prejudice. Despite his efforts, Mrs. Yolland talked incessantly, placing absolute trust in him. His final attempt came as we checked our watches and prepared to depart.

"I shall now wish you good-night, ma'am," Cuff said. "Only at parting, I must say that Rosanna Spearman has a sincere well-wisher in myself. However, she will never succeed in her present position; my advice is to leave it."

"Bless your heart! She is *going* to leave it!" cried Mrs. Yolland.

Rosanna intending to leave! I pricked up my ears. It seemed strange that she had given no prior warning to my lady or me. A doubt crept into my mind: had Cuff's final random shot hit the mark? Was I complicit in mystifying an honest woman with a web of lies? Remembering that the father of lies is the Devil, and that mischief often follows close behind, I sensed trouble in the air. I attempted to extract Cuff, but he sat down immediately, requesting another sip from the Dutch bottle. Mrs. Yolland obliged, sitting opposite him. I moved to the door, feeling excessively uncomfortable, intending to bid them good-night yet unable to leave.

"So she means to leave?" Cuff asked. "What will she do when she does? Sad, sad! The poor creature has no friends in the world except you and me."

"Ah, but she has!" Mrs. Yolland interjected. "She came in this evening, spoke with my girl Lucy and me, then asked to go upstairs to Lucy's room—the only room with pen and ink. 'I want to write a letter to a friend,' she said, 'but I cannot do it with the servants prying.' I cannot tell you who the letter was for, but it must have been very long, judging by the time she spent upstairs. I offered her a postage stamp when she returned; she did not have the letter in her hand and declined the stamp. She is a little close about her affairs, poor soul, but she has a friend somewhere, and she will go to that friend."

"Soon?" Cuff asked.

"As soon as she can," Mrs. Yolland replied.

Stepping back from the door, I could not allow such loose talk about a servant's departure to continue unchecked. "You must be mistaken about Rosanna Spearman," I declared. "If she intended to leave, she would have informed me first."

"Mistaken?" Mrs. Yolland exclaimed. "Only an hour ago she purchased items for traveling from me, right here in this room. And that reminds me," she added, suddenly feeling in her pocket, "of something concerning Rosanna and her money. Are you likely to see her when you return?"

"I would be delighted to deliver a message," Cuff offered before I could speak.

Mrs. Yolland produced several shillings and sixpences, counting them out with meticulous, exasperating care. She offered the money to Cuff, looking reluctant to part with it.

"Might I ask you to return this to Rosanna with my love?" she said. "She insisted on paying for the few things she bought, and money is welcome in our house. Still, I am uneasy taking a poor girl's savings. To tell the truth, I do not think my husband would like to hear I took Rosanna's money when he returns tomorrow. Please tell her she is welcome to the items as a gift. And do not leave the money on the table," she added, placing it down abruptly as if it burned her fingers—"times are hard, and flesh is weak; I might be tempted to put it back in my pocket."

"Come along!" I urged. "I cannot wait; I must return to the house."

"I will follow you directly," Cuff said.

For the second time, I moved to the door, yet for the second time, I could not cross the threshold.

"It is a delicate matter, ma'am," Cuff said, "returning money. You

The room was dim, illuminated only by the harsh, focused glow of a reading lamp whose shade was screwed down tight, casting a shadow over my lady's face. She sat hunched at the table, refusing to lift her gaze. Instead of meeting us with her customary directness, she stared obstinately at an open book, her posture rigid.

"Officer," she said, her voice steady despite the tension in the air, "does the inquiry you are conducting require to know beforehand if anyone currently residing in this house intends to depart?"

"It is of paramount importance, my lady," Sergeant Cuff replied.

"I must inform you," she continued, "that Miss Verinder has decided to travel to stay with her aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite, of Frizinghall. She has arranged to leave us at first light tomorrow morning."

Sergeant Cuff glanced at me. I stepped forward instinctively to speak to my mistress, but a sudden failure of courage seized me. My heart hammered against my ribs with a violence that would have made a man of twenty-five blush. I took a step back, swallowed my words, and remained silent.

"May I inquire," the Sergeant asked, turning his gaze back to my lady, "at what precise moment Miss Verinder informed you of her intention to visit her aunt?"

"Approximately an hour ago," my mistress answered.

Sergeant Cuff looked at me again. They say the hearts of the elderly are calloused, immune to sudden shocks. My own heart, however, was far from hardened; it thumped with a ferocity that would have belied my age if I had been five-and-twenty once more!

"I have no authority," the Sergeant said, "to command Miss Verinder's movements. What I can request is that you delay her departure until later in the day. I must proceed to Frizinghall myself tomorrow morning, and I shall return by two o'clock, or sooner. If Miss Verinder can be kept here until that hour, I wish to speak to her unexpectedly before she leaves."

My lady nodded and immediately turned to me. "Give the coachman my orders," she instructed. "The carriage is not to collect Miss Rachel until two o'clock."

"Have you further instructions?" she asked the Sergeant once the order was given.

"Only one thing, your ladyship," Cuff replied. "Should Miss Verinder be surprised by this change of plans, please do not attribute the delay to me."

My mistress lifted her head abruptly from her book, as if to protest, but checked herself with a visible effort. She cast a final glance at the open page and dismissed us with a sharp wave of her hand.

"That is a remarkable woman," Sergeant Cuff observed as we stepped out into the hall. "Without her self-command, the mystery that baffles you, Mr. Betteredge, would have been solved tonight."

Those words struck a chord deep within my stupid, aging brain. For a fleeting moment, I believe I lost my senses entirely. I grabbed the Sergeant by the collar of his coat and pinned him against the wall.

"Damn you!" I shouted. "Something is terribly wrong with Miss Rachel—and you have been concealing it from me this entire time!"

Sergeant Cuff looked up at me, pressed flat against the plaster, without shifting a muscle of his somber face.

"Ah," he said calmly, "you have finally guessed it."

My hand dropped from his collar, and my head sank onto my chest. Please remember, as an excuse for my outburst, that I had served this family for fifty years. Miss Rachel had climbed onto my knees and tugged at my whiskers countless times when she was a child. To me, despite her faults, she had always been the dearest, prettiest, and most virtuous young mistress an old servant could love. I begged Sergeant Cuff's pardon, though I fear I did so with watery eyes and in a manner far from dignified.

"Do not distress yourself, Mr. Betteredge," the Sergeant said, with a kindness I did not expect from him. "In my line of work, if we were quick to take offense, we would be worthless. If it brings you any comfort, grab me again. You lack the skill to do it properly, but I will overlook your clumsiness out of consideration for your feelings."

He curled the corners of his lips upward, and in his own bleak fashion, seemed to think he had delivered a fine joke.

I led him into my small sitting-room and closed the door.

"Tell me the truth, Sergeant," I demanded. "What do you suspect? It is no kindness to hide it from me now."

"I do not suspect," said Sergeant Cuff. "I know."

My bad temper began to rise again.

"Do you mean to tell me, in plain English," I pressed, "that Miss Rachel has stolen her own diamond?"

"Yes," the Sergeant confirmed. "That is precisely what I mean. Miss Verinder has been in secret possession of the Moonstone from the beginning to the end; and she has drawn Rosanna Spearman into her confidence because she calculated that we would suspect Rosanna of the theft. There is the entire case in a nutshell. Grab me again, Mr. Betteredge. If it vents your feelings, do so."

God help me! My feelings were not to be so easily relieved. "Give me your reasons!" was all I could manage to say.

"You shall hear my reasons tomorrow," the Sergeant replied. "If Miss Verinder refuses to postpone her visit to her aunt—which you will find she does—I shall be obliged to lay the whole case before your mistress tomorrow. Since I do not know the outcome, I request your presence to hear what passes on both sides. Let the matter rest for tonight. No, Mr. Betteredge, you shall not get another word on the subject of the Moonstone from me. Your table is set for supper. That is one of the many human frailties I always treat with tenderness. If you will ring the bell, I shall say grace: 'For what we are about to receive...'"

"I wish you a good appetite, Sergeant," I said. "My own appetite has vanished. I shall wait to see you served, and then I shall ask to be excused if I retire to try to regain my composure alone."

I watched him eat the finest provisions available, and I would not have been sorry if the food had choked him. At that moment, the head gardener, Mr. Begbie, entered with his weekly account. The Sergeant immediately turned his attention to roses and the comparative merits of grass versus gravel paths. I left them to their debate and exited with a heavy heart. This was the first trouble I could recall in many years that tobacco could not blow away, nor could *Robinson Crusoe* solve.

Restless and miserable, with nowhere specific to go, I paced the terrace, thinking in solitude. It mattered little what my thoughts were; I felt wretchedly old, worn out, and unfit for my station. For the first time in my life, I began to wonder when God would see fit to take me. Yet, despite this despair, I held fast to my belief in Miss Rachel. Even if Sergeant Cuff had been Solomon in all his glory and declared my young lady involved in a mean and guilty plot, my answer would have been simple: "You do not know her; I do."

My reverie was broken by Samuel, who brought me a written message from my mistress. Entering the house to fetch a light, Samuel remarked that the weather seemed to be changing. My troubled mind had prevented me from noticing it before, but now, with my attention roused, I heard the dogs pacing uneasily and the wind moaning low. Looking up, I saw the cloud racks darkening and rushing faster over a watery moon. Wild weather was coming—Samuel was right.

The message informed me that the magistrate at Frizinghall had written to remind my lady about the three Indians. Early in the coming week, the rogues were to be released to follow their own devices. If we had further questions for them, there was no time to lose. Having forgotten to mention this during her last conversation with Sergeant Cuff, my mistress now ordered me to supply the omission. The Indians had completely faded from my mind (and likely from yours as well). I saw little use in reopening that subject, yet I obeyed my orders immediately.

I found Sergeant Cuff and the gardener engaged in a heated argument over a bottle of Scotch whisky regarding the cultivation of roses. The Sergeant was so absorbed that he held up his hand to signal me not to interrupt when I entered. As far as I could discern, the debate centered on whether the white moss rose required budding onto the dog-rose to thrive. Mr. Begbie argued "Yes"; Sergeant Cuff argued "No." They appealed to me with the fervor of schoolboys. Knowing nothing of horticulture, I steered a middle course, much like Her Majesty's judges when the scales of justice hang even to a hair. "Gentlemen," I remarked, "there is much to be said on both sides." In the temporary lull created by this impartial sentence, I placed my lady's written message on the table before Sergeant Cuff.

By this time, I had nearly reached the point of hating the Sergeant. Yet, truth compels me to acknowledge that, regarding mental agility, he was a wonderful man.

Within half a minute of reading the message, he had recalled Superintendent Seegrave's report, isolated the section concerning the Indians, and was ready with his response. A certain great traveler, expert in Indian languages, had been mentioned in Mr. Seegrave's report, had he not? Very well. Did I know the gentleman's name and address? Very well again. Would I write them on the back of my lady's note? Much obliged. Sergeant Cuff would look up this gentleman upon his arrival in Frizinghall the following morning.

"Do you expect anything to come of it?" I asked. "Superintendent Seegrave found the Indians as innocent as a newborn babe."

"Superintendent Seegrave has been proven wrong in all his conclusions thus far," the Sergeant answered. "It may be worthwhile to discover tomorrow whether he was also wrong about the Indians." With that, he turned back to Mr. Begbie and resumed the argument exactly where it had left off. "This question between us concerns soils and seasons, patience and diligence, Mr. Gardener. Now, let me put it to you from another angle. You take your white moss rose..."

By then, I had closed the door behind me, shutting out the rest of the dispute.

In the passage, I encountered Penelope hovering near the stairs. I asked what she was waiting for.

She was awaiting her young lady's bell, summoned to assist with packing for the journey the next day. Further inquiry revealed that Miss Rachel had cited the house as unbearable and the presence of a policeman under the same roof as intolerable as her reason for wishing to leave. Upon being informed half an hour prior that her departure was delayed until two in the afternoon, she had erupted into a violent rage. My lady, present at the time, had severely rebuked her and then, appearing to have something reserved for her daughter's private ear, had sent Penelope out of the room. The girl was in wretched spirits about the altered atmosphere. "Nothing goes right, father; nothing is like what it used to be," she lamented. "I feel as if some dreadful misfortune is hanging over us all."

That was my feeling too. But I put on a brave face for my daughter. Just then, Miss Rachel's bell rang. Penelope ran up the back stairs to resume packing. I took the other route to the hall to check the barometer regarding the weather change.

As I approached the swing-door leading from the servants' offices into the hall, it was violently thrown open from the inside. Rosanna Spearman ran past me, her face twisted in a look of misery, one hand pressed hard over her heart as if clutching a sharp pain. "What is the matter, my girl?" I asked, stopping her. "Are you ill?"

"For God's sake, don't speak to me," she answered, twisting herself out of my grasp and running toward the servants' staircase. I called to the cook, who was within hearing, to look after the poor girl. Two others proved to be listening as well. Sergeant Cuff darted softly out of my room and asked what was wrong. I answered, "Nothing." Mr. Franklin, on the other side, pulled open the swing-door, beckoned me into the hall, and inquired if I had seen Rosanna Spearman.

"She has just passed me, sir," I replied, "with a very disturbed face and in a very odd manner."

"I am afraid I am innocently the cause of that disturbance, Betteredge," Mr. Franklin said.

"You, sir!"

"I cannot explain it," Mr. Franklin admitted. "But if the girl is indeed concerned in the loss of the Diamond, I truly believe she was on the point of confessing everything—to me, of all people in the world—not two minutes ago."

Looking toward the swing-door as he spoke, I fancied I saw it open slightly from the inner side. Was someone listening? The door fell shut before I could reach it. Peering through the crack immediately afterward, I thought I saw the tails of Sergeant Cuff's respectable black coat disappearing around the corner of the passage. He knew, as well as I did, that he could expect no further help from me now that I had discerned the true direction of his investigation. Under those circumstances, it was quite in his character to help himself, utilizing the underground routes.

Not entirely sure I had actually seen the Sergeant—and unwilling to stir up needless mischief where there was already plenty—I told Mr. Franklin that I believed one of the dogs had entered the house. Then I begged him to describe what had occurred between Rosanna and himself.

"Were you passing through the hall, sir?" I asked. "Did you meet her accidentally when she spoke to you?"

Mr. Franklin pointed to the billiard table.

"I was knocking the balls about," he said, "trying to distract myself from this miserable business of the Diamond. I happened to look up—and there stood Rosanna Spearman beside me, like a ghost! Her stealthy approach was so strange that I hardly knew what to do at first. Seeing a very anxious expression on her face, I asked if

The night passed without incident, and despite the watchful eyes of Sergeant Cuff, neither Miss Rachel nor Rosanna made any attempt to communicate. I had anticipated that the Sergeant would depart for Frizinghall at first light, yet he lingered, seemingly occupied with some unseen task. Leaving him to his own devices, I ventured into the grounds shortly thereafter and encountered Mr. Franklin on his customary stroll along the shrubbery path.

We had scarcely exchanged greetings when the Sergeant appeared unexpectedly. He approached Mr. Franklin with a practiced ease, though the gentleman received him with palpable hauteur. "Have you anything to say to me?" Mr. Franklin asked sharply, offering no warmth to the Sergeant's polite morning wish.

"I have something to say to you, sir," the Sergeant replied, his tone shifting from deference to authority. "Regarding the inquiry I am conducting here. You detected the true direction of this investigation yesterday. Naturally, in your position, you are shocked and distressed. Naturally, too, you project your own anger regarding your family's scandal onto me."

"What do you want?" Mr. Franklin interrupted, his voice cutting through the air.

"I wish to remind you, sir, that I have not yet been *proved* wrong. Keeping that in mind, please recall that I am an officer of the law acting under the sanction of the mistress of this house. Under these circumstances, is it, or is it not, your duty as a good citizen to assist me with any special information you may possess?"

"I possess no special information," Mr. Franklin stated flatly.

The Sergeant dismissed this answer as if it had never been spoken. "You may save my time from being wasted on inquiries at a distance," he continued, pressing forward, "if you choose to understand me and speak out."

"I don't understand you," Mr. Franklin retorted, "and I have nothing to say."

"One of the female servants—I won't mention names—spoke to you privately last night."

Again, Mr. Franklin cut him short. Again, he answered, "I have nothing to say."

Standing silently nearby, I recalled the movement in the swing-door from the previous evening and the coat-tails disappearing down the passage. The Sergeant had undoubtedly gathered enough to suspect that Rosanna had relieved her conscience by confessing something to Mr. Franklin Blake. This thought had barely formed in my mind when Rosanna Spearman herself emerged from the end of the shrubbery walk. She was followed closely by Penelope, who was clearly attempting to guide her back toward the house. Seeing that Mr. Franklin was not alone, Rosanna halted, her face a mask of perplexity. Penelope waited behind her. Mr. Franklin spotted the girls as quickly as I did. The Sergeant, displaying his characteristic cunning, feigned total ignorance of their presence. All of this unfolded in a heartbeat. Before either Mr. Franklin or I could utter a word, the Sergeant stepped in smoothly, maintaining the appearance of continuing our previous conversation.

"You needn't be afraid of harming the girl, sir," he addressed Mr. Franklin, speaking loudly enough for Rosanna to hear every syllable. "On the contrary, I recommend you to honor me with your confidence if you feel any interest in Rosanna Spearman."

Mr. Franklin instantly adopted an air of obliviousness regarding the girls. He responded, his voice projecting across the garden:

"I take no interest whatever in Rosanna Spearman."

I glanced toward the end of the walk. From that distance, I saw only that the moment Mr. Franklin spoke, Rosanna turned abruptly. Instead of resisting Penelope as she had moments before, she allowed my daughter to take her arm and lead her back inside.

The breakfast bell rang just as the two girls vanished, and even the astute Sergeant Cuff was forced to concede defeat. He turned to me quietly. "I shall go to Frizinghall, Mr. Betteredge, and I shall be back before two." With that, he departed without further comment, leaving us free for several hours.

"You must make it right with Rosanna," Mr. Franklin said to me once we were alone. "I seem fated to say or do something awkward whenever that unfortunate girl is near. You saw it yourself; the Sergeant laid a trap for both of us. If he could confuse me or irritate her into breaking down, either she or I might have revealed something serving his purpose. On the spur of the moment, I saw no better escape than the path I took. It stopped the girl from speaking and demonstrated to the Sergeant that I saw through him. He was evidently listening, Betteredge, when I was speaking to you last night."

He had done more than merely listen, as I privately surmised. He had remembered my confession that the girl loved Mr. Franklin, and he had calculated precisely on that emotion when he appealed to Mr. Franklin's supposed interest in Rosanna, speaking within her hearing range.

"As to listening, sir," I remarked, holding back the other point, "we shall all be rowing in the same boat if this continues much longer. Prying, peeping, and listening are the natural occupations of those in our situation. Within a day or two, Mr. Franklin, we shall all be struck dumb together—for this reason: we shall all be listening to surprise each other's secrets, fully aware of what we are doing. Excuse my outburst, sir. The horrid mystery hanging over this house gets into my head like liquor and makes me wild. I will not forget what you have told me. I shall take the first opportunity to make it right with Rosanna Spearman."

"You haven't said anything to her yet about last night, have you?" Mr. Franklin asked.

"No, sir."

"Then say nothing now. I had better not invite the girl's confidence with the Sergeant watching to surprise us together. My conduct is not very consistent, Betteredge—is it? I see no way out of this business, which is dreadful to contemplate, unless the Diamond is traced to Rosanna. And yet I cannot, and will not, help Sergeant Cuff to find the girl out."

Unreasonable, no doubt. Yet it mirrored my own state of mind perfectly. I understood him entirely. If you will, for once in your life, remember that you are mortal, perhaps you will understand him too.

While the Sergeant was en route to Frizinghall, the domestic situation unfolded as follows: Miss Rachel remained obstinately confined to her room, awaiting the carriage for her journey to her aunt's. My lady and Mr. Franklin breakfasted together. Afterward, Mr. Franklin made one of his sudden resolutions and departed precipitately for a long walk to quiet his mind. I was the sole witness to his exit; he assured me he would return before the Sergeant's arrival. The weather, foreshadowed the night before, had shifted dramatically. Heavy rain followed by high winds had swept through at dawn. By mid-morning, the air was fresh and biting, though the clouds, threatening intermittently, failed to release another drop of rain. It was a challenging day for a walk, suitable only for the young and strong capable of braving the great gusts sweeping in from the sea.

I attended to my mistress after breakfast, assisting her in settling our household accounts. She mentioned the Moonstone only once, strictly forbidding any present discussion of it between us. "Wait till that man comes back," she said, referring to the Sergeant. "We *must* speak of it then; we are not obliged to speak of it now."

Leaving my mistress, I found Penelope waiting in my room.

"I wish, father, you would come and speak to Rosanna," she said. "I am very uneasy about her."

I suspected the cause readily enough. However, it is a maxim of mine that men, being superior creatures, are bound to improve women if they can. When a woman requests my assistance—whether my daughter or anyone else—it is essential that I insist on knowing the reason. The more often you compel them to rummage their own minds for a justification, the more manageable you will find them in all relations of life. It is not their fault (poor wretches!) that they act first and think afterwards; it is the fault of the fools who indulge them.

Penelope's reason, on this occasion, was delivered in her own words. "I am afraid, father," she said, "Mr. Franklin has hurt Rosanna cruelly, without intending it."

"What took Rosanna into the shrubbery walk?" I asked.

"Her own madness," Penelope replied. "I can call it nothing else. She was bent on speaking to Mr. Franklin this morning, come what might. I did my best to stop her; you saw that. If I could only have got her away before she heard those dreadful words..."

"There! There!" I said, soothing her. "Don't lose your head. I cannot recall anything happening to alarm Rosanna."

"Nothing to alarm her, father. But Mr. Franklin said he took no interest whatever in her—and, oh, he said it in such a cruel voice!"

"He said it to stop the Sergeant's mouth," I answered.

"I told her that," Penelope said. "But you see, father (though Mr. Franklin isn't to blame), he has been mortifying and disappointing her for weeks and weeks past; and now this comes on top of everything! She has no right, of course, to expect him to take any interest in her. It is quite monstrous that she should forget herself and her station in that way. But she seems to have lost pride, and proper feeling, and everything. She frightened me, father, when Mr. Franklin said those words. They seemed to turn her into stone. A sudden quiet came over her, and she has gone about her work ever since, like a woman in a dream."

I began to feel a distinct unease. There was something in the way Penelope phrased it that silenced my superior judgment. Recalling the events between Mr. Franklin and Rosanna the night before, I saw how she looked cut to the heart then; and now, as ill luck would have it, she had been stung again on a tender spot. Sad! Sad!—all the more tragic because the girl had no reason to justify her feelings and no right to feel them.

I had promised Mr. Franklin to speak to Rosanna, and this seemed the fittest time to keep my word.

We found the girl sweeping the corridor outside the bedrooms. She was pale and composed, neat as ever in her modest print dress. I noticed a curious dimness and dullness in her eyes—not as if she had been crying, but as if she had been staring at something for too long. Perhaps it was a misty haze raised by her own thoughts. Certainly, there was no object about her she had not seen hundreds of times before.

"Cheer up, Rosanna!" I said. "You mustn't fret over your own fancies. I have something to say to you from Mr. Franklin."

I then presented the matter to her in the friendliest and most comforting terms I could devise. My principles regarding the other sex are, as you may have noticed, very severe. Yet somehow, when I face women directly, my practice (I admit) does not conform to those strict rules.

"Mr. Franklin is very kind and considerate. Please to thank him." That was all the answer she gave me.

My daughter had already observed that Rosanna moved about her work like a woman in a dream. I now added to this observation that she also listened and spoke like one. I doubted if her mind was in a fit condition to comprehend what I had said.

"Are you quite sure, Rosanna, that you understand me?" I asked.

"Quite sure."

She echoed me, not like a living woman, but like a creature moved by machinery. She continued sweeping all the while. I gently took the broom from her hands.

"Come, come, my girl!" I said. "This is not like yourself. You have something on your mind. I'm your friend—and I'll stand by you, even if you have done wrong. Make a clean breast of it, Rosanna—make a clean breast of it!"

In days past, speaking to her in such a manner would have brought tears to her eyes. Now, I saw no change in them.

"Yes," she said. "I'll make a clean breast of it."

"To my lady?" I asked.

"No."

"To Mr. Franklin?"

"Yes; to Mr. Franklin."

I hardly knew what to reply. She was in no condition to understand the caution against speaking to him in private, which Mr. Franklin had directed me to convey. Feeling my way cautiously, I merely told her that Mr. Franklin had gone out for a walk.

"It doesn't matter," she answered. "I shan't trouble Mr. Franklin today."

"Why not speak to my lady?" I said. "The way to relieve your mind is to speak to the merciful and Christian mistress who has always been kind to you."

She looked at me for a moment with grave and steady attention, as if fixing my words in her mind. Then she took the broom from my hands and moved off slowly, a little way down the corridor.

"No," she said, continuing her sweeping and speaking to herself; "I know a better way of relieving my mind than that."

"What is it?"

"Please to let me go on with my work."

Penelope followed her, offering assistance.

She answered, "No. I want to do my work. Thank you, Penelope." She looked around at me. "Thank you, Mr. Betteredge."

There was no moving her; there was nothing more to be said. I signaled to Penelope to come away with me. We left her as we had found her, sweeping the corridor, like a woman in a dream.

"This is a matter for the doctor to look into," I declared. "It is beyond me."

My daughter reminded me of Mr. Candy's illness, caused by the chill he caught during the dinner party. His assistant, a certain Mr. Ezra Jennings, was at our disposal, to be sure. Yet, nobody knew much about him in our parts. He had been engaged by Mr. Candy under peculiar circumstances, and right or wrong, none of us liked or trusted him. There were other doctors at Frizinghall, but they were strangers to our house, and Penelope doubted whether strangers might not do Rosanna

Descending the steps to the front door, I encountered Sergeant Cuff. It chafed against my nature, following the friction we had recently shared, to display any semblance of interest in his inquiries. Yet, despite my resistance, a compelling curiosity took hold, dragging my sense of dignity down until I could no longer hold it up. The words escaped me involuntarily: "What news from Frizinghall?"

"I have seen the Indians," Cuff replied, his tone matter-of-fact. "I have also identified what Rosanna purchased privately in the town on Thursday. The Indians will be released on Wednesday next week. There is no doubt in my mind, nor in Mr. Murthwaite's, that they arrived here specifically to steal the Moonstone. Their calculations were naturally disrupted by the events of Wednesday night; consequently, they bear no responsibility for the actual loss of the jewel any more than you do. However, let me warn you, Mr. Betteredge: if we do not locate the Moonstone, they certainly will. You have not heard the final word from the three jugglers."

Mr. Franklin emerged from his walk just as these startling words left Cuff's lips. Governing his curiosity with a steadier hand than I had managed my own, he passed us silently and retreated into the house.

Having already surrendered my dignity, I resolved to reap the full benefit of this revelation. "So much for the Indians," I pressed. "What of Rosanna?"

Cuff shook his head.

"The mystery surrounding her has only deepened," he said. "I have traced her to a shop in Frizinghall, operated by a linen draper named Maltby. She purchased nothing at any other draper's, milliner's, or tailor's establishment; at Maltby's, she bought only a length of cloth. She was fastidious in her selection of quality. As for quantity, she acquired enough to construct a nightgown."

"Whose nightgown?" I asked.

"Her own, undoubtedly," Cuff answered. "Between twelve and three on Thursday morning, she must have slipped into your young lady's chamber to finalize the concealment of the Moonstone while the rest of the household slept. On her return to her own quarters, her nightgown must have brushed against the wet paint on the door. Unable to wash out the stain, and unable to safely destroy the garment without first procuring a replacement to maintain the completeness of her linen inventory..."

"Which proves it was Rosanna's nightgown?" I interjected.

"The material she purchased for the substitute dress," the Sergeant countered. "Had it been Miss Verinder's nightgown, she would have required lace, frills, and countless other embellishments; she would not have possessed the time to construct it in a single night. Plain long cloth signifies a plain servant's nightgown. No, Mr. Betteredge, the details are clear. The crux of the matter remains: why, after securing the replacement dress, did she hide the stained nightgown rather than destroy it? If the girl refuses to speak, there is only one resolution. The hiding-place at the Shivering Sand must be searched; the truth lies there."

"How shall you locate the place?" I inquired.

"I regret to disappoint you," Cuff said, "but that is a secret I intend to retain."

(To spare your curiosity, as he spared mine, I may inform you that he had returned from Frizinghall armed with a search warrant. His experience dictated that Rosanna likely carried a memorandum of the hiding-place, intended to guide her should she return under altered circumstances and after a lapse of time. Possessing this document, the Sergeant would be furnished with all he required.)

"Now, Mr. Betteredge," he continued, "let us abandon speculation and engage in business. I instructed Joyce to keep watch on Rosanna. Where is Joyce?"

Joyce was the Frizinghall constable, assigned to Cuff's command by Superintendent Seegrave. The clock struck two; punctual to the minute, the carriage arrived to escort Miss Rachel to her aunt's.

"One thing at a time," Cuff said, intercepting me as I moved to summon Joyce. "I must attend to Miss Verinder first."

Since the rain threatened, the enclosed carriage had been designated for Miss Rachel's journey to Frizinghall. Cuff beckoned Samuel, the footman, to descend from the rumble seat behind.

"You will see a friend of mine waiting among the trees on this side of the lodge gate," he instructed. "My friend, without halting the carriage, will climb into the rumble with you. Your sole duty is to remain silent and keep your eyes shut. Otherwise, you will find yourself in trouble."

With this directive, he dismissed the footman. I could not fathom Samuel's thoughts, but it was plain to me that Miss Rachel was to be secretly monitored from the moment she departed our house—or if she indeed departed. A guard on my young lady! A spy concealed within the rumble of her mother's carriage! I could have severed my own tongue for the foolishness of addressing Sergeant Cuff.

The first to emerge from the house was my lady. She positioned herself on the top step, observing silently. She spoke not a word to the Sergeant or to me. With lips sealed and arms folded within the light garden cloak she had wrapped around herself against the chill air, she stood as motionless as a statue, awaiting her daughter's appearance.

Moments later, Miss Rachel descended the stairs. She was dressed in soft yellow fabric that highlighted her dark complexion, the material tailored tightly around her waist in a jacket style. She wore a smart little straw hat with a white veil twisted beneath it, primrose-colored gloves that fit her hands like a second skin, and her black hair lay smooth as satin beneath the hat. Her ears, like rosy shells, bore dangling pearls. She moved swiftly and straight as a lily on its stem, lithe and supple in every motion, resembling a young cat. Nothing in her pretty face had altered, save for her eyes and lips. Her eyes burned with a brightness and fierceness that displeased me; her lips had lost all color and smile, rendering them almost unrecognizable. She kissed her mother in a hurried, abrupt manner on the cheek, murmuring, "Try to forgive me, mamma," before violently pulling down her veil, tearing it in the process. In an instant, she had rushed down the steps and bolted into the carriage as if seeking refuge.

Sergeant Cuff was equally swift. He guided Samuel back and stood before Miss Rachel, holding the open carriage door just as she settled inside.

"What do you want?" Miss Rachel demanded from behind her veil.

"I wish to say one word to you, miss," Cuff replied, "before you depart. I cannot presume to prevent your visit to your aunt. I can only venture to state that your departure, given the current circumstances, places an obstacle in my path to recovering your Diamond. Please comprehend this, and then decide for yourself whether to go or stay."

Miss Rachel offered no reply. "Drive on, James!" she commanded the coachman.

Without further hesitation, Cuff shut the carriage door. Just as it closed, Mr. Franklin ran down the steps. "Good-bye, Rachel," he said, extending his hand.

"Drive on!" Miss Rachel cried, louder than before, ignoring Mr. Franklin just as she had ignored the Sergeant.

Mr. Franklin stepped back, thunderstruck. The coachman, unsure of his orders, glanced toward my lady, who remained immovable on the top step. My lady, her face a turmoil of anger, sorrow, and shame, signaled the horses to start, then turned hastily back into the house. Mr. Franklin, regaining his composure, called after her as the carriage drove away, "Aunt! You were quite right. Accept my thanks for all your kindness—and let me go."

My lady turned as if to speak, then, seemingly distrusting her own resolve, waved her hand kindly. "Let me see you before you leave us, Franklin," she said in a broken voice, and retreated to her own room.

"Do me a last favor, Betteredge," Mr. Franklin said, turning to me with tears in his eyes. "Get me away to the train as soon as you can!"

He too withdrew into the house. For the moment, Miss Rachel had completely unmanned him. One could judge the depth of his affection for her by this reaction.

Sergeant Cuff and I were left face to face at the bottom of the steps. The Sergeant stood with his gaze fixed on a gap in the trees, commanding a view of one of the windings of the drive leading from the house. His hands were in his pockets, and he was softly whistling "The Last Rose of Summer."

"There is a time for everything," I said with savage intensity. "This is not a time for whistling."

At that precise moment, the carriage appeared in the distance through the gap, heading toward the lodge gate. Another man, besides Samuel, was plainly visible in the rumble seat behind.

"All right!" Cuff muttered to himself. He turned to me. "It is no time for whistling, Mr. Betteredge, as you say. It is time to take this business in hand, now, without sparing anyone. We shall begin with Rosanna Spearman. Where is Joyce?"

We both called for Joyce, receiving no answer. I dispatched a stable-boy to search for him.

"You heard what I said to Miss Verinder?" Cuff remarked while we waited. "And you witnessed her reception of it? I told her plainly that her departure would hinder my recovery of her Diamond—and yet she leaves, in defiance of that statement! Your young lady has a traveling companion in her mother's carriage, Mr. Betteredge—and the name of that companion is the Moonstone."

I said nothing, clinging stubbornly to my belief in Miss Rachel's innocence.

The stable-boy returned, followed very reluctantly, as it seemed to me, by Joyce.

"Where is Rosanna Spearman?" Cuff asked.

"I cannot account for it, sir," Joyce began, his voice trembling. "And I am very sorry. But somehow or other—"

"Before I went to Frizinghall," Cuff interrupted sharply, "I instructed you to keep your eyes on Rosanna Spearman without allowing her to discover she was being watched. Do you mean to tell me you have allowed her to elude you?"

"I am afraid, sir," Joyce stammered, "that I was perhaps a little *too* careful not to let her discover me. There are so many passages in the lower parts of this house—"

"How long has it been since you lost sight of her?"

"Nigh on an hour, sir."

"You may return to your regular duties at Frizinghall," Cuff said, speaking with his usual quiet, dreary composure. "I do not believe your talents lie in our line, Mr. Joyce. Your current employment is a trifle beyond you. Good morning."

The man slunk away. I found it difficult to articulate the effect of discovering Rosanna Spearman was missing. I seemed to harbor fifty conflicting thoughts simultaneously. In that state, I stared at Sergeant Cuff, my powers of speech failing me entirely.

"No, Mr. Betteredge," Cuff said, as if he had anticipated my uppermost thought and was addressing it before the others. "Your young friend, Rosanna, will not slip through my fingers so easily as you suppose. As long as I know where Miss Verinder is, I possess the means to trace Miss Verinder's accomplice. I prevented them from communicating last night. Very well. They will meet at Frizinghall instead of here. The present inquiry must simply be shifted (rather sooner than I anticipated) from this house to the house where Miss Verinder is visiting. In the meantime, I fear I must trouble you to call the servants together again."

I accompanied him to the servants' hall. It is disgraceful, yet undeniably true, that I suffered another attack of detective fever upon hearing those words. I momentarily forgot my hatred for Sergeant Cuff. I seized him confidentially by the arm. "For goodness' sake, tell us what you intend to do with the servants now?"

The great Cuff stood stock-still, addressing himself in a kind of melancholy rapture to the empty air.

"If this man," the Sergeant said, apparently referring to me, "only understood the growing of roses, he would be the most perfectly formed character on the face of creation!" Following this strong expression of feeling, he sighed and put his arm through mine. "This is the situation," he said, dropping back to business. "Rosanna has done one of two things. She has either gone directly to Frizinghall (before I could arrive), or she has gone first to visit her hiding-place at the Shivering Sand. The first priority is to determine which of the servants saw her last before she left the house."

Upon instituting this inquiry, it became clear that the last person to see Rosanna was Nancy, the kitchenmaid.

Nancy had observed her slipping out with a letter in her hand and stopping the butcher's man who had just delivered meat at the back door. Nancy had heard her instruct the man to post the letter when he returned to Frizinghall. The man had examined the address and noted it was a roundabout method for delivering a letter directed to Cobb's Hole, to be posted at Frizinghall—and that, moreover, it was a Saturday, which would delay delivery until Monday morning. Rosanna had replied that the delay until Monday was of no consequence; the only thing she required was assurance that the man would comply. He had promised and driven away. Nancy had been recalled to the kitchen, and no one else had seen anything of Rosanna Spearman thereafter.

"Well?" I asked when we were alone again.

"Well," Cuff said. "I must proceed to Frizinghall."

"Regarding the letter, sir?"

"Yes. The memorandum of the hiding-place is contained within that letter. I must inspect the address at the post-office. If it is the address I suspect, I shall pay our friend, Mrs. Yolland, another visit on Monday next."

I accompanied the Sergeant to order the pony-chaise. In the stable-yard, a new light was cast upon the missing girl.

The rumor of Rosanna's vanishing had rippled quickly through the ranks of the outdoor staff. They, too, had scoured the grounds, and their search led them to a small, energetic boy known as "Duffy," who spent his days weeding the gardens. It was Duffy who claimed to have spotted Rosanna Spearman only thirty minutes prior. He insisted she had not merely walked past him in the fir plantation but had sprinted toward the sea-shore.

"Does this boy know the coastline?" Sergeant Cuff asked.

"He was born and raised right here on the coast," I replied.

"Duffy!" the Sergeant barked, his voice cutting through the damp air. "Do you want a shilling? If you do, come with me. Mr. Betteredge, keep the pony-chaise ready until I return."

Without waiting for a second response, the Sergeant launched himself toward the Shivering Sand, moving with a speed that defied my own aging legs. Behind him, the boy let out a wild cry of excitement and scrambled to keep pace.

Once the Sergeant was gone, a strange, paralyzing restlessness seized me. I wandered aimlessly through the house, performing a dozen futile tasks, none of which I could later recall. It was only when Duffy returned, breathless, that I realized the Sergeant had handed the boy a scrap of paper from his pocketbook. The pencil scrawl read: *Send me one of Rosanna Spearman's boots, and be quick about it.*

I dispatched a maid to Rosanna's chamber and sent the boy back to tell me I would follow with the boot. I knew this was not the swiftest route, yet I could not trust another soul to deliver the evidence. A sudden, desperate urge to protect the girl, a feeling that had resurfaced at the eleventh hour, drove me forward. Clutching the boot, I ran as hard as a man of seventy could manage.

As I approached the shore, the sky turned a bruised black, and rain lashed down in heavy sheets. The roar of the ocean crashed against the sandbank at the bay's mouth. Further along, I found the boy cowering beneath the lee of the dunes. Beyond him lay the churning sea, the waves tumbling over the bank, the rain sweeping across the water like a frantic cloak, and the vast, yellow expanse of the beach dominated by a single, dark figure: Sergeant Cuff.

He waved toward the north as I drew near. "Stay on that side!" he shouted, his voice strained. "Come down here!"

I stumbled toward him, gasping for air, my heart hammering against my ribs. I had a hundred questions burning in my throat, yet not one escaped my lips. His face terrified me; his eyes held a look of pure horror. He snatched the boot from my hand and pressed it firmly into a footprint on the sand. The print bore south from our position, pointing directly toward the rocky ledge known as the South Spit. The rain had not yet blurred the impression, and the fit was exact.

The Sergeant said nothing, simply indicating the boot in the track. I grabbed his arm, desperate to speak, but my voice failed me as it had before. He continued, following the trail down where the rocks met the sand. The South Spit was just submerged by the rising tide, the waters heaving over the hidden face of the Shivering Sand. With an obstinate, dreadful patience, Cuff tested the boot against the footprints, confirming they all pointed straight toward the rocks. Yet, no matter how he hunted, he found no marks leading *away* from them.

Finally, he gave up. Silently, he looked at me, then out at the deepening surge of the quicksand. I looked where he looked and saw the truth in his expression. A terrible, silent tremor racked my entire body. I sank to my knees on the wet sand.

"She has returned to the hiding place," Cuff murmured to himself. "Some fatal accident has befallen her on those rocks."

The memory of Rosanna's altered demeanor, her deadened responses when I had found her sweeping the corridor mere hours ago, surged through my mind. Even as Cuff spoke, I knew his guess was close, though perhaps not to the full, dreadful reality. I tried to warn him, to say that the death she had chosen was a death of her own seeking, but the words refused to form. The paralysis held me fast. I felt nothing of the driving rain, saw nothing of the rising tide. In a dreamlike vision, the lost girl returned to me as I had seen her that morning when I fetched her into the house. I heard her again, telling me that the Shivering Sand seemed to pull her against her will, wondering if her grave waited there. The horror struck me with the force of a physical blow, echoing through my own child, who was just Rosanna's age. My own daughter, subjected to such trials, might have lived that misery and met this end.

The Sergeant gently lifted me up and turned me away from the site of her demise.

With that relief, I began to draw breath again, seeing the world as it truly was. Looking toward the dunes, I saw the outdoor servants and the fisherman, Yolland, rushing down to us, shouting inquiries about the girl. In brief words, Cuff explained the footprints and declared that a fatal accident must have occurred. He singled out the fisherman and turned back to the sea. "Tell me," he commanded. "Could a boat have taken her off in weather like this from those rocks where her tracks end?"

Yolland pointed to the crashing rollers and the towering waves foaming against the headlands.

"No boat ever built," he answered, "could reach her through that."

Cuff cast one final glance at the footprints, which the rain was already beginning to wash away.

"There," he said, "is proof she did not leave by land. And here," he added, looking at the fisherman, "is proof she did not escape by sea." He paused, considering. "She was seen running toward this place half an hour before I arrived. Some time has passed since then. Call it an hour ago. How high would the water be on this side of the rocks at that time?" He pointed to the south side, the area not filled by the quicksand.

"As the tide makes today," Yolland replied, "there wouldn't have been enough water to drown a kitten on that side of the Spit an hour ago."

Cuff turned northward, toward the quicksand.

"How much on this side?" he asked.

"Less still," Yolland answered. "The Shivering Sand would have been just awash, and no more."

Turning to me, the Sergeant concluded the accident must have happened on the quicksand side. My tongue finally loosened. "No accident!" I cried out. "When she reached this place, she came weary of life to end it here."

Cuff recoiled slightly. "How do you know?" he asked. The others crowded around. The Sergeant regained his composure instantly. He pushed them back, noting my age and stating that the discovery had shaken me. "Let him alone a little," he ordered. Then he turned to Yolland. "Is there any chance of finding her when the tide ebbs?"

"None," Yolland replied grimly. "What the Sand takes, the Sand keeps forever."
