Stepping closer, the fisherman addressed me directly. "Mr. Betteredge, I have a word regarding the young woman's death. Four feet out, broadwise, along the side of the Spit, there is a shelf of rock about half a fathom deep under the sand. My question is: why didn't she strike that? If she slipped accidentally from the Spit, she would have fallen where there is footing at the bottom, at a depth that would barely cover her to the waist. She must have waded out or jumped into the Deeps beyond—or she wouldn't be missing now. No accident, sir! The Deeps of the Quicksand have got her. And they have got her by her own act."

Following the testimony of a man whose expertise was unquestionable, the Sergeant fell silent. We all held our peace. Together, we turned back up the slope of the beach.

At the dunes, we were met by the under-groom, running from the house. He was a good lad with an honest respect for me. He handed me a small note, his face etched with sorrow. "Penelope sent me with this, Mr. Betteredge," he said. "She found it in Rosanna's room."

It was her final farewell to the old man who had, thank God, always done his best to befriend her.

"You have often forgiven me, Mr. Betteredge, in past times. When you next see the Shivering Sand, try to forgive me once more. I have found my grave where my grave was waiting for me. I have lived, and died, sir, grateful for your kindness."

Those were the only words. Yet, small as they were, I lacked the strength to withstand them. Tears come easily when one is young and entering the world; they come easily when one is old and leaving it. I broke down sobbing.

Sergeant Cuff stepped closer, intending kindness, I had no doubt. I shrank back from him. "Don't touch me," I whispered. "It is the dread of you that has driven her to it."

"You are wrong, Mr. Betteredge," he answered quietly. "But there will be time enough to speak of it when we are indoors again."

I followed the others, supported by the groom's arm. Through the driving rain, we returned—to face the trouble and terror that awaited us inside the house.

The servants were already in a frenzy when we arrived, the news having spread like wildfire through the corridors. As we approached my mistress's door, it flew open from within, and she emerged, flanked by Mr. Franklin who struggled in vain to steady her. She was utterly undone by the horror of the discovery, her composure shattered.

"You are answerable for this!" she shrieked, pointing a trembling finger at the Sergeant. "Gabriel! Give that wretch his money—and let me be rid of his sight!"

The Sergeant was the only one among us capable of holding his ground, the only one who had not lost his wits.

"I am no more responsible for this calamity, my lady, than you are," he replied, his voice calm yet unyielding. "If, within half an hour, you still insist upon my departure, I shall accept your dismissal, but I will not accept your money."

His words were delivered with a respectful firmness that cut through her hysteria. She allowed Mr. Franklin to guide her back inside, and as the door shut them away, the Sergeant turned his observant gaze upon the female servants. While the others trembled in fear, he noted that Penelope was weeping. "When your father has changed his wet clothes," he instructed her gently, "come and speak to us in your father's room."

Before the half-hour elapsed, I had donned my dry garments and provided the Sergeant with the change of clothes he required. Penelope entered, and I felt a profound surge of pride in my role as her guardian. I seated her on my knee, clasped her close, and prayed for her safety. She buried her face in my bosom, her arms tight around my neck, and we waited in a heavy silence. It was clear to me that the poor dead girl's actions stemmed from a tragedy involving both my daughter and me. The Sergeant moved to the window, gazing out into the garden. I felt compelled to thank him for his consideration of our situation, and I did so sincerely.

Those of high station possess a luxury denied to the lower classes: the freedom to indulge their emotions. Necessity, which spares the wealthy, shows no mercy to us; we learn to suppress our feelings and endure our duties with patient stoicism. I make no complaint of this, merely observe it. Soon, Penelope and I were prepared to face the Sergeant.

When asked if she knew why her fellow-servant had taken her own life, my daughter answered, as one might expect, that it was out of love for Mr. Franklin Blake. When questioned further on whether she had shared this belief with anyone else, she replied, "I have not mentioned it, for Rosanna's sake." I felt it necessary to add a word. "And for Mr. Franklin's sake, my dear," I said. "If Rosanna died for love of him, it was without his knowledge and without his fault. Let him leave the house today, if he chooses, without the added pain of knowing the truth." The Sergeant nodded, agreeing wholeheartedly, and fell silent, seemingly comparing Penelope's confession with a private deduction of his own.

At the end of the allotted time, my mistress's bell rang.

On my way to answer it, I encountered Mr. Franklin emerging from his aunt's sitting-room. He informed me that her ladyship was ready to receive the Sergeant in my presence, as before, but added that he wished to speak with the Sergeant privately first. Returning to my room, he paused to examine the railway timetable in the hall.

"Are you truly going to leave us, sir?" I asked. "Miss Rachel will surely recover if you simply give her time?"

"She will recover," Mr. Franklin answered, "once she hears that I have departed and that she will see me no more."

I initially interpreted his tone as resentment over my young lady's treatment of him, but I was mistaken. My mistress had observed since the police's arrival that even the mention of his name ignited Miss Rachel's temper. Having loved his cousin too deeply to admit it to himself until the truth forced itself upon him, he had resolved to leave when she drove off to her aunt's. With eyes hardened by a cruel realization, he had made the only decision a man of spirit could make: to depart.

He addressed the Sergeant in my presence, stating that her ladyship was willing to acknowledge her hasty words. He asked if the Sergeant would, under these circumstances, accept his fee and leave the matter of the Diamond as it currently stood.

"No, sir," the Sergeant replied. "My fee is earned by performing my duty. I decline to accept it until that duty is fulfilled."

"I do not understand you," Mr. Franklin said.

"I shall explain, sir," the Sergeant responded. "When I arrived, I undertook to shed light on the disappearance of the Diamond. I am now ready to honor that pledge. Once I have presented the case to Lady Verinder as it stands and advised her plainly on the course of action to recover the Moonstone, the responsibility will shift entirely to her. Only then, after she decides whether to allow me to proceed further, will I consider my task complete and accept my fee."

In those words, the Sergeant reminded us that even within the Detective Police, a man possesses a reputation to protect.

His logic was undeniably sound, leaving no room for argument. As I rose to escort him to my lady's room, he asked if Mr. Franklin wished to attend. "Not unless Lady Verinder desires it," Mr. Franklin replied. He leaned in close to whisper to me as I followed the Sergeant out, "I know what that man is going to say about Rachel; and I am too fond of her to hear it and lose my temper. Leave me alone."

I left him there, desolate and leaning against the window sill, his face hidden in his hands, while Penelope peered through the doorway, longing to offer comfort. In his position, I would have summoned her immediately; there is great solace in confiding in another, for nine times out of ten, the listener takes your side. Perhaps, when my back was turned, he did call her in? If so, I can attest that my daughter would stop at nothing to comfort Mr. Franklin Blake.

Meanwhile, the Sergeant and I proceeded to my lady's room.

In our previous conference, she had been reluctant to look up from the book lying on the table. This time, however, there was a marked improvement. She met the Sergeant's gaze with a steadiness that mirrored his own. The family spirit shone in every line of her face, and I knew that the Sergeant would find a formidable opponent, for a woman of her mettle, strung up to hear the worst, was no easy match.

The first words spoken after we took our seats belonged to my lady.

"Sergeant Cuff," she said, "perhaps there was some excuse for the inconsiderate manner in which I spoke to you half an hour ago. I have no wish to claim that excuse, however. I say, with perfect sincerity, that I regret it, if I wronged you."

The grace of her voice and manner had its due effect on the Sergeant. He requested permission to justify himself, framing his defense as an act of respect toward my mistress. It was impossible, he stated, that he could be responsible for the calamity that had shocked us all, for the sufficient reason that his success in concluding the inquiry depended entirely on neither saying nor doing anything that could alarm Rosanna Spearman. He appealed to me to testify whether he had carried out that object. I could, and did, bear witness that he had. And there, as I thought, the matter might have been judiciously left to end.

Sergeant Cuff, however, took it a step further, evidently (as you shall now judge) with the purpose of forcing the most painful of all possible explanations to take place between her ladyship and himself.

"I have heard a motive assigned for the young woman's suicide," said the Sergeant, "which may possibly be the right one. It is a motive quite unconnected with the case which I am conducting here. I am bound to add, however, that my own opinion points the other way. Some unbearable anxiety in connection with the missing Diamond has, I believe, driven the poor creature to her own destruction. I don't pretend to know what that unbearable anxiety may have been. But I think (with your ladyship's permission) I can lay my hand on a person capable of deciding whether I am right or wrong."

"Is the person now in the house?" my mistress asked, after waiting a little.

"The person has left the house, my lady."

That answer pointed as straight to Miss Rachel as straight could be. A silence dropped on us which I thought would never come to an end. Lord! how the wind howled, and how the rain drove at the window, as I sat there waiting for one or other of them to speak again!

"Be so good as to express yourself plainly," said my lady. "Do you refer to my daughter?"

"I do," said Sergeant Cuff, in so many words.

My mistress had her cheque-book on the table when we entered the room—no doubt to pay the Sergeant his fee. She now put it back in the drawer. It went to my heart to see how her poor hand trembled—the hand that had loaded her old servant with benefits; the hand that, I pray God, may take mine, when my time comes, and I leave my place for ever!

"I had hoped," said my lady, very slowly and quietly, "to have recompensed your services, and to have parted with you without Miss Verinder's name having been openly mentioned between us as it has been mentioned now. My nephew has probably said something of this, before you came into my room?"

"Mr. Blake gave his message, my lady. And I gave Mr. Blake a reason—"

"It is needless to tell me your reason. After what you have just said, you know as well as I do that you have gone too far to go back. I owe it to myself, and I owe it to my child, to insist on your remaining here, and to insist on your speaking out."

The Sergeant looked at his watch.

"If there had been time, my lady," he answered, "I should have preferred writing my report, instead of communicating it by word of mouth. But, if this inquiry is to go on, time is of too much importance to be wasted in writing. I am ready to go into the matter at once. It is a very painful matter for me to speak of, and for you to hear."

There my mistress stopped him once more.

"I may possibly make it less painful to you, and to my good servant and friend here," she said, "if I set the example of speaking boldly, on my side. You suspect Miss Verinder of deceiving us all, by secreting the Diamond for some purpose of her own? Is that true?"

"Quite true, my lady."

"Very well. Now, before you begin, I have to tell you, as Miss Verinder's mother, that she is *absolutely incapable* of doing what you suppose her to have done. Your knowledge of her character dates from a day or two since. My knowledge of her character dates from the beginning of her life. State your suspicion of her as strongly as you please—it is impossible that you can offend me by doing so. I am sure, beforehand, that (with all your experience) the circumstances have fatally misled you in this case. Mind! I am in possession of no private information. I am as absolutely shut out of my daughter's confidence as you are. My one reason for speaking positively is the reason you have heard already. I know my child."

She turned to me, and gave me her hand. I kissed it in silence. "You may go on," she said, facing the Sergeant again as steadily as ever.

Sergeant Cuff bowed. My mistress had produced but one effect on him. His hatchet-face softened for a moment, as if he was sorry for her. As to shaking him in his own conviction, it was plain to see that she had not moved him by a single inch. He settled himself in his chair; and he began his vile attack on Miss Rachel's character in these words:

"I must ask your ladyship," he said, "to look this matter in the face, from my point of view as well as from yours. Will you please to suppose yourself coming down here, in my place, and with my experience? and will you allow me to mention very briefly what that experience has been?"

My mistress signed to him that she would do this. The Sergeant went on:

"For the last twenty years," he said, "I have been largely employed in cases of family scandal, acting in the capacity of confidential man. The one result of my domestic practice which has any bearing on the matter now in hand is a result which I may state in two words. It is well within my experience that young ladies of rank and position do occasionally have private debts which they dare not acknowledge to their nearest relatives and friends. Sometimes, the milliner and the jeweller are at the bottom of it. Sometimes, the money is wanted for purposes which I don't suspect in this case, and which I won't shock you by mentioning. Bear in mind what I have said, my lady—and now let us see how events in this house have forced me back on my own experience, whether I liked it or not!"

He considered with himself for a moment, and went on—with a horrid clearness that obliged you to understand him; with an abominable justice that favoured nobody.

"My first information relating to the loss of the Moonstone," said the Sergeant, "came to me from Superintendent Seegrave. He proved to my complete satisfaction that he was perfectly incapable of managing the case. The one thing he said which struck me as worth listening to was this—that Miss Verinder had declined to be questioned by him, and had spoken to him with a perfectly incomprehensible rudeness and contempt. I thought this curious—but I attributed it mainly to some clumsiness on the Superintendent's part which might have offended the young lady. After that, I put it by in my mind, and applied myself, single-handed, to the case. It ended, as you are aware, in the discovery of the smear on the door, and in Mr. Franklin Blake's evidence satisfying me that this same smear, and the loss of the Diamond, were pieces of the same puzzle. So far, if I suspected anything, I suspected that the Moonstone had been stolen, and that one of the servants might prove to be the thief. Very good. In this state of things, what happens? Miss Verinder suddenly comes out of her room, and speaks to me. I observe three suspicious appearances in that young lady. She is still violently agitated, though more than forty-eight hours have passed since the Diamond was lost. She treats me as she has already treated Superintendent Seegrave. And she is mortally offended with Mr. Franklin Blake. Very good again. Here (I say to myself) is a young lady who has lost a valuable jewel—a young lady, also, as my own eyes and ears inform me, who is of an impetuous temperament. Under these circumstances, and with that character, what does she do? She betrays an incomprehensible resentment against Mr. Blake, Mr. Superintendent, and myself—otherwise, the very three people who have all, in their different ways, been trying to help her to recover her lost jewel. Having brought my inquiry to that point—*then*, my lady, and not till then, I begin to look back into my own mind for my own experience. My own experience explains Miss Verinder's otherwise incomprehensible conduct. It associates her with those other young ladies that I know of. It tells me she has debts she daren't acknowledge, that must be paid. And it sets me asking myself, whether the loss of the Diamond may not mean—that the Diamond must be secretly pledged to pay them. That is the conclusion which my experience draws from plain facts. What does your ladyship's experience say against it?"

"What I have said already," answered my mistress. "The circumstances have misled you."

I said nothing on my side. *Robinson Crusoe*—God knows how—had got into my muddled old head. If Sergeant Cuff had found himself, at that moment, transported to a desert island, without a man Friday to keep him company, or a ship to take him off—he would have found himself exactly where I wished him to be! (*Nota bene:*—I am an average good Christian, when you don't push my Christianity too far. And all the rest of you—which is a great comfort—are, in this respect, much the same as I am.)

Sergeant Cuff went on:

"Right or wrong, my lady," he said, "having drawn my conclusion, the next thing to do was to put it to the test. I suggested to your ladyship the examination of all the wardrobes in the house. It was a means of finding the article of dress which had, in all probability, made the smear; and it was a means of putting my conclusion to the test. How did it turn out? Your ladyship consented; Mr. Blake consented; Mr. Ablewhite consented. Miss Verinder alone stopped the whole proceeding by refusing point-blank. That result satisfied me that my view was the right one. If your ladyship and Mr. Betteredge persist in not agreeing with me, you must be blind to what happened before you this very day. In your hearing, I told the young lady that her leaving the house (as things were then) would put an obstacle in the way of my recovering her jewel. You saw yourselves that she drove off in the face of that statement. You saw yourself that, so far from forgiving Mr. Blake for having done more than all the rest of you to put the clue into my hands, she publicly insulted Mr. Blake, on the steps of her mother's house. What do these things mean? If Miss Verinder is not privy to the suppression of the Diamond, what do these things mean?"

This time he looked my way. It was downright frightful to hear him piling up proof after proof against Miss Rachel, and to know, while one was longing to defend her, that there was no disputing the truth of what he said. I am (thank God!) constitutionally superior to reason. This enabled me to hold firm to my lady's view, which was my view also. This roused my spirit, and made me put a bold face on it before Sergeant Cuff. Profit, good friends, I beseech you, by my example. It will save you from many troubles of the vexing sort. Cultivate a superiority to reason, and see how you pare the claws of all the sensible people when they try to scratch you for your own good!

Finding that I made no remark, and that my mistress made no remark, Sergeant Cuff proceeded. Lord! how it did enrage me to notice that he was not in the least put out by our silence!

"There is the case, my lady, as it stands against Miss Verinder alone," he said. "The next thing is to put the case as it stands against Miss Verinder and the deceased Rosanna Spearman taken together. We will go back for a moment, if you please, to your daughter's refusal to let her wardrobe be examined. My mind being made up, after that circumstance, I had two questions to consider next. First, as to the right method of conducting my inquiry. Second, as to whether Miss Verinder had an accomplice among the female servants in the house. After carefully thinking it over, I determined to conduct the inquiry in, what we should call at our office, a highly irregular manner. For this reason: I had a family scandal to deal with, which it was my business to keep within the family limits. The less noise made, and the fewer strangers employed to help me, the better. As to the usual course of taking people in custody on suspicion, going before the magistrate, and all the rest of it—nothing of the sort was to be thought of, when your ladyship's daughter was (as I believed) at the bottom of the whole business. In this case, I felt that a person of Mr. Betteredge's character and position in the house—knowing the servants as he did, and having the honour of the family at heart—would be safer to take as an assistant than any other person whom I could lay my hand on. I should have tried Mr. Blake as well—but for one obstacle in the way. *He* saw the drift of my proceedings at a very early date; and, with his interest in Miss Verinder, any mutual understanding was impossible between him and me. I trouble your ladyship with these particulars to show you that I have kept the family secret within the family circle. I am the only outsider who knows it—and my professional existence depends on holding my tongue."

Here I felt that *my* professional existence depended on not holding *my* tongue. To be held up before my mistress, in my old age, as a sort of deputy-policeman, was, once again, more than

With my mistress gone, I was granted a rare moment of solitude to reflect upon Sergeant Cuff. I found him tucked away in a cozy corner of the hall, poring over his memorandum book, his lips curled in a distinctively vicious sneer.

"Taking notes on the case?" I inquired.

"No," replied the Sergeant. "Checking my schedule for the next engagement."

"Oh!" I exclaimed. "You believe the matter is concluded here, then?"

"I believe," Sergeant Cuff answered, "that Lady Verinder is one of the most astute women in England. I also believe a rose holds far more value than a diamond. Where is the gardener, Mr. Betteredge?"

No further words could be extracted from him regarding the Moonstone. His interest in his own investigation had evaporated; he was now singularly obsessed with locating the gardener. An hour later, I heard their voices rising in a heated dispute within the conservatory, the dog-rose once again the casualty of their argument.

Meanwhile, it fell to me to ascertain if Mr. Franklin intended to depart by the afternoon train. Having learned of the conference in my lady's room and its conclusion, he immediately resolved to await news from Frizinghall. This entirely natural shift in plans—which would have been inconsequential for ordinary people—produced a peculiarly objectionable result in Mr. Franklin's case. It left him unsettled, burdened with a surplus of idle time, and, consequently, it unleashed every foreign facet of his character, tumbling out of him like rats from a sack.

Now an Italian-Englishman, now a German-Englishman, and now a French-Englishman, he drifted in and out of every sitting room in the house, speaking only of Miss Rachel's treatment of him, addressing only me. I found him, for instance, in the library beneath the map of Modern Italy, utterly unaware of any solution to his troubles save the act of discussing them. "I possess several worthy aspirations, Betteredge; but what am I to do with them now? I am brimming with dormant virtues, if Rachel would only assist in bringing them forth!" He was so eloquent in painting a portrait of his neglected merits, and so pathetically lamenting their loss, that I was at my wits' end for a consolation, until it struck me that this was a case demanding the wholesome application of *Robinson Crusoe*.

I shuffled to my own room and returned with that immortal volume. The library was empty; the map of Modern Italy stared at me, and I stared back at it.

I checked the drawing-room. His handkerchief lay on the floor, proof of his brief visit, while the empty space confirmed his departure.

I checked the dining-room and found Samuel with a biscuit and a glass of sherry, silently surveying the vacant air. A moment prior, Mr. Franklin had rung furiously for refreshment. Upon its hurried production by Samuel, Mr. Franklin had vanished before the bell downstairs had even ceased its ringing.

I finally located him in the morning-room. He stood at the window, drawing hieroglyphics with his finger in the condensation on the glass.

"Your sherry awaits you, sir," I said. It might as well have been addressed to one of the four walls; he was sunk in the bottomless depths of his own meditations, beyond recall.

"How do *you* explain Rachel's conduct, Betteredge?" was the only response I received. Lacking a ready reply, I produced *Robinson Crusoe*, convinced that some explanation might be found therein if only we searched long enough. Mr. Franklin closed the book and immediately launched into his German-English gibberish. "Why not investigate?" he demanded, as if I had personally objected. "Why lose your patience, Betteredge, when patience is the key to truth? Do not interrupt me. Rachel's conduct is perfectly intelligible if you grant her the common justice of the Objective view first, the Subjective view second, and the Objective-Subjective view to conclude. What do we know? We know that the loss of the Moonstone on Thursday morning threw her into a state of nervous excitement from which she has not yet recovered. Do you deny the Objective view? Very well—do not interrupt. Being in such a state, how can we expect her to behave as she might otherwise? Arguing from within-outwards, what do we reach? The Subjective view. I defy you to controvert it. Then what follows? Good heavens! The Objective-Subjective explanation follows naturally! Rachel, properly speaking, is not Rachel, but Somebody Else. Do I mind being cruelly treated by Somebody Else? You are unreasonable enough, Betteredge; but you cannot accuse me of that. Then how does it end? It ends, despite your confounded English narrowness, in my perfect happiness and comfort. Where is the sherry?"

By this time, my head was in such a condition that I was unsure whether it belonged to me or to Mr. Franklin. In this deplorable state, I managed to accomplish three Objective acts: I secured Mr. Franklin his sherry, I retired to my own room, and I solaced myself with the most soothing pipe of tobacco I had ever smoked.

Do not suppose, however, that I was free of Mr. Franklin on such easy terms. Drifting once more from the morning-room into the hall, he found his way to the offices, smelled my pipe, and was instantly reminded that he had foolishly given up smoking for Miss Rachel's sake. In the twinkling of an eye, he burst in with his cigar-case and attacked me with his one eternal subject, in his neat, witty, unbelieving French style. "Give me a light, Betteredge. Is it conceivable that a man can smoke as long as I have without discovering a complete system for the treatment of women at the bottom of his cigar-case? Follow me carefully, and I will prove it in two words. You choose a cigar, try it, and it disappoints you. What do you do? You discard it and try another. Observe the application! You choose a woman, try her, and she breaks your heart. Fool! Take a lesson from your cigar-case. Discard her and try another!"

I shook my head. Wonderful philosophy, perhaps, but my own experience stood against it. "In the time of the late Mrs. Betteredge," I said, "I often felt inclined to adopt your philosophy, Mr. Franklin. But the law insists that you smoke your cigar once chosen." I punctuated this observation with a wink. Mr. Franklin burst into laughter, and we were as merry as crickets until the next new facet of his character emerged. Thus things continued between my young master and me; and thus, while the Sergeant and the gardener wrangled over the roses, we spent the interval awaiting news from Frizinghall.

The pony-chaise returned half an hour sooner than I dared expect. My lady had decided to remain for the present at her sister's house. The groom delivered two letters from his mistress: one for Mr. Franklin, the other for me.

I sent Mr. Franklin's letter to him in the library, where his wandering had now taken him for the second time. In my own room, I read my letter. A cheque, which fell out as I opened it, informed me (before I had even read the contents) that Sergeant Cuff's dismissal from the Moonstone inquiry was now a settled fact.

I summoned the Sergeant to the conservatory to speak with him directly. He appeared, his mind still full of the gardener and the dog-rose, declaring that no equal to Mr. Begbie for obstinacy had ever existed, nor ever would. I requested he dismiss such trifling from our conversation and attend to a truly serious matter. Upon this, he exerted himself sufficiently to notice the letter in my hand. "Ah!" he said, in a weary tone, "you have heard from her ladyship. Am I to have anything to do with it, Mr. Betteredge?"

"You shall judge for yourself, Sergeant." I then read the letter to him with my best emphasis and discretion:

"MY GOOD GABRIEL,—I request that you inform Sergeant Cuff that I have fulfilled the promise I made to him, with this result concerning Rosanna Spearman. Miss Verinder solemnly declares that she has never spoken a word in private to Rosanna since that unhappy woman first entered my house. They never met, even accidentally, on the night the Diamond was lost; and no communication of any sort whatsoever occurred between them from the Thursday morning when the alarm was raised until this present Saturday afternoon, when Miss Verinder left us. After telling my daughter suddenly and explicitly of Rosanna Spearman's suicide—this is the consequence."

Reaching this point, I looked up and asked Sergeant Cuff his opinion.

"I should only offend you if I expressed *my* opinion," the Sergeant answered. "Proceed, Mr. Betteredge," he said with the most exasperating resignation.

Recalling that this man had the audacity to complain of our gardener's obstinacy, my tongue itched to "proceed" in words other than my mistress's. This time, however, my Christianity held firm. I continued steadily with her ladyship's letter:

"Having appealed to Miss Verinder in the manner the officer deemed most desirable, I spoke to her next in the manner I believed most likely to impress her. On two separate occasions, before my daughter left my roof, I privately warned her that she was exposing herself to suspicion of the most unbearable and degrading kind. I have now told her, in the plainest terms, that my apprehensions have been realized.

"Her answer to this, on her own solemn affirmation, is as clear as words can be. First, she owes no money privately to any living soul. Second, the Diamond is not now, and never has been, in her possession since she placed it in her cabinet on Wednesday night.

"The confidence my daughter has placed in me extends no further. She maintains an obstinate silence when I ask her to explain the Diamond's disappearance. She refuses, with tears, when I appeal to her to speak for my sake. 'The day will come when you will know why I am careless about being suspected, and why I am silent even to *you_. I have done much to make my mother pity me—nothing to make my mother blush for me.' These are my daughter's own words.

"After what has passed between the officer and me, I think—stranger as he is—that he should be made acquainted with what Miss Verinder has said, as well as you. Read my letter to him, and then place in his hands the cheque which I enclose. In resigning all further claim on his services, I have only to say that I am convinced of his honesty and intelligence; but I am more firmly persuaded than ever that the circumstances in this case have fatally misled him."

The letter ended. Before presenting the cheque, I asked Sergeant Cuff if he had any comment.

"It is not my duty, Mr. Betteredge," he answered, "to comment on a case once concluded."

I tossed the cheque across the table to him. "Do you believe *that* part of her ladyship's letter?" I asked, indignantly.

The Sergeant looked at the cheque and raised his dismal eyebrows in acknowledgment of her ladyship's generosity.

"This is such a generous estimate of the value of my time," he said, "that I feel bound to make some return. I shall bear in mind the amount in this cheque, Mr. Betteredge, when the occasion arises for remembering it."

"What do you mean?" I asked.

"Her ladyship has smoothed matters over for the present very cleverly," said the Sergeant. "But *this* family scandal is of the sort that bursts forth when you least expect it. We shall have more detective business on our hands, sir, before the Moonstone is many months older."

If those words meant anything, and if the manner in which he spoke them meant anything, it came to this: My mistress's letter had proven, to his mind, that Miss Rachel was hardened enough to resist the strongest appeal and that she had deceived her own mother (good God, under what circumstances!) through a series of abominable lies. How others might have replied to the Sergeant, I do not know. I answered in plain terms:

"Sergeant Cuff, I consider your last observation an insult to my lady and her daughter!"

"Mr. Betteredge, consider it a warning to yourself, and you will be nearer the mark."

Hot and angry as I was, the infernal confidence with which he delivered that answer silenced me.

I walked to the window to compose myself. The rain had ceased, and who should I see in the courtyard but Mr. Begbie, the gardener, waiting outside to continue the dog-rose controversy with Sergeant Cuff.

"My compliments to the Sergeant," said Mr. Begbie the moment he saw me. "If he is minded to walk to the station, I am agreeable to accompany him."

"What!" cried the Sergeant behind me, "are you not convinced yet?"

"The de'il a bit I'm convinced!" answered Mr. Begbie.

"Then I'll walk to the station!" said the Sergeant.

"Then I'll meet you at the gate!" said Mr. Begbie.

I was angry enough, as you know—but how could any man's anger withstand such an interruption? Sergeant Cuff noticed the change in me and encouraged it with a timely word. "Come! Come!" he said, "why not treat my view of the case as her ladyship treats it? Why not say the circumstances have fatally misled me?"

To accept anything as her ladyship accepted it was a privilege worth enjoying—even with the disadvantage of it being offered by Sergeant Cuff. I cooled slowly to my customary level. I regarded any opinion of Miss Rachel other than my lady's or mine with lofty contempt. The only thing I could not do was to avoid the subject of the Moonstone! My own good sense ought to have warned me to let the matter rest—but there! The virtues distinguishing the present generation were not invented in my time. Sergeant Cuff had hit me on the raw, and though I looked down upon him with contempt, the tender place still tingled. The end was that I perversely led him back to the subject of her ladyship's letter. "I am quite satisfied myself," I said. "But never mind that! Proceed as if I were still open to

I had the pony-chaise standing ready, anticipating that Mr. Franklin might finally decide to depart by the train that very night. Yet, the arrival of the luggage, trailed by Mr. Franklin himself, confirmed that for once in his life, he had stood firm in his resolve.

"So you have truly made up your mind, sir?" I asked as we met in the hall. "Why not wait a day or two longer, and grant Miss Rachel another chance?"

The foreign polish seemed to have completely worn off Mr. Franklin now that the moment of departure had arrived. Instead of offering a verbal reply, he placed the letter addressed to him by her ladyship into my hand. Much of its content reiterated what I had already read in his previous correspondence, but the final paragraph regarding Miss Rachel revealed the depth of his determination.

"You will wonder, I dare say," her ladyship wrote, "at my allowing my own daughter to keep me perfectly in the dark. A Diamond worth twenty thousand pounds has been lost—and I am left to infer that the mystery of its disappearance is no mystery to Rachel, and that some incomprehensible obligation of silence has been laid on her, by some person or persons utterly unknown to me, with some object in view at which I cannot even guess. Is it conceivable that I should allow myself to be trifled with in this way? It is quite conceivable, in Rachel's present state. She is in a condition of nervous agitation pitiable to see. I dare not approach the subject of the Moonstone again until time has done something to quiet her. To help this end, I have not hesitated to dismiss the police-officer. The mystery which baffles us, baffles him too. This is not a matter in which any stranger can help us. He adds to what I have to suffer; and he maddens Rachel if she only hears his name.

"My plans for the future are as well settled as they can be. My present idea is to take Rachel to London—partly to relieve her mind by a complete change, partly to try what may be done by consulting the best medical advice. Can I ask you to meet us in town? My dear Franklin, you, in your way, must imitate my patience, and wait, as I do, for a fitter time. The valuable assistance which you rendered to the inquiry after the lost jewel is still an unpardoned offence, in the present dreadful state of Rachel's mind. Moving blindfold in this matter, you have added to the burden of anxiety which she has had to bear, by innocently threatening her secret with discovery, through your exertions. It is impossible for me to excuse the perversity that holds you responsible for consequences which neither you nor I could imagine or foresee. She is not to be reasoned with—she can only be pitied. I am grieved to have to say it, but for the present, you and Rachel are better apart. The only advice I can offer you is, to give her time."

I handed the letter back, feeling a profound sorrow for Mr. Franklin, for I knew how deeply he cherished my young lady; and I saw that her mother's assessment had struck him to the heart. "You know the proverb, sir," I said to him. "When things are at the worst, they're sure to mend. Things can't be much worse, Mr. Franklin, than they are now."

Mr. Franklin folded up his aunt's letter, showing little comfort in my remark.

"When I came here from London with that horrible Diamond," he said, "I don't believe there was a happier household in England than this. Look at the household now! Scattered, disunited—the very air of the place poisoned with mystery and suspicion! Do you remember that morning at the Shivering Sand, when we talked about my uncle Herncastle, and his birthday gift? The Moonstone has served the Colonel's vengeance, Betteredge, by means which the Colonel himself never dreamt of!"

With that, he shook my hand and stepped out to the pony-chaise.

I followed him down the steps. It was terribly sad to witness him leaving the old place, where he had spent his happiest years, in such a manner. Penelope, visibly shaken by the turmoil in the house, came running to bid him farewell, tears in her eyes. Mr. Franklin kissed her. I waved my hand, intending to convey, "You are heartily welcome, sir." Several of the other female servants appeared, peering after him around the corner. He was one of those men whom the women invariably liked. At the last moment, I stopped the pony-chaise and begged as a favor that he would let us hear from him by letter. He seemed to ignore my request—he was looking around from one thing to another, taking a sort of farewell of the old house and grounds. "Tell us where you are going to, sir!" I said, holding onto the chaise, trying to glean his future plans. Mr. Franklin pulled his hat down suddenly over his eyes. "Going?" he said, echoing the word. "I am going to the devil!" The pony startled at the word, as if it had sensed a Christian horror in it. "God bless you, sir, go where you may!" was all I had time to say before he vanished from sight and hearing. A sweet and pleasant gentleman! With all his faults and follies, a sweet and pleasant gentleman! He left a sad void behind him when he departed my lady's house.

The long summer evening closed in on that Saturday night, bringing with it a dull and dreary atmosphere.

I kept my spirits from sinking by clinging to my pipe and my copy of *Robinson Crusoe*. The women (excepting Penelope) passed the time discussing Rosanna's suicide. They were all stubbornly convinced that the poor girl had stolen the Moonstone and had destroyed herself in terror of being discovered. My daughter, of course, privately held fast to what she had always asserted. Her notion of the motive behind the suicide failed, oddly enough, just where my young lady's assertion of her innocence failed also. It left Rosanna's secret journey to Frizinghall and her actions regarding the nightgown entirely unexplained. There was no use pointing this out to Penelope; the objection made as much impression on her as a shower of rain on a waterproof coat. The truth is, my daughter inherits my superiority to reason—and, in respect to that accomplishment, has gotten far ahead of her own father.

On the following day (Sunday), the close carriage, which had been kept at Mr. Ablewhite's, returned empty. The coachman delivered a message for me, along with written instructions for my lady's maid and Penelope.

The message informed me that my mistress had decided to take Miss Rachel to her house in London on Monday. The written instructions detailed the clothing required and directed the two maids to meet their mistresses in town at a specific hour. Most of the other servants were to follow. My lady had found Miss Rachel so unwilling to return to the house after the events there that she had resolved to travel to London directly from Frizinghall. I was to remain in the country until further orders to manage affairs indoors and out. The servants staying behind were to be paid in full.

Reminded by all this of Mr. Franklin's words about our household being scattered and disunited, my thoughts naturally turned to him. The more I considered his future, the more uneasy I became. It ended with me writing, via Sunday's post, to his father's valet, Mr. Jeffco (whom I had known in former years), begging him to inform me of whatever Mr. Franklin had settled upon doing upon arriving in London.

The Sunday evening proved, if possible, even duller than Saturday's. We ended the day of rest, as hundreds of thousands of people do regularly once a week in these islands—that is, we all anticipated bedtime and fell asleep in our chairs.

How Monday affected the rest of the household I do not know. Monday gave *me* a good shake-up. The first of Sergeant Cuff's prophecies—that I would hear from the Yollands—came true on that day.

I had seen Penelope and my lady's maid off in the railway with the luggage for London, and was pottering about the grounds when I heard my name called. Turning round, I found myself face-to-face with the fisherman's daughter, Limping Lucy. Bating her lame foot and her leanness (the latter a horrid drawback to a woman, in my opinion), the girl possessed some pleasing qualities in the eye of a man. A dark, keen, clever face, a nice clear voice, and a beautiful brown head of hair counted among her merits. A crutch appeared in the list of her misfortunes. And a temper reckoned high in the sum total of her defects.

"Well, my dear," I said, "what do you want with me?"

"Where's the man you call Franklin Blake?" the girl asked, fixing me with a fierce look as she rested herself on her crutch.

"That is not a respectful way to speak of any gentleman," I answered. "If you wish to inquire for my lady's nephew, you will please to mention him as Mr. Franklin Blake."

She limped a step nearer to me and looked as if she could have eaten me alive. "_Mr._ Franklin Blake?" she repeated. "Murderer Franklin Blake would be a fitter name for him."

My practice with the late Mrs. Betteredge came in handy here. Whenever a woman tries to put *you* out of temper, turn the tables and put *her* out of temper instead. They are generally prepared for every effort you can make in your own defense, but that. One word does it as well as a hundred; and one word did it with Limping Lucy. I looked her pleasantly in the face and said—"Pooh!"

The girl's temper flared immediately. She poised herself on her sound foot and beat her crutch furiously three times on the ground. "He's a murderer! he's a murderer! he's a murderer! He has been the death of Rosanna Spearman!" she screamed at the top of her voice. One or two of the people working in the grounds nearby looked up, recognized Limping Lucy, knew what to expect from that quarter, and looked away again.

"He has been the death of Rosanna Spearman?" I repeated. "What makes you say that, Lucy?"

"What do you care? What does any man care? Oh! if she had only thought of the men as I think, she might have been living now!"

"She always thought kindly of *me*, poor soul," I said; "and, to the best of my ability, I always tried to act kindly by *her*."

I spoke those words in as comforting a manner as I could. The truth is, I hadn't the heart to irritate the girl with another of my sharp retorts. I had only noticed her temper at first. I noticed her wretchedness now—and wretchedness is not uncommonly insolent, you will find, in humble life. My answer melted Limping Lucy. She bent her head down and laid it on the top of her crutch.

"I loved her," the girl said softly. "She had lived a miserable life, Mr. Betteredge—vile people had ill-treated her and led her wrong—and it hadn't spoiled her sweet temper. She was an angel. She might have been happy with me. I had a plan for our going to London together like sisters, and living by our needles. That man came here, and spoilt it all. He bewitched her. Don't tell me he didn't mean it, and didn't know it. He ought to have known it. He ought to have taken pity on her. 'I can't live without him—and, oh, Lucy, he never even looks at me.' That's what she said. Cruel, cruel, cruel. I said, 'No man is worth fretting for in that way.' And she said, 'There are men worth dying for, Lucy, and he is one of them.' I had saved up a little money. I had settled things with father and mother. I meant to take her away from the mortification she was suffering here. We should have had a little lodging in London, and lived together like sisters. She had a good education, sir, as you know, and she wrote a good hand. She was quick at her needle. I have a good education, and I write a good hand. I am not as quick at my needle as she was—but I could have done. We might have got our living nicely. And, oh! what happens this morning? what happens this morning? Her letter comes and tells me that she has done with the burden of her life. Her letter comes, and bids me good-bye for ever. Where is he?" cries the girl, lifting her head from the crutch and flaring out again through her tears. "Where's this gentleman that I mustn't speak of, except with respect? Ha, Mr. Betteredge, the day is not far off when the poor will rise against the rich. I pray Heaven they may begin with *him*. I pray Heaven they may begin with *him*."

Here was another of your average good Christians, and here was the usual breakdown consequent on that same average Christianity being pushed too far! The parson himself (though I own this is saying a great deal) could hardly have lectured the girl in the state she was in now. All I ventured to do was to keep her to the point—in the hope of something turning up which might be worth hearing.

"What do you want with Mr. Franklin Blake?" I asked.

"I want to see him."

"For anything particular?"

"I have got a letter to give him."

"From Rosanna Spearman?"

"Yes."

"Sent to you in your own letter?"

"Yes."

Was the darkness going to lift? Were all the discoveries I was dying to make coming and offering themselves to me of their own accord? I was obliged to wait a moment. Sergeant Cuff had left his infection behind him. Certain signs and tokens, personal to myself, warned me that the detective-fever was beginning to set in again.

"You can't see Mr. Franklin," I said.

"I must, and will, see him."

"He went to London last night."

Limping Lucy looked me hard in the face and saw that I was speaking the truth. Without a word more, she turned about instantly towards Cobb's Hole.

I owe my orderly habits to my late parents, who instilled in me a rigid regimen of tidiness and reflection from my earliest days. I was taught to arrange my garments with military precision at the foot of my bed, to chronicle every event in a small diary, and to conclude the day with the "Evening Hymn" before seeking sleep. While the hymn has long since been replaced by the bitter meditations of adulthood and the sweet slumber by the fractured dreams of worry, I have retained the discipline of folding my clothes and recording my thoughts. The former anchors me to a happier childhood, before my father's ruin; the latter, originally intended to curb the fallen nature inherited from Adam, has unexpectedly become a tool for my survival. It allowed me to serve the whims of Mr. Franklin Blake, a wealthy relative into whose family I married through my late uncle.

Cut off from my kin by marriage and poverty, I reside in a small town in Brittany, surrounded by serious English friends and the comforts of a Protestant clergyman and cheap provisions. Here, in this quiet refuge amidst the roaring waves of Catholicism, a letter from England finally reached me. Mr. Franklin Blake, my affluent kinsman, remembered my insignificant existence and demanded my assistance. His whim was to investigate the scandalous theft of the Moonstone, and he commissioned me to recount my observations during my visit to Aunt Verinder's house in London. Though he offered pecuniary reward—a gesture typical of the rich—I resisted the temptation until Christian humility subdued my pride. Without my diary, I fear I could not have honestly earned my fee; with it, I am merely a laborer deserving her hire. Every detail, down to the minutest triviality, was recorded daily. My reverence for truth outweighs my respect for persons, and while Mr. Blake may edit my manuscript to spare the feelings of those involved, he cannot purchase my conscience.

According to my diary, I was passing Aunt Verinder's residence in Montagu Square on Monday, July 3, 1848. Seeing the shutters open, I knocked politely. The servant, a young woman of insolent demeanor and the daughter of the long-tolerated Betteredge, informed me that my aunt and her daughter, Rachel, had returned from the country a week prior and intended to stay in London. I declined to disturb them but inquired if I could be of service. The servant dismissed my offer in silence. Sitting in the dusty hall, I offered her a tract on the sinfulness of dress, titled "A Word With You On Your Cap-Ribbons." She rejected it with disdain, questioning its authorship and returning it. Undeterred, I slipped the tract into her letterbox and dropped another through the railings, feeling a slight relief in returning good for evil.

That evening, I attended a meeting of the Mothers'-Small-Clothes-Conversion-Society, an organization dedicated to rescuing fathers' trousers from pawnbrokers and resizing them for their sons. My friend, Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, was a prominent member, yet he was absent. When questioned by the committee, I learned of a shocking event that served as the catalyst for this narrative. On the preceding Friday, June 30, 1848, two gentlemen of vastly different social standings—Mr. Septimus Luker of Lambeth and Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite—were victims of a bizarre outrage that sent shockwaves through London.

Unable to quote the newspaper accounts directly, I rely on the testimony given to me that Monday evening, adhering to my lifelong habit of neatness and order. Early on that fateful day, Mr. Ablewhite was cashing a cheque at a bank in Lombard Street. As he exited, he encountered a stranger leaving simultaneously. A brief, polite contest ensued regarding who should pass first; the stranger insisted on yielding, and they parted ways. To the casual observer, this seems a trifling incident, but in the economy of divine providence, such trifles often lead to terrible consequences.

Upon reaching his residence in Kilburn, Mr. Ablewhite found a poorly dressed boy waiting in the hall. The boy handed him a letter from an unknown elderly lady, requesting his immediate attendance at a house in Northumberland Street, Strand. The letter claimed the lady wished to discuss matters concerning the Society and promised substantial donations if her questions were answered satisfactorily. True to his charitable nature, Mr. Ablewhite complied instantly.

At the appointed house, a portly man admitted him to an empty apartment on the drawing-room floor. Two anomalies struck Mr. Ablewhite immediately: a faint scent of musk and camphor, and an ancient, richly illuminated Oriental manuscript lying open on a table. Absorbed in examining the book, with his back turned toward the folding doors leading to the front room, he was suddenly seized from behind. A naked, tawny-brown arm clamped around his neck. Before he could react, his eyes were bandaged, his mouth gagged, and he was thrown to the floor by unseen assailants. A third man rifled his pockets thoroughly. The entire ordeal was conducted in dead silence, save for muttered words in an unknown language expressing disappointment and rage. Mr. Ablewhite was lifted, seated, and bound hand and foot.

Moments later, he heard footsteps ascending the stairs, followed by a woman's scream and a man's exclamation of "Hullo!" A pair of hands, guided by Christian compassion, removed his bindings. He found himself facing two respectable strangers who asked, "What does it mean?" The explanation followed. The landlord and landlady revealed that the premises had been rented the previous day by a respectable gentleman claiming to represent three Oriental noblemen visiting England. Two of these men, accompanied by their English agent, had taken possession early that morning. The third was expected shortly. However, just ten minutes before Mr. Ablewhite's arrival, all three foreigners and their agent had departed quietly. The landlord and landlady, noticing the agent remained alone upstairs, grew suspicious and investigated, discovering Mr. Ablewhite bound and gagged.

An examination of the room revealed that Mr. Ablewhite's belongings—watch, chain, purse, keys, and papers—had been scattered but left unharmed. The Oriental manuscript had been removed, along with nothing else. The police concluded that the perpetrators had acted on insufficient information, mistakenly believing Mr. Ablewhite to be one of the Oriental noblemen due to his accidental encounter with Mr. Luker at the bank.

Meanwhile, Mr. Luker had also fallen victim to the same scheme. After leaving the bank, he received a similar summons from a stranger claiming to be a patron of Oriental antiquities. Taken to lodgings in Alfred Place, Tottenham Court Road, he too encountered the illuminated manuscript. Just as Mr. Ablewhite had, he was seized by a tawny arm, blinded, gagged, and bound. Unlike Mr. Ablewhite, however, one of Mr. Luker's papers was stolen: a receipt acknowledging a valuable gem left with his bankers. This document rendered the receipt useless for fraud, as it stipulated that the gem could only be reclaimed by the owner in person. Upon recovering, Mr. Luker rushed to the bank, only to find the thieves had been warned off by the clerks who recognized the receipt.

Both incidents were reported to the police, who deemed them bungled robberies based on faulty intelligence. Mr. Ablewhite's absence from the Society's meeting was due to his consultation with the authorities. With the mystery of the double kidnapping seemingly resolved as a case of mistaken identity, I returned to my own narrative.

I arrived at Aunt Verinder's house precisely at two o'clock on Tuesday. My aunt received me with her customary grace, but I sensed an undercurrent of anxiety, particularly directed toward her daughter. Rachel, usually a figure of such insignificance compared to her distinguished parents, appeared profoundly disturbed. Her behavior was erratic; she laughed loudly without restraint and consumed her food with capricious wastefulness. I felt deep sorrow for her mother, even before the full extent of the tragedy was revealed.

After luncheon, my aunt reminded Rachel of the doctor's advice to read quietly. Rachel, eager for news of Mr. Ablewhite's adventure, excused herself to the library, bidding me a careless farewell. Left alone with my aunt, she confessed the horrifying story of the Indian Diamond. She revealed that the theft had occurred at her country estate, and despite her desire to keep the matter private, the involvement of servants and the subsequent newspaper reports made secrecy impossible. Strangers were already speculating on connections between the theft at her home and the kidnappings in London.

The revelation of the Moonstone's loss left me stunned. Knowing Rachel's unregenerate spirit, I had anticipated moral decay, perhaps even murder, but the course my aunt had taken to manage the situation shocked me. She had sought the counsel of physicians rather than a clergyman, a decision I attributed to her own godless upbringing. The doctors prescribed exercise and amusement, urging her to avoid dwelling on the past. My aunt admitted that Rachel had been restless and excited since hearing of the kidnapping, demanding that my nephew, Mr. Ablewhite, be summoned immediately. Remarkably, Rachel also expressed concern for Mr. Luker, a stranger to her.

I suggested that Rachel's extraordinary interest in these events hinted at a hidden secret threatened by discovery. My aunt, bewildered, asked what discovery I meant, specifically through Mr. Luker or Mr. Ablewhite. As the word "discovery" hung in the air, the servant announced Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite's arrival, interrupting the conversation just as it began to unravel deeper mysteries.

Mr. Godfrey arrived with the punctuality of a clockwork mechanism, entering the room at the precise moment his name was announced. He was neither so close behind the servant as to startle us, nor so distant as to force an awkward pause and the opening of the door. In the seamless integration of his daily routine, the true Christian reveals himself; and this dear man was remarkably complete in his execution.

"Go to Miss Verinder," my aunt instructed the servant, "and inform her that Mr. Ablewhite has arrived."

Both of us immediately inquired after his health, asking in unison if he had recovered from the terrifying ordeal of the previous week. With impeccable tact, he managed to answer us simultaneously. Lady Verinder received his verbal response, while I was granted the benefit of his charming smile.

"What," he cried with infinite tenderness, "have I done to merit all this sympathy? My dear aunt! My dear Miss Clack! I have simply been mistaken for someone else. I was merely blindfolded, strangled, and thrown flat upon a thin carpet over a hard floor. Consider how much worse it could have been! I might have been murdered or robbed. What have I lost? Only 'Nervous Force'—a concept the law does not recognize as property. Strictly speaking, I have lost nothing. Had it been my choice, I would have kept this adventure to myself; I shrink from all this fuss and publicity. However, since Mr. Luker made his injuries public, mine have inevitably followed suit. I have become the property of the newspapers until the gentle reader grows weary of the subject. I am thoroughly sick of it myself. May the gentle reader soon share my sentiment! And how is dear Rachel? Still enjoying the gaieties of London? I am delighted to hear it. Miss Clack, I require all your indulgence. I am sadly behindhand with my Committee Work and my dear Ladies. Yet, I sincerely hope to visit the Mothers'-Small-Clothes next week. Did you make cheerful progress at Monday's Committee? Was the Board optimistic about future prospects? And are we adequately supplied with Trousers?"

The heavenly gentleness of his smile rendered his apologies irresistible, while the richness of his deep voice added an indescribable charm to the mundane inquiry regarding our trousers. In truth, we were far too well-provided; we were completely overwhelmed. I was on the verge of confirming this when the door opened again, introducing an element of worldly disturbance in the person of Miss Verinder.

She approached Mr. Godfrey with a speed that was decidedly unladylike, her hair shockingly disheveled and her face flushed in a manner I would find unbecoming.

"I am charmed to see you, Godfrey," she said, addressing him with the casual familiarity of one young man speaking to another. "I wish you had brought Mr. Luker along. You and he are, as long as our present excitement lasts, the two most fascinating men in all London. It is morbid to say so; it is unhealthy; it is precisely the sort of thing a well-regulated mind like Miss Clack's instinctively shudders at. Never mind that. Tell me the entire story of Northumberland Street directly. I suspect the newspapers have omitted details."

Even the angelic Mr. Godfrey shares the fallen nature inherited from Adam—it is a small portion of our human legacy, alas, but he possesses it. I confess it pained me to see him take Rachel's hand in both of his own and lay it softly against the left side of his waistcoat. It was a direct encouragement to her reckless manner of speech and her insolent reference to me.

"Dearest Rachel," he replied in the same voice that had previously thrilled me when discussing our prospects and trousers, "the newspapers have informed you of everything—and they have done so far better than I could."

"Godfrey thinks we all make too much of the matter," my aunt interjected. "He has just stated that he cares not to speak of it."

"Why?"

She posed the question with a sudden flash in her eyes and a sharp upward glance at Mr. Godfrey. On his side, he looked down at her with an indulgence so injudicious and undeserved that I felt compelled to intervene.

"Rachel, darling!" I remonstrated gently, "true greatness and true courage are ever modest."

"You are a very good fellow in your way, Godfrey," she said, ignoring me entirely and continuing to address her cousin as if they were peers. "But I am quite certain you are not great; I do not believe you possess any extraordinary courage. And I am firmly persuaded that if you ever possessed any modesty, your lady-worshippers relieved you of that virtue many years ago. You have some private reason for avoiding discussion of your Northumberland Street adventure, and I intend to discover it."

"My reason is the simplest imaginable and the most easily acknowledged," he answered, enduring her with patience. "I am tired of the subject."

"You are tired of the subject? My dear Godfrey, I am about to make a remark."

"What is it?"

"You live too much in the company of women. Consequently, you have contracted two very bad habits. You have learned to speak nonsense seriously, and you have developed a habit of telling fibs for the sheer pleasure of it. You cannot walk straight with your lady-worshippers. I intend to make you walk straight with *me*. Come, sit down. I am brimful of direct questions, and I expect you to be brimful of direct answers."

She physically dragged him across the room to a chair by the window, ensuring the light would illuminate his face. I deeply regret the necessity of reporting such language and describing such conduct. Yet, hemmed in as I am between Mr. Franklin Blake's cheque on one side and my own sacred regard for truth on the other, what am I to do? I glanced at my aunt. She sat unmoved, seemingly devoid of any inclination to interfere. I had never witnessed such torpor in her before. Perhaps it was the reaction following the trying time she endured in the country. Regardless of the cause, it was an unpleasant symptom to note in dear Lady Verinder, considering her age and her autumnal exuberance of figure.

Meanwhile, Rachel had settled herself at the window with our amiable, forbearing—and too forbearing—Mr. Godfrey. She launched into her string of questions, paying no more attention to her mother or myself than if we were not present.

"Have the police done anything, Godfrey?"

"Nothing whatever."

"It is certain, I suppose, that the three men who laid the trap for you were the same three who later trapped Mr. Luker?"

"Humanly speaking, my dear Rachel, there can be no doubt of it."

"And not a trace of them has been discovered?"

"Not a trace."

"It is thought—is it not?—that these three men are the three Indians who visited our house in the country."

"Some people think so."

"Do you think so?"

"My dear Rachel, they blindfolded me before I could see their faces. I know nothing whatsoever of the matter. How can I offer an opinion?"

Even the angelic gentleness of Mr. Godfrey was beginning to falter under the relentless persecution he faced. Whether unbridled curiosity or ungovernable dread drove Miss Verinder's questions, I do not presume to inquire. I merely report that when Mr. Godfrey attempted to rise after giving his answer, she seized him by the shoulders and pushed him back into his chair. Oh, do not claim this was immodest! Do not even suggest that the recklessness of guilty terror alone accounts for such behavior! We must not judge others. My Christian friends, indeed, indeed, indeed, we must not judge others!

She continued her interrogation, unabashed. Earnest biblical students might recall, as I did, the blinded children of the devil who proceeded with their orgies unabashed in the days before the Flood.

"I want to know something about Mr. Luker, Godfrey."

"I am again unfortunate, Rachel. No man knows less of Mr. Luker than I do."

"You never saw him before you met him accidentally at the bank?"

"Never."

"You have seen him since?"

"Yes. We have been examined together, as well as separately, to assist the police."

"Mr. Luker was robbed of a receipt from his banker—was he not? What was the receipt for?"

"For a valuable gem he had placed in the bank's safekeeping."

"That is what the newspapers say. It may suffice for the general reader, but it is insufficient for me. The banker's receipt must have specified what the gem was?"

"The banker's receipt, Rachel—as I have heard it described—mentioned nothing of the kind. A valuable gem belonging to Mr. Luker; deposited by him; sealed with his seal; and to be released only upon his personal application. That was the form, and that is all I know."

She paused after he finished, looking at her mother and sighing, then returning her gaze to Mr. Godfrey.

"Some of our private affairs at home," she said, "appear to have leaked into the newspapers?"

"I grieve to say, it is so."

"And some idle strangers are attempting to trace a connection between the events at our Yorkshire house and what has occurred since in London?"

"The public curiosity, in certain quarters, is, I fear, taking that turn."

"The people who claim the three men who ill-treated you and Mr. Luker were the three Indians also assert that the valuable gem—"

She stopped abruptly. Gradually, over the last few moments, her face had grown whiter and whiter. The extraordinary blackness of her hair made this pallor so ghastly that we all feared she would faint at the moment she checked herself mid-sentence. Dear Mr. Godfrey made a second attempt to leave his chair. My aunt entreated her to say no more. I followed suit with a modest medicinal peace-offering in the form of a bottle of salts. None of us achieved the slightest effect on her.

"Godfrey, stay where you are," she commanded. "Mamma, there is no reason to be alarmed about me. Clack, you're dying to hear the end of it—I won't faint, expressly to oblige *you*."

These were the exact words she used, recorded in my diary the moment I returned home. But, oh, let us not judge! My Christian friends, let us not judge!

She turned once more to Mr. Godfrey. With an obstinacy dreadful to witness, she returned to the point where she had halted and completed her question:

"I spoke to you a minute ago about rumors in certain quarters. Tell me plainly, Godfrey, do any of them say that Mr. Luker's valuable gem is—the Moonstone?"

As the name of the Indian Diamond passed her lips, I perceived a transformation in my admirable friend. His complexion darkened. He lost the genial suavity of manner that was one of his greatest charms. A noble indignation animated his reply.

"They *do* say it," he answered. "There are people who do not hesitate to accuse Mr. Luker of lying to serve some private interest. He has repeatedly declared that, until this scandal assailed him, he had never even heard of the Moonstone. And these vile people reply, without a shadow of proof, that he has reasons for concealment; we refuse to believe him on his oath. Shameful! Shameful!"

Rachel looked at him strangely—I cannot adequately describe the expression—while he spoke. When he finished, she said, "Considering that Mr. Luker is merely a chance acquaintance of yours, you take up his cause, Godfrey, rather warmly."

My gifted friend offered one of the most truly evangelical responses I have ever heard.

"I hope, Rachel, I take up the cause of all oppressed people rather warmly," he said.

The tone in which those words were spoken might have melted a stone. But, oh dear, what is the hardness of stone? Nothing compared to the hardness of the unregenerate human heart! She sneered. I blush to record it—she sneered at him in his face.

"Keep your noble sentiments for your Ladies' Committees, Godfrey. I am certain the scandal that has assailed Mr. Luker has not spared you."

Even my aunt's torpor was roused by those words.

"My dear Rachel," she remonstrated, "you have really no right to say that!"

"I mean no harm, mamma—I mean good. Have a moment's patience, and you will see."

She looked back at Mr. Godfrey with what appeared to be sudden pity. She went the very unladylike length of taking his hand.

"I am certain," she said, "that I have discovered the true reason for your unwillingness to speak of this matter before my mother and before me. An unlucky accident has associated you in people's minds with Mr. Luker. You have told me what scandal says of *him*. What does scandal say of *you*?"

Even at the eleventh hour, dear Mr. Godfrey—always ready to return good for evil—tried to spare her.

"Don't ask me!" he said. "It's better forgotten, Rachel—it is, indeed."

"I *will* hear it!" she cried fiercely, at the top of her voice.

"Tell her, Godfrey!" my aunt entreated. "Nothing can do her such harm as your silence is doing now!"

Mr. Godfrey's fine eyes filled with tears. He cast one last appealing look at her—and then spoke the fatal words:

"If you insist, Rachel—scandal says that the Moonstone is in pledge to Mr. Luker, and that I am the man who pawned it."

She sprang to her feet with a scream. She looked backward and forward from Mr. Godfrey to my aunt, and from my aunt to Mr. Godfrey, in such a frantic manner that I genuinely believed she had gone mad.

"Don't speak to me! Don't touch me!" she exclaimed, shrinking back from all of us (I declare like a hunted animal!) into a corner of the room. "This is my fault! I must set it right. I have sacrificed myself—I had a right to do that, if I liked. But to let an innocent man be ruined; to keep a secret that destroys his character for

My conscience, weighed down by the heavy burden of my aunt's suffering, forbade me to whisper the truth I had uncovered until she chose to speak it herself. I sat in silent vigil, my heart prepared for whatever duty lay ahead, however painful, trusting that a few sustaining words would find their proper moment.

"I have been seriously ill, Drusilla, for some time past," my aunt began, her voice trembling slightly. "And, strange to say, without knowing it myself."

I thought of the countless souls perishing in spiritual darkness, unaware of their own decay, and a deep dread seized me that my poor aunt might be among them. "Yes, dear," I replied softly, my eyes filling with tears. "Yes."

"I brought Rachel to London for medical advice," she continued, her gaze fixed on the fire. "I deemed it right to consult two physicians."

Two doctors! And, oh, the thought that not a single clergyman had been summoned for Rachel's state! "Yes, dear?" I asked again, my voice thick with emotion. "Yes?"

"One of the two medical men was a stranger to me," my aunt explained, her composure slowly fracturing. "The other had been a close friend of my husband's, a man who had always held a sincere affection for me. After examining Rachel, he requested to speak with me privately. I expected specific instructions for my daughter's care. Instead, he took my hand with grave solemnity and said, 'Lady Verinder, I have observed you with both professional and personal concern. You are, I fear, in far more urgent need of medical aid than your daughter.' He questioned me, and though I initially treated his inquiries lightly, I saw the distress they caused him. He arranged to return the following day, accompanied by a colleague, at a time when Rachel would be absent."

"The result of that visit," she whispered, tears finally spilling over, "was delivered to me with kindness, yet it shattered my world. Both physicians agreed that precious time had been lost, irretrievable. My condition has now passed beyond the reach of their art. For over two years, I have suffered from a silent, insidious heart disease that has slowly broken me down without alarming symptoms. I may live for months, or I may die before tomorrow dawns; the doctors dare not be more precise. It would be cruel to say I have not suffered miserably since learning the truth, but I am more resigned now. I am doing my best to settle my worldly affairs. My greatest fear is that Rachel should learn of this. If she knew, she would blame herself for my decline, attributing it to worry over the Diamond, when it is entirely her mother's fault. Both doctors agree the illness began two, perhaps three years ago. I trust you will keep this secret, Drusilla—I see genuine sorrow and sympathy in your face."

Sorrow and sympathy! Oh, what pagan emotions to expect from a Christian Englishwoman so firmly anchored in her faith!

Yet, a torrent of devout gratitude surged through me as she concluded her tragic tale. Here was a field of usefulness opened before me! Here was a beloved relative, a dying soul, utterly unprepared for the Great Change, yet providentially led to reveal her plight to *me*. How could I describe the joy that flooded my heart, remembering that the clerical friends I could call upon were counted not by ones or twos, but by tens and twenties? I drew my aunt into my arms, my overflowing tenderness demanding more than mere words. "Oh!" I cried fervently, "the indescribable interest you inspire in me! Oh, the good I mean to do you before we part!"

After offering earnest warnings, I presented her with a choice of three devoted friends, all engaged in works of mercy within her neighborhood, all inexhaustible in exhortation, all ready to serve at my command. Alas, the response was discouraging. Lady Verinder looked puzzled and frightened, rejecting my offers with the worldly objection that she was too weak to face strangers. I yielded—for the moment. My extensive experience as a Reader and Visitor, guided by fourteen beloved clerical friends, taught me that this required preparation through scripture. I possessed a small library of works tailored to this emergency, designed to arouse, convince, prepare, enlighten, and fortify her. "You will read, dear, won't you?" I pleaded in my most winning tone. "You will read if I bring my own precious books? Turned down at the right passages, aunt. Marked in pencil where you must pause and ask, 'Does this apply to me?'" Even this simple appeal, so thoroughly heathenizing in its worldly influence, seemed to startle her. She replied, "I will do what I can, Drusilla, to please you," with a look of surprise that was both instructive and terrifying.

Not a moment was to be wasted. The clock on the mantelpiece indicated I had just enough time to rush home, select a dozen essential readings, and return to witness the signing of Lady Verinder's Will. Promising to be back by five o'clock, I left the house on my mission of mercy.

When my own interests are not at stake, I am content to travel by omnibus. Yet, to demonstrate my devotion to my aunt's welfare, I committed the extravagance of hiring a cab.

I drove home, carefully selected and marked my first series of readings, and returned to Montagu Square with a carpet-bag containing a dozen volumes, a collection I firmly believed unrivaled in European literature. I paid the cabman his exact fare. He received it with an oath; instantly, I handed him a religious tract. If I had drawn a pistol to his head, this abandoned wretch could scarcely have shown greater consternation. He leaped onto his box, shouting profane exclamations of dismay, and drove off furiously. Fortunately, it was useless! I had sown the good seed, despite him, by throwing a second tract through the cab window.

The servant who answered the door was not the footman with the cap-ribbons, to my great relief, but the footman himself. He informed me that the doctor had called and remained with Lady Verinder. Mr. Bruff, the family solicitor, had arrived a minute prior and was waiting in the library. I was shown into the library to wait.

Mr. Bruff looked surprised to see me. As the family solicitor, we had met several times under Lady Verinder's roof. He was a man grown old and grizzled in the service of the world, a prophet of Law and Mammon in business, yet equally capable of reading a novel or tearing up a tract in his leisure.

"Have you come to stay here, Miss Clack?" he asked, glancing at my carpet-bag.

To reveal the contents of my precious bag to such a person would have invited an outburst of profanity. I lowered myself to his level and stated my business.

"My aunt has informed me she is about to sign her Will," I answered. "She has kindly asked me to be one of the witnesses."

"Aye? aye? Well, Miss Clack, you will do. You are over twenty-one, and you have not the slightest pecuniary interest in Lady Verinder's Will."

Not the slightest pecuniary interest! Oh, how thankful I felt! If my aunt, possessing thousands, had remembered poor me, to whom five pounds is a significant sum, and left me a comforting legacy, my enemies might have doubted the motive behind loading me with the choicest treasures of my library and drawing upon my failing resources for the prodigal expense of a cab.
