Not the cruellest scoffer could doubt my motives now. Much better as it was! Oh, surely, much better!

I was aroused from these consoling reflections by Mr. Bruff's voice. My meditative silence seemed to weigh upon his spirits, forcing him to talk against his will.

"Well, Miss Clack, what's the last news in the charitable circles? How is your friend Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, after the mauling he received from the rogues in Northumberland Street? Egad! They're telling a pretty story about that charitable gentleman at my club!"

I had overlooked his remark regarding my age and lack of financial interest, but the tone in which he alluded to Mr. Godfrey was too much for my forbearance. Bound to assert the innocence of my admirable friend whenever questioned, and feeling compelled to include a stinging rebuke for Mr. Bruff, I spoke up.

"I live very much out of the world," I said; "and I lack the advantage of belonging to a club. But I happen to know the story you allude to, and I also know that a viler falsehood than that story was never told."

"Yes, yes, Miss Clack—you believe in your friend. Natural enough. Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite won't find the world quite so easy to convince as a committee of charitable ladies. Appearances are dead against him. He was in the house when the Diamond was lost. And he was the first person to go to London afterwards. Those are ugly circumstances, viewed by the light of later events."

I should have corrected him immediately, for he was speaking in ignorance of testimony offered by the only person competent to speak from positive knowledge. Yet, the temptation to lead the lawyer artfully to his own discomfiture proved too strong. I asked what he meant by "later events," feigning the utmost innocence.

"By later events, Miss Clack, I mean events involving the Indians," Mr. Bruff continued, growing increasingly superior. "What do the Indians do the moment they are released from Frizinghall prison? They go straight to London and fix on Mr. Luker. Mr. Luker, alarmed for the safety of a 'valuable of great price' he possesses, lodges it privately in his bankers' strongroom. Wonderfully clever of him; but the Indians are just as clever. Suspecting the 'valuable' is being moved, they devise a bold plan to clear their doubts. Whom do they seize and search? Not only Mr. Luker, but Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite as well. Why? Mr. Ablewhite claims they acted on blind suspicion after seeing him speak to Mr. Luker. Absurd! Half-a-dozen others spoke to Mr. Luker that morning. Why weren't they followed home too? No! The plain inference is that Mr. Ablewhite had a private interest in the 'valuable,' just as Mr. Luker did. The Indians, uncertain which held the disposal, searched them both. Public opinion says this, Miss Clack. And public opinion, on this occasion, is not easily refuted."

He spoke with such worldly conceit that I, to my shame, could not resist leading him further before overwhelming him with the truth.

"I don't presume to argue with a clever lawyer like you," I said. "But is it fair to Mr. Ablewhite to ignore the opinion of the famous London police officer who investigated this case? Not the shadow of a suspicion rested upon anyone but Miss Verinder in the mind of Sergeant Cuff."

"Do you mean to tell me, Miss Clack, that you agree with the Sergeant?"

"I judge nobody, sir, and offer no opinion."

"And I commit both those enormities, ma'am. I judge the Sergeant to have been utterly wrong; and I offer the opinion that, if he had known Rachel's character as I do, he would have suspected everyone in the house but *her*. I admit she has faults—she is secret, self-willed, odd, and wild. But true as steel, high-minded, and generous to a fault. If the plainest evidence pointed one way, and only Rachel's word of honor pointed the other, I would take her word before the evidence, lawyer as I am! Strong language, Miss Clack; but I mean it."

"Would you object to illustrate your meaning, Mr. Bruff, so I may understand? Suppose you found Miss Verinder unaccountably interested in what has happened to Mr. Ablewhite and Mr. Luker? Suppose she asked strange questions about this scandal and displayed ungovernable agitation upon discovering its direction?"

"Suppose anything you please, Miss Clack; it wouldn't shake my belief in Rachel Verinder by a hair's-breadth."

"She is so absolutely to be relied upon as that?"

"So absolutely to be relied upon as that."

"Then permit me to inform you, Mr. Bruff, that Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite was in this house not two hours ago, and his entire innocence of any concern in the disappearance of the Moonstone was proclaimed by Miss Verinder herself in the strongest language I have ever heard from a young lady."

I enjoyed the triumph—the unholy triumph, I fear I must admit—of seeing Mr. Bruff utterly confounded. He started to his feet and stared at me in silence. I remained seated, undisturbed, and recounted the entire scene. "And what do you say about Mr. Ablewhite *now*?" I asked with utmost gentleness.

"If Rachel has testified to his innocence, Miss Clack, I don't scruple to say I believe in his innocence as firmly as you do. I have been misled by appearances, like the rest of the world; and I will make the best atonement I can by publicly contradicting the scandal assailing your friend wherever I meet it. In the meantime, allow me to congratulate you on the masterly manner in which you have opened the full fire of your batteries on me at the moment I least expected it. You would have done great things in my profession, ma'am, if you had happened to be a man."

With those words, he turned away, pacing irritably up and down the room.

I could plainly see that the new light I had thrown on the subject greatly surprised and disturbed him. Certain expressions dropped from his lips as he became absorbed in his own thoughts, suggesting the abominable view he had previously taken of the mystery. He had not scrupled to suspect Mr. Godfrey of stealing the Diamond and attribute Rachel's conduct to a generous resolve to conceal the crime. Now, on Miss Verinder's own authority—an unassailable authority in Mr. Bruff's estimation—that explanation was shown to be utterly wrong. The perplexity into which I had plunged this high legal authority was overwhelming. "What a case!" I heard him mutter to himself, stopping

The signing of the Will was a far more hurried affair than I had anticipated, conducted with a haste that bordered on indecency. Samuel, the footman, was summoned to serve as the second witness, and the pen was placed directly into my aunt's trembling hand. I felt a strong urge to offer a few appropriate words on this solemn occasion, yet Mr. Bruff's demeanor convinced me that silence was the wiser course while he remained in the room. Within two minutes, the ceremony was concluded, and Samuel, receiving no spiritual benefit from my potential sermon, descended the stairs once more.

Mr. Bruff folded the document and glanced my way, seemingly uncertain whether to leave me alone with my aunt. I carried my mission of mercy upon my heart and my bag of sacred publications ready on my lap. He might as well have expected to topple St. Paul's Cathedral by mere inspection as to move me. Yet, he possessed one merit, likely due to his worldly training: he was quick to perceive things. He appeared to receive from me nearly the same impression as the cabman had; he too uttered a profane exclamation and retreated in a violent hurry, leaving me mistress of the field.

Immediately we were alone, my aunt reclined upon the sofa and alluded, with an air of confusion, to the subject of her Will.

"I hope you won't think yourself neglected, Drusilla," she said. "I mean to give you your little legacy, my dear, with my own hand."

Here lay a golden opportunity! Seizing it instantly, I opened my bag and extracted the top publication. It was an early edition—number twenty-five—of the famous anonymous work, believed to be by the virtuous Miss Bellows, titled *The Serpent at Home*. Its design, perhaps unfamiliar to the worldly reader, is to demonstrate how the Evil One lurks within the most innocuous actions of daily life. The chapters best suited for female perusal include "Satan in the Hair Brush," "Satan behind the Looking Glass," "Satan under the Tea Table," and "Satan out of the Window," among others.

"Give your attention, dear aunt, to this precious book," I said, handing it to her open at a marked passage—a continuous burst of burning eloquence. The subject was "Satan among the Sofa Cushions."

Poor Lady Verinder, reclining thoughtlessly upon her own cushions, glanced at the volume and handed it back, looking more bewildered than before.

"I'm afraid, Drusilla," she replied, "I must wait until I am a little better before I can read that. The doctor—"

The moment she mentioned the physician's name, I knew the obstacle. In my past experience among my perishing fellow-creatures, the members of that notoriously infidel profession had repeatedly stepped between me and my mission of mercy, claiming under the miserable pretext that the patient required quiet and that the disturbing influence of all others was that of Miss Clack and her books. This same blinded materialism, working treacherously behind my back, now sought to rob me of the only right of property my poverty could claim: my right of spiritual inheritance in my dying aunt.

"The doctor tells me," my poor misguided relative continued, "that I am not well today. He forbids me to see any strangers; and he orders me, if I read at all, only the lightest and most amusing books. 'Do nothing, Lady Verinder, to weary your head, or to quicken your pulse'—those were his last words, Drusilla, when he left me."

There was no choice but to yield—for the moment only. Any open assertion of the infinitely superior importance of my ministry compared to that of the medical man would only provoke the doctor to exploit the human weakness of his patient and threaten to abandon the case. Fortunately, there are more ways than one of sowing the good seed, and few are better versed in these methods than myself.

"You might feel stronger, dear, in an hour or two," I said. "Or you might wake tomorrow morning with a sense of something wanting, and even this unpretending volume might supply it. Will you let me leave the book, aunt? The doctor can hardly object to that!"

I slipped it under the sofa cushions, half in and half out, close by her handkerchief and smelling-bottle. Every time her hand searched for either item, it would brush against the book; sooner or later, the book might touch her. Having made this arrangement, I deemed it wise to withdraw. "Let me leave you to repose, dear aunt; I will call again tomorrow." As I spoke, I glanced accidentally toward the window. It was filled with flowers in boxes and pots. Lady Verinder was extravagantly fond of these perishable treasures and had a habit of rising to admire and smell them. A new idea flashed across my mind. "Oh! may I take a flower?" I said, moving unsuspectedly to the window. Instead of removing a flower, I added one—in the shape of another book from my bag—which I left to surprise my aunt among the geraniums and roses. The happy thought followed: "Why not do the same for her, poor dear, in every other room she enters?" I immediately bid farewell and crossed the hall, slipping into the library. Samuel, coming up to let me out and assuming I had departed, went downstairs again. On the library table, I noticed two of the "amusing books" recommended by the infidel doctor. Instantly, I covered them from sight with two of my own precious publications. In the breakfast-room, I found my aunt's favorite canary singing in his cage; she always fed the bird herself. Some groundsel was strewn on a table standing directly beneath the cage. I placed a book among the weeds. In the drawing-room, I discovered further opportunities to empty my bag. My aunt's favorite musical pieces lay on the piano; I slipped two more books among the sheet music. I disposed of another in the back drawing-room, hidden under some unfinished embroidery known to be Lady Verinder's work. A third small room opened from the back drawing-room, separated by curtains rather than a door. My aunt's plain, old-fashioned fan rested on the chimney-piece. I opened my ninth book at a particularly special passage and inserted the fan as a marker to keep the place. Then the question arose: Should I ascend higher still, risking the insult of being discovered by the person with the cap-ribbons in the upper regions of the house? But oh, what of that? It is a poor Christian who fears insult. I went upstairs, prepared to bear anything.

The house was silent and solitary; it was likely the servants' tea-time. My aunt's room was at the front. The miniature of my late dear uncle, Sir John, hung on the wall opposite the bed. It seemed to smile at me, whispering, "Drusilla! deposit a book." Tables stood on either side of the bed. She was a bad sleeper and often desired—or thought she desired—many things at night. I placed a book near the matches on one side and another under the box of chocolate drops on the other. Whether she sought a light or a drop, there was a precious publication to meet her eye or hand, silently urging, "Come, try me! try me!" One book remained in my bag, and but one apartment was unexplored: the bathroom, opening from the bedroom. I peeped in, and the holy inner voice that never deceives whispered, "You have met her, Drusilla, everywhere else; meet her at the bath, and the work is done." I observed a dressing-gown thrown across a chair; it had a pocket, and into that pocket I placed my final book. Can words express my exquisite sense of duty fulfilled when I slipped out of the house, unsuspected by anyone, and found myself in the street with my empty bag under my arm? Oh, my worldly friends, pursuing the phantom of Pleasure through the guilty mazes of Dissipation, how easy it is to be happy if you will only be good!

That night, when I folded up my things and reflected on the true riches I had scattered with such a lavish hand from the top to the bottom of my wealthy aunt's house, I declared I felt as free from anxiety as a child. I was so light-hearted that I sang a verse of the Evening Hymn, and so buoyant that I fell asleep before I could sing another. Quite like a child again! Quite like a child again!

Thus I passed that blissful night. Rising the next morning, I felt how young I was! I might add how young I looked, if I were capable of dwelling on the concerns of my own perishable body. But I am not capable, and I add nothing.

Towards luncheon time—not for the sake of creature comforts, but for the certainty of finding my dear aunt—I donned my bonnet to go to Montagu Square. Just as I was ready, the maid at my lodgings looked in and announced, "Lady Verinder's servant, to see Miss Clack."

I occupied the parlour-floor during my residence in London. The front parlour served as my sitting-room. It was very small, very low-ceilinged, and poorly furnished—but oh, so neat! I looked into the passage to identify which of Lady Verinder's servants had requested me. It was the young footman, Samuel—a civil, fresh-colored person with a teachable look and an obliging manner. I had always felt a spiritual interest in Samuel and a desire to try him with a few serious words. On this occasion, I invited him into my sitting-room.

He entered carrying a large parcel under his arm. When he set it down, it appeared to frighten him. "My lady's love, Miss; and I was to say that you would find a letter inside." Having delivered this message, the fresh-faced young footman surprised me by looking as though he wished to flee.

I detained him to make a few kind inquiries. Could I see my aunt at Montagu Square? No; she had gone out for a drive. Miss Rachel had accompanied her, and Mr. Ablewhite had taken a seat in the carriage as well. Knowing how sadly dear Mr. Godfrey's charitable work was in arrears, I found it odd that he should be going out driving like an idle man. I stopped Samuel at the door to make further inquiries. Miss Rachel was attending a ball that night, and Mr. Ablewhite had arranged to come for coffee and accompany her. A morning concert was advertised for the following day, and Samuel was ordered to secure places for a large party, including one for Mr. Ablewhite. "All the tickets may be gone, Miss," said this innocent youth, "if I don't run and get them at once!" He ran as he spoke, and I found myself alone again, burdened with anxious thoughts.

We had a special meeting of the Mothers'-Small-Clothes-Conversion Society that night, summoned expressly to obtain Mr. Godfrey's advice and assistance. Instead of sustaining our sisterhood under an overwhelming flow of trousers that prostrated our little community, he had arranged to take coffee in Montagu Square and attend a ball afterward! The afternoon of the next day had been selected for the Festival of the British-Ladies'-Servants'-Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision Society. Instead of being present, the life and soul of that struggling institution, he had engaged to join a party of worldlings at a morning concert! I asked myself what this meant. Alas, it meant that our Christian Hero was to reveal himself to me in a new character and become associated in my mind with one of the most awful backslidings of modern times.

Returning to the history of the passing day, upon finding myself alone in my room, I naturally turned my attention to the parcel that had so strangely intimidated the fresh-colored young footman. Had my aunt sent me my promised legacy? Would it take the form of cast-off clothes, worn-out silver spoons, unfashionable jewelry, or something similar? Prepared to accept all and resent nothing, I opened the parcel—and what met my view? The twelve precious publications I had scattered through the house the previous day, all returned to me by the doctor's orders! Well might the youthful Samuel shrink when bringing his parcel into my room! Well might he run after performing his miserable errand! As for my aunt's letter, it simply amounted, poor soul, to this—that she dared not disobey her medical man.

What was to be done now? With my training and principles, I never had a moment's doubt.

Once self-supported by conscience and embarked on a career of manifest usefulness, the true Christian never yields. Neither public nor private influences produce the slightest effect on us once we have secured our mission. Taxation may be the consequence of a mission; riots may follow; wars may ensue: we proceed with our work, irrespective of every human consideration that moves the world outside us. We are above reason; we are beyond ridicule; we see with nobody's eyes, hear with nobody's ears, feel with nobody's hearts, but our own. Glorious, glorious privilege! And how is it earned? Ah, my friends, you may spare yourselves the useless inquiry! We are the only people who can earn it—for we are the only people who are always right.

In the case of my misguided aunt, the form that pious perseverance was next to take revealed itself plainly.

Preparation by clerical friends had failed due to Lady Verinder's own reluctance. Preparation by books had failed owing to the doctor's infidel obstinacy. So be it! What was the next step? The next thing to try was—Preparation by Little Notes. In other words, since the books themselves had been sent back, select extracts from them, copied by various hands and addressed as letters to my aunt, were to be sent by post or distributed throughout the house according to the plan I had adopted the previous day. As letters, they would excite no suspicion; as letters, they would be opened—and once opened, they might be read. Some I wrote myself: "Dear aunt, may I ask your attention to a few lines?" "Dear aunt, I was reading last night and chanced upon the following passage," etc. Other letters were written for me by my valued fellow-workers, the sisterhood at the Mothers'-Small-Clothes: "Dear madam, pardon the interest taken in you by a true, though humble, friend." "Dear madam, may a serious person surprise you by saying a few cheering words?" Using these

My hand slipped from the curtain rod, but let us not mistake the physical sensation for the mental state. My mind was not clouded by the sheer, dreadful embarrassment of my hiding place; rather, it was consumed by a fervent, sisterly concern for Mr. Godfrey. Why, I wondered, was he not at the concert? The thought of his absence was secondary to the terrifying words he had spoken moments ago. *He would do it today.* Those were the words, heavy with a terrible resolution. What could "it" be? Was he about to apostatize from his faith? Was he abandoning the Mothers'-Small-Clothes? Had the angelic smile vanished from the committee-room? Had the unrivaled eloquence of Exeter Hall come to a final, tragic halt? The mere possibility of such degradation in such a man was so overwhelming that I believed I would have burst forth from my concealment, begging the Ladies' Committees of London to force an explanation.

But then, another voice cut through the fabric of the room. It was loud, bold, and utterly devoid of feminine charm. It was the voice of Rachel Verinder.

"Why have you come up here, Godfrey?" she demanded. "Why didn't you go into the library?"

He laughed softly. "Miss Clack is in the library."

"Clack in the library!" She immediately settled onto the ottoman in the back drawing-room. "You are quite right, Godfrey. We had much better stop here."

A moment ago, I had been burning with feverish anxiety, unsure of my next move. Now, I was ice-cold, my doubts evaporating into absolute certainty. To reveal myself after overhearing this was impossible. To retreat anywhere but the fireplace was equally futile. A martyrdom awaited me. With a spirit reminiscent of a primitive Christian, I quietly adjusted the curtains to ensure I could both see and hear everything. Then, I prepared to endure my trial.

"Don't sit on the ottoman," the young lady continued. "Bring a chair, Godfrey. I like people to be opposite to me when I talk to them."

He took the nearest seat, a low chair that was many sizes too small for his towering frame. I had never seen his legs so exposed, so disadvantageous.

"Well?" she pressed. "What did you say to them?"

"Just what you said, dear Rachel, to me."

"That mamma was not at all well today? And that I didn't quite like leaving her to go to the concert?"

"Those were the exact words. They were grieved to lose you, but they quite understood. All sent their love and expressed a cheering belief that Lady Verinder's indisposition would soon pass."

"You don't think it's serious, do you, Godfrey?"

"Far from it! In a few days, I feel quite sure, all will be well again."

"I think so, too. I was frightened at first, but I do think so. It was very kind to go and make my excuses for me to people who are almost strangers to you. But why not have gone with them to the concert? It seems very hard that you should miss the music too."

"Don't say that, Rachel! If you only knew how much happier I am—here, with you!"

He clasped his hands and looked at her. In that posture, he turned slightly toward my hiding spot. I felt a wave of sickness wash over me when I noticed the exact same pathetic expression on his face—the one that had once charmed me as he pleaded for destitute millions at Exeter Hall.

"It's hard to get over one's bad habits, Godfrey. But do try to get over the habit of paying compliments—do, to please me."

"I never paid *you* a compliment, Rachel, in my life. Successful love may sometimes use the language of flattery, I admit. But hopeless love, dearest, always speaks the truth."

He drew his chair close and took her hand as he uttered those words. A momentary silence fell. He, who thrilled crowds, had undoubtedly thrilled her. I finally understood the words he had dropped when alone in the drawing-room: *I'll do it today.* Alas, the most rigid propriety could hardly fail to discover that he was doing it now.

"Have you forgotten what we agreed on, Godfrey, when you spoke to me in the country? We agreed that we were to be cousins, and nothing more."

"I break the agreement, Rachel, every time I see you."

"Then don't see me."

"Quite useless! I break the agreement every time I think of you. Oh, Rachel! how kindly you told me, only the other day, that my place in your estimation was a higher place than it had ever been yet! Am I mad to build the hopes I do on those dear words? Am I mad to dream of some future day when your heart may soften to me? Don't tell me so, if I am! Leave me my delusion, dearest! I must have *that* to cherish, and to comfort me, if I have nothing else!"

His voice trembled, and he pressed a white handkerchief to his eyes. Exeter Hall again! Nothing was needed to complete the parallel but the audience, the cheers, and the glass of water.

Even her obdurate nature was touched. I saw her lean closer, hearing a new tone of interest in her voice.

"Are you really sure, Godfrey, that you are so fond of me as that?"

"Sure! You know what I was, Rachel. Let me tell you what I am. I have lost every interest in life, but my interest in you. A transformation has come over me which I can't account for. Would you believe it? My charitable business is an unendurable nuisance to me; and when I see a Ladies' Committee now, I wish myself at the uttermost ends of the earth!"

If the annals of apostasy contain anything comparable to such a declaration, I can only say it is not producible from the stores of my reading. I thought of the Mothers'-Small-Clothes, the Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision, and the other societies too numerous to mention, all built up on this man as a tower of strength. I thought of the struggling Female Boards who drew the breath of their business-life through the nostrils of Mr. Godfrey—of that same Mr. Godfrey who had just reviled our good work as a "nuisance" and declared he wished to be elsewhere when found in our company! My young female friends will feel encouraged to persevere when I mention that it tried even my discipline before I could devour my righteous indignation in silence. Yet, I did not lose a syllable of the conversation.

"You have made your confession," she said. "I wonder whether it would cure you of your unhappy attachment to me, if I made mine?"

He started. I confess I started too. We both thought she was about to divulge the mystery of the Moonstone.

"Would you think, to look at me," she went on, "that I am the wretchedest girl living? It's true, Godfrey. What greater wretchedness can there be than to live degraded in your own estimation? That is my life now."

"My dear Rachel! it's impossible you can have any reason to speak of yourself in that way!"

"How do you know I have no reason?"

"Can you ask me the question! I know it, because I know *you*. Your silence, dearest, has never lowered you in the estimation of your true friends. The disappearance of your precious birthday gift may seem strange; your unexplained connection with that event may seem stranger still——"

"Are you speaking of the Moonstone, Godfrey?"

"I certainly thought that you referred——"

"I referred to nothing of the sort. I can hear of the loss of the Moonstone, let who will speak of it, without feeling degraded in my own estimation. If the story of the Diamond ever comes to light, it will be known that I accepted a dreadful responsibility; it will be known that I involved myself in the keeping of a miserable secret—but it will be as clear as the sun at noon-day that I did nothing mean! You have misunderstood me, Godfrey. It's my fault for not speaking more plainly. Cost me what it may, I will be plainer now. Suppose you were not in love with me? Suppose you were in love with some other woman?"

"Yes?"

"Suppose you discovered that woman to be utterly unworthy of you? Suppose you were quite convinced that it was a disgrace to you to waste another thought on her? Suppose the bare idea of ever marrying such a person made your face burn, only with thinking of it?"

"Yes?"

"And, suppose, in spite of all that—you couldn't tear her from your heart? Suppose the feeling she had roused in you (in the time when you believed in her) was not a feeling to be hidden? Suppose the love this wretch had inspired in you——? Oh, how can I find words to say it in! How can I make a *man* understand that a feeling which horrifies me at myself, can be a feeling that fascinates me at the same time? It's the breath of my life, Godfrey, and it's the poison that kills me—both in one! Go away! I must be out of my mind to talk as I am talking now. No! you mustn't leave me—you mustn't carry away a wrong impression. I must say what is to be said in my own defence. Mind this! *He* doesn't know—he never will know, what I have told *you*. I will never see him—I don't care what happens—I will never, never, never see him again! Don't ask me his name! Don't ask me any more! Let's change the subject. Are you doctor enough, Godfrey, to tell me why I feel as if I was stifling for want of breath? Is there a form of hysterics that bursts into words instead of tears? I dare say! What does it matter? You will get over any trouble I have caused you, easily enough now. I have dropped to my right place in your estimation, haven't I? Don't notice me! Don't pity me! For God's sake, go away!"

She turned round suddenly and beat her hands wildly on the back of the ottoman. Her head dropped on the cushions, and she burst out crying. Before I had time to feel shocked, I was horror-struck by an entirely unexpected proceeding on the part of Mr. Godfrey. Will it be credited that he fell on his knees at her feet?—on *both* knees, I solemnly declare! May modesty mention that he put his arms round her next? And may reluctant admiration acknowledge that he electrified her with two words?

"Noble creature!"

No more than that! But he said it with one of the bursts which have made his fame as a public speaker. She sat, either quite thunderstruck or quite fascinated—I don't know which—without even making an effort to push his arms back where they ought to have been. As for me, my sense of propriety was completely bewildered. I was so painfully uncertain whether it was my first duty to close my eyes or to stop my ears that I did neither. I attribute my ability to hold the curtain in the right position for looking and listening entirely to suppressed hysterics. In suppressed hysterics, it is admitted, even by doctors, that one must hold something.

"Yes," he said, with all the fascination of his evangelical voice and manner, "you are a noble creature! A woman who can speak the truth, for the truth's own sake—a woman who will sacrifice her pride, rather than sacrifice an honest man who loves her—is the most priceless of all treasures. When such a woman marries, if her husband only wins her esteem and regard, he wins enough to ennoble his whole life. You have spoken, dearest, of your place in my estimation. Judge what that place is—when I implore you on my knees, to let the cure of your poor wounded heart be my care. Rachel! will you honour me, will you bless me, by being my wife?"

By this time, I should certainly have decided to stop my ears if Rachel had not encouraged me to keep them open by answering him in the first sensible words I had ever heard fall from her lips.

"Godfrey!" she said, "you must be mad!"

"I never spoke more reasonably, dearest—in your interests, as well as in mine. Look for a moment to the future. Is your happiness to be sacrificed to a man who has never known how you feel towards him, and whom you are resolved never to see again? Is it not your duty to yourself to forget this ill-fated attachment? and is forgetfulness to be found in the life you are leading now? You have tried that life, and you are wearying of it already. Surround yourself with nobler interests than the wretched interests of the world. A heart that loves and honours you; a home whose peaceful claims and happy duties win gently on you day by day—try the consolation, Rachel, which is to be found *there!* I don't ask for your love—I will be content with your affection and regard. Let the rest be left, confidently left, to your husband's devotion, and to Time that heals even wounds as deep as yours."

She began to yield already. Oh, what a bringing-up she must have had! Oh, how differently I should have acted in her place!

"Don't tempt me, Godfrey," she said; "I am wretched enough and reckless enough as it is. Don't tempt me to be more wretched and more reckless still!"

"One question, Rachel. Have you any personal objection to me?"

"I! I always liked you. After what you have just said to me, I should be insensible indeed if I didn't respect and admire you as well."

"Do you know many wives, my dear Rachel, who respect and admire their husbands? And yet they and their husbands get on very well. How many brides go to the altar with hearts that would bear inspection by the men who take them there? And yet it doesn't end unhappily—somehow or other the nuptial establishment jogs on. The truth is, that women try marriage as a Refuge, far more numerously than they are willing to admit; and,

The envelope arrived with the stiffness of a formal decree, bearing the seal of Miss Clack's indignation. Inside, she presented her compliments to Mr. Franklin Blake, declaring herself utterly unequal to the task of expanding upon such a terrible event as Lady Verinder's death. Instead, she had appended to her manuscript a collection of "copious Extracts" from various publications, hoping these would sound like a trumpet blast in the ears of her kinsman, guiding him through the darkness of the mystery.

Mr. Blake's reply was brief and cool. He thanked her for the chapter but politely declined the extracts. He made no mention of his personal distaste for such sensationalist literature, choosing instead to state plainly that these additions were unnecessary for the purpose he had set out to achieve. He sought only the truth as he saw it, not the embellishments of others.

Undeterred, Miss Clack acknowledged the return of her materials with a mixture of hurt and resolve. She reminded him gently that she was a Christian and that it was impossible for him to truly offend her. Yet, she persisted in her deep interest in Mr. Blake, pledging to offer her extracts again should illness ever lay him low. For now, however, she asked a crucial question: before she began the final chapters of her narrative, might she be permitted to complete her work by incorporating the light shed by later discoveries regarding the Moonstone?

Mr. Blake found himself disappointed in having to disappoint her again. He reiterated the instructions he had given at the very start of her narrative. He requested that she limit her account strictly to her own individual experiences as recorded in her diary. Any subsequent discoveries were matters for other pens, those of actual witnesses who could speak from the scene, rather than the speculative musings of a correspondent.

Miss Clack wrote again, her tone sharpening with frustration. She was painfully conscious that she ought to feel "put down" in worldly terms, yet she had learned Perseverance in the School of Adversity. Her true object, she insisted, was to know if Mr. Blake prohibited the appearance of their correspondence within her narrative itself. Some explanation of the position in which his interference had placed her seemed due on grounds of common justice. She was anxious that her letters should be produced to speak for themselves, standing as evidence of her dedication.

Mr. Blake agreed to her proposal, but with a clear condition: he considered this intimation of consent as the formal closing of their correspondence. There would be no further letters.

Before the exchange ended, Miss Clack felt it her Christian duty to inform him that his last letter, evidently intended to wound her pride, had failed in its objective. She affectionately requested that he retire to the privacy of his own room and consider whether the training required to elevate a poor, weak woman above the reach of insult was not worthy of greater admiration than he currently felt. On receiving an acknowledgment of this sentiment, she solemnly pledged to return the complete series of her extracts.

To this final letter, no answer was received. Comment was needless.

The letters preceding this account suffice to explain why I shall pass over Lady Verinder's death with a simple statement of fact, concluding my fifth chapter.

Restricting myself strictly to my own personal experience, I must relate that a full month elapsed between my aunt's decease and the day Rachel Verinder and I met again. That reunion became the occasion for my spending several days under the same roof as her. During this visit, an event occurred concerning her engagement to Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite that demands special notice in these pages. Once this final, painful circumstance of the family history is disclosed, my task will be complete; for I shall then have recounted everything I know as an actual, though most unwilling, witness to these events.

Lady Verinder's remains were removed from London and interred in the small cemetery attached to the church in her own park. I was invited to the funeral alongside the rest of the family. Yet, it was impossible for me, given my religious convictions, to summon the strength to rise from the shock this death had caused me within such a brief span. Furthermore, I was informed that the rector of Frizinghall was to conduct the service. Having previously witnessed this clerical castaway playing whist at Lady Verinder's table, I doubted—even if I had been physically fit to travel—whether I would feel justified in attending the ceremony.

Lady Verinder's passing left her daughter under the guardianship of her brother-in-law, Mr. Ablewhite the elder. Appointed guardian by the will until his niece married or reached majority, Mr. Godfrey presumably informed his father of this new relationship. Within ten days of my aunt's death, the secret of the engagement was no longer a secret within the family circle. The pressing question for Mr. Ablewhite senior—another confirmed castaway!—became how to render himself and his authority most agreeable to the wealthy young lady destined to marry his son.

Rachel gave him some initial trouble regarding the choice of residence. The house in Montagu Square was tainted by the calamity of her mother's death; the Yorkshire estate was haunted by the scandal of the lost Moonstone. Her guardian's own home at Frizinghall faced neither objection, yet Rachel's presence there, following her recent bereavement, acted as a check on the gaieties of her cousins, the Miss Ablewhites. She herself requested that her visit be deferred to a more favorable opportunity. This culminated in a proposal from old Mr. Ablewhite to rent a furnished house at Brighton. His wife, an invalid daughter, and Rachel were to inhabit it together, expecting him to join them later in the season. They would see little society save a few old friends, and they would have his son Godfrey traveling back and forth by the London train, always at their disposal.

I describe this aimless wandering from one residence to another—this insatiable restlessness of body and appalling stagnation of soul—merely to arrive at the result. The event which, under Providence, proved the means of bringing Rachel Verinder and myself together again was nothing less than the hiring of the house at Brighton.

My Aunt Ablewhite is a large, silent woman of fair complexion, possessing one noteworthy trait in her character: from the hour of her birth, she has never been known to do anything for herself. She has gone through life accepting everyone's help and adopting everyone's opinions. A more hopeless person, spiritually, I have never met; there is absolutely, in this perplexing case, no obstructive material upon which to work. Aunt Ablewhite would listen to the Grand Lama of Tibet exactly as she listens to me, reflecting his views quite as readily as mine. She found the furnished house at Brighton by staying at a London hotel, composing herself on a sofa, and summoning her son. She discovered the necessary servants by breakfasting in bed one morning (still at the hotel) and granting her maid a holiday on the condition that the girl "would begin enjoying herself by fetching Miss Clack." I found her placidly fanning herself in her dressing-gown at eleven o'clock. "Drusilla, dear, I want some servants. You are so clever—please get them for me." I looked around the untidy room. The church bells were tolling for a weekday service, suggesting a word of affectionate remonstrance on my part. "Oh, aunt!" I said sadly. "Is _this_ worthy of a Christian Englishwoman? Is the passage from time to eternity to be made in _this_ manner?" My aunt answered, "I'll put on my gown, Drusilla, if you will be kind enough to help me." What was there to say after that? I have done wonders with murderesses; I have never advanced an inch with Aunt Ablewhite. "Where is the list," I asked, "of the servants whom you require?" My aunt shook her head; she lacked even the energy to keep the list. "Rachel has got it, dear," she said, "in the next room." I went into the next room and saw Rachel again for the first time since we parted in Montagu Square.

She looked pitiably small and thin in her deep mourning. If I attached any serious importance to such a perishable trifle as personal appearance, I might be inclined to add that hers was one of those unfortunate complexions which always suffer when not relieved by a border of white next the skin. But what are our complexions and our looks? Hindrances and pitfalls, dear girls, which beset us on our way to higher things! To my great surprise, Rachel rose when I entered the room and came forward to meet me with an outstretched hand.

"I am glad to see you," she said. "Drusilla, I have been in the habit of speaking very foolishly and very rudely to you on former occasions. I beg your pardon. I hope you will forgive me."

My face, I suppose, betrayed the astonishment I felt at this. She colored for a moment and then proceeded to explain herself.

"In my poor mother's lifetime," she continued, "her friends were not always my friends, too. Now that I have lost her, my heart turns for comfort to the people she liked. She liked you. Try to be friends with me, Drusilla, if you can."

To any rightly constituted mind, the motive thus acknowledged was simply shocking. Here in Christian England was a young woman in a state of bereavement, with so little idea of where to look for true comfort, that she actually expected to find it among her mother's friends! Here was a relative of mine, awakened to a sense of her shortcomings towards others, under the influence, not of conviction and duty, but of sentiment and impulse! Most deplorable to think of—but, still, suggestive of something hopeful to a person of my experience in plying the good work. There could be no harm, I thought, in ascertaining the extent of the change the loss of her mother had wrought in Rachel's character. I decided, as a useful test, to probe her on the subject of her marriage engagement to Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

Having first met her advances with all possible cordiality, I sat by her on the sofa at her own request. We discussed family affairs and future plans—always excepting that one future plan which was to end in her marriage. Try as I might to turn the conversation that way, she resolutely declined to take the hint. Any open reference to the question on my part would have been premature at this early stage of our reconciliation. Besides, I had discovered all I wanted to know. She was no longer the reckless, defiant creature whom I had heard and seen on the occasion of my martyrdom in Montagu Square. This was, of itself, enough to encourage me to take her future conversion in hand—beginning with a few words of earnest warning directed against the hasty formation of the marriage tie, and so getting on to higher things. Looking at her now with this new interest—and recalling the headlong suddenness with which she had met Mr. Godfrey's matrimonial views—I felt the solemn duty of interfering with a fervor which assured me that I should achieve no common results. Rapidity of proceeding was, as I believed, of importance in this case. I returned at once to the question of the servants wanted for the furnished house.

"Where is the list, dear?"

Rachel produced it.

"Cook, kitchen-maid, housemaid, and footman," I read. "My dear Rachel, these servants are only wanted for a term—the term during which your guardian has taken the house. We shall have great difficulty in finding persons of character and capacity to accept a temporary engagement of that sort if we try in London. Has the house in Brighton been found yet?"

"Yes. Godfrey has taken it; and persons in the house wanted him to hire them as servants. He thought they would hardly do for us, and came back having settled nothing."

"And you have no experience yourself in these matters, Rachel?"

"None whatever."

"And Aunt Ablewhite won't exert herself?"

"No, poor dear. Don't blame her, Drusilla. I think she is the only really happy woman I have ever met with."

"There are degrees in happiness, darling. We must have a little talk, some day, on that subject. In the meantime, I will undertake to meet the difficulty about the servants. Your aunt will write a letter to the people of the house——"

"She will sign a letter, if I write it for her, which comes to the same thing."

"Quite the same thing. I shall get the letter, and I will go to Brighton tomorrow."

"How extremely kind of you! We will join you as soon as you are ready for us. And you will stay, I hope, as _my_ guest. Brighton is so lively; you are sure to enjoy it."

In those words the invitation was given, and the glorious prospect of interference was opened before me.

It was then the middle of the week. By Saturday afternoon the house was ready for them. In that short interval I had sifted, not only the characters but the religious views as well, of all the disengaged servants who applied to me, and had succeeded in making a selection which my conscience approved. I also discovered and called upon two serious friends of mine, residents in the town, to whom I knew I could confide the pious object which had brought me to Brighton. One of them—a clerical friend—kindly helped me to take sittings for our little party in the church in which he himself ministered. The other—a single lady, like myself—placed the resources of her library (composed throughout of precious publications) entirely at my disposal. I borrowed half-a-dozen works, all carefully chosen with a view to Rachel. When these had been judiciously distributed in the various rooms she would likely occupy, I considered that my preparations were complete. Sound doctrine in the servants who waited on her; sound doctrine in the minister who preached to her; sound doctrine in the books that lay on her table—such was the treble welcome which my zeal had prepared for the motherless girl! A heavenly composure filled my mind on that Saturday afternoon as I sat at the window waiting for the arrival of my relatives. The giddy throng passed and repassed before my eyes. Alas! how many of them felt my exquisite sense of duty done? An awful question. Let us not pursue it.

Between six and seven the travelers arrived. To my indescribable surprise, they were escorted, not by Mr. Godfrey (as I had anticipated), but by the lawyer, Mr. Bruff.

"How do you do, Miss Clack?" he said. "I mean to stay this time."

That reference to the occasion on which I had obliged him to postpone his business to mine, when we were both visiting in Montagu Square, satisfied me that the old worldling had come to Brighton with some object of his own in view. I had prepared quite a little Paradise for my beloved Rachel—and here was the Serpent already!

"Godfrey was very much vexed, Drusilla, not to be able to come with us," said my Aunt Ablewhite. "There was something in the way which kept him in town. Mr. Bruff volunteered to take his place, and make a holiday of it till Monday morning. By-the-by, Mr. Bruff, I'm ordered to take exercise, and I don't like it. That," added Aunt Ablewhite, pointing out of the window to an invalid going by in a chair on wheels, drawn by a man, "is my idea of exercise. If it's air you want, you get it in your chair. And if it's fatigue you want, I am sure it's fatigue enough to look at the man."

Rachel stood silent, at a window by herself, with her eyes fixed on the sea.

"Tired, love?" I inquired.

"No. Only a little out of spirits," she answered. "I have often seen the sea, on our Yorkshire coast, with that light on it. And I was thinking, Drusilla, of the days that can never come again."

Mr. Bruff remained to dinner and stayed through the evening. The more I saw of him, the more certain I felt that he had some private end to serve in coming to Brighton. I watched him carefully. He maintained the same appearance of ease and talked the same godless gossip, hour after hour, until it was time to take leave. As he shook hands with Rachel, I caught his hard and cunning eyes resting on her for a moment with a peculiar interest and attention. She was plainly concerned in the object that he had in view. He said nothing out of the common to her or to anyone on leaving. He invited himself to luncheon the next day, and then he went away to his hotel.

It was impossible the next morning to get my Aunt Ablewhite out of her dressing-gown in time for church. Her invalid daughter (suffering from nothing, in my opinion, but incurable laziness, inherited from her mother) announced that she meant to remain in bed for the day. Rachel and I went alone together to church. A magnificent sermon was preached by my gifted friend on the heathen indifference of the world to the sinfulness of little sins. For more than an hour his eloquence (assisted by his glorious voice) thundered through the sacred edifice. I said to Rachel, when we came out, "Has it found its way to your heart, dear?" And she answered, "No; it has only made my head ache." This might have been

The rain lashed against the windowpanes of the Brighton villa, mirroring the turmoil within the drawing room. Mr. Godfrey stood before me, his magnificent head bowed low, his shoulders slumped in a posture of profound defeat.

"I have lost a beautiful girl, an excellent social position, and a handsome income," he began, his voice thick with a despair that seemed to drain the color from the room. "And I have submitted to it without a struggle. What can be the motive for such extraordinary conduct? My precious friend, there is no motive."

"No motive?" I repeated, my pen pausing over my journal.

"Let me appeal to your experience of children, Miss Clack," he continued, his eyes pleading with a mixture of confusion and shame. "A child pursues a certain course of conduct. You are struck by it, and you attempt to discern the reason. But the dear little thing is incapable of telling you its motive. You might as well ask the grass why it grows or the birds why they sing. Well! In this matter, I am like the child—like the grass—like the birds. I don't know why I proposed to Miss Verinder. I don't know why I have neglected my Ladies. I don't know why I have abandoned the Mothers' Small-Clothes. You ask the child, 'Why have you been naughty?' And the little angel puts its finger in its mouth and knows nothing. My case is exactly that, Miss Clack! I couldn't confess it to anyone else. I feel impelled to confess it to *you*!"

I began to recover my composure. A mental problem was presented here, and I am deeply interested in such puzzles, possessing, I believe, a certain skill in their resolution.

"Best of friends, exert your intellect and help me," he urged. "Tell me—why does a time come when these matrimonial proceedings begin to look like a dream? Why does it suddenly occur to me that my true happiness lies in helping my dear Ladies, in my modest rounds of useful work, in speaking my few earnest words when called upon by my Chairman? What do I want with a position? I have one! What do I want with an income? I can afford my bread, cheese, lodging, and two coats a year. What do I want with Miss Verinder? She has told me with her own lips that she loves another man, and that her only idea in marrying me is to try and put that other man out of her head. What a horrid union is this! Oh, dear me, what a horrid union! Such are my reflections on my way to Brighton. I approach Rachel with the feeling of a criminal awaiting his sentence. When I found she had changed her mind, when I heard her propose to break the engagement, I experienced a most overpowering sense of relief. A month ago I was pressing her rapturously to my bosom. An hour ago, the happiness of knowing I shall never press her again intoxicates me like strong liquor. The thing seems impossible—and yet there are the facts. I have lost a beautiful girl, an excellent social position, and a handsome income; and I have submitted to it without a struggle. Can *you* account for it, dear friend? It's quite beyond *me*."

His head sank further onto his breast, and he surrendered his own mental problem to despair.

I was deeply touched. To the spiritual physician, the case was now plain. It is no uncommon event in the experience of us all to see the possessors of exalted ability occasionally humbled to the level of the most poorly-gifted people around them. The object, no doubt, in the wise economy of Providence, is to remind greatness that it is mortal and that the power which confers it can also take it away. It was now easy to discern one of these salutary humiliations in Mr. Godfrey's deplorable proceedings, of which I had been the unseen witness. Equally easy was it to recognize the welcome reappearance of his finer nature in the horror with which he recoiled from the idea of a marriage with Rachel, and in the charming eagerness he showed to return to his Ladies and the Poor.

I put this view before him in a few simple, sisterly words. His joy was beautiful to see. He compared himself, as I spoke, to a lost man emerging from darkness into light. When I assured him of a loving reception at the Mothers' Small-Clothes, the grateful heart of our Christian Hero overflowed. He pressed my hands alternately to his lips. Overwhelmed by the exquisite triumph of having reclaimed him, I let him do what he liked with my hands. I closed my eyes. I felt my head, in an ecstasy of spiritual self-forgetfulness, sinking on his shoulder. In a moment more I should certainly have swooned away in his arms, but for an interruption from the outer world which brought me back to reality. A horrid rattling of knives and forks sounded outside the door, and the footman entered to lay the table for luncheon.

Mr. Godfrey started up, glancing at the clock on the mantelpiece.

"How time flies with *you*!" he exclaimed. "I shall barely catch the train."

I ventured to ask why he was in such a hurry to return to town. His answer reminded me of family difficulties yet to be reconciled and disagreements yet to come.

"I have heard from my father," he said. "Business obliges him to leave Frizinghall for London today, and he proposes coming here, either this evening or tomorrow. I must tell him what has happened between Rachel and me. His heart is set on our marriage—there will be great difficulty, I fear, in reconciling him to the breaking-off of the engagement. I must stop him, for all our sakes, from coming here until he *is* reconciled. Best and dearest of friends, we shall meet again!"

With those words, he hurried out. In equal haste, I ran upstairs to compose myself in my own room before meeting Aunt Ablewhite and Rachel at the luncheon table.

I am well aware that the all-profaning opinion of the world has charged Mr. Godfrey with having his own private reasons for releasing Rachel at the first opportunity she gave him. It has also reached my ears that his anxiety to recover my estimation has been attributed in certain quarters to a mercenary eagerness to make his peace, through me, with a venerable committee-woman at the Mothers' Small-Clothes, abundantly blessed with the goods of this world and a beloved friend of my own. I only notice these odious slanders to declare that they never had a moment's influence on my mind. In obedience to my instructions, I have exhibited the fluctuations in my opinion of our Christian Hero exactly as recorded in my diary. In justice to myself, let me add that, once reinstated in his place in my estimation, my gifted friend never lost that place again. I write with tears in my eyes, burning to say more. But no—I am cruelly limited to my actual experience of persons and things. In less than a month from the time of which I am now writing, events in the money-market (which diminished even my miserable little income) forced me into foreign exile, leaving me with nothing but a loving remembrance of Mr. Godfrey which the slander of the world has assailed, and assailed in vain.

Let me dry my eyes and return to my narrative.

I went downstairs to luncheon, naturally anxious to see how Rachel was affected by her release from her marriage engagement. It appeared to me—that I own I am a poor authority in such matters—that the recovery of her freedom had set her thinking again of that other man whom she loved, and that she was furious with herself for not being able to control a revulsion of feeling of which she was secretly ashamed. Who was the man? I had my suspicions—but it was needless to waste time in idle speculation. When I had converted her, she would, as a matter of course, have no concealments from me. I should hear all about the man; I should hear all about the Moonstone. If I had had no higher object in stirring her up to a sense of spiritual things, the motive of relieving her mind of its guilty secrets would have been enough to encourage me to proceed.

In the afternoon, Aunt Ablewhite took her exercise in an invalid chair, accompanied by Rachel. "I wish I could drag the chair," she broke out recklessly. "I wish I could fatigue myself till I was ready to drop."

She was in the same humor in the evening. I discovered in one of my friend's precious publications—the *Life, Letters, and Labours of Miss Jane Ann Stamper*, forty-fourth edition—passages which bore with a marvelous appropriateness on Rachel's present position. Upon my proposing to read them, she went to the piano. Conceive how little she must have known of serious people if she supposed that my patience was to be exhausted in that way! I kept Miss Jane Ann Stamper by me, waiting for events with the most unfaltering trust in the future.

Old Mr. Ablewhite never made his appearance that night. But I knew the importance which his worldly greed attached to his son's marriage with Miss Verinder, and I felt a positive conviction that we should see him the next day. With his interference, the storm on which I had counted would certainly come, and the salutary exhaustion of Rachel's resisting powers would as certainly follow. I am not ignorant that old Mr. Ablewhite has the reputation generally (especially among his inferiors) of being a remarkably good-natured man. According to my observation of him, he deserves his reputation as long as he has his own way, and not a moment longer.

The next day, exactly as I had foreseen, Aunt Ablewhite was as near to being astonished as her nature would permit by the sudden appearance of her husband. He had barely been a minute in the house before he was followed, to my astonishment this time, by an unexpected complication in the shape of Mr. Bruff.

I never remember feeling the presence of the lawyer to be more unwelcome than I felt it at that moment. He looked ready for anything in the way of an obstructive proceeding—capable even of keeping the peace with Rachel for one of the combatants!

"This is a pleasant surprise, sir," said Mr. Ablewhite, addressing himself with his deceptive cordiality to Mr. Bruff. "When I left your office yesterday, I didn't expect to have the honor of seeing you at Brighton today."

"I turned over our conversation in my mind after you had gone," replied Mr. Bruff. "And it occurred to me that I might perhaps be of some use on this occasion. I was just in time to catch the train, and I had no opportunity of discovering the carriage in which you were travelling."

Having given that explanation, he seated himself by Rachel. I retired modestly to a corner—with Miss Jane Ann Stamper on my lap, in case of emergency. My aunt sat at the window, placidly fanning herself as usual. Mr. Ablewhite stood up in the middle of the room, his bald head much pinker than I had ever seen it, and addressed himself in the most affectionate manner to his niece.

"Rachel, my dear," he said, "I have heard some very extraordinary news from Godfrey. And I am here to inquire about it. You have a sitting-room of your own in this house. Will you honor me by showing me the way to it?"

Rachel never moved. Whether she was determined to bring matters to a crisis or prompted by some private sign from Mr. Bruff, is more than I can tell. She declined doing old Mr. Ablewhite the honor of conducting him into her sitting-room.

"Whatever you wish to say to me," she answered, "can be said here—in the presence of my relatives, and in the presence" (she looked at Mr. Bruff) "of my mother's trusted old friend."

"Just as you please, my dear," said the amiable Mr. Ablewhite. He took a chair. The rest of them looked at his face—as if they expected it, after seventy years of worldly training, to speak the truth. I looked at the top of his bald head; having noticed on other occasions that the temper which was really in him had a habit of registering itself *there*.

"Some weeks ago," pursued the old gentleman, "my son informed me that Miss Verinder had done him the honor to engage herself to marry him. Is it possible, Rachel, that he can have misinterpreted—or presumed upon—what you really said to him?"

"Certainly not," she replied. "I did engage myself to marry him."

"Very frankly answered!" said Mr. Ablewhite. "And most satisfactory, my dear, so far. In respect to what happened some weeks since, Godfrey has made no mistake. The error is evidently in what he told me yesterday. I begin to see it now. You and he have had a lovers' quarrel—and my foolish son has interpreted it seriously. Ah! I should have known better than that at his age."

The fallen nature in Rachel—the mother Eve, so to speak—began to chafe at this.

"Pray let us understand each other, Mr. Ablewhite," she said. "Nothing in the least like a quarrel took place yesterday between your son and me. If he told you that I proposed breaking off our marriage engagement, and that he agreed on his side—he told you the truth."

The self-registering thermometer at the top of Mr. Ablewhite's bald head began to indicate a rise of temper. His face was more amiable than ever—but *there* was the pink at the top of his face, a shade deeper already!

"Come, come, my dear!" he said, in his most soothing manner, "now don't be angry, and don't be hard on poor Godfrey! He has evidently said some unfortunate thing. He was always clumsy from a child—but he means well, Rachel, he means well!"

"Mr. Ablewhite, I have either expressed myself very badly, or you are purposely mistaking me. Once for all, it is a settled thing between your son and myself that we remain, for the rest of our lives, cousins and nothing more. Is that plain enough?"

The tone in which she said those words made it impossible, even for old Mr. Ablewhite, to mistake her any longer. His thermometer went up another degree, and his voice when he next spoke

My fair friend, Miss Clack, having laid down her pen, there are two compelling reasons for me to take up the quill in my turn.

First, I stand in a unique position to shed light on certain shadows that have lingered over this affair. I was privy to the private reason behind Miss Verinder's sudden dissolution of her marriage engagement, and I was at the very bottom of it. Furthermore, I uncovered the hidden motive driving Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite to abandon his claim to his charming cousin.

Second, fate—or perhaps misfortune—cast me directly into the vortex of the Indian Diamond mystery at the very moment I am writing. I was honored to host an interview in my own office with an Oriental gentleman of distinguished bearing, who was none other than the chief of the three Indians. To this, I must add my subsequent meeting with the celebrated traveler, Mr. Murthwaite, and the critical conversation we held regarding the Moonstone, a dialogue that bears profound weight on the events to come. Thus, I lay claim to the narrative role I now occupy.

The chronology of the broken engagement demands precedence, so we shall begin there. Tracing the chain of events backward, I find it necessary to open this scene, though it may seem odd, at the bedside of my late client and friend, Sir John Verinder.

Sir John possessed a generous share of the harmless, amiable weaknesses common to mankind. Among these, relevant to our tale, was an invincible reluctance to confront the solemnity of making his will, provided he enjoyed his customary robust health. Lady Verinder exerted her influence to rouse him to a sense of duty, and I did likewise. He acknowledged the justice of our views but took no action until illness finally claimed him. Only then was I summoned to draft his testament. The instructions were the simplest I have ever received in my entire professional career.

Sir John was dozing when I entered. He roused himself at my approach. "How do you do, Mr. Bruff?" he said. "I sha'n't be very long about this. And then I'll go to sleep again." He watched with keen interest as I gathered pens, ink, and paper. "Are you ready?" he asked. I bowed, dipped my pen, and waited.

"I leave everything to my wife," Sir John declared simply. "That's all." Turning on his pillow, he settled back to rest.

I was compelled to interrupt his slumber. "Am I to understand," I asked, "that you leave the entirety of your property, of every description, absolutely to Lady Verinder?"

"Yes," said Sir John. "Only, *I* put it shorter. Why can't *you* put it shorter and let me sleep? Everything to my wife. That's my Will."

His estate comprised land and money. In most cases, I would have urged reconsideration, but regarding Sir John, I knew Lady Verinder to be not only worthy of such unreserved trust but also uniquely capable of administering it—a rare talent among women. Within ten minutes, the Will was drawn, executed, and Sir John resumed his nap.

Lady Verinder fully justified her husband's confidence. In the early days of her widowhood, she called upon me to make her own Will. Her perspective was so sound and sensible that I required no advice beyond shaping her instructions into legal form. Before Sir John had been in his grave a fortnight, the future of his daughter was secured with wisdom and affection.

This Will remained safely stored in my fireproof box for many years. It was not until the summer of 1848, under melancholy circumstances, that I revisited it. The doctors had pronounced a sentence of death upon poor Lady Verinder. As the first to be informed, I found her anxious to review her Will once more.

The provisions for her daughter were immutable. However, her wishes regarding minor legacies to other relatives had shifted over time, necessitating several Codicils. To avoid confusion and repetition, I obtained her permission to draft a second Will incorporating these changes. As Miss Clack, who witnessed the signing, noted, the second Will was identical to the first regarding Rachel Verinder's financial interests. The only alterations concerned the appointment of a guardian, made under my advice. Upon Lady Verinder's passing, the document was submitted to my proctor for probate.

Approximately three weeks later, a warning sign emerged. While visiting my friend the proctor, I noticed an unusual air of agitation. "I have news for you," he said. "What do you think I heard at Doctors' Commons this morning? Lady Verinder's Will has been requested and examined!"

This was startling. The Will was indisputable, and no one seemed to have a legitimate interest in scrutinizing it. (For those unaware, the law permits anyone to examine a Will at Doctors' Commons for a shilling fee.)

"Did you hear who requested it?" I asked.

"Yes," he replied. "The clerk told me immediately. Mr. Smalley, of the firm of Skipp and Smalley, asked for it. Since it hadn't yet been copied into the Great Folio Registers, he was granted access to the original. He studied it carefully and made notes in his pocket-book. Do you have any idea why he wanted it?"

I shook my head. "I shall find out," I answered, "before I am a day older."

Returning to my office, I utilized a leverage I held over the firm of Skipp and Smalley. My common-law clerk was Mr. Smalley's brother, and for years, my office had served as a feeder for their practice. I intended to remind them of this patronage if necessary. I dispatched my clerk to Mr. Smalley's office with a formal inquiry regarding the examination of the Will.

Mr. Smalley returned promptly, accompanied by his brother. He admitted acting on client instructions but initially claimed professional confidentiality. We engaged in a sharp discussion; he was technically correct, but I was driven by anger and suspicion. I insisted on knowing more, refusing to accept any information as a secret, and claiming the freedom to use my own discretion. Pushing further, I threatened the risk of losing their client's business against the risk of losing mine. An act of tyranny, I admit, but effective nonetheless. Mr. Smalley chose his alternative without hesitation.

He smiled resignedly and revealed the name of his client: Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

That was sufficient for me.

Armed with this knowledge, I must now place the reader on equal footing with myself regarding Lady Verinder's Will. Let it be stated clearly: Rachel Verinder held only a life-interest in the property. Her mother's foresight and my experience had shielded her from the dangers of managing wealth, ensuring she could not touch the principal assets—land or money—nor could she raise capital against them. She would enjoy the London and Yorkshire residences and a handsome income, but nothing more.

Reflecting on this discovery left me perplexed. Hardly a week had passed since I learned of Miss Verinder's proposed marriage to Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite. I held her in the highest esteem and was grieved to see her cast herself upon a man I had long suspected as a smooth-tongued impostor. Now, his lawyer's findings confirmed my worst suspicions, revealing the mercenary nature of his intent. Yet, one must ask: would you judge so lightly if the matter involved your own sister?

My primary concern was whether Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite would honor his engagement after learning the truth of the Will. This depended entirely on his financial desperation. If he was not in dire need of funds, marrying Miss Verinder for her income would be worthwhile. However, if he required a large sum urgently, the Will would provide exactly that, effectively saving his daughter from a scoundrel.

If he needed the money, I could delay revealing the truth until after her mother's funeral. If he did not, remaining silent would be complicity in a lifetime of misery for her.

My doubts led me to the London hotel where Mrs. Ablewhite and Miss Verinder were staying. They informed me they were departing for Brighton the next day, but an unexpected obstacle prevented Mr. Godfrey from joining them. I immediately offered to take his place. Though I hesitated initially, thinking only of Rachel, the moment I saw her, my resolve hardened. Come what might, I would tell her the truth.

I found my opportunity during a walk the day after my arrival.

"May I speak to you," I asked, "about your marriage engagement?"

"Yes," she replied indifferently, "if you have nothing more interesting to talk about."

"Will you forgive an old friend and servant of your family, Miss Rachel, if I venture to ask whether your heart is truly set on this marriage?"

"I am marrying in despair, Mr. Bruff—on the chance of dropping into some sort of stagnant happiness which may reconcile me to my life."

Such strong language! It hinted at a deeper romance or turmoil beneath the surface. Yet, I declined to pursue side issues.

"Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite can hardly share your sentiments," I countered. "His heart must be set on the marriage at any rate?"

"He says so, and I suppose I ought to believe him. He would hardly marry me, after what I have confessed to him, unless he was fond of me."

Poor soul! The notion that a man might marry her solely for selfish, mercenary ends had never crossed her mind. The task ahead seemed far harder than anticipated.

"It sounds strangely," I continued, "in my old-fashioned ears..."

"What sounds strangely?" she asked.

"To hear you speak of your future husband as if you were not quite sure of the sincerity of his attachment. Are you conscious of any reason in your own mind for doubting him?"

Her keen perception detected a shift in my tone, warning her that I had ulterior motives. She stopped, withdrew her arm from mine, and searched my face intently.

"Mr. Bruff," she said, "you have something to tell me about Godfrey Ablewhite. Tell it."

Knowing her well, I took her at her word. I spoke the truth.

She put her arm back into mine, walking slowly beside me. I felt her hand tighten mechanically on my arm and saw her growing paler with every word, yet she remained silent. When I finished, she still said nothing. Her head drooped slightly, and she walked by my side, lost in her own thoughts, oblivious to her surroundings.

I made no attempt to disturb her. My experience taught me that she required time to process such revelations.

Generally, girls react to news by asking endless questions and running to a friend. Rachel Verinder's instinct was to retreat inward, to think it over alone. This self-reliance is a great virtue in men but often isolates women, exposing them to misunderstanding. I suspect I share the world's view on this, except in Rachel's case. Her self-dependence was a virtue in my eyes, born partly of my admiration for her and partly of my belief that she had done nothing unworthy regarding the Diamond. Even though it was shocking to associate her with an undiscovered theft, I was convinced she had taken no steps without first shutting herself away to deliberate.

We walked nearly a mile before she roused herself. Suddenly, she looked up, wearing a faint reflection of her happier smile—the most irresistible expression I have ever seen on a woman's face.

"I owe much already to your kindness," she said. "And I feel more deeply indebted to it now than ever. If you hear any rumors of my marriage when you return to London, contradict them at once, on my authority."

"Have you resolved to break your engagement?" I asked.

"Can you doubt it?" she returned proudly, "after what you have told me!"

"My dear Miss Rachel, you are very young—and you may find more difficulty in withdrawing from your present position than you anticipate. Have you no one—a lady, of course—with whom you could consult?"

"No one," she answered.

It distressed me greatly to hear this. She was so young and so lonely, yet she bore it so well! The impulse to help her overcame any sense of my own unfitness. I offered advice on the spur of the moment. I recommended that she invite Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite to a private interview and inform him that, to her certain knowledge, he had betrayed the mercenary nature of his motives. She was to declare their marriage impossible under such circumstances and challenge him: would he secure her silence by agreeing to part, or force her to expose his motives publicly? If he defended himself or denied the facts, she was to refer him to me.

Miss Verinder listened attentively. She then thanked me prettily but informed me it was impossible for her to follow this plan.

"May I ask," I said, "what objection you see to following it?"

She hesitated, then met me with a question. "Suppose you were asked to express your opinion of Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite's conduct?"

"Yes?"

"What would you call it?"

"I should call it the conduct of a meanly deceitful man."

"Mr. Bruff! I have believed in that man. I have promised to marry that man. How can I tell him he is mean, how can I tell him he has deceived me, how can I disgrace him in the eyes of the world after that? I have degraded myself by ever thinking of him as my husband. If I say what you tell me to say to him—I am owning that I have degraded myself to his face. I can't do that. After what has passed between us, I can't do that! The shame of it would be nothing to *him*. But the shame of it would be unendurable to *me*."

Here was another marked peculiarity of her character revealing itself without reserve. Her sensitive horror of anything mean blinded her to what she owed herself, hurrying her into a false position that might compromise her in the eyes of her friends. Up to this point, I had been diffident about the propriety of my advice. But after her words, I had no doubt it was the best course; I pressed it upon her again.

She only shook her head, repeating her objection. "He has been intimate enough with me to ask me to be his wife. He has stood high enough in my estimation to obtain my consent. I can't tell him to his face that he is the most contemptible of living creatures, after that!"

My next obligation is to lay before you the scant but critical details regarding the Moonstone—or, more accurately, the Indian conspiracy to steal it. Though brief, these facts hold a peculiar significance, foreshadowing events yet to unfold.

Approximately a week after Miss Verinder departed, a clerk entered my private study, holding a calling card. A gentleman awaited in the lobby, desiring an audience.

I examined the card. The name was foreign, now faded from my memory, but beneath it, a line in English remained vividly etched in my mind: "Recommended by Mr. Septimus Luker."

The sheer audacity of a man in Mr. Luker's station to recommend anyone to *me* stunned me into silence. I stared at the paper, doubting my own eyes. The clerk, noticing my confusion, offered his assessment of the man downstairs.

"He is a striking figure, sir. His complexion is so dark we initially mistook him for an Indian or someone of similar origin."

Connecting the clerk's observation with the inscription on the card, I suspected the Moonstone was the catalyst for both Mr. Luker's endorsement and this stranger's visit. To my clerk's astonishment, I immediately granted an audience.

To justify this lapse in professional decorum driven by mere curiosity, I need only remind the reader that no living soul in England possesses a connection to the romance of the Indian Diamond as intimate as mine. I was entrusted with Colonel Herncastle's escape plans and his periodic letters confirming his survival. I drafted his will, bequeathing the Moonstone to Miss Verinder, and convinced his executor to act on the hope of a valuable acquisition. Furthermore, I allayed Mr. Franklin Blake's scruples, persuading him to transport the jewel to Lady Verinder's residence.
