If anyone holds a prescriptive right to the Moonstone and its entanglements, it is undoubtedly me.

When my mysterious client was ushered in, I felt an immediate certainty: I stood before one of the three Indians—likely the chief. He wore European attire, yet his swarthy skin, lithe frame, and grave, graceful politeness betrayed his Oriental heritage to any discerning eye.

I gestured to a chair and requested the nature of his business.

After apologizing in flawless English for the intrusion, the Indian produced a small parcel wrapped in cloth of gold. Beneath this and a second layer of silk lay a tiny casket, exquisitely inlaid with jewels upon an ebony base.

"I have come, sir," he said, "to request a loan. I leave this casket as assurance that my debt shall be repaid."

I pointed to his card. "And you seek me at Mr. Luker's recommendation?"

The Indian bowed.

"May I inquire why Mr. Luker himself did not advance the funds you require?"

"Mr. Luker informed me, sir, that he possessed no money to lend."

"And thus, he directed you to me?"

The Indian indicated the card again. "It is written there."

Concise and to the point! I knew that if the Moonstone had been in my possession, this Oriental gentleman would have murdered me without hesitation. Yet, barring that lethal risk, I must testify that he was the epitome of a client. He might not have valued my life, but he respected my time—a courtesy none of my countrymen had ever shown me in my years of practice.

"I regret," I said, "that you were compelled to trouble me. Mr. Luker is mistaken in sending you here. While I am trusted with funds to lend, as any practitioner of my trade, I never lend to strangers, nor on such security as you have presented."

Rather than attempting to sway me, the Indian merely bowed again, rewrapped his casket in its two coverings without protest, and rose. This admirable assassin prepared to depart the instant I had answered.

"Will your condescension toward a stranger permit one final question before I leave?" he asked.

I bowed in return. One question at parting! The average in my experience was fifty.

"Supposing, sir, it had been possible—and customary—for *you* to lend me the money," he inquired, "in what timeframe would it have been possible—and customary—for *me* to repay it?"

"According to standard practice in this country," I replied, "you would be entitled to repay, if you chose, within one year from the date of the loan."

The Indian made his final, lowest bow, then suddenly and softly exited the room.

He moved with a momentary, noiseless, supple grace that startled me. Once composed, I reached a singular conclusion regarding this incomprehensible visitor. His face, voice, and manner had been under such perfect control during our meeting that they defied scrutiny. Yet, he had offered me a glimpse beneath his smooth exterior. He showed no sign of retaining what I said until I mentioned the customary period for earliest repayment. At that precise moment, he looked me straight in the face. I inferred that his final question served a special purpose, and my answer held a specific interest for him. The more I reflected on our exchange, the more shrewdly I suspected the casket and the loan request were mere formalities designed to pave the way for that parting inquiry.

I had confirmed this theory when a letter arrived from none other than Mr. Septimus Luker himself. He begged my pardon in sickeningly servile terms, promising an explanation if I would grant him a personal interview.

Curiosity, that fatal flaw, compelled me to honor him with an appointment for the following day.

Mr. Luker was, in every respect, an inferior creature to the Indian—vulgar, ugly, cringing, and prosy—rendering him unworthy of detailed report. The substance of his confession was as follows:

The day prior to the Indian's visit, Mr. Luker had been summoned by that accomplished gentleman. Despite the visitor's European disguise, Mr. Luker instantly recognized him as the chief of the three Indians who had previously harassed him, leaving him no choice but to consult a magistrate. From this revelation, he naturally concluded he was in the company of the men who had blindfolded, gagged, and robbed him of his banker's receipt. Overcome by terror, he believed his last hour had arrived.

On his side, the Indian maintained the guise of a perfect stranger. He produced the casket and made the identical application he later used with me. Seeking the swiftest means to dismiss him, Mr. Luker immediately declared he had no money. The Indian then asked for the safest person to approach for a loan. In his panic, Mr. Luker suggested a respectable solicitor. When asked to name one, Mr. Luker mentioned me—the sole reason being that, in his extreme fright, my name was the first that came to mind. "The perspiration poured off me like rain, sir," the wretched man concluded. "I knew not what I spoke. I hope you will overlook it, Mr. Bruff, in consideration of my having been truly frightened out of my wits."

I excused him graciously, eager to release myself from his presence. Before he departed, I detained him for one inquiry: Had the Indian said anything notable when leaving Mr. Luker's house?

"Yes!" Mr. Luker confirmed. "The Indian asked precisely the same question of me at parting that he asked of you, receiving, of course, the identical answer."

What did it signify? Mr. Luker's explanation offered no aid in solving the puzzle. My own ingenuity proved insufficient to grapple with the difficulty. That evening, I had a dinner engagement; I ascended the stairs in a mood far from genial, little suspecting that the path to my dressing room and the path to discovery were, on this occasion, one and the same.

Among the distinguished guests at the dinner, Mr. Murthwaite stood out as a figure of singular fascination. His return to England, following years of perilous wandering, had ignited a fervent curiosity within the social circle. He was hailed as a survivor of the extraordinary, a man who had navigated dangers that would have claimed lesser souls. Now, he declared his intent to revisit the very theaters of his exploits, venturing into territories previously untouched. This audacious disregard for personal safety, even in the face of recurring odds, reignited the waning adoration of his admirers. Indeed, the statistical probability suggested that his escape would be impossible this time; yet, the thrill of witnessing an eminent figure dine, only to potentially hear of his demise as the evening's headline, added a peculiar edge to the gathering.

Once the ladies retired, leaving the gentlemen to the private quarters of the dining room, the atmosphere shifted. With the moderating influence of female company removed, the conversation inevitably drifted toward politics—a staple of English discourse. However, regarding this ubiquitous national obsession, I found myself distinctly un-English. To me, political debate seemed the most tedious and futile of pursuits. Observing Mr. Murthwaite as the wine circulated, I noted a striking similarity in our sentiments. He, too, appeared to be engaging in the conversation with a dexterity born of politeness, while mentally preparing for a nap. It occurred to me that a strategic diversion might serve a dual purpose: it could rouse him from his lethargy and reveal his perspective on the latest developments concerning the Indian conspiracy, as detailed in my office.

"If I am not mistaken, Mr. Murthwaite," I initiated, "you were acquainted with the late Lady Verinder, and you followed the peculiar sequence of events surrounding the loss of the Moonstone with interest?"

The traveler, seemingly asleep, jolted instantly into alertness, asking abruptly who I was. I introduced myself, clarifying my professional ties to the Herncastle family and recalling my specific role regarding the Colonel and his diamond during those bygone days. Mr. Murthwaite adjusted his posture, positioning the rest of the company behind him, and focused his gaze entirely on me.

"Have you heard anything recently of the Indians?" he inquired.

"I have every reason to believe," I replied, "that one of them visited my office yesterday."

Mr. Murthwaite, a man not easily startled, was nonetheless staggered by this revelation. I recounted the encounter with Mr. Luker and my own experience in precise detail. "It is evident," I concluded, "that the Indian's final question held a specific objective. Why was he so eager to know the deadline by which a borrower must repay a loan?"

"Do you not perceive his motive, Mr. Bruff?"

"I am ashamed to admit my ignorance, Mr. Murthwaite—I truly cannot see it."

The traveler grew genuinely engaged in probing the depths of my apparent confusion. "Let me pose a question," he said. "Where does the conspiracy to seize the Moonstone currently stand?"

"I cannot say," I answered. "The Indian plot remains a mystery to me."

"The Indian plot, Mr. Bruff, is a mystery only because you have not scrutinized it closely. Shall we review it together, from the drafting of Colonel Herncastle's Will to the Indian's visit to your office? Given your position, it is of critical importance to Miss Verinder that you possess a clear understanding of this matter. Will you attempt to decipher the Indian's motive yourself, or shall I spare you the effort of inquiry?"

I recognized the practical utility of his proposal and selected the first option.

"Very good," Mr. Murthwaite agreed. "Let us begin with the ages of the three Indians. I can attest that they appear roughly the same age; you can determine if the man you saw was in the prime of life. I would wager he was not forty. Now, consider the time when Colonel Herncastle arrived in England and implemented his plan to preserve his life. Without counting the years, it is clear that the current Indians, at their age, are the successors of three other high-caste Brahmins who accompanied the Colonel to these shores. These men have inherited not only their predecessors' positions but also the organization they established here. While this organization may seem trivial by our standards—comprising access to funds, the services of shady English figures operating in London's fringes, and the secret sympathies of a few compatriots employed in the city—it is significant enough to warrant our attention. Now, I ask you: what event provided the Indians with their first opportunity to seize the Diamond?"

I understood the reference to my own experience.

"The first opportunity," I replied, "was undoubtedly created by Colonel Herncastle's death. They would naturally be aware of his passing."

"As a matter of course. His death opened the door. Until then, the Moonstone remained secure in the bank's strongroom. You drafted the Will bequeathing the jewel to his niece, and it was probated conventionally. As a lawyer, you understand the course the Indians would take under English counsel."

"They would procure a copy of the Will from Doctors' Commons," I stated.

"Precisely. One of those shadowy English intermediaries would obtain the copy for them. This document would inform them that the Moonstone was destined for Lady Verinder's daughter and that Mr. Blake the Elder, or his appointee, would deliver it. Information regarding Lady Verinder and Mr. Blake would be easily acquired. The Indians' dilemma would be deciding whether to strike while the diamond was being moved from the bank or to wait until it reached Lady Verinder's home in Yorkshire. The latter path was obviously the safer one, explaining their appearance at Frizinghall disguised as jugglers. In London, their organization kept them informed; two agents tracked movements from Mr. Blake's house to the bank and gathered intelligence from servants. These measures revealed that Mr. Franklin Blake had visited the bank and was the sole individual intending to travel to Yorkshire. You recall what followed."

I remembered that Franklin Blake had detected a spy in the street, thereby delaying his arrival in Yorkshire by several hours. Thanks to the prudent advice of old Betteredge, he had secured the diamond in the bank at Frizinghall before the Indians could even approach the neighborhood. The logic held firm. Yet, a question lingered: knowing the precautions taken, why had the Indians made no attempt on Lady Verinder's house during the intervening weeks before Rachel's birthday? I mentioned my awareness of the boy, the drop of ink, and similar details, noting that any explanation relying on clairvoyance held no weight for me.

"Nor do I accept it," Mr. Murthwaite responded. "The so-called clairvoyance here is merely a romantic embellishment of the Indian character. It would be refreshing for them to cloak their weary and dangerous mission with a halo of the marvelous. Their boy is undoubtedly sensitive to mesmeric influence; under such influence, he likely reflected thoughts already present in the mind of the mesmerizer. I have tested clairvoyance myself and found its manifestations limited. The Indians do not investigate via this method; they regard their boy as a Seer of the invisible. In this marvel, they find renewed interest in their shared purpose. However, we need not concern ourselves with mesmerism or the unbelievable in this practical inquiry. My goal is to trace results back to natural causes through rational means. Am I satisfying you so far?"

"Not a doubt," I affirmed. "I await, however, with some anxiety, your rational explanation of the difficulty I just presented."

Mr. Murthwaite smiled. "It is the simplest difficulty of all," he said. "I admit your summary is correct. The Indians were unaware of Mr. Franklin Blake's actions with the Diamond, evidenced by their initial mistake on the first night of his arrival at his aunt's house."

"Their first mistake?" I repeated.

"Certainly! The error of allowing themselves to be surprised on the terrace by Gabriel Betteredge. Yet, they demonstrated merit by recognizing their false step. With ample time at their disposal, they refrained from approaching the house for weeks thereafter."

"Why, Mr. Murthwaite? That is precisely what puzzles me!"

"Because no Indian, Mr. Bruff, ever takes an unnecessary risk. The clause in Colonel Herncastle's Will informed them that the Moonstone would pass absolutely into Miss Verinder's possession on her birthday. Which was the safer course? To attack while the diamond was under the control of Mr. Franklin Blake, who had proven capable of suspicion and outwitting them? Or to wait until it was in the hands of a young girl who would innocently delight in wearing the magnificent jewel? Consider the Indians' own conduct as proof. They appeared at the house after waiting weeks, on Miss Verinder's birthday, and were rewarded by seeing the Moonstone nestled in her dress. When I heard the story of the Colonel and the Diamond later that evening, I was convinced of the risk Mr. Franklin Blake had incurred—they would certainly have attacked him had he not ridden back with others. I was equally certain of the greater risk awaiting Miss Verinder, leading me to recommend following the Colonel's plan: destroying the gem's identity by cutting it into separate stones. Had the extraordinary disappearance that night not thwarted my advice and defeated the Hindoo plot, subsequent action would have been paralyzed the next day by their imprisonment as rogues and vagabonds. You know this as well as I do. The first act of the conspiracy concludes there. Before proceeding to the second, have I resolved your difficulty to the satisfaction of a practical mind?"

It was impossible to deny that he had addressed my concern fairly, thanks to his superior knowledge of the Indian character and his lack of distraction by other legal matters since Colonel Herncastle's time.

"So far, so good," Mr. Murthwaite resumed. "The first chance the Indians had to seize the Diamond was lost on the day they were committed to the prison at Frizinghall. When did the second chance arise? As I can prove, it occurred while they were still in confinement."

He produced his pocket-book, opening it to a specific page. "I was staying with friends at Frizinghall at the time. A day or two before the Indians were released—on a Monday, I believe—the prison governor brought me a letter. It had been left for the Indians by Mrs. Macann, the landlady where they lodged, and delivered at her door the previous morning. The authorities noted the postmark 'Lambeth' and observed that, while the address was in correct English, its format was oddly inconsistent with standard mailing practices. Upon opening it, they found the contents written in a foreign language, which they correctly identified as Hindustani. Their request was for translation. I took copies of the original and my translation, which remain at your service."

He handed me the open book. The address, written in a single unpunctuated paragraph, read: "To the three Indian men living with the lady called Macann at Frizinghall in Yorkshire." Following the Hindoo characters was the English translation:

"In the name of the Regent of the Night, whose seat is on the Antelope, whose arms embrace the four corners of the earth.

"Brothers, turn your faces to the south, and come to me in the street of many noises, which leads down to the muddy river.

"The reason is this.

"My own eyes have seen it."

The letter ended without date or signature. I returned it to Mr. Murthwaite, admitting that this curious specimen of correspondence puzzled me.

"I can explain the first sentence," he said. "The Hindu mythology depicts the moon god as a four-armed deity seated on an antelope, titled the Regent of the Night. Thus, we have an indirect reference to the Moonstone. Now, observe the Indians' reaction. On the very day of their release, they immediately proceeded to the railway station and boarded the first train to London. We regretted at Frizinghall that their movements were not privately monitored. However, after Lady Verinder dismissed the police officer and halted inquiries into the diamond's loss, no one else dared intervene. Free to go to London, they did so. What was the next news of them, Mr. Bruff?"

"They were annoying Mr. Luker," I answered, "by loitering about his house in Lambeth."

"Did you read the report of Mr. Luker's application to the magistrate?"

"Yes."

"In his statement, he referred to a foreign workman he had recently dismissed on suspicion of attempted theft, whom he also distrusted for potential collusion with the Indians. The inference is plain, Mr. Bruff, regarding who wrote that puzzling letter and which of Mr. Luker's Oriental treasures the workman attempted to steal."

The inference was too obvious to require elaboration. I had never doubted that the Moonstone had found its way into Mr. Luker's hands at the time Mr. Murthwaite mentioned. My sole question had been how the Indians discovered this fact. This most difficult query now received its answer, as did the others. Feeling that I could trust Mr. Murthwaite to guide me through the labyrinth's final twists, I expressed my confidence, a concession he received graciously.

"You shall provide me with information in return before we proceed," he said. "Someone must have transported the Moonstone from Yorkshire to London. Someone must have raised money on it, or it would never have been in Mr. Luker's possession. Has anyone discovered who that person was?"

"None that I know of," I replied.

"There was a story about Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite. I am told he is an eminent philanthropist—a trait decidedly contrary to his character."

I heartily agreed, though I felt bound to inform him, without mentioning Miss Verinder's name, that Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite had been cleared of all suspicion by evidence beyond dispute.

"Very well," Mr. Murthwaite said calmly. "Let us leave it to time to clarify the matter. Meanwhile, Mr. Bruff, we must return to the Indians. Their journey to London ended in another defeat. The loss of their second chance is primarily attributable to the cunning and foresight of Mr. Luker, who does not occupy the pinnacle of the usurious profession without cause. By promptly dismissing the man in his employ, he deprived the Indians of the assistance their confederate would have provided. By swiftly transporting the Moonstone to his banker, he caught the conspirators off guard before they

Spring, 1849. I was wandering the East, having recently abandoned the itinerary I had meticulously planned and communicated to my lawyer and banker in London. This sudden shift necessitated sending a servant to retrieve my letters and funds from the English Consul in a city I was no longer visiting. He was to rejoin me at a designated time and place. Fate, however, intervened; an accident beyond his control delayed him. For a week, my party and I camped on the desert's edge, waiting. Finally, he appeared at my tent's entrance, bearing the money and letters.

"I fear I bring bad news, sir," he said, pointing to a letter marked with a mourning border and addressed in Mr. Bruff's handwriting.

Suspense is a torture I know well. That letter with the black border was the first I tore open. It announced my father's death and declared me heir to his vast fortune. With this wealth came heavy responsibilities, and Mr. Bruff urged me immediately to return to England.

By dawn, I was on my way home.

My old friend Betteredge's account of my departure paints a picture slightly exaggerated, in his own quaint fashion. He took my mistress's satirical jibes about my foreign education too seriously, convincing himself that I possessed genuine French, German, and Italian facets of character. My lively cousin merely teased about these traits, which never truly existed outside Betteredge's imagination. Yet, he was right about the core truth: I was heartbroken by Rachel's treatment and fled England in the raw agony of life's bitterest disappointment.

I went abroad, resolved that change and distance would aid my healing. I believed, and still believe, that separation does help a man; it forces the mind away from the exclusive contemplation of sorrow. I did not forget her, but the sting of memory dulled gradually as time, miles, and novelty interposed themselves between us.

Yet, as I turned homeward, the remedy that had worked so steadily began to falter. The closer I drew to the land she inhabited, the more irresistibly her influence reclaimed me. Upon leaving England, she was the last name I would utter; upon returning, she was the first I inquired about when I met Mr. Bruff again.

Of course, I was informed of everything that had transpired in my absence—the very events Betteredge had chronicled—with one crucial exception. Mr. Bruff felt no liberty to reveal the private motives that had driven Rachel and Godfrey Ablewhite to recall their mutual marriage promise. I spared him the embarrassment of questioning this delicate matter. It was sufficient relief, after the jealous pain of learning she had considered marrying Godfrey, to know that reflection had convinced her of her rashness and secured her release from the engagement.

Having heard the past, my inquiries naturally turned to the present: Who cared for her now? Where did she live?

She resided under the guardianship of the late Sir John Verinder's widow, Mrs. Merridew, appointed by her mother's executors. Reports stated they were getting along admirably, currently residing in Mrs. Merridew's house in Portland Place.

Within half an hour of receiving this news, I was on my way to Portland Place, lacking the courage to confess my haste to Mr. Bruff. The man who answered the door hesitated, unsure if Miss Verinder was home. I sent him upstairs with my card to settle the matter quickly. He returned with an impenetrable expression, informing me she was out.

I might have suspected others of deliberately denying me access, but it was impossible to suspect Rachel. I left word to call again at six.

At six, the servant repeated that Miss Verinder was not at home. Had she left a message? None. Had she even received my card? The servant apologized, insisting she had. The inference was undeniable: Rachel refused to see me.

Refused to be treated thus without cause, I demanded to know why. I sent my name to Mrs. Merridew, requesting an interview at her convenience. She agreed immediately, showing me into a comfortable sitting-room where I found a pleasant, elderly lady. She expressed great regret and surprise on my behalf but admitted she could offer no explanation or pressure Rachel on a matter of private feeling. Her polite patience was endless, yet it yielded nothing.

My final recourse was writing. My servant delivered a letter the next day with strict instructions to await a reply. The answer arrived in a single, stinging sentence: "Miss Verinder begs to decline entering into any correspondence with Mr. Franklin Blake."

Though fond of her, I felt the insult acutely. Before I could compose myself, Mr. Bruff entered on business matters. I dismissed his concerns instantly and laid the entire case before him. He proved as incapable of enlightening me as Mrs. Merridew. I asked if slander had been spoken against me. He knew of none. Had she mentioned me while under his roof? Never. Had she not asked, during my long absence, if I lived or died? No such question had ever passed her lips.

I produced from my pocket-book the letter Lady Verinder had written to me from Frizinghall the day I departed Yorkshire. I pointed out two specific sentences: *"The valuable assistance which you rendered to the inquiry after the lost jewel is still an unpardoned offence, in the present dreadful state of Rachel's mind. Moving blindfold in this matter, you have added to the burden of anxiety which she has had to bear, by innocently threatening her secret with discovery through your exertions."*

"Is it possible," I asked, "that the bitterness described there remains unchanged?"

Mr. Bruff looked deeply distressed. "If you insist on an answer," he said, "I can place no other interpretation on her conduct than that."

I rang the bell, ordering my servant to pack my portmanteau and fetch a railway guide. Mr. Bruff, astonished, asked what I intended.

"I am going to Yorkshire," I answered, "by the next train."

"For what purpose?" he asked.

"Mr. Bruff, the assistance I innocently gave regarding the Diamond was an unpardoned offence in Rachel's mind nearly a year ago, and it remains so. I will not accept that position! I am determined to uncover the secret of her silence toward her mother and her enmity toward *me*. If time, effort, and money can achieve it, I will lay my hand on the thief who stole the Moonstone!"

The worthy old gentleman attempted to remonstrate, to urge reason, to remind me of my duty. I was deaf to everything. No earthly consideration could shake my resolve.

"I shall resume the inquiry where I left off," I continued. "I will follow it step by step until I reach the present. There are missing links in the evidence as I left it, which Gabriel Betteredge can supply. To Gabriel Betteredge, I go!"

That evening, as the sun set, I stood once more on the well-remembered terrace, gazing at the peaceful old country house. The gardener was the first I saw in the deserted grounds. He had left Betteredge an hour prior, sunning himself in the customary corner of the back yard. I knew the routine well; I would seek him out.

Walking round the familiar paths, I peered through the open gate of the yard.

There he was—the dear old friend of the happy days that would never return. He sat in the old corner on the beehive chair, pipe in mouth, *Robinson Crusoe* on his lap, flanked by his two dog companions dozing on either side. From my vantage point, the last slanting rays of the sun cast my shadow forward. Either the dogs saw it or their keen scent detected my approach; they sprang up with a growl. Starting in his turn, the old man quieted them with a word, then shaded his failing eyes with his hand and looked inquiringly at the figure at the gate.

My own eyes filled with tears. I was forced to wait a moment before I could trust myself to speak.

"Betteredge!" I called out, gesturing to the familiar volume resting on his knees. "Has *Robinson Crusoe* informed you this evening that I might expect to see Franklin Blake?"

"By the Lord Harry, Mr. Franklin!" the old man cried, his voice trembling with delight. "That is precisely what *Robinson Crusoe* has done!"

With my assistance, he scrambled to his feet, standing for a moment to gaze back and forth between the book and me, seemingly unable to decide which of us had surprised him more. The verdict ultimately favored the book. Clutching it open in both hands, he stared at the pages with a look of unutterable anticipation, as if expecting Robinson Crusoe himself to stride out of the text to offer us a personal audience.

"Here is the passage, Mr. Franklin!" he exclaimed once he had regained his composure. "As I live by bread, sir, this is exactly what I was reading the moment before you arrived! Page one hundred and fifty-six reads: 'I stood like one Thunderstruck, or as if I had seen an Apparition.' If that does not mean 'Expect the sudden appearance of Mr. Franklin Blake,' then the English language holds no meaning!" Betteredge slammed the book shut, finally freeing one hand to grasp mine warmly.

I had naturally expected him to bombard me with questions. Yet, the hospitable impulse dominated the old servant's mind whenever a family member appeared, however unexpectedly, as a visitor.

"Walk in, Mr. Franklin," he said, bowing with his quaint, old-fashioned grace and opening the door behind him. "I shall inquire what brings you here afterward—I must first make you comfortable. Sad changes have occurred since you departed. The house is shuttered, and the servants have gone. Never mind that! I shall cook your dinner; the gardener's wife will prepare your bed; and if a bottle of our renowned Latour claret remains in the cellar, Mr. Franklin, it shall be poured down your throat. I bid you welcome, sir! I bid you a hearty welcome!" said the poor old fellow, battling bravely against the gloom of the deserted estate, receiving me with the sociable courtesy of a bygone era.

It pained me to disappoint him. Yet, this was Rachel's house now. Could I eat within its walls, or sleep upon its floors, after the events that unfolded in London? The most basic sense of self-respect—forbade me properly, strictly forbade me—to cross the threshold.

Taking Betteredge by the arm, I led him out into the garden. There was no alternative. I was compelled to tell him the truth. Caught between his devotion to Rachel and his affection for me, he was deeply perplexed and distressed by the turn of events. When he voiced his opinion, it came in his characteristic blunt manner, seasoned with the most positive philosophy I had ever encountered—the philosophy of the Betteredge school.

"Miss Rachel has her faults—I have never denied it," he began. "And riding the high horse, now and then, is one of them. She has been attempting to ride over *you*—and you have endured it. Lord, Mr. Franklin, do you not know women by this time better than that? You have heard me speak of the late Mrs. Betteredge?"

I had heard him speak of the late Mrs. Betteredge quite often—always presenting her as his undeniable proof of the innate frailty and perversity of the fair sex. In that role, he exhibited her now.

"Very well, Mr. Franklin. Listen to me. Different women ride the high horse in different ways. The late Mrs. Betteredge took her exercise on that favorite female animal whenever I happened to deny her something she had set her heart upon. Just as surely as I returned home from work on such occasions, just as surely did my wife call up the kitchen stairs to declare that, following my brutal treatment, she lacked the heart to cook my dinner. I endured it for some time—just as you are enduring it now from Miss Rachel. Finally, my patience wore thin. I descended the stairs, and I took Mrs. Betteredge—affectionately, you understand—into my arms and carried her, holus-bolus, into the best parlour where she received her guests. I declared, 'That is the proper place for you, my dear,' and returned to the kitchen. I locked myself in, removed my coat, rolled up my shirt-sleeves, and cooked my own dinner. When it was ready, I served it with my best manner and enjoyed it heartily. I smoked my pipe and had a drop of grog afterward; then I cleared the table, washed the crockery, scrubbed the knives and forks, put everything away, and swept the hearth. When everything was as bright and clean as possible, I opened the door and let Mrs. Betteredge in. 'I have had my dinner, my dear,' I said; 'and I hope you will find the kitchen left exactly as your fondest wishes desire.' For the remainder of that woman's life, Mr. Franklin, I never had to cook my dinner again! Moral: You have endured Miss Rachel in London; do not endure her in Yorkshire. Return to the house!"

Quite unanswerable! I could only assure my good friend that even *his* powers of persuasion were, in this instance, wasted on me.

"It is a lovely evening," I said. "I shall walk to Frizinghall, stay at the hotel, and you must come tomorrow morning to breakfast with me. I have something to say to you."

Betteredge shook his head gravely.

"I am heartily sorry for this," he said. "I had hoped, Mr. Franklin, to hear that matters were smooth and pleasant again between you and Miss Rachel. If you must have your own way, sir," he continued after a moment's reflection, "there is no need to travel to Frizinghall tonight for a bed. It can be found nearer than that. There is Hotherstone's Farm, barely two miles from here. You could hardly object to *that* on Miss Rachel's account," the old man added slyly. "Hotherstone lives, Mr. Franklin, on his own freehold."

The place flashed into my memory the moment Betteredge mentioned it. The farmhouse stood in a sheltered inland valley, on the banks of the prettiest stream in that part of Yorkshire; the farmer possessed a spare bedroom and parlour, which he was accustomed to rent to artists, anglers, and tourists alike. A more agreeable place of abode during my stay in the neighborhood, I could not have wished to find.

"Are the rooms available?" I inquired.

"Mrs. Hotherstone herself, sir, asked for my good word to recommend the rooms yesterday."

"I shall take them, Betteredge, with the greatest pleasure."

We returned to the yard where I had left my traveling bag. After threading a stick through the handle and swinging the bag over his shoulder, Betteredge appeared to relapse into the bewilderment my sudden arrival had caused when I surprised him in the beehive chair. He gazed incredulously at the house, then wheeled about to look even more incredulously at me.

"I have lived a goodish long time in the world," said this best and dearest of all old servants, "but the like of this, I never expected to witness. There stands the house, and here stands Mr. Franklin Blake—and, Damme, if one of them is not turning his back on the other to go sleep in a lodging!"

He led the way out, wagging his head and growling ominously. "There is only one more miracle that *can* happen," he said over his shoulder. "The next thing you will do, Mr. Franklin, will be to repay me that seven-and-sixpence you borrowed when you were a boy."

This stroke of sarcasm lifted his spirits toward himself and toward me. We left the house and passed through the lodge gates. Once clear of the grounds, the duties of hospitality (in Betteredge's moral code) ceased, and the privileges of curiosity began.

He fell back to walk beside me. "A fine evening for a stroll, Mr. Franklin," he said, as if we had merely encountered each other by chance at that moment. "Supposing you had gone to the hotel at Frizinghall, sir?"

"Yes?"

"I should have had the honor of breakfasting with you tomorrow morning."

"Come and breakfast with me at Hotherstone's Farm instead."

"Much obliged for your kindness, Mr. Franklin. But it was not exactly breakfast I had in mind. I believe you mentioned having something to say to me? If it is no secret, sir," Betteredge said, suddenly abandoning the crooked path for the straight one, "I am burning to know what brought you down here in such a sudden manner."

"What brought me here before?" I asked.

"The Moonstone, Mr. Franklin. But what brings you now, sir?"

"The Moonstone again, Betteredge."

The old man stopped abruptly, looking at me in the grey twilight as if he suspected his own ears were deceiving him.

"If that is a joke, sir," he said, "I fear I am becoming somewhat dull in my old age. I do not take it."

"It is no joke," I answered. "I have returned to resume the inquiry I abandoned when I left England. I have come here to do what no one has yet done—to discover who took the Diamond."

"Let the Diamond be, Mr. Franklin! Take my advice and let the Diamond be! That cursed Indian jewel has misled everyone who has approached it. Do not waste your money and your temper—in the prime of your life, sir—by meddling with the Moonstone. How can *you* possibly succeed (saving your presence), when Sergeant Cuff himself made a mess of it? Sergeant Cuff!" Betteredge repeated, shaking his forefinger at me sternly. "The greatest policeman in England!"

"My mind is made up, my old friend. Even Sergeant Cuff does not daunt me. By the way, I may wish to speak to him sooner or later. Have you heard anything of him recently?"

"The Sergeant will not assist you, Mr. Franklin."

"Why not?"

"There has been an event, sir, in police circles since you departed. The great Cuff has retired from business. He has acquired a small cottage at Dorking and is buried up to his eyes in the cultivation of roses. I have it in his own handwriting, Mr. Franklin. He has grown the white moss rose without budding it onto the dog-rose first. And Mr. Begbie the gardener is to go to Dorking to acknowledge that the Sergeant has finally bested him."

"It matters little," I said. "I must proceed without Sergeant Cuff's aid. And I must rely on you to begin."

It is likely I spoke somewhat carelessly.

At any rate, Betteredge seemed piqued by something in my reply. "You might trust to worse than me, Mr. Franklin—I can tell you that," he said sharply.

The tone of his retort, coupled with a certain disturbance I detected in his manner, suggested to me that he possessed information he hesitated to disclose.

"I expect you to help me," I said, "in gathering the fragments of evidence Sergeant Cuff has left behind. I know you can do that. Can you do no more?"

"What more can you expect from me, sir?" Betteredge asked with an appearance of utmost humility.

"I expect more—from what you said just now."

"Mere boasting, Mr. Franklin," the old man returned obstinately. "Some people are born boasters and never overcome it to their dying day. I am one of them."

There was only one approach to take with him. I appealed to his interest in Rachel and his affection for me.

"Betteredge, would you be glad to hear that Rachel and I are friends again?"

"I have served your family, sir, to mighty little purpose if you doubt it!"

"Do you remember how Rachel treated me before I left England?"

"As well as if it were yesterday! My lady herself wrote you a letter regarding it; and you were so kind as to show it to me. It stated that Miss Rachel was mortally offended with you for the part you played in attempting to recover her jewel. Neither my lady, nor you, nor anyone else could guess why.

"Quite true, Betteredge! And I return from my travels to find her still mortally offended with me. I knew last year that the Diamond lay at the bottom of it, and I know now that the Diamond lies at the bottom of it. I have tried to speak to her, and she will not see me. I have tried to write to her, and she will not answer me. How, in Heaven's name, am I to clear this matter up? The chance of investigating the loss of the Moonstone is the only chance of inquiry that Rachel herself has left me."

Those words evidently presented the case to him as he had not seen it before. He asked a question that satisfied me I had shaken him.

"There is no ill-feeling on your side in this, Mr. Franklin—is there?"

"There was some anger," I answered, "when I left London. But that is entirely worn out now. I wish to bring Rachel to an understanding with me—and I desire nothing more."

"You feel no fear, sir—supposing you make discoveries—in regard to what you may uncover about Miss Rachel?"

I understood the jealous belief in his young mistress that prompted those words.

"I am as certain of her as you are," I answered. "The fullest disclosure of her secret will reveal nothing that can alter her place in your estimation, or in mine."

Betteredge's last lingering scruples vanished instantly.

"If I am doing wrong to help you, Mr. Franklin," he exclaimed, "all I can say is—I am as innocent of seeing it as the babe unborn! I can set you on the road to discovery if you can only proceed alone. You remember that poor girl of ours—Rosanna Spearman?"

"Of course!"

"You always believed she held some sort of confession regarding this matter of the Moonstone, which she wished to make to you?"

"I certainly could not account for her strange conduct in any other way."

"You may set that doubt at rest, Mr. Franklin, whenever you please."

It was

My memory of Hotherstone's Farm is a blur, a hazy dream rather than a clear record. I recall a warm welcome and a supper so abundant it seemed designed to feed a small town, followed by a bedroom spotlessly clean, save for that archaic relic of our ancestors' foolishness: the feather bed. My sleep was fitful, plagued by the frantic striking of matches and the flickering of a single candle, until the dawn brought a profound sense of relief.

That evening, I had arranged with Betteredge to meet him on our journey to Cobb's Hole, whenever my impatience to secure Rosanna's letter dictated. Driven by this urgency, I skipped breakfast, clutching a crust of bread, and set out, half-expecting to find the faithful servant still in bed. Instead, I found him ready and waiting, his walking stick gripped firmly in his hand, his excitement mirroring my own.

"How are you this morning, Betteredge?" I asked.

"Very poorly, sir," he replied.

"I'm sorry to hear that. What ails you?"

"I suffer from a new ailment, Mr. Franklin, one of my own invention. I won't alarm you, but you'll catch it before the day is done."

"The devil I shall!"

"Do you feel a burning heat in your stomach, sir? And a dull thumping at the crown of your head? Ah, not yet? It will seize you at Cobb's Hole. I call it the detective fever. I contracted it first while in the company of Sergeant Cuff."

"Aye, aye! And the cure, in this case, is to open Rosanna Spearman's letter, I suppose? Come, let us get it."

Though early, the fisherman's wife was already stirring in her kitchen. Upon my introduction by Betteredge, Mrs. Yolland performed a ritual of hospitality usually reserved for distinguished strangers. She placed a bottle of Dutch gin and two clean pipes upon the table and opened the conversation with, "What news from London, sir?"

Before I could answer this sweeping query, a figure emerged from a shadowed corner. A pale, wild-looking girl, her hair strikingly beautiful, approached with a fierce intensity in her eyes. She limped forward on a crutch to the table where I sat, regarding me with a mixture of fascination and horror that held me transfixed.

"Mr. Betteredge," she said, never breaking her gaze, "mention his name again, if you please."

"This gentleman's name," Betteredge answered, stressing the word *gentleman*, "is Mr. Franklin Blake."

The girl turned her back on me and fled the room. Mrs. Yolland offered some apologies for her daughter's eccentricity, which Betteredge likely translated into proper English, though I cannot be certain. My attention was entirely consumed by the sound of the crutch: *thump-thump* up the stairs, *thump-thump* across the floor above, *thump-thump* down again. Then, the apparition stood at the open door, a letter clutched in her hand, beckoning me outside.

I left my apologies trailing behind me and followed her strange procession, limping faster and faster down the beach slope. She led me behind some boats, away from the few villagers, and stopped, turning to face me for the first time.

"Stand there," she commanded. "I wish to look at you."

There was no ambiguity in her expression. I inspired in her the deepest feelings of abhorrence and disgust. I shall not claim vanity in saying no woman had ever looked at me thus; I will only assert the modest truth that no woman had ever allowed me to perceive it. There is a limit to how long a man can endure such scrutiny. I attempted to divert Limping Lucy's gaze to something less repulsive.

"I believe you have a letter to give me," I began. "Is it the one in your hand?"

"Say that again," was her only response.

I repeated the words, like a child reciting a lesson.

"No," the girl muttered, her eyes still mercilessly fixed on my face. "I cannot understand what she saw in his face. I cannot guess what she heard in his voice." She suddenly looked away, resting her weary head on her crutch. "Oh, my poor dear!" she whispered, her tone softening for the first time. "Oh, my lost darling! What could you see in this man?" She lifted her head fiercely, staring at me once more. "Can you eat and drink?"

I maintained my composure and answered, "Yes."

"Can you sleep?"

"Yes."

"When you see a poor girl in service, do you feel no remorse?"

"Certainly not. Why should I?"

She abruptly thrust the letter into my face.

"Take it!" she screamed furiously. "I never set eyes on you before. God Almighty forbid I should ever set eyes on you again."

With those final words, she limped away at full speed. The obvious conclusion was that she was mad. Accepting this, I turned my attention to the letter from Rosanna Spearman. The address read: "For Franklin Blake, Esq. To be given into his own hands (and not to be trusted to anyone else), by Lucy Yolland."

Breaking the seal, I found a letter inside, which in turn contained a slip of paper. I read the letter first:

"Sir,—If you are curious to know the meaning of my behaviour to you, whilst you were staying in the house of my mistress, Lady Verinder, do what you are told to do in the memorandum enclosed with this—and do it without any person being present to overlook you. Your humble servant, ROSANNA SPEARMAN."

Next, I examined the memorandum:

"Memorandum:—To go to the Shivering Sand at the turn of the tide. To walk out on the South Spit, until I get the South Spit Beacon, and the flagstaff at the Coast-guard station above Cobb's Hole in a line together. To lay down on the rocks, a stick, or any straight thing to guide my hand, exactly in the line of the beacon and the flagstaff. To take care, in doing this, that one end of the stick shall be at the edge of the rocks, on the side of them which overlooks the quicksand. To feel along the stick, among the seaweed (beginning from the end of the stick which points towards the beacon), for the Chain. To run my hand along the Chain, when found, until I come to the part of it which stretches over the edge of the rocks, down into the quicksand. And then, to pull the chain."

As I read the final words, underlined in the original, Betteredge's voice rang out behind me. The inventor of the detective fever had finally succumbed to it. "I can't stand it any longer, Mr. Franklin. What does her letter say? For mercy's sake, sir, tell us, what does her letter say?"

I handed him the documents. He read the letter with little interest, but the memorandum struck him deeply.

"The Sergeant said it!" cried Betteredge. "From start to finish, sir, the Sergeant said she had a memorandum of the hiding-place. And here it is! Lord save us, Mr. Franklin, here is the secret that puzzled everyone, from the great Cuff downwards, ready and waiting to show itself to *you!* It's ebb tide now, sir, as anyone can see. How long until the turn?"

He spotted a boy mending a net nearby. "Tammie Bright!" he shouted.

"I hear you!" the boy called back.

"When's the turn of the tide?"

"In an hour's time."

Checking our watches, Betteredge suggested we take the coastal route to the quicksand to ensure we had ample time. "Come along!" I agreed.

On the way, I asked Betteredge to refresh my memory regarding Rosanna during Sergeant Cuff's inquiry. With his help, the sequence of events became clear: Rosanna's trip to Frizinghall while the household thought her ill; her mysterious nocturnal activities with her door locked; her suspicious purchase of the japanned tin case and the two dog chains from Mrs. Yolland; and the Sergeant's firm belief that she had hidden something at the Shivering Sand, though he knew not what. These strange threads of the failed inquiry coalesced as we reached the quicksand and walked onto the low ledge of rocks known as the South Spit.

With Betteredge's assistance, I positioned myself to align the Beacon and the Coast-guard flagstaff. Following the memorandum, we laid my stick carefully on the uneven rocks. We checked our watches again.

Twenty minutes remained until the turn of the tide. I suggested waiting on the dry beach rather than the wet, slippery rocks. As I sat down, to my surprise, Betteredge prepared to depart.

"What are you going away for?" I asked.

"Look at the letter again, sir, and you will see."

A glance reminded me that I was instructed to make the discovery alone.

"It is hard enough for me to leave you at such a critical moment," Betteredge said, "but she met a dreadful death, poor soul, and I feel a duty to humour her wishes. Besides," he added confidentially, "there is nothing in the letter preventing you from revealing the secret later. I shall wait in the fir-plantation and signal when you are ready. Do not keep me waiting too long, sir. The detective fever is no easy companion under these circumstances."

With that warning, he left.

The interval, brief in clock time, stretched into an eternity of suspense. The habit of smoking became a precious comfort. I lit a cigar and sat on the beach slope. The sunlight poured down unclouded, making the very act of breathing a luxury. The lonely bay seemed cheerful, and the exposed surface of the quicksand glittered with a golden brightness, masking its treacherous nature beneath a deceptive smile. It was the finest day I had seen since returning to England.

The tide turned before my cigar burned down. I watched the preliminary heaving of the sand, followed by a terrifying shudder that seemed to ripple through the depths below. Discarding my cigar, I returned to the rocks.

Following the memorandum, I advanced more than halfway along the stick's line, encountering only rock edges. A few inches further, my patience was rewarded. In a narrow fissure, just within reach of my forefinger, I felt the chain. Attempting to follow it toward the quicksand, my progress was halted by a thick growth of seaweed that had entangled itself in the crack since Rosanna hid it there.

I could neither pull up the weed nor force my hand through it. Marking the spot indicated by the stick's end nearest the quicksand, I devised a new plan. I intended to "sound" immediately beneath the rocks, hoping to recover the chain's trace where it entered the sand. Taking up the stick, I knelt on the brink of the South Spit.

My face was mere feet from the quicksand's surface. Its intermittent, hideous shivering shook my nerves. A horrific thought seized me: that the dead woman might rise from the heaving sand to assist my search, pointing to the location. This dread turned me cold in the warm sun. I closed my eyes as the stick's tip first touched the water.

Instantly, my superstitious terror vanished, replaced by a throbbing excitement from head to toe. Sounding blindly on my first attempt, I struck true! The stick hit the chain.

Gripping the seaweed roots with my left hand, I lay over the brink and felt under the overhanging rock with my right. My fingers found the chain. I drew it up effortlessly, revealing the japanned tin case fastened to its end.

The water had rusted the chain so severely that I could not unfasten it from the hasp. Placing the case between my knees, I exerted my utmost strength to pry off the cover. Inside, a white substance filled the space. Reaching in, I found it was linen.

As I drew out the linen, a crumpled letter emerged. Checking the direction, I saw it bore my name. I pocketed the letter and removed the linen completely. It came out as a thick roll, molded to the shape of the case, perfectly preserved from injury by the sea.

Carrying the linen to the dry sand, I unrolled and smoothed it. There was no mistake; it was an article of dress, a nightgown. The upper side showed only folds and creases. Turning it over, I instantly discovered the smear of paint from Rachel's boudoir door!

My eyes fixed on the stain, and my mind leaped from the present to the past. The words of Sergeant Cuff echoed in my mind as if he stood beside me, pointing to the unanswerable inference drawn from that very mark.

"Find out whether there is any article of dress in this house with the stain of paint on it. Find out who that dress belongs to. Find out how the person can account for having been in the room, and smeared the paint between midnight and three in the morning. If the person can't satisfy you, you haven't far to look for the hand that took the Diamond."

Those words replayed in my memory with a mechanical, wearisome repetition. I was roused from a trance-like state—what felt like hours, though it was merely moments—by Betteredge's voice calling to me. His patience had finally snapped. He was visible between the sandhills, returning to the beach.

His reappearance grounded me, reminding me that my inquiry remained incomplete. I had found the paint stain on the nightgown. To whom did it belong?

My first impulse was to consult the letter in my pocket. But as I raised my hand, I realized there was a shorter path to the truth. The nightgown itself would reveal it; surely, it would bear the owner's name.

I picked up the gown and searched for the mark. I found it and read: MY OWN NAME.

The familiar letters told me the nightgown was mine. I looked up. The sun shone bright; the bay waters glittered; Betteredge advanced closer. I looked back at the letters. My own name. Plainly confronting me—my own name.

"If time, pains, and money can do it, I will

I possess no words to describe my own sensations.

My impression is that the shock inflicted upon me completely suspended my thinking and feeling powers. I certainly could not have known what I was doing when Betteredge joined me—for I have it on his authority that I laughed, when he asked what was the matter, and putting the nightgown into his hands, told him to read the riddle for himself.

Of what was said between us on the beach, I have not the faintest recollection. The first place in which I can now see myself again plainly is the plantation of firs. Betteredge and I are walking back together to the house; and Betteredge is telling me that I shall be able to face it, and he will be able to face it, when we have had a glass of grog.

The scene shifts from the plantation, to Betteredge's little sitting-room. My resolution not to enter Rachel's house is forgotten. I feel gratefully the coolness and shadiness and quiet of the room. I drink the grog (a perfectly new luxury to me, at that time of day), which my good old friend mixes with icy-cold water from the well. Under any other circumstances, the drink would simply stupefy me. As things are, it strings up my nerves. I begin to "face it," as Betteredge has predicted. And Betteredge, on his side, begins to "face it," too.

The picture which I am now presenting of myself, will, I suspect, be thought a very strange one, to say the least of it. Placed in a situation which may, I think, be described as entirely without parallel, what is the first proceeding to which I resort? Do I seclude myself from all human society? Do I set my mind to analyse the abominable impossibility which, nevertheless, confronts me as an undeniable fact? Do I hurry back to London by the first train to consult the highest authorities, and to set a searching inquiry on foot immediately? No. I accept the shelter of a house which I had resolved never to degrade myself by entering again; and I sit, tippling spirits and water in the company of an old servant, at ten o'clock in the morning. Is this the conduct that might have been expected from a man placed in my horrible position? I can only answer that the sight of old Betteredge's familiar face was an inexpressible comfort to me, and that the drinking of old Betteredge's grog helped me, as I believe nothing else would have helped me, in the state of complete bodily and mental prostration into which I had fallen. I can only offer this excuse for myself; and I can only admire that invariable preservation of dignity, and that strictly logical consistency of conduct which distinguish every man and woman who may read these lines, in every emergency of their lives from the cradle to the grave.

"Now, Mr. Franklin, there's one thing certain, at any rate," said Betteredge, throwing the nightgown down on the table between us, and pointing to it as if it was a living creature that could hear him. "He's a liar, to begin with."

This comforting view of the matter was not the view that presented itself to my mind.

"I am as innocent of all knowledge of having taken the Diamond as you are," I said. "But there is the witness against me! The paint on the nightgown, and the name on the nightgown are facts."

Betteredge lifted my glass, and put it persuasively into my hand.

"Facts?" he repeated. "Take a drop more grog, Mr. Franklin, and you'll get over the weakness of believing in facts! Foul play, sir!" he continued, dropping his voice confidentially. "That is how I read the riddle. Foul play somewhere—and you and I must find it out. Was there nothing else in the tin case, when you put your hand into it?"

The question instantly reminded me of the letter in my pocket. I took it out, and opened it. It was a letter of many pages, closely written. I looked impatiently for the signature at the end. "Rosanna Spearman."

As I read the name, a sudden remembrance illuminated my mind, and a sudden suspicion rose out of the new light.

"Stop!" I exclaimed. "Rosanna Spearman came to my aunt out of a reformatory? Rosanna Spearman had once been a thief?"

"There's no denying that, Mr. Franklin. What of it now, if you please?"

"What of it now? How do we know she may not have stolen the Diamond after all? How do we know she may not have smeared my nightgown purposely with the paint?"

Betteredge laid his hand on my arm, and stopped me before I could say any more.

"You will be cleared of this, Mr. Franklin, beyond all doubt. But I hope you won't be cleared in that way. See what the letter says, sir. In justice to the girl's memory, see what it says."

I felt the earnestness with which he spoke—felt it as a friendly rebuke to me. "You shall form your own judgment on her letter," I said. "I will read it out."

I began—and read these lines:

"Sir—I have something to own to you. A confession which means much misery, may sometimes be made in very few words. This confession can be made in three words. I love you."

The letter dropped from my hand. I looked at Betteredge. "In the name of Heaven," I said, "what does it mean?"

He seemed to shrink from answering the question.

"You and Limping Lucy were alone together this morning, sir," he said. "Did she say nothing about Rosanna Spearman?"

"She never even mentioned Rosanna Spearman's name."

"Please to go back to the letter, Mr. Franklin. I tell you plainly, I can't find it in my heart to distress you, after what you have had to bear already. Let her speak for herself, sir. And get on with your grog. For your own sake, get on with your grog."

I resumed the reading of the letter.

"It would be very disgraceful to me to tell you this, if I was a living woman when you read it. I shall be dead and gone, sir, when you find my letter. It is that which makes me bold. Not even my grave will be left to tell of me. I may own the truth—with the quicksand waiting to hide me when the words are written.

"Besides, you will find your nightgown in my hiding-place, with the smear of the paint on it; and you will want to know how it came to be hidden by me? and why I said nothing to you about it in my life-time? I have only one reason to give. I did these strange things, because I loved you.

"I won't trouble you with much about myself, or my life, before you came to my lady's house. Lady Verinder took me out of a reformatory. I had gone to the reformatory from the prison. I was put in the prison, because I was a thief. I was a thief, because my mother went on the streets when I was quite a little girl. My mother went on the streets, because the gentleman who was my father deserted her. There is no need to tell such a common story as this, at any length. It is told quite often enough in the newspapers.

"Lady Verinder was very kind to me, and Mr. Betteredge was very kind to me. Those two, and the matron at the reformatory, are the only good people I have ever met with in all my life. I might have got on in my place—not happily—but I might have got on, if you had not come visiting. I don't blame you, sir. It's my fault—all my fault.

"Do you remember when you came out on us from among the sandhills, that morning, looking for Mr. Betteredge? You were like a prince in a fairy-story. You were like a lover in a dream. You were the most adorable human creature I had ever seen. Something that felt like the happy life I had never led yet, leapt up in me at the instant I set eyes on you. Don't laugh at this if you can help it. Oh, if I could only make you feel how serious it is to me!

"I went back to the house, and wrote your name and mine in my work-box, and drew a true lovers' knot under them. Then, some devil—no, I ought to say some good angel—whispered to me, 'Go and look in the glass.' The glass told me—never mind what. I was too foolish to take the warning. I went on getting fonder and fonder of you, just as if I was a lady in your own rank of life, and the most beautiful creature your eyes ever rested on. I tried—oh, dear, how I tried—to get you to look at me. If you had known how I used to cry at night with the misery and the mortification of your never taking any notice of me, you would have pitied me perhaps, and have given me a look now and then to live on.

"It would have been no very kind look, perhaps, if you had known how I hated Miss Rachel. I believe I found out you were in love with her, before you knew it yourself. She used to give you roses to wear in your button-hole. Ah, Mr. Franklin, you wore my roses oftener than either you or she thought! The only comfort I had at that time, was putting my rose secretly in your glass of water, in place of hers—and then throwing her rose away.

"If she had been really as pretty as you thought her, I might have borne it better. No; I believe I should have been more spiteful against her still. Suppose you put Miss Rachel into a servant's dress, and took her ornaments off? I don't know what is the use of my writing in this way. It can't be denied that she had a bad figure; she was too thin. But who can tell what the men like? And young ladies may behave in a manner which would cost a servant her place. It's no business of mine. I can't expect you to read my letter, if I write it in this way. But it does stir one up to hear Miss Rachel called pretty, when one knows all the time that it's her dress does it, and her confidence in herself.

"Try not to lose patience with me, sir. I will get on as fast as I can to the time which is sure to interest you—the time when the Diamond was lost.

"But there is one thing which I have got it on my mind to tell you first.

"My life was not a very hard life to bear, while I was a thief. It was only when they had taught me at the reformatory to feel my own degradation, and to try for better things, that the days grew long and weary. Thoughts of the future forced themselves on me now. I felt the dreadful reproach that honest people—even the kindest of honest people—were to me in themselves. A heart-breaking sensation of loneliness kept with me, go where I might, and do what I might, and see what persons I might. It was my duty, I know, to try and get on with my fellow-servants in my new place. Somehow, I couldn't make friends with them. They looked (or I thought they looked) as if they suspected what I had been. I don't regret, far from it, having been roused to make the effort to be a reformed woman—but, indeed, indeed it was a weary life. You had come across it like a beam of sunshine at first—and then you too failed me. I was mad enough to love you; and I couldn't even attract your notice. There was great misery—there really was great misery in that.

"Now I am coming to what I wanted to tell you. In those days of bitterness, I went two or three times, when it was my turn to go out, to my favourite place—the beach above the Shivering Sand. And I said to myself, 'I think it will end here. When I can bear it no longer, I think it will end here.' You will understand, sir, that the place had laid a kind of spell on me before you came. I had always had a notion that something would happen to me at the quicksand. But I had never looked at it, with the thought of its being the means of my making away with myself, till the time came of which I am now writing. Then I did think that here was a place which would end all my troubles for me in a moment or two—and hide me for ever afterwards.

"This is all I have to say about myself, reckoning from the morning when I first saw you, to the morning when the alarm was raised in the house that the Diamond was lost.

"I was so aggravated by the foolish talk among the women servants, all wondering who was to be suspected first; and I was so angry with you (knowing no better at that time) for the pains you took in hunting for the jewel, and sending for the police, that I kept as much as possible away by myself, until later in the day, when the officer from Frizinghall came to the house.

"Mr. Seegrave began, as you may remember, by setting a guard on the women's bedrooms; and the women all followed him upstairs in a rage, to know what he meant by the insult he had put on them. I went with the rest, because if I had done anything different from the rest, Mr. Seegrave was the sort of man who would have suspected me directly. We found him in Miss Rachel's room. He told us he wouldn't have a lot of women there; and he pointed to the smear on the painted door, and said some of our petticoats had done the mischief, and sent us all downstairs again.

"After leaving Miss Rachel's room, I stopped a moment on one of the landings, by myself, to see if I had got the paint-stain by any chance on my gown. Penelope Betteredge (the only one of the women with whom I was on friendly terms) passed, and noticed what I was about.

"'You needn't trouble yourself, Rosanna,' she said. 'The paint on Miss Rachel's

Betteredge finished reciting the name of Mr. Candy's assistant with a shrug that suggested we had exhausted the well of trivialities for the day. He folded Rosanna Spearman's letter, his movements deliberate, and returned to the silence of the room.

I remained seated by the window, the afternoon light casting long, indifferent shadows across the floor. The memory of Ezra Jennings—the sheer, inexplicable weight of his presence in such a chaotic atmosphere—began to dissolve, retreating like mist before the sun. My mind, restless and sharp, forced itself back into the rigid channels of survival. I had to face the absurdity of my own position, the precipice upon which I stood, and chart a course forward.

My plan was clear, though born of desperation: return to London immediately, lay the entire case before Mr. Bruff, and, above all, secure an audience with Rachel. Whatever the cost, whatever the sacrifice, I had to see her. There was still an hour before the train departed. In that interim, I clung to a fragile hope: perhaps Betteredge, in his quiet scrutiny, had found a clue in the unread portions of Rosanna's letter that I needed before leaving the house where the diamond had vanished.

The letter concluded with a confession that chilled the blood more effectively than any accusation.

*"You have no need to be angry, Mr. Franklin,"* Rosanna wrote, her voice seemingly echoing through the paper. *"Even if I felt a little triumph knowing I held your prospects in my hands, anxieties soon returned. With Sergeant Cuff's obsession with the diamond, every corner of the house felt unsafe. How to hide the nightgown so thoroughly that not even he could find it? These were not easy questions."*

She described her desperate solution: she had worn the nightgown herself, a fleeting pleasure in possessing what he had coveted. But luck, or fate, had turned against her. When Sergeant Cuff demanded the washing book, she had delivered it in the lady's sitting-room. The Sergeant's gaze had been cold, his politeness suspicious. She had fled to the shrubbery, hoping for a final word, only to find him passing by, his eyes locking onto hers. He had seen her.

*"I had no choice but to return to my work,"* she continued, her tone trembling with suppressed agony. *"Just as I stepped across the path, you returned from the railway. You saw me—I am certain you did—and you turned away as if I carried a contagion, retreating into the house."*

A footnote from Franklin Blake interrupted the narrative, a bitter correction: *The writer is entirely mistaken. I never noticed her. Remembering my aunt might wish to see me, I altered my course and went inside.*

Rosanna's letter poured out her internal torment. She had retreated to the laundry, consumed by the "Shivering Sand"—the very spot where she had hidden the diamond. The temptation to end it all had surged, a dark whisper suggesting that enduring his indifference was harder than death itself. Yet, she had hesitated. Why? Because she loved him. Behind his back, she adored him; before his face, she was paralyzed by fear. She had tried to provoke anger to rouse her courage, but all she felt was humiliation. He was a gentleman; she was a maid with a crooked shoulder. He had taken the diamond to sell it, she reasoned, and she possessed the key to his salvation—a man in London who would buy it without asking questions.

*"Why didn't I stop you?"* she cried out in the text. *"Why didn't I tell you I could help you? I could have been useful!"*

Her resolve crumbled further when Penelope entered the room. Penelope, who knew the secret, spoke of Rachel's deteriorating temper and the likelihood of Franklin's departure. *"If they don't make it up before tomorrow, you will see Miss Rachel go one way, and Mr. Franklin another,"* Penelope had warned. *"He will never stay here after she leaves."*

This news struck Rosanna with the force of a physical blow. The prospect of Franklin leaving meant the loss of her only chance to speak to him, and consequently, the loss of her life. She realized then that she could not destroy the nightgown. It was her only proof of her loyalty, her only claim on his gratitude. Without it, how could she prove she hadn't stolen it? How could she win his trust if he suspected her of malice?

*"I determined to hide it,"* she wrote, *"in the place I knew best—the Shivering Sand."*

After the interrogation by Sergeant Cuff, which had confirmed his suspicions but failed to pinpoint her as the sole culprit, Rosanna had slipped away to Yolland's cottage. There, in Lucy Yolland's bedroom, she penned this letter, rolling the nightgown beneath her cloak. She planned to bury it in the sand, deep enough that no living creature could find it without her guidance.

*"If I miss my next opportunity,"* she pleaded, *"good-bye to the world... Good-bye to life, which nothing but a little kindness from you can ever make pleasurable to me again."*

She begged him to find her ghost, if she died, and speak kindly of her, just as he did to Rachel. She hoped to tear up the letter in joy if they reconciled, but feared the silence of the grave if they did not.

Betteredge finished reading in absolute silence. He placed the letter back in its envelope, his head bowed, eyes fixed on the floorboards.

"Betteredge," I asked, breaking the heavy quiet, "is there any hint to guide me at the end of the letter?"

He looked up slowly, a heavy sigh escaping his lips. "There is nothing to guide you, Mr. Franklin. If you take my advice, keep the letter in the cover until these anxieties have passed. It will sorely distress you whenever you read it. Do not read it now."

I tucked the letter safely into my pocket-book.

Looking back at the narrative of the previous weeks, the tragedy of Rosanna's fate becomes starkly clear. Twice she had made her last attempt to reach me. On the Friday night, at the billiard table, her manner suggested she was about to confess her knowledge of the diamond's disappearance. For her own sake, I had deliberately averted my gaze, staring at the balls rather than her, sending her away wounded and heartbroken.

On Saturday, the day her departure seemed imminent, she sought me in the shrubbery again. This time, I was accompanied by Betteredge and Sergeant Cuff. In an effort to protect her, I had loudly declared to the Sergeant—and to her—that I felt "no interest whatever in Rosanna Spearman." Designed to warn her against seeking my private ear, those words had been her death knell. Cautioned of her danger, believing herself self-doomed, she had turned away, walking straight into the trap that would lead to the quicksand.

From that point, the chain of events leading to my discovery at the Shivering Sand had begun. The retrospect is now complete. I may leave the miserable story of Rosanna Spearman, a tale that still brings a pang of distress even at this distance, to focus on the living. I must move forward from the darkness of that suicide to the toilsome journey toward the light, attending to the interests of those who remain.

I walked to the railway station accompanied, as was customary, by Gabriel Betteredge. In my pocket lay the incriminating letter, and in a small bag, safely packed, the nightgown—both destined for the immediate scrutiny of Mr. Bruff before I retired that night.

We departed the house in silence. For the first time in my long acquaintance with him, I found old Betteredge in my company without a single word spoken to me. Having something urgent to say, I broke the quiet as soon as we cleared the lodge gates.

"Before I go to London," I began, "I have two questions to ask you. They concern myself, and I believe they will rather surprise you."

"If they serve to put that poor creature's letter out of my head, Mr. Franklin, they may do anything else they like with me. Please, begin surprising me, sir, as soon as you can."

"My first question, Betteredge, is this: Was I drunk on the night of Rachel's Birthday?"

"You drunk!" the old man exclaimed. "It is the great defect of your character, Mr. Franklin, that you only drink with your dinner and never touch a drop of liquor afterwards!"

"But the birthday was a special occasion. I might have abandoned my regular habits on that night of all others."

Betteredge considered for a moment.

"You did go out of your habits, sir," he said. "And I'll tell you how. You looked wretchedly ill—and we persuaded you to have a drop of brandy and water to cheer you up a little."

"I am not used to brandy and water. It is quite possible..."

"Wait a bit, Mr. Franklin. I knew you were not used, too. I poured you out half a wineglass-full of our fifty-year-old Cognac; and (more shame for me!) I drowned that noble liquor in nigh on a tumbler-full of cold water. A child couldn't have got drunk on it—let alone a grown man!"

I knew I could depend on his memory in matters of this kind. It was plainly impossible that I could have been intoxicated. I passed on to the second question.

"Before I was sent abroad, Betteredge, you saw a great deal of me when I was a boy? Now tell me plainly, do you remember anything strange of me, after I had gone to bed at night? Did you ever discover me walking in my sleep?"

Betteredge stopped, looked at me for a moment, nodded his head, and walked on again.

"I see your drift now, Mr. Franklin!" he said. "You're trying to account for how you got the paint on your nightgown, without knowing it yourself. It won't do, sir. You're miles away still from getting at the truth. Walk in your sleep? You never did such a thing in your life!"

Here again, I felt that Betteredge must be right. Neither at home nor abroad had my life ever been of the solitary sort. If I had been a sleep-walker, there were hundreds upon hundreds of people who must have discovered me, and who, in the interest of my own safety, would have warned me of the habit and taken precautions to restrain it.

Still, admitting all this, I clung—with an obstinacy which was surely natural and excusable under the circumstances—to one or other of the only two explanations that I could see which accounted for the unendurable position in which I then stood. Observing that I was not yet satisfied, Betteredge shrewdly adverted to certain later events in the history of the Moonstone; and scattered both my theories to the wind at once and for ever.

"Let's try it another way, sir," he said. "Keep your own opinion, and see how far it will take you towards finding out the truth. If we are to believe the nightgown—which I don't for one—you not only smeared off the paint from the door, without knowing it, but you also took the Diamond without knowing it. Is that right, so far?"

"Quite right. Go on."

"Very good, sir. We'll say you were drunk, or walking in your sleep, when you took the jewel. That accounts for the night and morning after the birthday. But how does it account for what has happened since that time? The Diamond has been taken to London, since that time. The Diamond has been pledged to Mr. Luker, since that time. Did you do those two things, without knowing it, too? Were you drunk when I saw you off in the pony-chaise on that Saturday evening? And did you walk in your sleep to Mr. Luker's, when the train had brought you to your journey's end? Excuse me for saying it, Mr. Franklin, but this business has so upset you that you're not fit yet to judge for yourself. The sooner you lay your head alongside Mr. Bruff's head, the sooner you will see your way out of the dead-lock that has got you now."

We reached the station with only a minute or two to spare.

I hurriedly gave Betteredge my address in London, so that he might write to me if necessary; promising, on my side, to inform him of any news which I might have to communicate. This done, and just as I was bidding him farewell, I happened to glance towards the book-and-newspaper stall. There was Mr. Candy's remarkable-looking assistant again, speaking to the keeper of the stall! Our eyes met at the same moment. Ezra Jennings took off his hat to me. I returned the salute, and got into a carriage just as the train started. It was a relief to my mind, I suppose, to dwell on any subject which appeared to be, personally, of no sort of importance to me.
