At all events, I began the momentous journey back which was to take me to Mr. Bruff, wondering—absurdly enough, I admit—that I should have seen the man with the piebald hair twice in one day!

The hour at which I arrived in London precluded all hope of my finding Mr. Bruff at his place of business. I drove from the railway to his private residence at Hampstead, and disturbed the old lawyer dozing alone in his dining-room, with his favourite pug-dog on his lap and his bottle of wine at his elbow.

I shall best describe the effect which my story produced on the mind of Mr. Bruff by relating his proceedings when he had heard it to the end. He ordered lights and strong tea to be taken into his study; and he sent a message to the ladies of his family, forbidding them to disturb us on any pretence whatever. These preliminaries disposed of, he first examined the nightgown, and then devoted himself to the reading of Rosanna Spearman's letter.

The reading completed, Mr. Bruff addressed me for the first time since we had been shut up together in the seclusion of his own room.

"Franklin Blake," said the old gentleman, "this is a very serious matter, in more respects than one. In my opinion, it concerns Rachel quite as nearly as it concerns you. Her extraordinary conduct is no mystery now. She believes you have stolen the Diamond."

I had shrunk from reasoning my own way fairly to that revolting conclusion. But it had forced itself on me, nevertheless. My resolution to obtain a personal interview with Rachel rested really and truly on the ground just stated by Mr. Bruff.

"The first step to take in this investigation," the lawyer proceeded, "is to appeal to Rachel. She has been silent all this time, from motives which I (who know her character) can readily understand. It is impossible, after what has happened, to submit to that silence any longer. She must be persuaded to tell us, or she must be forced to tell us, on what grounds she bases her belief that you took the Moonstone. The chances are that the whole of this case, serious as it seems now, will tumble to pieces, if we can only break through Rachel's inveterate reserve and prevail upon her to speak out."

"That is a very comforting opinion for me," I said. "I own I should like to know—"

"You would like to know how I can justify it," interposed Mr. Bruff. "I can tell you in two minutes. Understand, in the first place, that I look at this matter from a lawyer's point of view. It's a question of evidence, with me. Very well. The evidence breaks down, at the outset, on one important point."

"On what point?"

"You shall hear. I admit that the mark of the name proves the nightgown to be yours. I admit that the mark of the paint proves the nightgown to have made the smear on Rachel's door. But what evidence is there to prove that you are the person who wore it on the night when the Diamond was lost?"

The objection struck me all the more forcibly that it reflected an objection which I had felt myself.

"As to this," pursued the lawyer, taking up Rosanna Spearman's confession, "I can understand that the letter is a distressing one to you. I can understand that you may hesitate to analyse it from a purely impartial point of view. But I am not in your position. I can bring my professional experience to bear on this document, just as I should bring it to bear on any other. Without alluding to the woman's career as a thief, I will merely remark that her letter proves her to have been an adept at deception, on her own showing; and I argue from that that I am justified in suspecting her of not having told the whole truth. I won't start any theory, at present, as to what she may or may not have done. I will only say that, if Rachel has suspected you on the evidence of the nightgown only, the chances are ninety-nine to a hundred that Rosanna Spearman was the person who showed it to her. In that case, there is the woman's letter, confessing that she was jealous of Rachel, confessing that she changed the roses, confessing that she saw a glimpse of hope for herself in the prospect of a quarrel between Rachel and you. I don't stop to ask who took the Moonstone (as a means to her end, Rosanna Spearman would have taken fifty Moonstones)—I only say that the disappearance of the jewel gave this reclaimed thief, who was in love with you, an opportunity of setting you and Rachel at variance for the rest of your lives. She had not decided on destroying herself then, remember; and, having the opportunity, I distinctly assert that it was in her character and in her position at the time to take it. What do you say to that?"

"Some such suspicion," I answered, "crossed my own mind as soon as I opened the letter."

"Exactly! And when you had read the letter, you pitied the poor creature and couldn't find it in your heart to suspect her. Does your credit, my dear sir—does your credit!"

"But suppose it turns out that I did wear the nightgown? What then?"

"I don't see how the fact can be proved," said Mr. Bruff. "But assuming the proof to be possible, the vindication of your innocence would be no easy matter. We won't go into that now. Let us wait and see whether Rachel hasn't suspected you on the evidence of the nightgown only."

"Good God, how coolly you talk of Rachel suspecting me!" I broke out. "What right has she to suspect me, on any evidence, of being a thief?"

"A very sensible question, my dear sir. Rather hotly put—but well worth considering for all that. What puzzles you puzzles me too. Search your memory and tell me this. Did anything happen while you were staying at the house—not, of course, to shake Rachel's belief in your honour—but, let us say, to shake her belief (no matter with how little reason) in your principles generally?"

I started, in ungovernable agitation, to my feet. The lawyer's question reminded me, for the first time since I had left England, that something _had_ happened.

In the eighth chapter of Betteredge's Narrative, an allusion will be found to the arrival of a foreigner and a stranger at my aunt's house, who came to see me on business. The nature of his business was this.

I had been foolish enough (being, as usual, straitened for money at the time) to accept a loan from the keeper of a small restaurant in Paris, to whom I was well known as a customer. A time was settled between us for paying the money back; and when the time came, I found it (as thousands of other honest men have found it) impossible to keep my engagement. I sent the man a bill. My name was unfortunately too well known on such documents: he failed to negotiate it. His affairs had fallen into disorder in the interval since I had borrowed of him; bankruptcy stared him in the face; and a relative of his, a French lawyer, came to England to find me and to insist upon the payment of my debt. He was a man of violent temper; and he took the wrong way with me. High words passed on both sides; and my aunt and Rachel were unfortunately in the next room and heard us. Lady Verinder came in and insisted on knowing what was the matter. The Frenchman produced his credentials and declared me to be responsible for the ruin of a poor man who had trusted in my honour. My aunt instantly paid him the money and sent him off. She knew me better of course than to take the Frenchman's view of the transaction. But she was shocked at my carelessness and justly angry with me for placing myself in a position which, but for her interference, might have become a very disgraceful one. Either her mother told her or Rachel heard what passed—I can't say which. She took her own romantic, high-flown view of the matter. I was "heartless"; I was "dishonourable"; I had "no principle"; there was "no knowing what I might do next"—in short, she said some of the severest things to me which I had ever heard from a young lady's lips. The breach between us lasted for the whole of the next day. The day after, I succeeded in making my peace and thought no more of it. Had Rachel reverted to this unlucky accident at the critical moment when my place in her estimation was again, and far more seriously, assailed? Mr. Bruff,

As I stepped into the room, Rachel rose from the piano bench. I closed the door behind me, and we stood facing one another across the vast, silent space of the chamber. The single movement she had made to rise seemed to be the totality of her effort; every other faculty, physical or mental, appeared to have dissolved into the singular act of staring at me.

A sudden fear seized me that I had intruded too abruptly. I took a step forward. "Rachel!" I called softly.

The sound of my voice seemed to restore life to her limbs and color to her pale cheeks. She moved toward me, still silent. Slowly, as if driven by a force beyond her own will, she drew nearer. A warm, dusky hue flushed her cheeks, and a light of returning intelligence brightened her eyes with every passing second. In that moment, I forgot the purpose of my visit; I forgot the vile suspicion hanging over my reputation; I discarded every past, present, and future consideration I was bound to uphold. I saw only the woman I loved approaching me. She trembled, standing irresolute. I could resist no longer—I seized her in my arms and buried my face in her hair, covering her with kisses.

For a fleeting instant, I believed the kisses were returned; a moment when it seemed she, too, might have forgotten everything. But almost before the thought could fully form, her first voluntary action shattered the illusion. With a cry akin to horror, with a strength I doubted I could have withstood, she shoved me back. I saw merciless anger in her eyes and contempt on her lips. She scanned me from head to foot as one might inspect a stranger who had insulted her.

"You coward!" she cried. "You mean, miserable, heartless coward!"

Those were her first words. The most unbearable reproach a woman could hurl at a man was the one she chose to direct at me.

"I remember the time, Rachel," I said, "when you could have told me I had offended you in a worthier fashion than this. I beg your pardon."

Some of the bitterness I felt may have leaked into my voice. At my first reply, her eyes, which had turned away, looked back at me unwillingly. She answered in a low tone, with a sullen submission of manner entirely new to me.

"Perhaps there is some excuse for me," she said. "After what you have done, is it a manly action to find your way to me as you have today? It seems a cowardly experiment to try upon my weakness for you. A cowardly surprise to catch me letting you kiss me. But that is only a woman's view. I ought to have known it couldn't be yours. I should have done better if I had controlled myself and said nothing."

That apology was more unbearable than the insult itself. Even the most degraded man would feel humiliated by it.

"If my honor was not in your hands," I said, "I would leave you this instant and never see you again. You have spoken of what I have done. What have I done?"

"What have you done! *You* ask that question of *me*?"

"I ask it."

"I have kept your infamy a secret," she answered. "And I have suffered the consequences of concealing it. Have I no claim to be spared the insult of your asking me what you have done? Is *all* sense of gratitude dead in you? You were once a gentleman. You were once dear to my mother, and dearer still to me——"

Her voice failed. She sank into a chair, turned her back on me, and covered her face with her hands.

I waited a moment before trusting myself to speak again. In that silence, I hardly knew which hurt more deeply—the sting of her contempt or the proud resolution that shut me out from sharing her distress.

"If you will not speak first," I said, "I must. I have come here with something serious to say. Will you do me the common justice of listening?"

She neither moved nor answered. I made no second appeal; I never advanced an inch closer to her chair. With a pride as obstinate as hers, I recounted my discovery at the Shivering Sand and everything that had led to it. The narrative necessarily took some time. From beginning to end, she never turned her head, and she never uttered a word.

I kept my temper. My entire future likely depended on not losing my composure at that critical moment. The time had come to test Mr. Bruff's theory. Driven by the breathless interest of the experiment, I moved around to stand directly in front of her.

"I have a question to ask you," I said. "It obliges me to refer again to a painful subject. Did Rosanna Spearman show you the nightgown? Yes, or No?"

She sprang to her feet and walked close to me of her own accord. Her eyes searched my face intently, as if trying to read something there they had never seen before.

"Are you mad?" she asked.

I still restrained myself. "Rachel, will you answer my question?"

She ignored me.

"Have you some object to gain which I don't understand? Some mean fear about the future? They say your father's death has made you a rich man. Have you come here to compensate me for the loss of my Diamond? Do you have heart enough left to feel ashamed of your errand? Is *that* the secret of your pretended innocence, your story about Rosanna Spearman? Is there a motive of shame at the bottom of all this falsehood?"

I stopped her there. I could control myself no longer.

"You have done me an infamous wrong!" I broke out hotly. "You suspect me of stealing your Diamond. I have a right to know, and I *will* know, the reason why!"

"Suspect you!" she exclaimed, her anger rising with mine. "*You villain, I saw you take the Diamond with my own eyes!*"

The revelation that burst upon me in those words, the total overthrow of the case Mr. Bruff had built, struck me helpless. Innocent as I was, I stood before her in silence. To her eyes, to any eyes, I must have looked like a man overwhelmed by the discovery of his own guilt.

She drew back from the spectacle of my humiliation and her triumph. The sudden silence that had fallen upon me seemed to frighten her. "I spared you, at the time," she said. "I would have spared you now, if you had not forced me to speak." She moved away as if to leave the room—and hesitated before reaching the door. "Why did you come here to humiliate yourself?" she asked. "Why did you come here to humiliate me?" She took a few steps and paused again. "For God's sake, say something!" she exclaimed, passionately. "If you have any mercy left, don't let me degrade myself in this way! Say something—and drive me out of the room!"

I advanced toward her, hardly conscious of what I was doing. I had possibly some confused idea of detaining her until she told me more. From the moment I knew that the evidence condemning me in Rachel's mind was the evidence of her own eyes, nothing—not even my conviction of my own innocence—was clear to my mind. I took her by the hand; I tried to speak firmly and to the purpose. All I could say was, "Rachel, you once loved me."

She shuddered and looked away. Her hand lay powerless and trembling in mine. "Let go of it," she said faintly.

My touch seemed to have the same effect on her as the sound of my voice had when I first entered. After she had called me a coward, after she had branded me a thief—while her hand lay in mine, I was still her master!

I drew her gently back into the middle of the room. I seated her beside me. "Rachel," I said, "I can't explain the contradiction in what I am going to tell you. I can only speak the truth as you have spoken it. You saw me—with your own eyes, you saw me take the Diamond. Before God who hears us, I declare that I now know I took it for the first time! Do you doubt me still?"

She had neither heeded nor heard me. "Let go of my hand," she repeated faintly. That was her only answer. Her head sank on my shoulder; and her hand unconsciously closed on mine at the moment she asked me to release it.

I refrained from pressing the question. But there my forbearance stopped. My chance of ever holding up my head again among honest men depended on inducing her to make her disclosure complete. The one hope left for me was the hope that she might have overlooked something in the chain of evidence—some mere trifle, perhaps, which might nevertheless, under careful investigation, vindicate my innocence. I kept possession of her hand. I spoke to her with all the sympathy and confidence of the bygone time I could summon.

"I want to ask you something," I said. "I want you to tell me everything that happened, from the time when we wished each other good-night, to the time when you saw me take the Diamond."

She lifted her head from my shoulder and made an effort to release her hand. "Oh, why go back to it!" she said. "Why go back to it!"

"I will tell you why, Rachel. You are the victim, and I am the victim, of some monstrous delusion which has worn the mask of truth. If we look at what happened on the night of your birthday together, we may end in understanding each other yet."

Her head dropped back on my shoulder. Tears gathered in her eyes and fell slowly over her cheeks. "Oh!" she said, "have *I* never had that hope? Have *I* not tried to see it, as you are trying now?"

"You have tried by yourself," I answered. "You have not tried with me to help you."

Those words seemed to awaken in her something of the hope I felt myself. She replied to my questions with more than docility—she exerted her intelligence; she willingly opened her whole mind to me.

"Let us begin," I said, "with what happened after we had wished each other good-night. Did you go to bed? or did you sit up?"

"I went to bed."

"Did you notice the time? Was it late?"

"Not very. About twelve o'clock, I think."

"Did you fall asleep?"

"No. I couldn't sleep that night."

"You were restless?"

"I was thinking of you."

The answer almost unmanned me. Something in the tone, even more than the words, went straight to my heart. Only after pausing a little was I able to continue.

"Did you have any light in your room?" I asked.

"None—until I got up again and lit my candle."

"How long was that, after you had gone to bed?"

"About an hour after, I think. About one o'clock."

"Did you leave your bedroom?"

"I was going to leave it. I had put on my dressing-gown; and I was going into my sitting-room to get a book——"

"Had you opened your bedroom door?"

"I had just opened it."

"But you had not gone into the sitting-room?"

"No—I was stopped from going into it."

"What stopped you?"

"I saw a light under the door; and I heard footsteps approaching it."

"Were you frightened?"

"Not then. I knew my poor mother was a bad sleeper; and I remembered that she had tried hard that evening to persuade me to let her take charge of my Diamond. She was unreasonably anxious about it, as I thought; and I fancied she was coming to see if I was in bed and to speak to me about the Diamond again."

"What did you do?"

"I blew out my candle, so that she might think I was in bed. I was unreasonable, on my side—I was determined to keep my Diamond in the place of my own choosing."

"After blowing out the candle, did you go back to bed?"

"I had no time. At the moment I blew the candle out, the sitting-room door opened, and I saw——"

"You saw?"

"You."

"Dressed as usual?"

"No."

"In your nightgown?"

"In your nightgown—with your bedroom candle in your hand."

"Alone?"

"Alone."

"Could you see my face?"

"Yes."

"Plainly?"

"Quite plainly. The candle in your hand showed it to me."

"Were my eyes open?"

"Yes."

"Did you notice anything strange in them? Anything like a fixed, vacant expression?"

"Nothing of the sort. Your eyes were bright—brighter than usual. You looked about in the room, as if you knew you were where you ought not to be, and as if you were afraid of being found out."

"Did you observe one thing when I came into the room—did you observe how I walked?"

"You walked as you always do. You came in as far as the middle of the room—and then you stopped and looked about you."

"What did you do, on first seeing me?"

"I could do nothing. I was petrified. I couldn't speak, I couldn't call out, I couldn't even move to shut my door."

"Could I see you, where you stood?"

"You might certainly have seen me. But you never looked towards me. It's useless to ask the question. I am sure you never saw me."

"How are you sure?"

"Would you have taken the Diamond? Would you have acted as you did afterwards? Would you be here now—if you had seen that I was awake and looking at you? Don't make me talk of that part of it! I want to answer you quietly. Help me to keep as calm as I can. Go on to something else."

She was right—in every way, right. I went on to other things.

"What did I do, after I had got to the middle of the room and had stopped there?"

"You turned away, and went straight to the corner near the window—where my Indian cabinet stands."

"When I was at the cabinet, my back must have been turned towards you. How did you see what I was doing?"

"When you moved, I moved

Late that evening, a knock at my lodgings announced the arrival of Mr. Bruff. The lawyer's demeanor had undergone a profound transformation; the usual confidence and vigor that characterized his visits were absent, replaced by a somber, defeated air. He extended his hand to me, a gesture he had never before performed in silence, his grip heavy with unspoken weight.

"Are you returning to Hampstead?" I asked, seeking any thread of conversation.

"I have just departed Hampstead," he replied, his voice low. "Mr. Franklin, I know you have finally grasped the truth. Yet, if I could have anticipated the terrible price exacted for that knowledge, I would have preferred to leave you in ignorance."

"You have seen Rachel?"

"I returned her to Portland Place after bringing her here; it was impossible to allow her to travel alone in the carriage. I cannot hold you accountable for the shock this unfortunate meeting has inflicted upon her, considering you witnessed her in my home with my permission. My duty now is to prevent a recurrence of such distress. She is young, possessed of a resolute spirit, and with time and rest, she will recover. I require only your assurance that you will do nothing to impede her healing. Can I rely on your promise to make no further attempts to see her without my explicit sanction?"

"After the suffering she has endured, and the suffering I have borne," I said, "you may rely on my word."

"I have your promise?"

"You have my promise."

Relief washed over Mr. Bruff's features. He removed his hat and drew his chair closer. "That is settled. Now, regarding your future. In my view, the extraordinary turn events have taken leads to a singular conclusion. First, we are certain Rachel told you the entire truth as plainly as words allow. Second, though we acknowledge a dreadful error exists somewhere, we cannot blame her for believing you guilty based on the evidence of her senses, supported by circumstances that, on their face, condemn you."

I interjected immediately. "I do not blame Rachel. I only regret she could not force herself to speak more plainly to me at the time."

"You might as well regret that Rachel is not someone else," Mr. Bruff countered. "Even then, I doubt if a girl of any delicacy, whose heart was set on marrying you, could bring herself to accuse you openly of theft. It was not in Rachel's nature. In a matter entirely different from yours, yet placing her in a position not unlike hers toward you, I know she was influenced by a similar motive. Furthermore, as she told me on our way to town, if she *had* spoken plainly, she would no more have believed your denial then than she does now. What answer can you offer? None. Admit it, Mr. Franklin. My view of the case was proven wrong, but my advice remains valid. We shall waste our time and exhaust our minds if we attempt to unravel this frightful complication from the beginning. Let us resolutely close our minds to last year at Lady Verinder's country house and focus on what we *can* discover in the future, rather than what we *cannot* uncover in the past."

"Surely you forget," I argued, "that the entire matter is essentially of the past, so far as I am concerned?"

"Answer me this," Mr. Bruff retorted sharply. "Is the Moonstone at the bottom of all the mischief, or is it not?"

"It is—of course."

"Very well. What do we believe was done with the Moonstone when it was taken to London?"

"It was pledged to Mr. Luker."

"We know you are not the person who pledged it. Do we know who did?"

"No."

"Where do we believe the Moonstone is now?"

"Deposited in the keeping of Mr. Luker's bankers."

"Exactly. Observe. We are already in June. Towards the end of the month, a year will have elapsed since the jewel was pledged. There is a distinct possibility that the person who pawned it will be prepared to redeem it when the year expires. If he redeems it, Mr. Luker must, according to his own terms, retrieve the diamond from the bankers. Under these circumstances, I propose setting a watch at the bank as the month draws to a close to discover who claims the Moonstone. Do you see the logic?"

I admitted, albeit reluctantly, that the idea was novel.

"It is Mr. Murthwaite's idea as much as mine," Mr. Bruff noted. "It might never have occurred to me without our conversation some time ago. If Mr. Murthwaite is correct, the Indians will likely be watching the bank at the end of the month too, and something serious may ensue. What happens does not concern us directly, except as it helps us identify the mysterious individual who pawned the Diamond. That person, you may be certain, is responsible for your current predicament; only they can restore your standing in Rachel's eyes."

"I cannot deny," I said, "that your proposed plan meets the difficulty with daring ingenuity. But—"

"But you have an objection?"

"Yes. My objection is that your proposal obliges us to wait."

"Granted. As I calculate, it requires a fortnight—more or less. Is that so long?"

"It is a lifetime, Mr. Bruff, in my situation. My existence is unendurable unless I take immediate action to clear my name."

"Well, I understand. Have you considered what you can do?"

"I have thought of consulting Sergeant Cuff."

"He has retired from the police. It is useless to expect his aid."

"I know where to find him; I can but try."

"Try," Mr. Bruff said after a moment's pause. "The case has assumed such an extraordinary aspect since Sergeant Cuff's time that you *may* revive his interest. Try, and let me hear the result. Meanwhile, if you make no discoveries before the end of the month, am I free to attempt the bank vigil on my own?"

"Certainly," I answered, "unless I relieve you of the necessity of trying the experiment in the interim."

Mr. Bruff smiled and picked up his hat. "Tell Sergeant Cuff," he added, "that *I* say the discovery of the truth depends on identifying the person who pawned the Diamond. Let me hear what the Sergeant's experience says to that."

We parted ways.

Early the next morning, I set out for Dorking, the small town where Sergeant Cuff had retired, as Betteredge had indicated. Inquiring at the hotel, I received directions to his cottage. It was approached by a quiet by-road, situated snugly within its own garden plot, protected by brick walls and a high hedge. The gate, adorned with painted trellis work, was locked. After ringing the bell, I peered through the lattice and saw Cuff's favorite flowers blooming everywhere, clustering over his door and looking in at his windows. Far from the crimes and mysteries of the great city, the illustrious thief-taker was living out his final years in peaceful seclusion, smothered in roses.

A decent elderly woman opened the gate, instantly annihilating all the hopes I had built on securing Cuff's assistance. He had departed only the previous day on a journey to Ireland.

"Has he gone there on business?" I asked.

The woman smiled. "He has only one business now, sir," she replied. "And that is roses. A great man's gardener in Ireland has discovered something new in cultivating roses, and Mr. Cuff is away to investigate it."

"Do you know when he will return?"

"It is quite uncertain, sir. Mr. Cuff said he would return directly, or be away for some time, depending on whether he finds the discovery worthless or worthy of study. If you have a message for him, I will ensure he receives it."

I handed her my card, having first penciled on it: "I have something to say about the Moonstone. Let me hear from you as soon as you return." With that, there was nothing left but to submit to circumstances and return to London.

In the irritable state of my mind at this time, the failure of my journey to Dorking merely aggravated my restless impulse to act. On the day of my return, I resolved that the next morning would see me make a new effort to force my way from darkness to light.

What form should this next experiment take?

If Betteredge had been present during my deliberations, he would undoubtedly have declared that my German side was currently dominant. To speak seriously, my German training may have been partially responsible for the labyrinth of useless speculations in which I now entangled myself. For most of the night, I sat smoking, constructing theories, each more improbable than the last. When sleep finally claimed me, my waking fancies pursued me in dreams. I rose the next morning with "Objective-Subjective" and "Subjective-Objective" inextricably tangled in my mind. I began the day intended for practical action by doubting whether I had any philosophical right to consider any thing, including the Diamond, as existing at all.

How long I might have remained lost in the mist of my own metaphysics is impossible to say. As events proved, accident came to my rescue. Happening to wear the same coat I had worn during my interview with Rachel, I searched one pocket and found a crumpled piece of paper: Betteredge's forgotten letter.

It seemed hard on my good old friend to leave him without a reply. I went to my writing table and read the letter again. A letter containing nothing of slight importance is not always easy to answer. Betteredge's correspondence fell into this category. Mr. Candy's assistant, Ezra Jennings, had told his master that he had seen me; consequently, Mr. Candy wished to see me and say something when I was next near Frizinghall. What could I possibly say in response that would be worth the paper? I sat idly drawing likenesses from memory of Mr. Candy's remarkable assistant on the sheet I had vowed to dedicate to Betteredge—until it suddenly occurred to me that the irrepressible Ezra Jennings was getting in my way again. I threw a dozen portraits of the man with the piebald hair into the waste-paper basket and, there and then, wrote my answer to Betteredge. It was a perfectly commonplace letter, but it had one excellent effect on me: the effort of writing a few sentences in plain English completely cleared my mind of the cloudy nonsense that had filled it since the previous day.

Devoting myself once more to elucidating the impenetrable puzzle of my own position, I attempted to meet the difficulty by investigating it from a plainly practical standpoint. The events of the memorable night remaining unintelligible, I looked back to search my memory of the earlier hours of the birthday for any incident that might serve as a clue.

Had anything happened while Rachel and I finished painting the door? Later, when I rode to Frizinghall? Afterwards, when I returned with Godfrey Ablewhite and his sisters? Or later still, when I placed the Moonstone in Rachel's hands? Or finally, when the company assembled around the dinner table? My memory disposed of these questions readily until I reached the last. Looking back at the social event of the birthday dinner, I found myself brought to a standstill. I was not even capable of accurately remembering the number of guests who had sat at the same table as me.

To feel myself completely at fault here, and to conclude that the incidents of the dinner especially repaid investigation, formed part of the same mental process. I believe others in a similar situation would have reasoned as I did. When the pursuit of our own interests makes us objects of inquiry to ourselves, we are naturally suspicious of what we do not know. Once in possession of the names of those present at the dinner, I resolved—as a means to enrich the deficient resources of my own memory—to appeal to the memories of the other guests. I would write down all they could recall of the social events of the birthday and test the results against what happened afterwards when the company left.

This last and newest of my many contemplated experiments in the art of inquiry—which Betteredge would probably attribute to the clear-headed, or French, side of me being uppermost for the moment—may fairly claim record here on its own merits. Unlikely as it may seem, I had now actually groped my way to the root of the matter. All I needed was a hint to guide me in the right direction at the start. Before another day had passed, that hint was given to me by one of the company present at the birthday feast!

With the plan of proceeding now in view, it was first necessary to possess the complete list of guests. This I could easily obtain from Gabriel Betteredge. I determined to return to Yorkshire that day and begin my contemplated investigation the next morning.

It was just too late to start by the train leaving London before noon. There was no alternative but to wait nearly three hours for the departure of the next train. Was there anything I could do in London to usefully occupy this interval?

My thoughts returned obstinately to the birthday dinner.

Though I had forgotten the numbers and, in many cases, the names of the guests, I remembered readily that by far the larger proportion came from Frizinghall or its neighborhood. But the larger proportion was not all. Some few of us were not regular residents in the country. I myself was one of the few. Mr. Murthwaite was another. Godfrey Ablewhite was a third. Mr. Bruff—no: I recalled that business had prevented him from attending. Had any ladies been present whose usual residence was in London? I could only remember Miss Clack as falling into this category. However, here were three guests at least whom it was clearly advisable for me to see before leaving town. I drove off immediately to Mr. Bruff's office, not knowing the addresses of the persons I sought, thinking it probable he might put me in their way.

Mr. Bruff proved too busy to give me more than a minute of his valuable time. In that minute, however, he contrived to dispose, in the most discouraging manner, of all the questions I had to ask.

First, he considered my newly discovered method of finding a clue to the mystery as something too purely fanciful to be seriously discussed. Second, third, and fourth, Mr. Murthwaite was now on his way back to the scene of his

The doctor's pretty housemaid stood waiting for me, holding the street door open. As I turned to face her, the morning light flooded the hall, illuminating the face of Mr. Candy's assistant. It was undeniable, as Betteredge had claimed, that Ezra Jennings' appearance was a disadvantage. His gypsy complexion, fleshless cheeks, gaunt bones, dreamy eyes, and extraordinary, parti-colored hair created a confusing contradiction between old age and youth that struck a stranger unfavorably. Yet, despite my instinctive wariness, an inscrutable appeal to my sympathies held me rooted to the spot. While my knowledge of the world dictated that I should acknowledge Mr. Candy's sad change and depart, my interest in this strange man gave him the opportunity to speak to me privately, a conversation he had clearly been waiting for.

"Are you walking my way, Mr. Jennings?" I asked, noting he held his hat. "I am going to call on my aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite."

"I have a patient to see," Ezra replied, "but I am walking your way."

We left the house together. I noticed that the cheerful servant girl, who had greeted me with smiles, received a modest message from Ezra regarding his return time. Her pursed lips and eyes, which deliberately avoided his face, revealed she was no favorite. Outside, Betteredge confirmed he was unpopular everywhere. "What a life!" I thought as we descended the steps.

Ezra seemed determined to let me resume the topic of Mr. Candy's illness. His silence was significant; it was my turn. I readily accepted the responsibility.

"Judging by the change I see in him," I began, "Mr. Candy's illness must have been far more serious than I had supposed?"

"It is almost a miracle," Ezra said, "that he lived through it."

"Is his memory never any better than I found it today? He has been trying to speak to me..."

"Of something which happened before he was taken ill?" Ezra asked, sensing my hesitation.

"Yes."

"His memory of events at that past time is hopelessly enfeebled," Ezra continued. "It is almost to be deplored that even the wreck of it remains. He remembers dimly plans he formed, things he had to say or do, but he is incapable of recalling what those plans were or what he had to say. He is painfully conscious of his deficiency and anxious to hide it. If he could have recovered in a complete state of oblivion, he would have been happier. Perhaps we should all be happier if we could completely forget!"

"There are some events surely in all men's lives," I replied, "the memory of which they would be unwilling entirely to lose?"

"That is, I hope, to be said of most men, Mr. Blake. I am afraid it cannot truly be said of *all*." He paused, then asked, "Have you any reason to suppose that the lost remembrance Mr. Candy tried to recover was a remembrance important to *you* that he should recall?"

He had touched the very point I wished to discuss. My interest in this strange man had compelled me to give him the chance to speak, reserving my own questions until I was satisfied of his discretion. The little he had said convinced me he was a gentleman possessing an unsought self-possession, a sure sign of good breeding. Whatever his motives, I felt justified in answering without reserve.

"I believe I have a strong interest in tracing the lost remembrance Mr. Candy was unable to recall," I said. "May I ask whether you can suggest any method by which I might assist his memory?"

Ezra looked at me, a sudden flash of interest lighting his dreamy brown eyes.

"Mr. Candy's memory is beyond the reach of assistance," he said. "I have tried to help it often enough since his recovery to speak positively on that point."

This disappointed me. "I confess you led me to hope for a less discouraging answer," I admitted.

"It may not be a final answer, Mr. Blake," Ezra smiled. "It may be possible to trace Mr. Candy's lost recollection without appealing to Mr. Candy himself."

"Indeed? Is it an indiscretion to ask how?"

"By no means. My difficulty lies in explaining myself. May I trust to your patience if I refer once more to Mr. Candy's illness, speaking this time without sparing you certain professional details?"

"Pray go on! You have interested me already."

My eagerness amused him. We had left the town behind. Ezra stopped to pick wild flowers from the roadside hedge. "How beautiful they are!" he said simply, showing me his nosegay. "And how few people in England seem to admire them as they deserve!"

"You have not always been in England?" I asked.

"No. I was born and partly brought up in one of our colonies. My father was English, but my mother..." He stopped, realizing we were straying. "The truth is, I have associations with these modest flowers. To Mr. Candy, let us return."

Connecting his reluctant words about himself with his melancholy view that human happiness lay in forgetting the past, I felt satisfied that the story written in his face was true. He had suffered as few men suffer, and there was a mixture of foreign race in his English blood.

"You have heard of the original cause of Mr. Candy's illness?" he resumed. "The night of Lady Verinder's dinner-party was a night of heavy rain. My employer drove home wet to the skin and found an urgent message from a patient. He went immediately to visit the sick person without changing his clothes. I was detained elsewhere that night. When I returned the next morning, Mr. Candy's groom was waiting in alarm. By then, the mischief was done; the illness had set in."

"The illness has only been described to me as a fever," I said.

"I can add nothing to make the description more accurate," Ezra answered. "From first to last, the fever assumed no specific form. I consulted two of Mr. Candy's medical friends. They agreed it was serious but strongly dissented from my view of the treatment. We differed entirely on the patient's pulse. The two doctors argued from its rapidity that a lowering treatment was necessary. On my side, I pointed to its alarming feebleness as indicating an exhausted system requiring stimulants. They wanted gruel and lemonade; I wanted champagne, brandy, ammonia, and quinine. For the first few days, I had to yield to my elders. The patient sank steadily. I made a second attempt to appeal to the evidence of the pulse. Its rapidity was unchecked, and its feebleness had increased. The doctors took offense. 'Mr. Jennings, either we manage this case, or you manage it. Which is it to be?' I said, 'Give me five minutes.' When the time expired, I said, 'You positively refuse to try the stimulant treatment?' They refused. 'I mean to try it at once.' 'Try it, Mr. Jennings, and we withdraw.' I fetched a bottle of champagne and administered half a tumbler-full with my own hand. The two physicians took up their hats in silence and left."

"You had assumed a serious responsibility," I said. "In your place, I am afraid I should have shrunk from it."

"In my place, Mr. Blake, you would have remembered that Mr. Candy had taken you into his employment under circumstances that made you his debtor for life. You would have seen him sinking hour by hour and risked anything rather than let the one man on earth who had befriended you die before your eyes. Don't suppose I had no sense of the terrible position! There were moments when I felt all the misery of my friendlessness. If I had been a happy man, I believe I should have sunk under the task. But I had no happy time to look back at, no past peace of mind to contrast with my present anxiety. I held firm. I took an interval in the middle of the day for repose. For the rest of the twenty-four hours, as long as his life was in danger, I never left his bedside. Towards sunset, the delirium came on. It lasted through the night and intermitted at that terrible time in the early morning—from two to five—when Death gathers in his harvest most abundantly. It was then that Death and I fought over the man who lay on the bed. I never hesitated. When wine failed, I tried brandy. When stimulants lost their influence, I doubled the dose. After an interval of suspense—the like of which I hope to God I shall never feel again—there came a day when the rapidity of the pulse slightly diminished, and there came also a change in the beat to steadiness and strength. *Then*, I knew I had saved him; and then I own I broke down. I laid the poor fellow's wasted hand back on the bed and burst out crying. An hysterical relief, Mr. Blake—nothing more! Physiology says, and truly, that some men are born with female constitutions—and I am one of them!"

He made that bitterly professional apology for his tears, speaking quietly and unaffectedly. His tone showed he was morbidly anxious not to set himself up as an object of interest.

"You may well ask why I have wearied you with these details," he went on. "It is the only way I can see of properly introducing what I have to say next. Now you know my position, you will understand the sore need I had of lightening the burden on my mind. I have occupied my leisure for some years writing a book on the brain and nervous system, addressed to members of my profession. My work will probably never be finished or published, but it has been the friend of many lonely hours. I told you he was delirious? And I mentioned the time?"

"Yes."

"Well, I had reached a section of my book touching on delirium. I doubt whether we can justifiably infer that the loss of the faculty of speaking connectedly implies the loss of the faculty of thinking connectedly. Mr. Candy's illness gave me an opportunity to test this. I understand shorthand and took down the patient's 'wanderings' exactly as they fell from his lips. Do you see, Mr. Blake, what I am coming to?"

I waited with breathless interest.

"At odd and ends of time," Ezra continued, "I reproduced my shorthand notes in ordinary writing, leaving large spaces between the broken phrases and single words as they had fallen disconnectedly. I treated the result on the principle of putting together a child's puzzle. It was all confusion to begin with, but it may be brought into order if you find the right way. Acting on this plan, I filled in each blank space with what the words on either side suggested as the speaker's meaning, altering over and over until my additions followed naturally. The result was that I occupied many vacant and anxious hours and arrived at a confirmation of my theory. In plainer words, after putting the broken sentences together, I found the superior faculty of thinking going on, more or less connectedly, in my patient's mind, while the inferior faculty of expression was in a state of almost complete incapacity."

"One word!" I interposed eagerly. "Did my name occur in any of his wanderings?"

"You shall hear, Mr. Blake. Among my written proofs of the assertion I have just advanced—or, rather, among the written experiments tending to put my assertion to the proof—there *is* one in which your name occurs. For nearly the whole of one night, Mr. Candy's mind was occupied with *something* between himself and you. I have the broken words on one sheet of paper and the links of my own discovery connecting them on another. The product is an intelligible statement—first, of something actually done in the past; secondly, of something Mr. Candy contemplated doing in the future if his illness had not stopped him. The question is whether this represents the lost recollection he vainly attempted to find this morning?"

"Not a doubt of it!" I answered. "Let us go back directly and look at the papers!"

"Quite impossible, Mr. Blake."

"Why?"

"Put yourself in my position for a moment," Ezra said. "Would *you* disclose to another person what had dropped unconsciously from the lips of your suffering patient and your helpless friend, without first knowing that there was a necessity to justify you in opening your lips?"

I felt he was unanswerable, yet I tried to argue. "My conduct in such a delicate matter would depend greatly on whether the disclosure was of a nature to compromise my friend or not."

"I have disposed of all necessity for considering that side of the question long since," Ezra said. "Wherever my notes included anything Mr. Candy might have wished to keep secret, those notes have been destroyed. My manuscript experiments include nothing now which he would have hesitated to communicate to others if he had recovered his memory. In your case, I have every reason to suppose my notes contain something he actually wished to say to you."

"And yet, you hesitate?"

"And yet, I hesitate. Remember the circumstances under which I obtained the information! Harmless as it is, I cannot prevail upon myself to give it up to you unless you first satisfy me that there is a reason for doing so. He was so miserably ill, Mr. Blake! and so helplessly dependent upon me! Is it too much to ask if I request you only to hint to me what your interest is in the lost recollection—or what you believe that lost recollection to be?"

To answer him with the frankness his manner claimed would have been to commit myself to openly acknowledging that I was suspected of the theft of the Diamond. Strongly as Ezra had intensified my interest, he had not overcome my unconquerable reluctance to disclose the degrading position in which I stood. I took refuge once more in the explanatory phrases I had prepared for strangers.

This time, Ezra listened patiently, even anxiously, until I had done.

"I am sorry to have raised your expectations, Mr. Blake, only to disappoint them," he said. "Throughout the whole period of Mr. Candy's illness, not one word about the Diamond escaped his lips. The matter with which I heard him connect your name has, I can assure you, no discoverable relation whatever with the loss or recovery of Miss Verinder's jewel."

We arrived at a place where the highway branched into two roads. One led to Mr. Ablewhite's house, the other

The two hours of suspended animation had altered my physical state more profoundly than I could have anticipated. I found myself physically incapable of remaining stationary in any single location, and morally unable to engage in conversation with any human being until I had first heard the entirety of Ezra Jennings' account. In this agitated frame of mind, I abandoned my intended visit to Mrs. Ablewhite and even shrank from the prospect of encountering Gabriel Betteredge.

Returning to Frizinghall, I left a note for Betteredge explaining that an unexpected summons had detained me for a few hours, assuring him that I expected to return around three o'clock in the afternoon. I instructed him to order dinner at the usual hour and to amuse himself as he saw fit, knowing well that his numerous friends in the village would ensure he had no shortage of company until my return.

Having dispatched this message, I hurried out of the town once more, wandering the desolate moorlands surrounding Frizinghall until my watch indicated it was time to return to Mr. Candy's residence.

I found Ezra Jennings waiting for me.

He sat alone in a sparsely furnished room connected by a glazed door to a surgery. The barren buff-colored walls were adorned with hideous colored diagrams depicting the ravages of terrible diseases. A bookcase filled with dusty medical texts, topped by a skull rather than a customary bust, stood nearby. A large deal table was splattered with ink, flanked by wooden kitchen chairs, a threadbare rug, and a rough sink integrated into the wall, evoking the grim reality of surgical operations. Outside the window, bees hummed among flowers, birds sang in the garden, and the faint, intermittent jingle of a tuneless piano drifted from a neighboring house. In any other setting, these sounds would have signaled the comfort of the everyday world; here, they intruded upon a silence broken only by human suffering. I glanced at the mahogany instrument case and the large roll of lint on the shelves, shuddering inwardly at the familiar sounds associated with the room's daily use.

"I make no apology, Mr. Blake, for the place in which I am receiving you," he said. "It is the only room in the house, at this hour, where we can be certain of undisturbed privacy. Here are my papers ready for you, along with two books we may need to consult. Bring your chair to the table, and we shall begin."

I drew up to the table, and Ezra Jennings handed me his manuscript notes. They consisted of two large folio leaves. One contained writing scattered across the surface at intervals; the other was densely filled from top to bottom with writing in both red and black ink. In my irritated state of curiosity, I set aside the second sheet in despair.

"Have some mercy on me!" I exclaimed. "Tell me what I am to expect before I attempt to read this."

"Willingly, Mr. Blake! Do you mind if I ask you one or two more questions?"

"Ask me anything you like!"

He looked at me with a sad smile and kindly interest in his soft brown eyes.

"You have already told me," he said, "that you have never, to your knowledge, tasted opium in your life."

"To my knowledge," I repeated.

"You will understand directly why I speak with that reservation. Let us proceed. You are not aware of ever having taken opium. At this time last year, you were suffering from nervous irritation and slept wretchedly at night. However, on the night of the birthday, there was an exception to the rule—you slept soundly. Am I right so far?"

"Quite right!"

"Can you assign any cause for your nervous suffering and lack of sleep?"

"I can assign no cause. Old Betteredge made a guess, I remember, but it was hardly worth mentioning."

"Pardon me. Anything is worth mentioning in a case like this. Betteredge attributed your sleeplessness to something. To what?"

"To my leaving off smoking."

"Had you been an habitual smoker?"

"Yes."

"Did you leave off the habit suddenly?"

"Yes."

"Betteredge was perfectly right, Mr. Blake. When smoking is a habit, a man must have no common constitution who can cease it suddenly without temporary damage to his nervous system. Your sleepless nights are accounted for. My next question concerns Mr. Candy. Do you remember entering into any dispute with him at the birthday dinner or afterwards regarding his profession?"

The question instantly awakened a dormant memory. The foolish wrangle between Mr. Candy and myself, described at length in Betteredge's narrative, had faded from my mind. All I could recall was that I had attacked the art of medicine at the dinner table with sufficient rashness to put Mr. Candy out of temper temporarily. I remembered that Lady Verinder had intervened to stop the dispute, and that the little doctor and I had "made it up again" before shaking hands that night.

"There is one more thing," said Ezra Jennings, "which is very important I should know. Did you have any reason for feeling special anxiety about the Diamond at this time last year?"

"I had the strongest reasons. I knew it was the object of a conspiracy and was warned to take measures for Miss Verinder's protection."

"Was the safety of the Diamond the subject of conversation between you and anyone immediately before you retired to rest on the birthday night?"

"It was the subject of a conversation between Lady Verinder and her daughter..."

"Which took place in your hearing?"

"Yes."

Ezra Jennings took up his notes and placed them in my hands.

"Mr. Blake," he said, "if you read those notes now, illuminated by the light of my questions and your answers, you will make two astounding discoveries concerning yourself. First, that you entered Miss Verinder's sitting-room and took the Diamond in a state of trance produced by opium. Second, that the opium was given to you by Mr. Candy without your knowledge, as a practical refutation of the opinions you had expressed to him at the dinner table."

I sat with the papers in my hand, completely stupefied.

"Try and forgive poor Mr. Candy," said the assistant gently. "He has done dreadful mischief, I own; but he has done it innocently. If you look at the notes, you will see that—but for his illness—he would have returned to Lady Verinder's the morning after the party and acknowledged the trick. Miss Verinder would have questioned him, and the truth hidden for a year would have been discovered in a day."

I began to regain my self-possession. "Mr. Candy is beyond the reach of my resentment," I said angrily. "But the trick he played me is not the less an act of treachery. I may forgive, but I shall not forget it."

"Every medical man commits that act of treachery in the course of his practice, Mr. Blake. The ignorant distrust of opium in England is not confined to the lower classes. Every doctor in large practice finds himself, every now and then, obliged to deceive his patients, as Mr. Candy deceived you. I do not defend the folly of playing you a trick under the circumstances. I only plead with you for a more accurate and merciful construction of motives."

"How was it done?" I asked. "Who gave me the laudanum without my knowing it?"

"I am not able to tell you. Nothing relating to that part of the matter dropped from Mr. Candy's lips during his illness. Perhaps your own memory may point to the person to be suspected."

"No."

"It is useless, in that case, to pursue the inquiry. The laudanum was secretly given to you in some way. Let us leave it there and go on to matters of more immediate importance. Read my notes if you can. Familiarize your mind with what has happened in the past. I have something very bold and startling to propose to you regarding the future."

Those last words roused me.

I looked at the papers. The sheet with the smaller quantity of writing was on top. On it, the disconnected words and fragments of sentences dropped from Mr. Candy in his delirium appeared as follows:

"... Mr. Franklin Blake ... and agreeable ... down a peg ... medicine ... confesses ... sleep at night ... tell him ... out of order ... medicine ... he tells me ... and groping in the dark mean one and the same thing ... all the company at the dinner-table ... I say ... groping after sleep ... nothing but medicine ... he says ... leading the blind ... know what it means ... witty ... a night's rest in spite of his teeth ... wants sleep ... Lady Verinder's medicine chest ... five-and-twenty minims ... without his knowing it ... tomorrow morning ... Well, Mr. Blake ... medicine today ... never ... without it ... out, Mr. Candy ... excellent ... without it ... down on him ... truth ... something besides ... excellent ... dose of laudanum, sir ... bed ... what ... medicine now."

"That is what you heard at his bedside?" I said.

"Literally and exactly what I heard," he answered—"except that the repetitions are not transferred here from my shorthand notes. He reiterated certain words and phrases a dozen times over, fifty times over, just as he attached more or less importance to the idea they represented. These repetitions assisted me in putting the fragments together. Don't suppose," he added, pointing to the second sheet, "that I claim to have reproduced the expressions Mr. Candy would have used if he had been capable of speaking coherently. I only say that I have penetrated through the obstacle of disconnected expression to the thought underlying it. Judge for yourself."

I turned to the second sheet, which I now knew to be the key to the first.

Once more, Mr. Candy's wanderings appeared, copied in black ink; the intervals between the phrases filled in by Ezra Jennings in red ink. I reproduce the result here in one plain form:

"... Mr. Franklin Blake is clever and agreeable, but he wants taking down a peg when he talks of medicine. He confesses that he has been suffering from want of sleep at night. I tell him that his nerves are out of order, and that he ought to take medicine. He tells me that taking medicine and groping in the dark mean one and the same thing. This before all the company at the dinner-table. I say to him, you are groping after sleep, and nothing but medicine can help you to find it. He says to me, I have heard of the blind leading the blind, and now I know what it means. Witty—but I can give him a night's rest in spite of his teeth. He really wants sleep; and Lady Verinder's medicine chest is at my disposal. Give him five-and-twenty minims of laudanum tonight, without his knowing it; and then call tomorrow morning. 'Well, Mr. Blake, will you try a little medicine today? You will never sleep without it.'—'There you are out, Mr. Candy: I have had an excellent night's rest without it.' Then, come down on him with the truth! 'You have had something besides an excellent night's rest; you had a dose of laudanum, sir, before you went to bed. What do you say to the art of medicine, now?'"

Admiration for the ingenuity that had woven this smooth texture out of the raveled skein was my first impression. He modestly interrupted my surprise by asking if the conclusion he had drawn was also the conclusion at which my own mind had arrived.

"Do you believe as I believe," he said, "that you were acting under the influence of the laudanum in doing all that you did on the night of Miss Verinder's birthday?"

"I am too ignorant of the influence of laudanum to have an opinion of my own," I answered. "I can only follow your opinion and feel convinced that you are right."

"Very well. The next question is this. You are convinced; and I am convinced—how are we to carry our conviction to the minds of other people?"

I pointed to the two manuscripts lying on the table. Ezra Jennings shook his head.

"Useless, Mr. Blake! Quite useless, as they stand now, for three unanswerable reasons. In the first place, those notes have been taken under circumstances entirely out of the experience of the mass of mankind. In the second place, those notes represent a medical and metaphysical theory. In the third place, those notes are of my making; there is nothing but my assertion to guarantee that they are not fabrications. Remember what I told you on the moor—and ask yourself what my assertion is worth. No! my notes have but one value, looking to the verdict of the world outside. Your innocence is to be vindicated; and they show how it can be done. We must put our conviction to the proof—and you are the man to prove it!"

"How?" I asked.

He leaned eagerly nearer to me across the table.

"Are you willing to try a bold experiment?"

"I will do anything to clear myself of the suspicion that rests on me now."

"Will you submit to some personal inconvenience for a time?"

"To any inconvenience, no matter what it may be."

"Will you be guided implicitly by my advice? It may expose you to the ridicule of fools; it may subject you to the remonstrances of friends whose opinions you are bound to respect."

"Tell me what to do!" I broke out impatiently. "And, come what may, I'll do it."

"You shall do this, Mr. Blake," he answered. "You shall steal the Diamond, unconsciously, for the second time, in the presence of witnesses whose testimony is beyond dispute."

I started to my feet. I tried to speak. I could only look at him.

"I believe it _can_ be done," he went on. "And it _shall_ be done—if you will only help me. Try to compose yourself—sit down, and hear what I have to say to you. You have resumed the habit of smoking; I have seen that for myself. How long have you resumed it."

"For nearly a year."

"Do you smoke more or less than you did?"

"More."

"Will you give up the habit again? Suddenly, mind!—as you gave it up before."

I began dimly to see his drift. "I will give it up, from this moment," I answered.

"If the same consequences follow, which followed last June," said Ezra Jennings—"if you suffer once more

The narrative presented in the Journal of Ezra Jennings required but few words of completion from my own pen. Upon awakening on the morning of the twenty-sixth, I found myself utterly devoid of memory regarding the events that had transpired under the drug's influence, stretching from the moment the opium seized my faculties until I opened my eyes in Rachel's sitting-room.

Regarding the subsequent hours, I deemed a detailed account unnecessary. Suffice it to say that Rachel and I achieved a profound mutual understanding before a single explanatory word was spoken. Neither of us felt compelled to justify the extraordinary speed of our reconciliation. Those who recall their own moments of passionate attachment will understand the dynamic that unfolded after Ezra Jennings secured the sitting-room door; I witnessed it, and so did Rachel.

However, I must note that our reunion would have been discovered by Mrs. Merridew had it not been for Rachel's quick wit. Hearing the old lady's footsteps in the corridor, Rachel rushed out to intercept her. I heard Mrs. Merridew ask, "What is the matter?" and Rachel's immediate reply, "The explosion!" Mrs. Merridew was gently guided into the garden, safely away from the impending shock. Upon her return to the house, she encountered me in the hall, expressing a profound admiration for the advancements in science since her school days. "Explosions, Mr. Blake, are infinitely milder than they were," she remarked. "I barely heard Mr. Jennings's explosion from the garden, and there is no lingering smell now! I must apologize to your medical friend; he has managed it beautifully!"

Thus, after overcoming the skepticism of Betteredge and Mr. Bruff, Ezra Jennings also subdued Mrs. Merridew. There remains a vast reservoir of liberal sentiment in the world, after all!

At breakfast, Mr. Bruff openly expressed his desire for me to accompany him to London by the morning train. The watch kept at the bank, and the potential results of our inquiry, appealed irresistibly to Rachel's curiosity. She immediately decided to join us, provided Mrs. Merridew had no objection, so she might be within reach of the earliest news. Mrs. Merridew, gratified by the mild nature of the explosion, proved pliable and indulgent. Betteredge was accordingly informed that we were all four to travel together. I had expected him to volunteer, but Rachel wisely assigned him the task of completing the house's refurnishing. Burdened with domestic responsibilities, he was too occupied to succumb to the "detective-fever" he might otherwise have felt.

Our sole regret in departing was the necessity of parting with Ezra Jennings more abruptly than we wished. It was impossible to persuade him to come; I could only promise to write, and Rachel could only insist on his visiting her upon her return to Yorkshire. While we hoped to meet again within months, there was a poignant sadness in seeing our dearest friend standing alone on the platform as the train departed.

Upon arriving in London, Mr. Bruff was intercepted at the terminus by a small boy dressed in threadbare black cloth, distinguished by the extraordinary prominence of his eyes, which seemed to roll loosely within their sockets. After listening to the boy, Mr. Bruff asked the ladies if they would excuse his escorting them back to Portland Place. I had barely promised Rachel that I would return to explain everything before Mr. Bruff seized my arm and hurried me into a cab. The boy with the ill-secured eyes took his place on the box beside the driver, who was directed to Lombard Street.

"News from the bank?" I asked as we started.

"News of Mr. Luker," Mr. Bruff replied. "An hour ago, he was seen leaving his house in Lambeth in a cab, accompanied by two men recognized by my men as plainclothes police. If Mr. Luker's dread of the Indians motivates this precaution, the inference is plain: he intends to remove the Diamond from the bank."

"And we are going to the bank to see what comes of it?"

"Yes—or to hear what has come of it, if it is all over by now. Did you notice my boy on the box?"

"I noticed his eyes."

Mr. Bruff laughed. "They call him 'Gooseberry' at the office. I employ him for errands, and I wish my clerks who have nicknamed him were as reliable as he is. Despite his eyes, Gooseberry is one of the sharpest boys in London, Mr. Blake."

Twenty minutes to five, we drew up before the bank. Gooseberry looked longingly at his master as the cab door opened.

"Do you want to come in too?" Mr. Bruff asked kindly. "Come in, and keep at my heels. He's as quick as lightning," he whispered to me. "Two words suffice with Gooseberry where twenty would be needed with another boy."

We entered the bank. The outer office was crowded with people waiting to transact business before the five o'clock closing. Two men approached Mr. Bruff immediately.

"Well," asked the lawyer. "Have you seen him?"

"He passed us here half an hour since and went into the inner office."

"Has he not come out again?"

"No, sir."

Mr. Bruff turned to me. "Let us wait."

I scanned the crowd for the three Indians. None were visible. The only person with a noticeably dark complexion was a tall man in a pilot coat and round hat, resembling a sailor. Could he be one of them in disguise? Impossible; he was far too tall, and his face, partially hidden by a bushy black beard, was twice the breadth of any of the Indians'.

"They must have a spy somewhere," Mr. Bruff muttered, glancing at the dark sailor. "And he may be the man."

Before he could elaborate, his coat-tail was pulled by his attendant sprite, Gooseberry. Mr. Bruff looked where the boy pointed. "Hush!" he said. "Here is Mr. Luker!"

The money-lender emerged from the inner regions, flanked by his two guardian policemen.

"Keep your eye on him," Mr. Bruff whispered. "If he passes the Diamond to anyone, he will pass it here."

Ignoring us, Mr. Luker slowly navigated the crowd, moving from the thickest to the thinnest parts. I distinctly saw his hand move as he passed a short, stout man in a sober grey suit. The man started slightly and looked after him. Mr. Luker continued slowly to the door, where his guards positioned themselves on either side. They were followed by one of Mr. Bruff's men, and then they vanished from sight.

I looked at the lawyer and then signaled toward the man in the grey suit. "Yes!" Mr. Bruff whispered. "I saw it too!" He turned to search for his second man, who was nowhere to be seen, and then for Gooseberry, who had disappeared.

"What the devil does it mean?" Mr. Bruff said angrily. "They have both left us at the very moment we need them most."

It was the man in the grey suit's turn to transact business. He paid a cheque, received a receipt, and turned to leave.

"What is to be done?" Mr. Bruff asked. "_We_ can't degrade ourselves by following him."

"_I_ can!" I declared. "I wouldn't lose sight of that man for ten thousand pounds!"

"In that case," Mr. Bruff rejoined, "I wouldn't lose sight of _you_ for twice the money. A nice occupation for a man in my position," he muttered as we followed the stranger out. "For Heaven's sake don't mention it. I should be ruined if it were known."

The man in the grey suit boarded an omnibus heading west. We followed. Mr. Bruff, despite his age, still possessed latent reserves of youth; I assert it positively, he blushed when he took his seat. The man alighted in Oxford Street and entered a chemist's shop.

"My chemist!" Mr. Bruff exclaimed. "I am afraid we have made a mistake."

We entered. Mr. Bruff and the proprietor exchanged a few words in private. The lawyer returned with a crestfallen face.

"It's greatly to our credit," he said, taking my arm. "That's one comfort!"

"What is to our credit?" I asked.

"Mr. Blake! You and I are the two worst amateur detectives that ever tried their hands at the trade. The man in the grey suit has been in the chemist's service for thirty years. He was sent to the bank to pay money to his master's account—and he knows no more of the Moonstone than the babe unborn."

I asked what was next.

"Come back to my office," Mr. Bruff said. "Gooseberry and my second man have evidently followed someone else. Let us hope they had their eyes about them!"

At Gray's Inn Square, the second man had already arrived, having waited over a quarter of an hour.

"Well!" Mr. Bruff asked. "What's your news?"

"I am sorry to say, sir," the man replied, "that I have made a mistake. I could have sworn I saw Mr. Luker pass something to an elderly gentleman in a light-colored paletot. The elderly gentleman turns out to be a most respectable master iron-monger in Eastcheap."

"Where is Gooseberry?" Mr. Bruff asked resignedly.

The man stared. "I don't know, sir. I have seen nothing of him since I left the bank."

Mr. Bruff dismissed him. "One of two things," he told me. "Either Gooseberry has run away, or he is hunting on his own account. What do you say to dining here, on the chance the boy returns in an hour or two? I have good wine in the cellar and we can get a chop from the coffee-house."

We dined at Mr. Bruff's chambers. Before the cloth was removed, a person was announced. Was it Gooseberry? No, it was the man employed to follow Mr. Luker. His report presented no feature of interest. Mr. Luker had returned home, dismissed his guards, and barred his doors. No signs of the Indians were visible. Mr. Bruff dismissed the man for the night.

"Do you think Mr. Luker has taken the Moonstone home?" I asked.

"Not he," said Mr. Bruff. "He would never have dismissed his two policemen if he had run the risk of keeping the Diamond in his own house again."

We waited another half-hour for the boy in vain. It was time for Mr. Bruff to go to Hampstead and for me to return to Rachel in Portland Place. I left my card with the porter, instructing him to deliver a note stating I would be at my lodgings at half-past ten, to be given to the boy if he returned.

Some men have a knack of keeping appointments; others, like me, have a knack of missing them. I spent the evening at Portland Place on the same seat as Rachel, in a room forty feet long with Mrs. Merridew at the far end. Does anyone wonder I arrived home at half-past twelve instead of half-past ten? How heartless that person must be! And how earnestly I hope I may never make that person's acquaintance!

My servant handed me a morsel of paper. In neat legal handwriting, it read: "If you please, sir, I am getting sleepy. I will come back tomorrow morning, between nine and ten." Inquiry revealed that the boy with extraordinary eyes had called, presented my card and message, waited an hour, fallen asleep and woken again, written a line for me, and gone home after informing the servant that "he was fit for nothing unless he got his night's rest."

At nine the next morning, I was ready for my visitor. At half-past nine, I heard steps outside. "Come in, Gooseberry!" I called. "Thank you, sir," answered a grave and melancholy voice. The door opened. I started to my feet and confronted—Sergeant Cuff!

"I thought I would look in here, Mr. Blake, on the chance of your being in town, before I wrote to Yorkshire," the Sergeant said.

He was as dreary and lean as ever. His eyes retained their old trick of "looking as if they expected something more from you than you were aware of yourself." Yet, regarding dress, the great Cuff was changed beyond recognition. He wore a broad-brimmed white hat, a light shooting jacket, white trousers, and drab gaiters, carrying a stout oak stick. His entire aim seemed to be to look as if he had lived in the country all his life. When I complimented him on his metamorphosis, he declined to take it as a joke, complaining gravely of London's noises and smells. I offered him breakfast; the innocent countryman was quite shocked. His breakfast hour was half-past six, and he went to bed with the cocks and hens!

"I only got back from Ireland last night," the Sergeant said, turning to the practical object of his visit. "Before I went to bed, I read your letter telling me what has happened since my inquiry after the Diamond was suspended last year. There's only one thing to be said about the matter on my side. I completely mistook my case. How any man living was to have seen things in their true light in such a situation, I don't profess to know. But that doesn't alter the facts. I own that I made a mess of it. Not the first mess, Mr. Blake, which has distinguished my professional career! It's only in books that the officers of the detective force are superior to the weakness of making a mistake."

"You have come in the nick of time to recover your reputation," I said.

"I beg your pardon, Mr. Blake," he rejoined. "Now that I have retired from business, I don't care a straw about my reputation. I have done with my reputation, thank God! I am here, sir, in grateful remembrance of the late Lady Verinder's liberality to me. I will go back to my old work—if you want me, and if you will trust me—on that consideration, and on no other. Not a farthing of money is to pass from you to me. This is on honour. Now tell me, Mr. Blake, how the case stands since you

June 27th, 1849.

My instructions from Sergeant Cuff were clear and urgent: track three men suspected of murder, described vaguely as "Indians." They had been spotted at Tower Wharf that very morning, boarding a steamer destined for Rotterdam.

I departed London aboard a rival company's vessel on the morning of Thursday, the 28th. Upon arriving in Rotterdam, I located the commander of the Wednesday's ship. He confirmed that the three men had indeed been passengers, but only as far as Gravesend. There, the situation had taken a peculiar turn. Off the coast, the spokesman of the trio had asked for the time of arrival at Calais. When informed that the steamer was bound for Rotterdam, he feigned the utmost distress and surprise at their navigational error. Claiming they were willing to forfeit their passage money to correct the mistake, they pleaded to be set ashore. Moved by their plight as strangers in a foreign land, the commander signaled for a boat, and the three men disembarked.

It was obvious to me that this maneuver was premeditated—a calculated attempt to shed their trail. I returned to England immediately, leaving the steamer at Gravesend. My investigation revealed that the men had returned to London and subsequently traveled to Plymouth. Local inquiries confirmed that forty-eight hours prior, they had sailed on the *Bewley Castle*, an East Indiaman bound directly for Bombay.

Armed with this intelligence, Sergeant Cuff coordinated with the authorities in Bombay to intercept the vessel the moment it entered the port. With the police ready to board, my involvement in the case concluded. I have heard nothing further since.

*Statement regarding the passengers on the *Bewley Castle*, 1849.*

At the request of Sergeant Cuff, I record the following concerning three men, believed to be Hindus, who were passengers under my command on the voyage to Bombay.

These men joined us at Plymouth. Throughout the outward passage, I received no complaints regarding their conduct. They were quartered in the forward section of the vessel, and I had few opportunities to observe them personally.

However, the voyage was marred by a severe calmer lasting three days and nights off the coast of India. Without the ship's journal to consult, I cannot recall the exact coordinates, but I can attest that the currents drifted us closer to the land. When the wind finally returned, we reached port within twenty-four hours.

As any sailor knows, discipline inevitably relaxes during a prolonged calm. On our ship, this laxity permitted certain gentlemen passengers to lower the smaller boats. Amused by the cool evening air, they spent their time rowing and swimming. Protocol dictated that these boats be hoisted back into their davits upon completion. Instead, they were left moored alongside the hull. The oppressive heat and the frustration of the stillness had sapped the resolve of both officers and crew; duty seemed a burden too heavy to bear.

On the third night, the watch on deck reported nothing unusual. But when morning broke, the smallest boat was gone. Shortly thereafter, reports surfaced that the three Hindu passengers were missing as well.

If they had stolen the boat shortly after dark—as I am convinced they did—we were too close to the shore for a pursuit to be effective once the loss was discovered in the morning. Given the calm conditions, I have no doubt they reached land before dawn, despite the fatigue and difficulty of rowing against the current.

Upon docking, I learned for the first time why these passengers had seized the opportunity to flee. I could offer the authorities no more than what I have stated here. They held me responsible for the relaxation of discipline on board. I have expressed my regrets to them and to the ship's owners. Since that time, there has been no word from the three Hindus.
